# Sūrah al-Qamar (Chapter 54) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Introduction to the Sūrah

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Chapter Named After a Miracle**

Sūrah al-Qamar, "The Moon," is the fifty-fourth chapter of the Qur'an and one of its most compactly powerful. It contains fifty-five verses, and although it is short — roughly two pages of the standard muṣḥaf — it performs a task no longer sūrah performs in the same way: it begins with a miracle the eyewitnesses were standing under when it happened, and it ends with the eternal seat of those who believed it. Almost everything between those two poles is warning. The sūrah takes its name from the moon mentioned in its first verse, and the early community also knew it by its opening words, *Iqtarabat al-sāʿah*, "The Hour has drawn nigh" — a title preserved by Ibn Kathīr and al-Ālūsī among others. Naming a chapter by a celestial body is itself significant. The moon is the most universally visible object in the night sky, the one sign every human being on earth shares without effort, without cost, and without a teacher. When God chooses it as the theatre of His proof, He is choosing the witness that nobody can claim not to have had access to.

Most commentators hold that al-Qamar is a Makkan sūrah in its entirety, revealed in the years when the Muslim community was a persecuted minority with no state, no army, and no leverage of any kind except the truth it carried. A minority view records that verse 45, or verses 44–46, belong to the Madinan period, an opinion transmitted through al-Ālūsī and al-Jīlānī-style sources and mentioned in the commentary tradition. The overwhelming Makkan character of the chapter, however, is stamped on its language: short, percussive verses; oaths and refrains; long narrative sweeps across former nations; no legislation, no inheritance, no warfare rulings, no contractual law. This is the Qur'an in its earliest register — the register of *ʿaqīdah* and *ākhirah*, of belief and the Last Day — and it is aimed at hearts that had hardened rather than at a society that needed regulating.

**Its Place Between al-Najm and al-Raḥmān**

The sūrah's position in the written order is not accidental. It follows Sūrah al-Najm (53), which had just closed with the words:

> *"The imminent is nigh. None but God can disclose it."* (Qur'an 53:57–58)

Al-Qamar opens by repeating the claim with a different verb of approach: *"The Hour has drawn nigh"* (54:1). Where al-Najm declared that the imminent thing is near, al-Qamar supplies the evidence that nearness has already begun to disclose itself. Likewise, al-Najm's closing verses mentioned the destruction of the people of Noah, of ʿĀd, and of Thamūd in three compressed lines:

> *"And that it is He who destroyed the former ʿĀd, and Thamūd, and spared not a soul, and the people of Noah before — indeed they were more unjust and more rebellious."* (Qur'an 53:50–52)

Al-Qamar then takes those same three names and unfolds them, adding Lot and Pharaoh. The two chapters read as thesis and demonstration: al-Najm states the list, al-Qamar opens the files. Ahead of it stands Sūrah al-Raḥmān (55), the sūrah of mercy and the Garden, and the transition is equally deliberate. Al-Qamar ends with the reverent "amidst Gardens and a stream, upon a seat of truth before an Omnipotent King" (54:54–55); al-Raḥmān then begins with the Name from which all of that flows: *"The Compassionate (al-Raḥmān). He taught the Qur'an."* The sūrah of the Hour hands the reader over to the sūrah of mercy — warning completed, then consolation expanded.

**Recited on the Two Greatest Gatherings**

The Prophet ﷺ gave this chapter an honoured liturgical place. Muslim records in his Ṣaḥīḥ, from Abū Wāqid al-Laythī:

> *"The Messenger of God ﷺ used to recite in the prayers of the two ʿĪds Qāf and Iqtarabat al-sāʿah (al-Qamar)."* (Muslim 891)

The choice is instructive. ʿĪd is the largest public assembly of the Muslim year — a day of joy, of new clothes, of children and visiting relatives, of a city gathered in one open space. It is precisely the moment when the greatest number of people are attentive and the least likely to be thinking about death. Into that atmosphere the Prophet ﷺ placed a sūrah whose first verse announces the nearness of the Hour and whose body is a catalogue of civilizations erased from the earth. He did this not to spoil the celebration but to keep it honest: a community that celebrates without remembering that its celebration is borrowed time will eventually mistake the celebration for permanence. Ibn Kathīr notes the same pairing was used at other major prayer gatherings, including at ʿArafah. Al-Qamar is therefore a sūrah designed for crowds — for the day when Islam addresses not the individual seeker in a quiet room but the whole society in daylight.

**The Night the Moon Split**

The occasion traditionally associated with the opening verses is one of the most widely transmitted events of the Makkan period. Al-Bukhārī and Muslim both record it, in multiple chains, from ʿAbdullāh ibn Masʿūd, Anas ibn Mālik, and Jubayr ibn Muṭʿim. Ibn Masʿūd's version reads:

> *"While we were with the Messenger of God ﷺ at Minā, the moon split into two halves — one half behind the mountain and the other half on this side of it — and the Messenger of God ﷺ said to us: 'Bear witness.'"* (al-Bukhārī 3869; Muslim 2800)

Anas's version in Muslim gives the motive:

> *"The people of Makkah asked the Messenger of God ﷺ to show them a sign, and he showed them the moon split into two halves, until they saw the cave of Ḥirāʾ between its two parts."* (Muslim 2802; see also al-Bukhārī 3637, 4865)

The setting was the plain of Minā, just outside Makkah, in the evening. The Quraysh had been disputing with the Prophet ﷺ for days, demanding a physical sign as proof of his prophethood. He raised his hand and pointed at the moon, and it appeared to separate into two halves, one on either side of Mount Ḥirāʾ, so that the mountain's silhouette was visible through the gap. He then said, as Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī transmit, "Bear witness!" — and the line of separation closed. The commentators record the reaction of the opponents: stunned silence, followed almost immediately by the accusation that survives in verse 2, *"Incessant sorcery!"* One account preserves a disbeliever's reasoning: "Muhammad has merely bewitched us, but he cannot bewitch the entire world. Let us wait for travelers from faraway places and hear what reports they bring." When travelers arrived days later, they confirmed that they too had seen the moon split (Ibn Kathīr).

This detail is the hinge of the whole episode. The accusation of sorcery required that the event be local — an illusion projected onto one crowd. The arrival of independent witnesses from outside Makkah destroyed that explanation, and the opponents simply moved to a new one. That movement, from one excuse to the next without ever revisiting the first, is the psychological phenomenon the sūrah diagnoses in verses 2–3: this is not a failure of evidence but a failure of willingness.

**The Architecture of the Chapter**

The sūrah is built in a way that makes it unusually easy to hold in memory — which is fitting, given that its central refrain is about the Qur'an being easy to remember. It opens with the cosmic sign and the immediate diagnosis of rejection (vv. 1–8), a passage that moves from the moon in the sky to the graves of the earth. It then runs five case studies from sacred history, each with the same four-part shape: the people, their denial, their punishment, and the refrain. Noah occupies verses 9–17; ʿĀd verses 18–22; Thamūd verses 23–32; Lot verses 33–40; Pharaoh verses 41–42. The five are not of equal length, and the variation is meaningful: Noah, the first messenger to humanity and the longest-serving, receives nine verses; Pharaoh, the most powerful tyrant the Qur'an names, receives two. Length of coverage follows neither seniority nor strength but the lesson required.

After the five histories comes the confrontation with the present audience (vv. 43–46), then the verdict on the guilty and the punishment they will taste (vv. 47–48), then the theological centre of the sūrah — God's decree and the written record (vv. 49–53) — and finally the two verses of reward that close the chapter (vv. 54–55). The movement is from sign, to history, to warning, to doctrine, to promise. A reader who follows it experiences exactly the sequence the Qur'an intends for the heart: first amazement, then evidence, then fear, then understanding, then hope.

**Two Refrains and the Balance They Create**

Al-Qamar is the only sūrah in the Qur'an built on two alternating refrains. The first is severe:

> *"How, then, were My Punishment and My warnings?"* (54:16, 18, 21, 30)

It appears at the close of each destroyed nation's account, four times, in the form of a question that expects no answer because the answer is lying in the rubble. The second refrain is merciful and appears equally often:

> *"And indeed We have made the Qur'an easy to remember; so is there any who remembers?"* (54:17, 22, 32, 40)

Between every act of destruction God places an act of facilitation. The commentators observed the deliberate counterpoint: the first refrain emphasizes the wrath that falls on those who reject the signs; the second emphasizes the mercy by which God makes access to those signs easy. Al-Qushayrī and Ibn ʿAjībah draw out the pastoral logic — the destruction is announced, and immediately the door is reopened. Nobody in the sūrah is punished without first having been made able to understand. That is why the question at the end of every refrain is not "so were they not destroyed?" but "so is there any who remembers?" The emphasis never rests on the catastrophe; it always returns to the reader.

**Why This Chapter Still Speaks**

Modern readers sometimes assume that a chapter about the moon splitting and nations being drowned belongs to a pre-scientific imagination and has nothing to say to them. The assumption misses what the sūrah is actually about. Its subject is not the moon; its subject is the human capacity to look directly at evidence and refuse it. Every institution that has ever failed — a family, a company, a state, a person's own health — failed at the same point al-Qamar describes: the warning was delivered, the warning was understood, and the warning was reclassified as exaggeration, then as hostility, then as nonsense, until the thing it warned about arrived. The five nations in this sūrah are five demonstrations of one law. God states that law in verse 43 in the form of a question addressed to every confident generation that has ever believed itself exempt:

> *"Are your disbelievers better than those?"* (Qur'an 54:43)

The sūrah's final answer is not despair but an invitation so generous it borders on startling: We have made it easy. The Hour has drawn near, the moon has split, nations have been buried, and yet the last word before the Garden is the word *dhākir* — one who remembers. This is the chapter that begins with the sky falling open and ends with a seat of truth.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:1

> **The Hour has drawn nigh, and the moon has been cleaved.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Clauses, One Announcement**

The sūrah opens with a single sentence containing two statements of fact, joined by *wa*, "and": *iqtarabati al-sāʿatu wa-inshaqqa al-qamar* — "The Hour has drawn near, and the moon has been split." There is no oath, no disconnected letters, no invocation of mercy at the head of the verse. God simply states two things, in the past tense, as though both were already accomplished history. The first concerns time; the second concerns the sky. Read together, they form an argument: the object by which human beings have always measured time has itself been fractured, and the time it measures is running out.

The verb *iqtaraba* is the eighth form of the root *q-r-b*, "to be near." The eighth form is not the neutral statement of nearness but the announcement of an approach already underway — the difference between "the train is nearby" and "the train is arriving." The Qur'an uses this same verb for the same subject in another Makkan sūrah:

> *"For mankind their reckoning draws nigh (iqtaraba), while they turn away, heedless."* (Qur'an 21:1)

Al-Ṭabarī connects the two passages directly, and Ibn Kathīr reads al-Qamar's opening as an expansion of al-Najm's closing line, *"The imminent is nigh"* (53:57). The commentators — Ibn al-Jawzī among them — note that *al-sāʿah*, "the Hour," carries a double reference: the Final Hour of the world's destruction, and the Resurrection that immediately follows it. In Qur'anic usage *al-sāʿah* is almost always a proper name rather than a common noun. It is not "an hour" but "the Hour," the single appointed moment toward which all previous hours have been counting down.

**The Passive That Assigns the Actor**

The second verb, *inshaqqa*, is the seventh form of *sh-q-q*, "to split, to cleave, to tear open." The form is reflexive-passive in effect: the moon was split, or split apart. The grammatical point matters theologically. The Qur'an does not say the moon split itself, nor does it name a splitter; it presents the event as something done to the moon, and the agent is understood from everything else in the sentence. When God speaks of His own acts in this sūrah He uses the royal "We" with unambiguous force — *"So We opened the gates of the sky"* (54:11), *"Truly We sent upon them a howling wind"* (54:19), *"Truly We sent upon them a single cry"* (54:31). The splitting of the moon belongs to that same series of divine acts, and the passive verb quietly places it there without argument.

The verb is in the perfect tense, and the majority of commentators take it as reporting a completed historical event: the miracle at Minā narrated by ʿAbdullāh ibn Masʿūd, Anas ibn Mālik, and Jubayr ibn Muṭʿim and recorded in both Ṣaḥīḥ collections:

> *"While we were with the Messenger of God ﷺ at Minā, the moon split into two halves — one half behind the mountain and the other half on this side of it — and the Messenger of God ﷺ said to us: 'Bear witness.'"* (al-Bukhārī 3869; Muslim 2800)

A minority reading mentioned by al-Iṣfahānī, al-Maybudī, and al-Zamakhsharī understands the second clause as future in sense: "and the moon will be cleaved," making it one of the signs of the end of time alongside the darkening described elsewhere:

> *"And the moon is darkened (khasafa), and the sun and the moon are joined together."* (Qur'an 75:8–9)

Significantly, every commentator who mentions this second opinion also narrates the miracle. The two readings were never treated as rivals in the classical tradition; they were treated as two disclosures of one reality. The moon was split once, before witnesses, as a proof given to a single generation — and it will be split finally, before everyone, as a sign that requires no proof.

**Arabic Grammar as an Act of Faith**

Why does the Qur'an use the past tense for something that has not yet happened? Arabic rhetoric answers this with a principle the grammarians called *taḥqīq al-wuqūʿ* — "the affirmation of occurrence": when an event is certain, the language of certainty is the past tense, because for the Knower of the unseen the distinction between "will be" and "has been" has no purchase. The Qur'an applies the principle at its most extreme in another eschatological passage:

> *"The Command of God has come; so seek not to hasten it."* (Qur'an 16:1)

*Atā*, "has come," for an event nobody had yet seen. Al-Zamakhsharī explains the device as deliberate: the perfect tense removes the possibility of doubt from the listener's mind by presenting the future as though it had already been filed among completed facts. The Hour is not merely going to arrive; in the register of divine speech it has, in effect, already arrived. Every human being alive is standing inside a day that has already been declared finished.

**The Moon Is the Instrument of Human Reckoning**

There is a deeper fitness in the choice of the moon. Of all celestial bodies, the moon is the one human beings turned into a calendar. God says so explicitly:

> *"It is He who made the sun a shining glory and the moon a light, and measured for it stages, that you might know the number of years and the reckoning. God created not that save in truth."* (Qur'an 10:5)

> *"The sun and the moon follow a reckoning (ḥusbān)."* (Qur'an 55:5)

> *"They ask thee of the new moons. Say: They are appointed times for mankind and the pilgrimage."* (Qur'an 2:189)

The moon is the clock. Islamic worship is lunar in its skeleton: Ramadan begins with its sighting, Shawwāl with its sighting, Dhū al-Ḥijjah with its sighting, and the fasts and festivals of the entire ummah hinge on a crescent. When God therefore splits the moon before a crowd in Makkah, He is not performing a spectacle on a random object. He is fracturing the measuring instrument itself — the very thing by which people count the passing of their months — as a demonstration that the thing being measured is close to its end. It is the equivalent of stopping a clock in front of a man who keeps insisting there is plenty of time.

**How Much Time Is "Near"?**

The nearness is stated relative to what the tradition called the world's remaining lifespan, and the Prophet ﷺ gave the comparison a physical form. Al-Bukhārī and Muslim record from Sahl ibn Saʿd and from Anas:

> *"I was sent, and the Hour, like these two"* — pointing with his index finger and his middle finger. (al-Bukhārī 4936, 6504; Muslim 2950–2951)

The gesture is the smallest possible distance the hand can express: two fingers with nothing between them. In another report the image is expanded into a parable of hired laborers, in which the Jews worked from dawn to midday for one qīrāṭ, the Christians from midday to ʿAṣr for one qīrāṭ, and this community from ʿAṣr to sunset for two qīrāṭs — and the earlier workers complained that they had labored longer for less pay (al-Bukhārī 3459, from Ibn ʿUmar). The final stretch of the day is the shortest stretch of the day. Fourteen centuries have passed since Minā, and the proportion is unchanged: a single afternoon does not become long merely because the sun seems slow.

**The Nearness That Each Reader Carries**

The commentators did not leave the verse at the cosmic level, because the cosmic level does not by itself change a person. The Hour has a private version. Every human being has an appointed hour, and when it arrives, the general Hour has arrived for him in every way that matters to him: the accounting begins, the testing ends, and no further information will change his position. This is why the Prophet ﷺ's instruction to his Companions was framed not in terms of the world's end but in terms of the individual's evening:

> *"If you reach the evening, do not wait for the morning; and if you reach the morning, do not wait for the evening. Take from your health for your illness, and from your life for your death."* (al-Bukhārī 6416, from Ibn ʿUmar)

That is the practical exegesis of *iqtarabat*. A person who genuinely believes an hour is near behaves differently in the last ten minutes of it: he stops rearranging, stops postponing the apology, stops treating the remaining time as though it were renewable. The verse does not ask for terror; it asks for the ordinary, unsentimental attention of someone who has looked at the clock and understood it.

## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:2

> **And if they see a sign, they turn away and say, "Incessant sorcery!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Word That Carries Three Meanings at Once**

*Wa-in yaraw āyatan yuʿriḍū wa-yaqūlū siḥrun mustamirr.* The first substantive word of the verse is *āyah*. In Arabic it denotes a sign, a mark, a token, a proof, and a landmark — anything that indicates something other than itself. The Qur'an then uses the single word in three overlapping senses, and this verse activates all of them simultaneously. *Āyah* is a verse of revelation (the Qur'an's own units are called *āyāt*); *āyah* is a miracle performed by a prophet as a warrant for his claim; and *āyah* is a natural phenomenon functioning as evidence of God — the alternation of night and day, the ships on the sea, the rain.

The moon that split was all three at once. It was a miracle, visible to a crowd; it was a sign in creation, involving a body God had made the instrument of time; and it was confirmed by a verse of revelation recited immediately afterward. The verse therefore begins by granting the opponents everything they had asked for. They demanded a sign (*āyah*); a sign (*āyah*) arrived. What follows is not a story about insufficient evidence.

**Iʿrāḍ: The Turn of the Shoulder**

The response is described with a precise verb: *yuʿriḍū*, from the root *ʿ-r-ḍ*, meaning to turn the side away, to present one's flank, to angle the body off from something. Arabic has softer words for inattention — *ghafula* (to be heedless), *nasīya* (to forget), *sahā* (to lapse mentally). The Qur'an chooses none of them. *Iʿrāḍ* is a physical act performed with a functioning body: the person sees, understands, and rotates away. The commentators link the usage to another Makkan verse:

> *"And who does greater wrong than one who has been reminded of the signs of his Lord, then turns away (aʿraḍa) from them?"* (Qur'an 32:22; cf. 6:157; 18:57)

The grammatical construction is also deliberate: *in yaraw āyatan* — "if they see a sign." The verb *yaraw* is the jussive of *raʾā*, "to see," and here it means genuine seeing, not glancing. The condition is fulfilled. The seeing happens. And the result clause, *yuʿriḍū*, follows automatically, as though the turning were a reflex wired into the same nerve as the sight. That is the psychology of a settled disposition: at a certain point of resistance, perception no longer leads to consideration but directly to defense.

**Siḥr: Why the Accusation Was Chosen**

*Siḥr* comes from a root whose basic sense is hiddenness and subtlety — the Arabs used *saḥar* for the last part of the night because it is faint and concealed, and *sāḥir* for one who produces effects by means the observer cannot detect. Magic in the Arabic conception is not a violation of nature but the exploitation of concealed causes: something happens, the cause is hidden, and the observer cannot tell the difference between a hidden cause and no cause at all. That is exactly why the accusation was intellectually attractive. It preserves the event's reality while cancelling its meaning. The moon did split — nobody denies it — but it means nothing, because it was produced by a trick.

The Qur'an records that this is not an original response but a recycled one, and it says so with a generalization that covers every prophet:

> *"Thus not a messenger came unto those before them, but they said: 'A sorcerer or a madman!' (sāḥirun aw majnūn)"* (Qur'an 51:52)

Pharaoh's magicians' court made the same claim about Moses's staff:

> *"When the truth came unto them from Us, they said: 'This is manifest sorcery.'"* (Qur'an 10:76)

And the Makkan elite made it about the Qur'an itself:

> *"Truly this is nothing but sorcery handed down (siḥrun yuʾthar)."* (Qur'an 74:24)

The pattern is so consistent that Ibn Kathīr treats verse 2 less as a report about Minā than as a diagnosis of a permanent human type. The accusation of sorcery is the cheapest available explanation for a sign, because it is the only explanation that requires nothing of the person making it. To say "this is a miracle" is to accept a Lord. To say "this is magic" is to accept nothing at all and go home unchanged.

**Mustamirr: A Word That Means Both "Unceasing" and "Well-Twisted"**

The qualifier *mustamirr* is the tenth form of the root *m-r-r*, and it carries several senses the commentators worked through. Al-Ṭabarī records the primary meaning as "continuous, unceasing, persistent" — sorcery that keeps coming, that has become a habit of this man's. The tenth form intensifies and seeks, so *mustamirr* suggests something that presses onward without stopping.

Al-Zamakhsharī and others note a second sense drawn from the same root: *marr al-ḥabl*, a rope that has been twisted tightly, is *mumarr*/*mustamirr*, meaning strong, firm, well-bound. On this reading the opponents are conceding potency: "This is powerful sorcery — sorcery so strong it can split the moon." The admission is revealing. A man who says "this is fake" has dismissed the event; a man who says "this is powerfully fake" has granted the event's magnitude and only contests its source. The Quraysh were not doubting what they saw; they were reassigning it.

A third sense lies in the root's association with bitterness and with passing (*marra*, "he passed by"). Some readers heard an irony in the opponents' own phrase: sorcery *mustamirr* is sorcery that passes, that will be gone by morning. And they were right — the line of separation closed, and the moon was whole again. But the passing of the sign did not pass the sign's meaning, which is the very point of the refrain that comes fifteen verses later.

**The Slogan and the Sentence**

There is a social observation buried in the verse's rhythm. *Siḥrun mustamirr* is four syllables, metrically tight, easy to shout, and it needs no argument to sustain it. A crowd under stress reaches for the shortest available phrase, and once the phrase exists it spreads, because repeating it costs nothing and questioning it costs status. The Qur'an has preserved the actual slogan rather than paraphrasing the opponents' reasoning, which is a subtle point of honesty: their position is reported at its real intellectual length. There was no reasoning. There was a chant.

This is a mechanism every modern reader can recognize. Contemporary argument has an entire vocabulary of *iʿrāḍ* — dismissive labels attached to a claim so that the claim need never be examined: "propaganda," "ideology," "marketing," "conspiracy," "lobbying," "outdated." Each of these words, like *siḥr*, can be true; and each, like *siḥr*, functions most often as a way to convert an inconvenient fact into a comfortable story about the person reporting it. The logical fallacy has a name in modern usage — the genetic fallacy, judging a claim by its source rather than its content — and the Qur'an described it fourteen centuries before it was named, in the middle of an account of a split moon.

**What the Turn-Away Costs, and What It Cannot Cancel**

Verse 2 leaves the reader with a hard truth about the human heart: exposure to evidence does not produce belief, because belief requires the will's cooperation. The sign is given to the eye, and the eye delivers it to a judge who has already ruled. Al-Qurṭubī draws the pastoral consequence for the one who carries the message. When the response to a clear sign is *iʿrāḍ* plus a slogan, further demonstration is not merely useless but counterproductive, because it gives the resistant party additional material to reclassify. The Qur'an therefore moves on almost immediately — verse 6 will tell the Prophet ﷺ to turn away from them in return — and turns instead to history, where the evidence is no longer negotiable because the outcomes have already been settled.

The turn-away also cannot cancel what was turned away from. The moon was split whether or not the observers filed the event correctly, and the record of it survived precisely because the ones who turned away were not the only witnesses. Ibn Kathīr preserves the detail that travelers arriving in Makkah days later confirmed they had seen the same thing from outside the city. The accusation of sorcery required locality; the witnesses from abroad removed it. The response to that discovery, in the sūrah's own account, was not revision but repetition.

## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:3

> **They denied and followed their caprices — and every matter shall be made to endure.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Verb in the Intensive Form**

*Kadhdhabū wa-ttabaʿū ahwāʾahum.* The first verb is the second form of *k-dh-b*, and the second form in Arabic does two things: it intensifies, and it makes habitual. *Kadhaba* is to tell a lie or to deny; *kadhdhaba* is to deny repeatedly, thoroughly, as a settled practice. The Qur'an uses this intensive form for the rejection of the messengers throughout: *"The people of Noah denied (kadhdhabat) before them"* (54:9); *"ʿĀd denied (kadhdhabat)"* (54:18); *"Thamūd denied (kadhdhabat) the warnings"* (54:23). Al-Ṭabarī explains the verb here as denial of God's signs after they had come, and Ibn al-Jawzī adds the denial of the Prophet himself. What the intensive form contributes is the notion of a process completed: this was not a first hesitation before new information but a repeated, deliberate refusal after it.

The verse thus identifies two acts, and their order is not incidental. They denied — and they followed their caprices. Al-Zamakhsharī and al-Bayḍāwī both note the conjunction *wa* linking two things that belong together as cause and effect. In the Qur'an's moral psychology, denial is rarely a conclusion reached by reasoning; it is almost always a downstream product of a preference already established. A person first wants a thing to be untrue, and only then discovers he cannot believe it.

**Hawā: The Desire That Falls**

*Hawā* (plural *ahwāʾ*) comes from the root *h-w-y*, whose basic sense is to fall, to plunge, to drop through empty air, and to be void. *Hawā* is the appetite considered precisely in its falling character: not desire in general — Arabic has *shahwah* for that, and *raghbah* for longing — but the ungoverned pull that carries a person downward past his own judgment. The Qur'an uses it consistently in that negative sense, and it pairs it, again consistently, with its opposite.

> *"O you who believe! Be upholders of justice, witnesses for God, even though it be against yourselves, or your parents and kinsmen... So follow not caprice (fa-lā tattabiʿū al-hawā), that you may act justly."* (Qur'an 4:135)

The construction is exact: do not follow caprice *so that* you may be just. Justice and caprice are presented not as competing values but as incompatible operating systems — one cannot run both at once. The same pairing appears in God's address to David:

> *"O David! We have made thee a viceroy upon the earth; so judge between men in truth, and follow not caprice, lest it lead thee astray from the path of God."* (Qur'an 38:26)

And the Qur'an names the ultimate consequence of this following in a verse that should be read alongside 54:3 by anyone who thinks the subject is minor:

> *"Hast thou seen him who takes his own caprice (hawāhu) as his god (ilāh)? Wouldst thou then be a guardian over him?"* (Qur'an 25:43)

The verse does not say that the man with a caprice also has a god. It says his caprice *is* his god — the thing he ultimately obeys, the authority he cannot overrule, the voice that receives final say when it conflicts with every other voice. Ibn ʿAbbās and the early exegetes explained the description as referring to the pre-Islamic Arab who would worship a stone, and upon finding a finer stone would discard the first for the second. Al-Rāzī generalizes it: whoever obeys his appetite against his knowledge has installed it in the place where God should be. This is why the tradition treats *hawā* not as a temptation but as a rival divinity — and why the sūrah of al-Nāziʿāt, revealed in the same Makkan period, defines salvation as its refusal:

> *"But as for him who feared the standing before his Lord and forbade the soul from caprice (nahā al-nafs ʿan al-hawā), truly the Garden is the refuge."* (Qur'an 79:40–41)

**Two Obediences and No Neutral Ground**

The logic beneath the pairing is a simple binary that the Qur'an presses repeatedly: a human being is a creature who follows. He is built to obey some ordering authority — a text, a tradition, a leader, an appetite, a mood, a market. The question is never whether he follows but what he follows. God states the alternative plainly to Moses's successor community:

> *"And were We to send down unto them a scripture upon parchment, and they were to touch it with their hands, those who disbelieve would surely say: 'This is nothing but manifest sorcery.'"* (Qur'an 6:7)

And to the Prophet ﷺ:

> *"And they will not cease fighting you until they turn you from your religion, if they are able. And whosoever among you turns away (yartadid) from his religion and dies a disbeliever — their works shall fail in this world and the next."* (Qur'an 2:217)

Al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī summarized the anthropology in a sentence preserved in the ascetic literature: the son of Adam is either governed by his Lord or by his stomach and his appetite, and there is no third government. Ibn Taymiyyah develops the same point in his critique of both the philosophers and the antinomians: knowledge that does not produce obedience has not actually been received as knowledge, because the seat of knowing in the Qur'anic account is the heart, and the heart is an organ of love and will before it is an organ of calculation. The man who says "I see the evidence but I choose my desire" has described the Qur'anic diagnosis of himself exactly, and the sūrah's verdict on him is one word: *kadhdhabū*.

**Mustaqirr: Everything Settles Somewhere**

The verse ends by stepping back from the Makkan crowd to a universal law: *wa-kullu amrin mustaqirr* — "and every matter shall be made to endure," or, more literally, "and every affair has a settling." The word is the tenth form of *q-r-r*, the root of stillness, of stability, of coming to rest — the same root as *qarār*, a place of settled dwelling, and *taqrīr*, a confirmation or a final report. The tenth form adds the sense of something brought to its settled state and held there.

Al-Ṭabarī reads the phrase eschatologically: good will be established permanently for those who did good in the Garden, and evil will be established permanently for those who did evil in the Fire. Al-Bayḍāwī reads it more broadly — "everything has a reality of its own and is bound to display that reality in the Hereafter." A third reading, recorded by al-Qurṭubī, takes the word as pointing to an appointed end for each affair, its *mustaqarr*, the point at which it can go no further and must show what it was. The grammarians note the variant vocalization *mustaqarr*, a noun of place or time, which yields "every affair has its settling-point."

All these readings converge on one claim: nothing remains in suspension forever. Human beings live with an unexamined assumption that consequences are optional, or at least indefinitely deferrable — that a wrong can be committed and left unresolved, a truth acknowledged and left unacted upon, a warning heard and left unanswered, and that the situation will simply continue as it is. The verse denies the continuation. Every matter settles. It settles into a fixed state and stays there, the way sediment settles in standing water and can no longer be stirred back into solution, or the way a court file, once closed, cannot be reopened by the defendant's regret.

**The Hinge on Which the Sūrah Turns**

Structurally, this final clause is the door through which the rest of the chapter enters. The sūrah has described a specific confrontation — a sign shown, a shoulder turned, a slogan shouted — and it now generalizes the situation into a law: every affair reaches its settled end. With that law established, the sūrah is licensed to do what it will do for the next forty verses: open the files. It will show five settled matters, five affairs that reached their *mustaqirr* — Noah's people under water, ʿĀd under wind, Thamūd under a cry, Lot's cities under stones, Pharaoh under the sea — and each will be presented as evidence for the general law, and the general law as a warning to the crowd that just chanted.

**Application: The Age of Permanent Provisionality**

The verse addresses a condition characteristic of modern life with unusual precision. Contemporary culture has made postponement into a virtue: options kept open, commitments left undefined, decisions deferred, relationships and careers and beliefs held in a permanent state of "not yet decided." The psychological appeal is obvious — the unsettled state feels like freedom. But the verse identifies the cost: an unsettled matter is not a free matter, it is an unfinished one, and unfinished things do not stay unfinished. They settle, whether or not the person involved chooses the moment. A debt settles. An illness settles. A marriage settles into what it has been trained to be. A character settles into its habits until the habits become the person, and the point at which change was possible passes without being noticed.

The practical counsel that follows from *wa-kullu amrin mustaqirr* is not anxiety but timing. Because every matter settles, the person who wishes to influence the settlement must act before it happens, and must act on the matters that matter. The Prophet ﷺ's formulation of the same principle is short enough to carry through a day:

> *"Take advantage of five before five: your youth before your old age, your health before your sickness, your wealth before your poverty, your free time before your busyness, and your life before your death."* (recorded by al-Ḥākim and al-Bayhaqī, from Ibn ʿAbbās; graded ṣaḥīḥ by al-Albānī)

The verse that began with a denial ends with a settled fact, and the two belong together: those who denied did so because they believed nothing would ever be settled. They were wrong, and the sūrah is about to prove it five times.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:4

> **Indeed reports have come to them wherein is a reproof.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Grammar of Certainty**

*Wa-laqad jāʾahum mina al-anbāʾi mā fīhi muzdajar.* The verse opens with two particles of emphasis stacked upon one another: the lām of oath and *qad*, which together produce "indeed, certainly, without any doubt." Arabic has milder ways of affirming a statement, and the Qur'an reserves this doubled construction for claims it wants placed beyond dispute. The affirmation is necessary because of what has just been said: verse 3 announced that they denied. A reader might suppose the denial resulted from a lack of information — nobody had told them. Verse 4 closes that door. The information had come, in quantity, and in a form designed to restrain.

The verb *jāʾahum*, "has come to them," is worth pausing on. Revelation in the Qur'an is consistently described as arrival rather than as discovery. Human beings do not excavate the truth; it comes to them, from outside, and reaches them where they stand. The same imagery governs the entire prophetic vocabulary: *balāgh*, delivery; *risālah*, message sent; *tanzīl*, sent down. The consequence is a shift in responsibility. If the truth were something to be found by the clever, then its absence among the dull would be excusable. But if the truth *comes*, then the only question is what the recipient does with what arrived — and verse 3 has already answered that question.

**Anbāʾ: The Weightiest Kind of News**

The reports are *anbāʾ*, the plural of *nabaʾ*. Arabic distinguishes between *khabar*, a piece of information of any weight, and *nabaʾ*, news of consequence — a report that matters, that carries implication, that changes something in the one who hears it. The distinction is embedded in the Qur'an's own usage:

> *"Whereof is a great report (nabaʾun ʿaẓīm) — whereabout they differ."* (Qur'an 38:67–68)

> *"Say: It is a great report (nabaʾun ʿaẓīm), from which you turn away."* (Qur'an 38:67–68)

The same root gives Arabic its word for prophet. *Nabī* is most commonly derived by the philologists from *nabaʾ*: the prophet is the one who brings a *nabaʾ*, a report of consequence from God. Al-Rāghib al-Iṣfahānī records this derivation in his lexicon of Qur'anic vocabulary, noting that the prophet is named for his function as a carrier of weighty news rather than for any personal quality. When verse 4 therefore says the reports have come, the word carries a second layer: the reports are of the same genus as prophecy itself. History, in the Qur'an's hands, is not background material. It is *nabaʾ* — news with a verdict attached.

**Muzdajar: The Report That Restraint Is Built Into**

The key term is *muzdajar*, the eighth form of *z-j-r*, a root meaning to drive away, to repel, to rebuke with force. The Arabs used *zajr* for the sharp cry that turns a camel back from a wrong path, and for the harsh word that stops a child mid-act. The eighth form, *izdajara*, means to be deterred, to take the rebuke to heart and desist; and *muzdajar* is the noun of that process — a deterrent, a restraining thing, something built to make a person stop.

Al-Ṭabarī explains the reports as the accounts of destroyed communities who denied their messengers, of which this sūrah will shortly give five. Ibn al-Jawzī specifies the same. The word *muzdajar* adds the function: the report is not merely informative but deterrent. It rebukes the disbeliever for his own denial and idolatry, and it is intended to stop him from persisting. The Qur'an states the same principle about sacred history in another place, in terms that could serve as a heading for this whole sūrah:

> *"Indeed in their stories is a lesson (ʿibratun) for those of understanding. It is no fabricated discourse, but a confirmation of what came before it, a detailed exposition of all things, and a guidance and a mercy for a people who believe."* (Qur'an 12:111)

*ʿIbrah* is from the root *ʿ-b-r*, to cross over — the same root as *ʿibārah*, an expression, and *maʿbar*, a crossing-place. A lesson is called *ʿibrah* because it carries the mind across from the seen event to the unseen law. That is the mechanism the Qur'an is using here, and it explains why the sūrah devotes more than half its length to five old civilizations. The report of the flood is not about water. It is a crossing-place from Noah's generation to ours.

**Why God Argues With History Rather Than With Logic**

There is a deliberate method in the choice of narrative as the primary instrument of deterrence. The Qur'an contains rational argument — the argument from design in 30:20–27, the argument from the impossibility of multiple gods in 21:22, the argument against resurrection's absurdity in 36:78–79 — but its dominant mode is case law. Five destroyed nations function the way five precedents function in a legal system: they establish that a rule has been enforced, not merely announced.

The reason is anthropological, and the tradition understood it. A proposition can be debated indefinitely; an outcome cannot. When a person is told "injustice leads to ruin," he can dispute the definition of injustice, the mechanism of ruin, and the timescale of the claim. When he is told "Thamūd said this, then this happened, and the stone houses they carved are still standing in the valley you pass through on the caravan road," the debate closes. The Quraysh were traders. Their caravans went north to Syria through the territory of Thamūd and passed the ruins of al-Ḥijr, and they went south through the land of ʿĀd. The Qur'an reminds them of this directly:

> *"And how many a town have We destroyed whose way of life was insolent! These are their dwellings, uninhabited after them, save a little. And We are the Inheritors."* (Qur'an 28:58)

> *"You pass by them in the morning, and by night. Will you not then reflect?"* (Qur'an 37:137–138)

The reports are not distant legends requiring faith in a foreign archive. They are roadside. Ibn Kathīr makes the point explicitly when commenting on the parallel passages: God's proof against the Quraysh was visible from their own trade route.

**The Precedent and the Person Who Will Not Learn From It**

The Prophet ﷺ gave the principle of precedent a memorable practical form, in a report both collections record:

> *"The believer is not bitten from the same hole twice."* (al-Bukhārī 6133; Muslim 2998)

The saying was spoken in a specific circumstance — concerning a poet who had harmed the Muslims and sought release — but its generality is why the tradition preserved it. A believer is defined, in part, as a person from whom precedent works. He observes the hole, he notes that it bites, and he does not put his hand back in. Unbelief, in the Qur'an's usage of this verse, is the refusal of that economy: the same hole, the same hand, the same bite, generation after generation.

Al-Qurṭubī adds a moral dimension to the deterrence. The report restrains not only by fear but by shame: it shows the reader that the position he is defending has been defended before, by people stronger and richer than him, and that the defense failed. There is a particular humility in discovering that one's own sophistication is not new. The Quraysh believed themselves uniquely advantaged — custodians of the Kaʿbah, masters of the pilgrimage economy, the most articulate Arabs alive. The reports told them that the people of ʿĀd had said the same thing in almost the same words: *"Who is greater than us in strength?"* (41:15), and that the sentence was followed by their extinction.

**Modern Reading: The Report Nobody Files**

The verse has an uncomfortable contemporary edge. Modern civilization produces more *anbāʾ* than any before it: continuous reports of collapsed institutions, of financial frauds, of failed states, of public-health disasters, of marriages and companies destroyed in identifiable, repeatable ways. The reports have come to us in a quantity no previous generation possessed. What the verse asks is whether *muzdajar* is present in them — whether anyone is being restrained.

The answer usually given by the reports themselves is no, and the reason is instructive. Deterrence requires a connection between the event and the observer: the observer must believe the destroyed party was destroyed *for* something he is also doing. Remove that connection and a catastrophe becomes mere content — a story to consume, an anniversary to mark, a documentary to watch. The Qur'an's histories resist that consumption by refusing to describe the destruction without describing the offense first. In every one of the five accounts that follow, the sin is named before the punishment, and the sin is always one the reader could recognize in himself: arrogance, ingratitude, sexual excess, tyranny, mockery of the messenger.

The practical test the verse proposes is simple. When a report of collapse reaches you, ask not "how did it happen?" but "what in me resembles what in them?" If nothing does, the report has not yet become *muzdajar*; it is still entertainment. The sūrah will now deliver five such reports, and after each it will turn to the reader with the same question — not whether the punishment was real, but whether anyone is remembering.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:5

> **Conclusive wisdom — but the warnings availed not.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Ḥikmah: The Knowledge That Holds a Person Back**

Verse 4 said the reports contain a deterrent; verse 5 says what they are in themselves. *Ḥikmatun bālighah* — "wisdom conclusive," "wisdom that has reached its end." The word *ḥikmah* is one of the Qur'an's great terms, and its root *ḥ-k-m* means, at bottom, to restrain and to prevent. The bridle by which a rider controls a horse is called *ḥakamah*; the judge is *ḥākim* because he restrains the oppressor from the oppressed; a legal ruling is *ḥukm* because it puts a limit on a dispute. *Ḥikmah* is therefore not cleverness or accumulated information. Al-Rāghib al-Iṣfahānī defines it as knowledge of the best of things by means of the best of knowledge, and adds the operative point: *ḥikmah* is what restrains a person from acting harmfully. Wisdom is a brake that knows the road.

Applied to these reports, the word says something precise. The histories of Noah, ʿĀd, Thamūd, Lot, and Pharaoh are not merely frightening stories. They are *ḥikmah* — they contain the correct ordering of knowledge into action, and they are designed to hold a person back from the behavior that destroys. This is why the Qur'an pairs *ḥikmah* with the Book itself so often:

> *"He gives wisdom (al-ḥikmah) unto whomsoever He will, and whosoever is given wisdom has been given much good."* (Qur'an 2:269)

**Bālighah: Wisdom That Has Nowhere Left to Go**

The qualifier *bālighah* is the active participle of *b-l-gh*, to reach, to attain, to arrive at the far limit. Speech is called *balāghah* in Arabic because effective speech reaches the hearer's heart and lands there; a message is *balāgh* because it has been delivered and has arrived. *Ḥikmatun bālighah* is thus wisdom that has reached its target and its completion — wisdom beyond which there is nothing further to say. Al-Jīlānī-style sources and Ibn al-Jawzī gloss *bālighah* as complete or perfect. Al-Ṭabarī takes the phrase to mean either that the reports themselves are conclusive wisdom or that the reproof they contain is wisdom.

The claim has a polemical edge the reader should not miss. The opponents had just declared the miracle to be *siḥrun mustamirr* — persistent sorcery, or, on another reading, powerful sorcery. Verse 5 answers with the same structure of a noun plus an intensive participle: this is not sorcery that goes on and on; it is *wisdom that has reached its end*. One phrase describes something endless but empty; the other describes something finite and complete. The Qur'an's answer to the accusation of magic is not "we are not magicians" but "what you have received is finished wisdom, and there is no more of it to come."

That has a consequence for the recipient. If the wisdom is *bālighah*, then additional evidence will not be supplied. The person waiting for a clearer sign, a better argument, a more convincing miracle is waiting for something the sender has already declared complete. The Qur'an states this elsewhere with finality:

> *"Or is it not enough for them that We have sent down unto thee the Book, recited unto them?"* (Qur'an 29:51)

**Tughnī: The Warning That Cannot Enrich**

The second half of the verse is *fa-mā tughnī al-nudhur*. The verb is from *gh-n-y*, a root whose sense is to suffice, to make needless, to render independent — the same root as *ghanī*, one of the Divine Names, "the Self-Sufficient," and as *istighnāʾ*, the state of feeling one needs nothing. To say the warnings do not *tughnī* is to say they do not enrich, do not suffice, do not close the gap. Al-Ṭabarī records that the clause may be read as a rhetorical question — "What did the warnings avail them?" — with the answer supplied by the silence: nothing. Both readings carry the same content; the interrogative form simply forces the reader to answer it himself.

*Nudhur* is the plural of *nadhīr*, which can mean either the warnings themselves or the warners — the prophets who carry them. The Qur'an uses the same double sense elsewhere:

> *"Say: Observe that which is in the heavens and on the earth. But neither signs nor warnings (al-nudhur) avail a people who believe not."* (Qur'an 10:101)

The verse is a generalization of the case at Minā, and it establishes a rule the entire Qur'an then applies: there exists a state of the heart in which signs cease to function as evidence. This is not skepticism in the philosophical sense — the honest doubt of a person who wants to know and lacks grounds. It is the fixed disposition of a person who has decided. Verse 3 named the mechanism (*ittabaʿū ahwāʾahum*); verse 5 names the result.

**Where the Failure Is Located**

The structure of the verse performs a delicate operation of justice. It describes the message in superlative terms and the reception in negative ones, and it puts the two in a single sentence with a *fa*, "so/but," between them. *Ḥikmatun bālighah* — perfect, complete, conclusive. *Fa-mā tughnī al-nudhur* — and it availed them nothing. The failure is therefore located entirely on the receiving end, and the location matters for two reasons.

First, it clears the messenger. The Prophet ﷺ spent thirteen Makkan years delivering a message that produced, by every visible measure, failure: a handful of converts, a boycott, torture, exile, and the death of his wife and his uncle in a single year. A man in that position would naturally conclude he was doing something wrong — arguing badly, choosing the wrong audience, presenting the material ineffectively. Verse 5 tells him the material was *bālighah*. The deficiency was not in the delivery. This is the same consolation given in another form:

> *"And remind, for truly the Reminder benefits the believers."* (Qur'an 51:55)

Second, it warns the recipient without flattering him. The man who hears conclusive wisdom and gains nothing from it is in a worse position than the man who never heard it, because the ground of his excuse has been removed. The Qur'an will say so directly at the end of this sūrah: *"And thy Lord never destroys towns until He sends a messenger unto their mother city"* (28:59). The destructions about to be recounted were all preceded by delivery.

**The Medicine and the Closed Mouth**

The commentators drew the obvious analogy, and it is worth stating plainly because it explains a great deal of religious life. A medicine can be perfect in composition, correct in diagnosis, and sufficient in dose, and it can still fail — if the patient will not swallow it. The physician's excellence is not impugned by the patient's refusal, but the patient does not get better. This is exactly the relationship verse 5 describes, and it explains why the Qur'an so often addresses not the content of revelation but the condition of the hearer: hearts that are sealed (2:7), hearts covered (6:25), ears in which is heaviness (41:5), a barrier behind them (17:46). The problem is never a shortage of truth. It is an obstruction in the organ of reception.

Ibn Taymiyyah develops the point in his discussion of guidance, distinguishing between *hidāyat al-irshād*, the guidance of instruction and explanation, which belongs to the messenger, and *hidāyat al-tawfīq*, the guidance of enabling and acceptance, which belongs to God alone:

> *"Truly thou guidest not whom thou lovest, but God guideth whomsoever He will."* (Qur'an 28:56)

The verse does not make the hearer passive. The Qur'an insists throughout that the refusal is chosen — verse 3's *ittabaʿū ahwāʾahum* is an act they performed. What it establishes is that the act of receiving truth requires a disposition that the truth itself cannot manufacture. A proof does not create humility; humility decides whether the proof will be weighed.

**Practical Consequence: How to Receive a Warning**

The verse proposes a discipline. If conclusive wisdom can fail to avail, then the variable under the reader's control is not the quality of what arrives but his own readiness. Three habits follow, each drawn from the Qur'an's own description of those who did benefit.

The first is to receive the report as addressed. The destroyed nations heard the accounts of other destroyed nations and classified them as history. What made them history rather than warning was the assumption that the case was closed. The believer hears a warning and asks first which of his own actions it describes.

The second is to resist the demand for more. The person who insists that he will act once the evidence is stronger has, in most cases, already decided not to act, and is using the demand for more evidence as a delay. *Ḥikmatun bālighah* means the evidence has reached its limit; the next move is not intellectual but volitional.

The third is to notice the direction of one's own appetite. Verse 3 diagnosed following the *hawā* as the cause of denial, and the cure named in 79:40–41 is *nahā al-nafs ʿan al-hawā* — restraining the soul from its caprice. The warnings avail a person to the degree that he has practiced restraining himself in small matters. A man who has never said no to a minor desire will not manage it on the day a major one is required of him, and he will find, like the audience of this verse, that the wisdom was conclusive and it did not enrich him at all.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:6

> **So turn away from them on the Day wherein the caller will call unto a terrible thing.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command to Withdraw**

*Fa-tawalla ʿanhum.* After five verses diagnosing a rejection that will not reverse, the sūrah issues its first command, and it is not a command to argue harder. It is a command to leave. *Tawalla* is the fifth form of *w-l-y*, a root whose basic sense is proximity and guardianship — *walī* is a close protector, *mawlā* a master, *wilāyah* authority. The fifth form moves in both directions at once: to turn away from one thing is to turn toward another, and the same verb can mean to abandon and to take as a patron. The commentators exploit exactly this doubleness. Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn al-Jawzī note that the Prophet ﷺ is enjoined to turn away from the disbelievers both to stop them from persisting in blasphemy and to turn toward God, citing:

> *"So turn away from them (fa-tawalla ʿanhum) and trust in God. God suffices as a Guardian."* (Qur'an 4:81)

The Qur'an repeats the command in many places, and each repetition adds a facet:

> *"And when thou seest those who engage in vain discussion about Our signs, turn away from them (fa-aʿriḍ ʿanhum) until they engage in other discourse."* (Qur'an 6:68)

> *"So turn away from them, for thou shalt not be blamed (fa-mā anta bi-malūm). And remind, for truly the Reminder benefits the believers."* (Qur'an 51:54–55)

See also 4:63; 7:199; 9:95; 15:94; 37:174, 178. The accumulation is significant. A messenger told eleven times to withdraw from a particular audience has been relieved of a responsibility he was carrying as a burden. Verse 5 said the warnings did not avail; verse 6 says, then stop pouring them into that vessel. The command is not contempt for the people but stewardship of the message and of the messenger.

**The Two Turnings**

There is a structural irony that the classical commentators noticed. In verse 2 the disbelievers turned away (*yuʿriḍū*) from a sign. In verse 6 God tells His Prophet to turn away (*tawalla*) from them. The same motion, performed by two parties, with opposite meanings: their turning is refusal of truth; his turning is obedience to God. And later in the same verse, everyone will turn toward a caller they cannot avoid. The day described here reverses the direction of verse 2 entirely — those who turned their shoulders away will be scrambling with their eyes down, unable to look anywhere except where they are called.

**Yawma Yadʿu al-Dāʿ**

The verse continues: *yawma yadʿu al-dāʿi ilā shayʾin nukur* — "on the Day when the caller calls to a thing of dread." The word *yawm* is in the accusative as an adverb of time, attached by the grammarians to an implied verb: remember that Day, or: turn away from them, for the Day is coming. Al-Qurṭubī mentions the alternative reading in the nominative, treating the phrase as an independent statement: "the Day is a Day when the caller calls." Either way, the abrupt switch from the command to the Day is the point — the withdrawal is commanded *because* the reckoning is real, and because the one commanded to withdraw will not be the one who settles accounts.

*Al-dāʿī*, "the caller," is identified by Ibn al-Jawzī as the Archangel Isrāfīl (Seraphiel), who blows the trumpet that heralds the Hour. Others take the caller to be the angel appointed to summon the creatures to the gathering, or God's own command. The Qur'an describes the scene elsewhere with the caller drawn even nearer:

> *"And listen for the Day when the Caller will call from a near place (min makānin qarīb)."* (Qur'an 50:41)

> *"On the Day when the trumpet is blown, and We shall gather the guilty that day, blue-eyed."* (Qur'an 20:102)

> *"And the trumpet shall be blown; then whatsoever is in the heavens and whatsoever is on the earth shall swoon, save whomever God wills. Then it shall be blown a second time, and behold, they shall be risen, looking on."* (Qur'an 39:68)

The nearness in 50:41 is the detail that makes the image unbearable. Distance allows the possibility of not hearing. The caller will be near enough that no one can claim the summons was faint, and the sound will come from every direction at once — the commentators, explaining 50:41 together with 20:105–107 (*"on the Day when the trumpet is blown... and We shall leave it a level plain, wherein thou wilt see neither crookedness nor curve"*), note that the earth will be gathered flat, with no mountain and no valley to deflect a voice.

**Nukur: The Thing No One Can Recognize**

What the caller calls to is *shayʾin nukur*. The root *n-k-r* means to be unknown, unrecognized, unfamiliar — the same root as *nakīr*, denial, and *munkar*, the reprehensible act, both of which describe things the soul does not recognize as its own. Al-Zamakhsharī explains *nukur* as something so unprecedented that human beings cannot comprehend it, because they have never seen anything like it. The word choice is exact: the Day is described not primarily as painful but as *unrecognizable*. Pain can be anticipated; the human imagination has a catalogue of it. What the Day will be is outside that catalogue, and language therefore uses the term for the unfamiliar rather than the term for the terrible.

This is a point of real theological honesty. The Qur'an repeatedly declines to translate the Hereafter into worldly terms, and where it does use worldly images it flags them as approximations. The Prophet ﷺ said of the Garden that it contains *"what no eye has seen, no ear has heard, and what has never crossed the heart of man"* (al-Bukhārī 3244; Muslim 2824, from Abū Hurayrah) — a statement about reward that removes reward from the imagination's reach. Verse 6 does the same for punishment. It does not describe the dreadful thing; it says it is dreadful in the specific sense of being unlike anything the listener has met.

**How the Prophet ﷺ Carried This Knowledge**

The tradition preserves a report that shows the weight of the trumpet upon the one who announced it. Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī narrated:

> *"How can I be at ease when the Companion of the Horn has taken hold of the horn, bent his head, and listened, waiting to be commanded to blow?"* — and when the Companions asked what they should say, he told them: *"Say: God is sufficient for us, and the best disposer of affairs; upon God we rely."* (al-Tirmidhī 2431, 3243; graded ḥasan by the hadith scholars)

The image is of an angel already in position, instrument raised, waiting for a signal — and of the Prophet ﷺ describing himself as unable to relax because of it. The verse commands him to turn away from the mockers; the report shows what occupied him instead. The withdrawal in verse 6 is not disengagement from reality. It is a reallocation of attention from people who will not listen to a Day that everyone will hear.

**Application: The Discipline of Walking Away**

The command has an ordinary application that is easy to miss because it comes from God to a prophet. Much of human exhaustion is produced by arguing with people who have already decided. The pattern is familiar: a conversation that has passed the point of inquiry and entered the territory of identity, where every argument produced is met with a reinterpretation of the arguer's motive, and where continued engagement damages the one who engages without benefiting the one who resists.

The Qur'an legislates withdrawal from exactly this situation, and it does so without moralizing about the resistant party. It does not say they are worthless; it says the warnings do not avail *them*, and that the messenger's time has a better use. The instruction that follows withdrawal is always the same: *wa dhakkir* — "and remind," directed at those who will benefit (51:55). The energy withdrawn from the closed door is deposited at the open one.

There is also a private form of the command. A person arguing with his own appetites, or with an addiction, or with a resentment he has nurtured for years, reaches a point where further internal debate produces only further entrenchment. The *hawā* does not lose arguments; it postpones them. The Qur'anic counsel for that condition is not a better argument but *tawalla* — a physical change of position, of company, of environment, of what the eyes are permitted to look at. The man who cannot win the discussion in his head is told to leave the room, and the sūrah immediately shows him why: because there is a Day when the discussion ends and the calling begins, and on that Day nobody will have the option to turn away.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:7

> **With their eyes humbled they emerge from the graves as if they were scattered locusts.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Khāshiʿah: The Posture That Replaces Pride**

*Khāshiʿatan abṣāruhum yakhrujūna mina al-ajdāth.* The verse begins with a circumstantial phrase describing the state of the emerging crowd: *khāshiʿatan abṣāruhum*, "their eyes humbled" or "their gazes low." The participle is from *kh-sh-ʿ*, a root denoting humility expressed in the body — the lowering of the head, the softening of the voice, the stillness of a person who has stopped asserting himself. Arabic distinguishes *khushūʿ* from *tawāḍuʿ*: *tawāḍuʿ* is humility as a chosen virtue, while *khushūʿ* is humility as a condition imposed by the presence of something overwhelming. Prayer is described with the second: *"Who are humble (khāshiʿūn) in their prayer"* (23:2). The Day of Judgment is described with the same word, but involuntary.

The choice of *abṣār*, the eyes or gazes, rather than heads or faces, is precise. The eye is the organ through which a person asserts himself in the world — the steady look of a man who does not lower his gaze, the contemptuous glance, the appraising stare. Pride lives in the gaze before it lives in the tongue. The Qur'an says of Pharaoh's courtiers that they were arrogant, and of the disbelievers that *"when Our signs are recited unto them, you see the denial in the faces of those who disbelieve"* (22:72). On the Day, that organ is quieted. The identical phrase appears in another Makkan sūrah, and there it is attached to the impossibility of prostration:

> *"On the Day when the shin shall be bared and they shall be called unto prostration, but they shall not be able — their eyes humbled (khāshiʿatan abṣāruhum), abasement covering them."* (Qur'an 68:42–43)

The parallel is instructive. In 68:43 the humbled gaze belongs to people who were invited to bow in the world and refused; the humility arrives too late to be worship and becomes merely shame. The commentators — al-Qurṭubī among them — read the two passages together as one description of a single state: the soul that would not lower itself before God will be lowered by circumstances, and the lowering will not be rewarded, because reward attaches to the choice and the choice has passed.

**Ajdāth: The Plural of the Place Everyone Enters Alone**

*Al-ajdāth* is the plural of *jadath*, a grave. Arabic has a more common word, *qabr*, whose plural is *qubūr*; the Qur'an uses both, and *ajdāth* appears in eschatological contexts specifically:

> *"And the trumpet shall be blown, and behold! from the graves (ajdāth) they will come forth swiftly unto their Lord."* (Qur'an 36:51)

> *"The Day they come forth from the tombs (ajdāth) in haste, as if they were racing unto a goal."* (Qur'an 70:43)

The word's rarity serves the scene. The Qur'an is describing an event for which ordinary vocabulary has no precedent, and it reaches for a less common term. More importantly, the plural is doing work: *ajdāth*, graves, in the plural, all of them, at once. Every grave in every cemetery in every century — the mass burial grounds of wars and plagues, the unmarked graves of the poor, the tombs of kings sealed with stone, the bodies lost at sea whose graves are the seabed. The verse asserts their simultaneous emptiness.

The Prophet ﷺ described the condition of those who emerge with a detail that removes every worldly distinction:

> *"Mankind will be gathered on the Day of Resurrection barefoot, naked, uncircumcised."* — and when ʿĀʾishah asked whether men and women would look at one another, he replied: *"The matter will be too grave for that."* (al-Bukhārī 3349; Muslim 2859)

Too grave to be embarrassed. The verse's *khāshiʿatan abṣāruhum* says the same thing from the other side: the gaze is lowered not out of modesty but because there is nothing left to assert.

**Jarādun Muntashir: Locusts, Not an Army**

The simile is *ka-annahum jarādun muntashir* — "as if they were scattered locusts." The comparison operates on several levels at once, and the commentators worked through them.

The first is number. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that the locust image conveys sheer quantity: every human being who has ever lived, brought out at once. The Arabs knew locust swarms as the only natural phenomenon capable of darkening the sky with living bodies, and the Qur'an uses the same creature for the plagues of Egypt:

> *"So We sent upon them the flood, the locusts (al-jarād), the lice, the frogs, and the blood, as signs, distinct, but they waxed arrogant and were a guilty people."* (Qur'an 7:133)

The second is disorder. Locusts in a swarm move without formation, without ranks, without leadership, each one propelled by the mass. An army is described in the Qur'an with the language of order — *ṣaff*, rows, ranks; *"God loves those who fight in His cause in ranks, as though they were a building bonded together"* (61:4). The crowd of the Resurrection is described with the opposite: *muntashir*, spread out, scattered, dispersed. Every hierarchy humans built — tribe, class, nation, profession — dissolves into undifferentiated mass.

The third is direction and helplessness. A locust is carried by wind; it does not decide where it lands. The simile captures the total loss of agency: those who in the world insisted they would follow nothing but their own appetites are now moved by a force they cannot resist.

**Two Phases: Moths, Then Locusts**

Al-Qurṭubī offers a reading that harmonizes this verse with another eschatological image and, in doing so, produces a small chronology of the Day:

> *"On the Day when mankind will be like scattered moths (farāshun mabthūth), and the mountains will be like carded wool."* (Qur'an 101:4–5)

According to al-Qurṭubī, the moth image refers to the first phase: the rising from the graves in confusion, moving this way and that, without orientation — moths around a flame, blind and disoriented. The locust image refers to the second phase, when the trumpet sounds and they turn together toward the caller and move as a mass in one direction. The two similes are not competing descriptions but sequential ones.

The reading is confirmed by verse 8, which immediately supplies the direction: *muhṭiʿīna ilā al-dāʿī*, "scrambling toward the caller." Scattered and yet convergent — that is exactly the behavior of a locust swarm, which appears chaotic at close range and moves with unmistakable purpose at scale. The theological point is severe and orderly: individual confusion does not prevent collective arrival. Nobody will be lost. Nobody will be late. The mass will reach the appointed place whether its members understand the journey or not.

**The Graveyard That Empties**

The verse is one of the Qur'an's most concrete assertions about bodily resurrection, and it belongs to a set of passages that the tradition treats as decisive on the question. The Qur'an's opponents doubted resurrection on empirical grounds — the body decays, the bones crumble, the person is dispersed into the earth — and the Qur'an answers by appealing to the same power that made the body first:

> *"And he strikes a similitude for Us and forgets his own creation. He says: Who will give life to bones when they are rotten? Say: He will give them life Who created them the first time, and He is Knower of every creation."* (Qur'an 36:78–79)

> *"Does man reckon that We shall not assemble his bones? Nay! We are able to reshape his very fingertips."* (Qur'an 75:3–4)

Verse 7 presents the answer as a scene rather than an argument. The graves open and the occupants come out — not as souls, not as visions, but as beings with eyes that can be humbled. The emphasis on the eyes is therefore doctrinally loaded: the resurrection described here is of the person who sees, and who is seen.

**Application: The Equality Nobody Believes Until He Sees It**

The image has a practical function that goes beyond terror. Human society is organized around visible markers of rank, and the markers operate on the gaze before they operate on the mind — people adjust their posture, their tone, and their assumptions within a second of seeing a uniform, a title, a car, a building. Verse 7 dismantles the entire apparatus in one picture: everyone out of the ground at once, no clothes of office, no buildings, no entourage, no documents, gazes lowered by the same event, moving in the same direction with the same helplessness.

The tradition made this equality a matter of daily practice rather than abstract belief. The Muslim is instructed to pray shoulder to shoulder with no gap and no elevation, to stand in the same rows regardless of wealth, and to be buried in the same simple shroud regardless of rank. The funeral prayer is performed standing, in ranks, for the beggar and the king identically. These are rehearsals. Al-Qurṭubī's remark on such practices is that the believer trains in this world for a posture he will be forced into in the next, so that the lowering of the gaze may be voluntary and rewarded rather than compulsory and useless.

The verse asks a question that is worth putting plainly: which of the distinctions you currently organize your life around will be visible on the Day the graves empty? The sūrah answers it two verses later, and the answer will be that only two things survive the emergence — what a person believed, and what he did about it.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:8

> **Scrambling toward the caller. The disbelievers say, "This is a calamitous day."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Muḥṭiʿīn: A Word With Two Motions in It**

*Muḥṭiʿīna ilā al-dāʿ.* The participle comes from the root *ḥ-ṭ-ʿ*, and the lexicons give it a compound meaning that makes it exactly right for this scene. *Iḥṭāʿ* is to move quickly with the neck bent forward and the gaze lowered — the run of a person hurrying under compulsion, or of an animal driven ahead of its driver. The word contains both haste and humiliation in a single image: the head is down because the pace is forced, and the pace is forced because the head is down. Al-Ṭabarī records a related sense, that *muḥṭiʿīn* can mean "looking toward" — gazing fixedly at the caller. The two readings converge rather than compete: they are running toward what they cannot stop looking at.

The verse thereby completes the description begun in verse 7. There, the eyes were humbled; here, the humbled eyes are attached to moving bodies. There, they were scattered locusts; here, they are converging on a voice. The grammar reinforces the continuity — *muḥṭiʿīna* is in the accusative as a circumstantial qualifier attached to the same subject, so the sentence reads as one unbroken scene: they come out of the graves, humbled in their gazes, scattered like locusts, scrambling toward the caller.

The phrase *ilā al-dāʿ*, "toward the caller," ends without the final *yāʾ* in the standard recitation — *al-dāʿ* rather than *al-dāʿī* — a pausal elision that the reciters preserve. The effect in Arabic is a clipped, abrupt ending to the word, as though the sentence itself were hurrying. The commentators note that the same elision appears in 54:6 (*yawma yadʿu al-dāʿ*), binding the two verses together acoustically: the caller mentioned in the command to withdraw is the caller the crowd is now running toward.

**The Direction Nobody Chose**

Verse 2 described people turning away from a sign; verse 6 commanded the Prophet to turn away from them; verse 8 shows everyone turning toward one point. The sūrah has constructed a small geometry of direction, and its lesson is that the choice of direction is temporary. A person may angle his body away from the truth for a lifetime, and the angle will be corrected in an instant he does not control.

This is the meaning of the Qur'an's repeated insistence that the disbelievers will be gathered whether they wish it or not:

> *"And on the Day We gather them all together, then We shall say unto those who associated partners: Where are your partners whom you claimed?"* (Qur'an 6:22)

> *"On the Day when the trumpet shall be blown and We shall gather the guilty that day, blue-eyed."* (Qur'an 20:102)

The "blue-eyed" (*zurqan*) of 20:102 has been read by the commentators as blindness, or as the fixed stare of thirst, or as disfigurement — in every case a mark on the very organ verse 7 described as humbled. The sequence is consistent across the sūrah: the eye that refused to see in the world becomes the eye that cannot look away in the next.

**Yaqūlu al-Kāfirūn: The Present Tense of a Future Sentence**

The second half of the verse shifts register: *yaqūlu al-kāfirūna hādhā yawmun ʿasir* — "the disbelievers say: this is a calamitous day." The verb is present tense describing a future event, and Arabic grammar uses this construction for vividness (*ḥikāyat al-ḥāl*), placing the listener inside the scene as it happens. The effect is that the reader does not learn that they will say it; he overhears them saying it.

The content of the sentence is a confession, and confessions matter in the Qur'an's eschatology. Throughout the sūrah the opponents have been described as producing explanations — "incessant sorcery," "possessed," "an insolent liar." Here they produce no explanation. They produce a description of their situation, and the description is accurate. There is no argument left, no reclassification available, no slogan. The Day has arrived and the only remaining speech is the admission that it is bad.

The Qur'an collects such admissions repeatedly, and their function is to establish that the final position of the disbeliever is not misunderstanding. He understands perfectly, at last:

> *"And the trumpet shall be blown... and they will say: O woe unto us! Who has raised us from our sleeping-place? This is what the Compassionate promised, and the messengers spoke truly."* (Qur'an 36:51–52)

> *"And they will say, had we but listened or reasoned, we would not be among the companions of the Blaze."* (Qur'an 67:10)

> *"O that I had forwarded [good deeds] for my life!"* (Qur'an 89:24)

In every case the deficiency named is not intellectual capacity but attention: *law kunnā nasmaʿu aw naʿqil* — "had we but listened or reasoned." The same people who declared the moon-splitting to be sorcery will declare on that Day that the messengers spoke truth. Nothing will have changed in the evidence. Everything will have changed in the willingness.

**Yawmun ʿAsir: The Difficulty Named in the Same Root as Ease**

*ʿAsir* is from *ʿ-s-r*, meaning difficulty, hardship, straitness — the same root as *ʿusr*, hardship, and *taʿsīr*, to make hard. The Qur'an uses the word for this Day in another Makkan sūrah and immediately specifies whom it afflicts:

> *"So when the trumpet is sounded, that Day will be a difficult day (yawmun ʿasīr) — for the disbelievers, not easy (ʿalā al-kāfirīna ghayru yasīr)."* (Qur'an 74:8–10)
