The specification is essential to the sūrah's balance. The Day is not uniformly terrible. The Qur'an insists that the same Day will be experienced differently by different people, and it uses the same vocabulary in both directions:

> *"Truly the disbelievers are in a painful punishment, and truly those who believe and do righteous deeds are in Gardens and bliss."* (Qur'an 52:47 — cf. 54:47–54)

> *"Then as for him who is given his book in his right hand, he shall be reckoned with an easy reckoning (ḥisāban yasīran)... But as for him who is given his book behind his back, he shall call for destruction."* (Qur'an 84:7–11)

The word *yasīr* in 84:8 is the exact negation of the *ʿasir* here — and the two are named by the same people, on the same Day. Al-Bayḍāwī and al-Shawkānī both note that verse 8's phrase is spoken by the disbelievers specifically because for them the difficulty has no compensating element; the believer's difficulty is followed by relief, and his hardship is a passage rather than a residence.

There is a further resonance the tradition did not miss. The same root *ʿ-s-r* appears in the promise every Muslim recites:

> *"For truly with hardship comes ease; truly with hardship comes ease."* (Qur'an 94:5–6)

The repetition of the verse, with the definite *al-ʿusr* and the indefinite *yusrā*, was explained by the Prophet's Companions — the report is transmitted from Ibn Masʿūd and ʿUmar in the exegetical collections — as meaning that one hardship cannot overcome two eases. The verse in al-Qamar inverts the situation: for the disbeliever there is hardship without a paired ease, because the ease offered in the world was refused. The sūrah's own second refrain, repeated four times, is precisely that offer of ease: *"We have made the Qur'an easy (yassarnā) to remember."* The same chapter that promises the ease of remembering warns of the day that is *ʿasir* for those who did not use it.

**Application: The Sentence You Will Be Able to Say**

Verse 8 leaves the reader with an uncomfortable and useful thought. The words *hādhā yawmun ʿasir* will be spoken by people who spent their lives insisting the opposite. The gap between the two positions was not filled by new information; it was filled by the arrival of the Day.

There is a smaller version of this available in ordinary life, and it is why the verse is worth more than a shudder. Every human being has days of reckoning in miniature — the medical result, the audit, the confrontation he postponed, the morning after the decision he made at midnight. On those days people say, with perfect accuracy, sentences they refused for years: "I should not have done that," "I knew this would happen," "I wasted the time." The confession is not new knowledge. It is old knowledge finally admitted.

The Qur'an's counsel is to move the confession earlier. The believer is instructed to say daily, in prayer and outside it, *astaghfiru Allāh* — "I seek God's forgiveness" — which is a small, voluntary, unhurried version of the sentence the disbelievers will be forced to say in verse 8. The Prophet ﷺ practiced it in a state of no obvious need:

> *"O people, repent to God and seek His forgiveness; truly I repent to Him a hundred times in a day."* (Muslim 2702, from al-Agharr al-Muzanī)

That is the practical difference the sūrah draws between the two crowds in these eight verses. Both will speak the truth eventually. One speaks it while there is still time for it to change something, and the other speaks it when the only thing left to do is to scramble toward the caller.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:9

> **The people of Noah denied before them, for they denied Our servant and said, "Possessed!" And he was reproved.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The First File Opened**

*Kadhdhabat qablahum qawmu Nūḥ.* "Before them" — *qablahum* — the people of Noah denied before the Quraysh did. The word establishes priority in two senses at once. Chronologically, Noah is the first messenger sent to the people of the earth; the intercession report in al-Bukhārī has the nations coming to him on the Day of Judgment and saying, *"You are the first messenger God sent to the people of the earth"* (al-Bukhārī 3339, from Abū Hurayrah). Structurally, his case is placed first in the sūrah's dossier because it is the archetype: everything the Quraysh are doing to Muhammad ﷺ, someone did first, and the outcome is on record.

The commentators note that this is most likely the first account of Noah in the chronological order of revelation, and that the fuller narratives — 11:25–48, 23:23–30, and the whole of Sūrah 71 — come later. The allusive style here is itself evidence of a shared memory: the audience already knew the outline, so the sūrah does not need to tell the story, only to draw the verdict. Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī both treat the passage as consolation for the Prophet ﷺ, and the consolation is arithmetic. Noah's people resisted for centuries:

> *"And We sent Noah unto his people, and he remained among them a thousand years save fifty."* (Qur'an 29:14)

Against that, thirteen Makkan years is a beginning, not a failure. And as God helped Noah and those with him prevail, so He would ensure that Muhammad ﷺ and his people prevailed.

**"Our Servant": The Title Against the Insult**

The verse's central phrase is *fa-kadhdhabū ʿabdanā* — "they denied Our servant." The word *ʿabd*, servant or slave, is the Qur'ananic honorific of highest rank, and its use is not incidental. The Qur'an deploys it at the moments of greatest prophetic elevation:

> *"Glory be to Him Who carried His servant (ʿabdahu) by night from the Sacred Mosque to the Farthest Mosque."* (Qur'an 17:1)

> *"And remember Our servants Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob, men of might and vision."* (Qur'an 38:45)

> *"And Job, when he called unto his Lord: Truly affliction has touched me, and Thou art the most Merciful of the merciful."* (Qur'an 21:83 — cf. 38:41, "We found him patient; an excellent servant")

The addition of the possessive pronoun — *ʿabdanā*, Our servant, not merely a servant — is a direct answer to the insult that follows in the same verse. They said: *majnūn*, possessed, mad. God says: *ʿabdunā*, ours. The confrontation is between two descriptions of the same man, and the verse places them adjacent so that the reader must choose which one is true. Ibn Kathīr notes the pattern: whenever the Qur'an wants to defend a prophet against the mockery of his people, it does so by attaching him to God rather than by listing his merits.

**Majnūn: The Accusation Whose Root Means "Covered"**

*Majnūn* is the passive participle of *j-n-n*, a root whose fundamental sense is to cover, to conceal, to hide. A garden is *jannah* because its ground is covered by foliage; a shield is *junnah* because it covers the body; the embryo is *janīn* because it is hidden in the womb; the unseen beings are *jinn* because they are concealed from sight. To be *majnūn* is therefore to have the intellect covered — the reasoning faculty present but obscured.

The accusation is not original to Noah's people. The Qur'an states plainly that it is the standard response to prophecy across all history:

> *"Thus not a messenger came unto those before them, but they said: A sorcerer (sāḥir) or a madman (majnūn)!"* (Qur'an 51:52)

> *"So turn thou from them. On the Day when the Caller calls unto a hateful thing, with their eyes humbled..."* — and of the Prophet ﷺ: *"And truly thou art upon a mighty character"* (68:4) follows immediately upon *"By the grace of thy Lord, thou art not a madman (bi-majnūn)"* (68:2).

See also 15:6; 44:14; 81:22, where God Himself testifies: *"Your companion is not mad."* The Qur'an defends the Prophet ﷺ with a single negative, and then spends the rest of the sūrah describing the cosmos instead — as though the accusation were too small to require more.

There is a psychological observation here that deserves naming. In any society that has decided its own way of life is correct, the person who says otherwise is not treated as mistaken but as malfunctioning. Mistake can be corrected by argument; malfunction cannot, and therefore requires no argument. Calling Noah mad released his people from the obligation to answer him. The same mechanism is visible whenever a truth-teller is described as obsessive, unstable, extreme, or unwell — descriptions that are sometimes accurate and that function, most of the time, as a substitute for a reply.

**Wa-Zdujir: The Same Root as the Deterrent**

The verse ends with *wa-zdujir* — the eighth form passive of *z-j-r*, "and he was rebuked, driven off, threatened." The root is the same one the sūrah used five verses earlier in *muzdajar*, "a reproof, a deterrent." The echo is deliberate and it works in both directions. Verse 4 said the reports contain *muzdajar* — something meant to restrain a wrongdoer. Verse 9 says Noah was *zdujir* — restrained, threatened, driven back by wrongdoers. The word for the medicine appears in the same sūrah as the word for the disease.

Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn al-Jawzī give two senses for the passive. It may mean that they rebuked and threatened him, as in the parallel account:

> *"They said: If thou cease not, O Noah, thou shalt surely be among the stoned."* (Qur'an 26:116)

Or it may mean that he was hindered and prevented from reaching them, blocked in his mission. The second reading fits the sūrah's theme of obstruction: the man whose whole task was communication was cut off from communication, and then told he was insane for trying.

Sūrah 71 supplies the detail that makes this concrete. Noah describes his own exhausting campaign:

> *"He said: My Lord! Truly I have called unto my people night and day, but my calling has only increased them in flight. And truly whenever I called unto them that Thou mightest forgive them, they put their fingers in their ears and wrapped themselves in their garments, and persisted, and waxed proud with great pride. Then I called unto them openly, then I spoke unto them in public and in private."* (Qur'an 71:5–9)

Fingers in ears, garments pulled over heads, public preaching met with private refusal — the same crowd that called him mad behaved like people who could not bear to hear. Verse 9 compresses all of that into one passive verb: *wa-zdujir*.

**The Long Patience Behind the Short Sentence**

It is worth dwelling on what the verse leaves out. Nine hundred and fifty years of preaching reduced to: they denied, they called him mad, they drove him off. The compression is not a loss of information but a statement of value. In the divine accounting, centuries of unrewarded effort do not lengthen the record; the record consists of the position taken and the verdict reached. Noah's reward is described elsewhere in terms of permanence rather than duration:

> *"And We left for him [a mention] among the later generations: Peace be upon Noah among all creatures. Truly thus do We requite the virtuous."* (Qur'an 37:78–80)

Every Muslim who recites the Qur'an says his name with honor, forty centuries after the men who called him mad are dust. The verse teaches the believer how to evaluate a life spent losing: the outcome that matters is not the local one, and the local one is described in three verbs so that the reader will not mistake it for the whole story.

**Application: When You Are the One Called Mad**

Verse 9 has a use for anyone who has told an unwelcome truth. The sequence it records is nearly universal: you speak, you are insulted, you are blocked. The Qur'an does not tell the reader that the sequence means he is wrong, and it does not tell him that it means he is right either. It tells him that it means he is in the company of the first messenger — and then it immediately moves to verse 10, where the response is recorded. Noah does not answer the insult. He does not defend his sanity. He does not list his credentials. He turns to his Lord and says four words. That is the whole of the Qur'anic counsel for the person who has been called mad for telling the truth: do not argue with the accusation; report to the One who made you.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:10

> **Then he called upon his Lord: "Truly I am overcome. Help!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Prophet's Weapon Is a Sentence**

*Fa-daʿā rabbahu annī maghlūbun fa-ntaṣir.* Four elements, and not one of them is a rebuttal. Noah calls (*daʿā*); he addresses his Lord specifically (*rabbahu* — his Lord, his Sustainer, the One who raised him); he states his condition (*annī maghlūbun*, "that I am overcome"); and he asks for the action only God can take (*fa-ntaṣir*, "so give victory"). The whole of it occupies a single verse, and it follows the insult of verse 9 with no interval. The sequence is the lesson: rebuke from people, immediately answered by supplication to God.

Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr both note the directness of the address. Noah does not begin by describing his people's crimes, though he has grounds. He begins by describing himself — *maghlūb*, overcome, defeated, outmatched. The root *gh-l-b* means to overpower, to prevail against, to conquer; *ghālib* is a victor and *maghlūb* is the one beaten. The word is used elsewhere in the Qur'an for military defeat:

> *"The Romans have been defeated (ghulibat) in the nearest land, and they, after their defeat, will prevail."* (Qur'an 30:2–3)

Noah is therefore describing a loss, not a difficulty. He has preached for centuries, he has been called mad, he has been threatened with stoning, and he reports to God the plain fact: I have lost.

**Why the Admission Is the Point**

There is a temptation to read such a supplication as despair, and the tradition reads it as something better: as accuracy. A servant who reports his real condition to his Lord is performing the first act of correct relationship with Him. The alternative — a prayer that exaggerates one's own endurance, that presents the difficulty as manageable, that asks for nothing because asking would be an admission — is not piety but performance. Ibn ʿAjībah and the Sufi commentators treat *annī maghlūbun* as the model of *faqr*, spiritual poverty: the servant's honest declaration that he has nothing, which is the only ground on which God's giving can operate.

The Qur'an gives the same shape to other prophets' prayers, and the pattern is consistent:

> *"And Job, when he called unto his Lord: Truly affliction has touched me, and Thou art the most Merciful of the merciful."* (Qur'an 21:83)

> *"And Jonah, when he went off in anger and deemed that We had no power over him, and cried out in the darknesses: There is no god save Thee! Glory be to Thee! Truly I was among the wrongdoers."* (Qur'an 21:87)

> *"And Zakariyyā, when he called unto his Lord: My Lord, leave me not alone, though Thou art the best of inheritors."* (Qur'an 21:89)

Each begins with a statement of deficiency and ends with an affirmation of God. Al-Qurṭubī draws from these the rule that supplication is most likely to be answered when it proceeds from acknowledged need rather than from a sense of merit. Noah had nine centuries of merit available to him and did not mention it.

**Fa-Ntaṣir: The Verb That Means "Take Up My Cause"**

*Intaṣir* is the eighth form imperative of *n-ṣ-r*, the root of *naṣr*, help and victory. The eighth form adds a reflexive or acquisitive force: "seek victory for yourself," or, in this construction, "obtain victory [for me], take up my cause, vindicate me." The word is not a request for personal comfort. It is a request that God act as the party wronged.

Noah's fuller prayer in Sūrah 71 shows what the request contained:

> *"So Noah said: My Lord, leave not upon the earth a single dweller of the disbelievers. Truly if Thou leavest them, they will lead Thy servants astray, and will beget only wicked ingrates. My Lord, forgive me and my parents, and whosoever enters my house as a believer, and the believing men and the believing women; and increase the wrongdoers in nothing but perdition."* (Qur'an 71:26–28)

Sūrah 23 preserves a shorter version, and it names the reason for the plea:

> *"He said: My Lord, help me, because they have denied me (rabbī-nṣurnī bimā kadhdhabūn)."* (Qur'an 23:26)

The commentators emphasize that this prayer was made only after the revelation that no more would believe:

> *"And it was revealed unto Noah: None of thy people will believe save those who have believed already; so be not distressed by what they were doing."* (Qur'an 11:36)

That sequencing matters ethically. Noah did not call for the end of his people while there was hope for them; he endured, preached, and was rebuked for centuries. The prayer for judgment came when judgment had already been decreed, and it is therefore an act of alignment rather than of revenge. Ibn Kathīr makes this point explicitly: the supplication of a prophet against his people is uttered when the possibility of their guidance has closed.

**The Answer and Its Speed**

The Qur'an reports the response in a single verse elsewhere, and the response begins with the word of rescue:

> *"And Noah, when he called aforetime — We answered him (fa-istajabnā lahu), and delivered him and his household from the great calamity, and We aided him (wa-naṣarnāhu) against the people who denied Our signs. Truly they were an evil people, so We drowned them all."* (Qur'an 21:76–77)

Note the verbs: *istajāba*, He answered; *najjā*, He delivered; *naṣara*, He aided — the same root Noah used in his request. He asked for *nuṣrah* and received *nuṣrah*, in the form of a flood. Al-Bayḍāwī observes that the answer exceeded the request, as answers to supplication usually do: Noah asked to be vindicated, and he was given the survival of his household, the preservation of his name, and the drowning of the entire opposition.

The tradition makes the general principle explicit. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Supplication is worship (al-duʿāʾ huwa al-ʿibādah)."* (al-Tirmidhī 2969, 3247, 3372, from al-Nuʿmān ibn Bashīr; graded ṣaḥīḥ) — and he then recited: *"And your Lord said: Call upon Me; I shall answer you."* (Qur'an 40:60)

And concerning the person in Noah's exact position:

> *"Beware the supplication of the oppressed, for between it and God there is no veil."* (al-Bukhārī 1496; Muslim 19, from Ibn ʿAbbās)

There is also the report that supplication alone can alter what has been measured:

> *"Nothing averts the Divine Decree except supplication, and nothing increases the lifespan except righteousness."* (al-Tirmidhī 2139, from Salmān; graded ḥasan by al-Albānī)

**Why a Servant Reports Rather Than Retaliates**

The logic of the verse is best understood through the relationship it assumes. A servant insulted by strangers has no standing to punish them; his remedy lies with his master, who owns both parties. A messenger rejected by his audience has no authority to destroy them; his authority comes from the One who sent him, and only that One can withdraw the audience's time. Noah's move from verse 9 to verse 10 is therefore not weakness but correct jurisdiction. He refuses to act outside his mandate, and he transfers the case to the court that has jurisdiction over it.

This is the same discipline the Prophet ﷺ was commanded into repeatedly:

> *"So remind — thou art only a reminder. Thou art not a dictator over them."* (Qur'an 88:21–22)

> *"Yours is only the delivery (al-balāgh), and Ours is the reckoning."* (Qur'an 13:40; cf. 16:82)

The messenger delivers; the Sender judges. Confusing the two roles produces either tyranny — the preacher who compels — or despair, the preacher who thinks the refusal of his audience is a verdict on his Lord.

**Application: The Prayer of the Person Who Has Lost**

Verse 10 supplies a template that any exhausted person can use, and its usefulness lies in its shortness. Four moves: address God by the Name that fits your situation (*rabbī* — the One who nurtures and sustains me); state the fact without decoration (*I am overcome*); do not pretend to strength you do not have; ask God to act in the matter (*help, take up my cause*).

The discipline the verse imposes is on the fourth move. Noah did not ask for the destruction of his enemies in his own voice; he asked God to vindicate a case that belonged to God. There is a difference between "destroy them" and "You are the judge of this," and the difference is whether the petitioner has handed the file over or is still trying to run the court himself. Most human bitterness consists of continuing to prosecute, privately and endlessly, a case one has no power to decide. The verse's counsel is to file it with the only competent court, and then to stop working on it — which is what Noah did, and the next verse shows what happened when the court took up the case.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:11

> **So We opened the gates of the sky with pouring water.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Fa of Immediate Sequence**

*Fa-fataḥnā abwāba al-samāʾi bi-māʾin munhamir.* The verse begins with *fa*, the particle of immediate consequence — "so," "thereupon," "and at once." Arabic has particles that indicate sequence with delay (*thumma*) and particles that indicate sequence without delay (*fa*). The Qur'an uses *thumma* when it wants the reader to feel an interval, and *fa* when it wants no interval at all. Between Noah's four-word prayer and the opening of the sky's gates there is nothing. The comparison in the same sūrah is instructive: after the flood, the earth is told to swallow its water and the sky to withhold, and the verse uses *wa-qīla*, "and it was said" (11:44) — the command and its effect are again simultaneous.

The royal plural — *fataḥnā*, "We opened" — appears here for the first time in the sūrah and then dominates its historical sections: *We opened*, *We caused to gush*, *We carried*, *We sent*, *We left*. Al-Zamakhsharī and the rhetoricians note that the plural of majesty in these verses performs a specific function: the destructions are attributed directly and personally to God, with no intermediaries and no natural causes given the credit. The flood was not weather. It was an act.

**Abwāb al-Samāʾ: Gates, Not Clouds**

The Qur'an could have said "We sent rain." It says instead: *We opened the gates of the sky*. The image is architectural — the sky has doors, and the doors were opened. The same vocabulary appears in the description of the Last Day:

> *"And the heaven shall be opened, and it shall become gates (fa-kānat abwābā)."* (Qur'an 78:19)

And, in a negative use of the same image, in the description of the disbelievers' fate:

> *"Truly those who deny Our signs and scorn them — unto them the gates of heaven shall not be opened, nor shall they enter the Garden until the camel passes through the eye of the needle."* (Qur'an 7:40)

The three uses form a set. The gates of the sky opened for water in Noah's time; they will open as gates on the Day itself; and they are shut forever against those who scorn the signs. Al-Qurṭubī draws the moral connection: the same doors that poured destruction on a denying generation are the doors through which the deeds of the faithful ascend and through which they will themselves enter.

The word choice also removes the possibility of a naturalistic reading of the event. Rain falls from clouds; clouds form by processes; processes can be explained and predicted and dismissed. Gates are opened by an opener. The verse assigns agency in the strongest available terms, and it does so before describing a single drop of water.

**Munhamir: Water That Pours Without Restraint**

The water is *munhamir*, the active participle of the seventh form of *h-m-r*, a root meaning to pour, to gush, to flow abundantly. Arabic uses *hamrāʾ* for a downpour and *inhimār* for the pouring of something in a continuous, uncontrolled stream. The seventh form adds intensity and continuity: not water that falls but water that is falling and does not stop.

The same root gives Arabic *khamr*, wine, and *khimār*, the covering veil — both from the idea of pouring-over and covering. The lexicon thus connects the word for this flood to the word for the intoxicant, and the commentators found the connection worth noting in the parallel case of the punishment of Thamūd, where a different substance — a cry — covers a people as water covers the earth.

The scale implied is enormous. The Qur'an describes the water elsewhere with an emphasis on the deliberate coordination of two sources:

> *"Then We inspired: Make the Ark before Our Eyes and according to Our revelation. Then when Our Command comes and the oven boils, embark therein with thy household, save him against whom the word has gone before; and address Me not concerning those who did wrong — truly they shall be drowned. And when thou art settled, thou and whosoever is with thee, upon the Ark, say: Praise be to God who delivered us from the wrongdoing people."* (Qur'an 23:27–28)

> *"And We carried him upon a thing of planks and nails."* (Qur'an 54:13)

The phrase "the oven boils" (*fāra al-tannūr*) in 11:40 was understood by the earliest exegetes — Ibn ʿAbbās among them — as the sign of the water rising from the earth itself, from the place of fire, so that the two elements reversed their natures. The sky, which does not hold water, poured it; the oven, which holds fire, produced water. The world's ordinary arrangement was suspended, and the suspension is the point: the punishment came through the reversal of the very order by which life is sustained.

**Rain as Blessing, and the Same Rain as Punishment**

The Qur'an's ordinary description of rain is unambiguously positive, and the contrast with this verse is part of its design:

> *"And We send down from the sky purifying water (māʾan ṭahūrā), that We may give life thereby to a dead land, and give drink thereof to cattle and to many men whom We created."* (Qur'an 25:48–49)

> *"And We send down from the sky blessed water (mubārak), and cause gardens thereby to grow, and grain for harvest."* (Qur'an 50:9)

> *"God is He Who sends the winds, and they stir up clouds, and He spreads them in the sky however He wills, and He makes them fragments so that thou seest the rain issue from their midst."* (Qur'an 30:48)

Water is the Qur'an's most frequent image of divine mercy — sent down, measured, distributed, reviving. In verse 11 the identical substance, sent from the identical direction, arrives in a quantity that drowns every living thing. The theological instruction is severe and simple: there is no substance in creation that is inherently safe. Blessing and destruction differ not in the thing given but in the measure of the giving and the condition of the receiver. God says of the same water in another context:

> *"And if We willed, We could make it bitter; so why do you not give thanks?"* (Qur'an 56:70)

The verse is a general law with an immediate application to daily life. The sun that ripens a harvest can produce a drought; the same river that irrigates a valley can flood it; food that nourishes at one quantity kills at another; medicine at a dose is poison at a dose and a half. Human beings therefore live inside a system in which nothing is safe by nature, and safety is a continuous act of measurement by the One who measures. That is precisely what verse 12 will say: the waters met *ʿalā amrin qad qudir* — "upon a matter already measured."

**Application: When the Source of Life Becomes the Instrument of Loss**

Verse 11 has a form that recurs constantly in human experience, though usually in smaller scale. The thing that destroyed a family, a career, or a health was very often the thing that had built it. A talent becomes an obsession; a business's speed becomes its recklessness; a relationship's intensity becomes its jealousy; a nation's prosperity becomes its decadence — which is exactly the diagnosis the Qur'an gives of ʿĀd a few verses later. The people of Noah were not destroyed by something alien to their world; they were destroyed by the water that made their world possible, arriving without the restraint that made it beneficial.

The practical wisdom the verse teaches is therefore not to fear rain but to notice quantity. Gratitude in the Qur'anic sense is not merely an emotion; it is an accurate perception of measure, and the ungrateful are consistently described as people who took the supply for granted and thereby lost the ability to notice when the supply became excessive. Verse 11 asks the reader to look at the sources of his own life — his income, his health, his relationships, his access — and to remember that every one of them is a gate that God opens, and that He closes and opens according to a measure the receiver does not control.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:12

> **And We caused the earth to burst forth with springs, such that the waters met for a matter determined.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Fajjarnā: The Earth Cleaved From Within**

*Wa-fajjarnā al-arḍa ʿuyūnan.* The second form of *f-j-r* means to cleave open and cause to gush in abundance. The root gives Arabic *fajr*, the dawn — called that because it splits the darkness — and *fujūr*, licentiousness, because it is the breaking of a limit. *Fajjarnā* is therefore "We caused to burst forth," and the object is the whole earth, *al-arḍ*, not a region or a valley. Al-Ṭabarī explains the verse as God's causing springs to erupt from the ground everywhere at once.

The noun that follows is *ʿuyūnan*, the plural of *ʿayn*. The word means a spring, and it also means an eye. Arabic retains both senses in a single form, and the classical commentators did not treat the coincidence as accidental. Al-Zamakhsharī and al-Qurṭubī both note the image: the earth became a face full of eyes, and every eye was pouring water. The rhetorical effect is of a world weeping — not a landscape being flooded from above but a planet discharging from within, from its own organs of sight.

There is a further resonance in the root *ʿ-y-n*. The same letters give *ʿayn* as essence or self (*bi-ʿaynihi*, "himself, in his very person") and *maʿīn*, visible and manifest. The earth's hidden water was made manifest through openings named after the organ of seeing. Verse 11 had the sky opening gates; verse 12 has the earth opening eyes. Above and below, the world's two great bodies are described in the language of architecture and of perception, and both are described as acting on divine command.

**Fa-ltaqā al-māʾu: The Meeting of Two Waters**

*Fa-ltaqā al-māʾ*, "and the water met." The verb is the eighth form of *l-q-y*, to meet, to encounter, to be faced with. Arabic uses *iltiqāʾ* for the meeting of two armies as well as for the meeting of two people, and the Qur'an uses it for the meeting of two seas:

> *"He let loose the two seas; they meet. Between them is a barrier they do not transgress."* (Qur'an 55:19–20)

In 55:19–20 two seas meet and are held apart by a barrier — a picture of divine restraint, of the order that keeps salt from fresh. In 54:12 two waters meet and there is no barrier. The contrast is exact, and it identifies what the flood was: the suspension of the boundary that normally makes the world habitable. The same Qur'an that marvels at the barrier between the seas reports its removal as an act of judgment.

Note also the grammatical number: *al-māʾ*, "the water," singular and definite, even though the sources are two. The water of the sky and the water of the earth are described as one water, because in the event they became one thing — a single continuous mass with no dry land in it. The Qur'an elsewhere describes the moment of resolution:

> *"And it was said: O earth, swallow thy water! And O sky, withhold! And the water was made to cease, and the Command was fulfilled, and it settled upon al-Jūdī; and it was said: Away with the wrongdoing people!"* (Qur'an 11:44)

Four imperatives and one result. The water that had been sent from two directions is commanded by one voice, and the verse's verb for its ending — *ghīḍa*, "was swallowed, was made to subside" — is the same verb used for the earth drinking its water. The One who opened the gates and burst the springs is the One who closes them, and the interval between the two is the length of a decree.

**ʿAlā Amrin Qad Qudir: The Measure Already Set**

The verse ends with the phrase that gives the whole event its theological meaning: *ʿalā amrin qad qudir* — "upon a matter that had been measured," or "for a purpose already determined." The verb *qudir* is the passive of *q-d-r*, the root of *qadar*, measure, decree, apportionment, and power. It is the same root the sūrah will return to in its doctrinal centre at verse 49: *"Truly We have created everything according to a measure (bi-qadar)."*

Al-Ṭabarī and al-Bayḍāwī read the phrase as indicating that the flood happened according to a predetermined decree — its timing, its extent, and its end all fixed in advance. Ibn Kathīr connects it to the promise given to Noah before the event:

> *"So when Our Command comes and the oven boils..."* (Qur'an 11:40)

The Command (*amr*) in verse 12 is the same *amr* that was announced in advance. Nothing about the flood was improvisation or excess. It was executed on schedule, to specification, and terminated on order.

This is a claim of considerable weight, and it answers an objection that arises whenever divine punishment is described. The objection is that such events look like the loss of control — a world gone wrong, a catastrophe without a manager. The verse asserts the opposite with a single passive verb: the water met *upon a matter already measured*. The flood was the most precisely administered event in the history of the earth. The same phrase is what allows the Qur'an to describe destructions as justice rather than as disaster, and it prepares the reader for verses 49–53, where the sūrah states the general principle: everything is created by measure, everything is written, everything small and great is inscribed.

**The Two-Way Trap**

There is a physical detail in the pairing of verses 11 and 12 that the commentators note. Water from the sky alone can be escaped by shelter; water from the earth alone can be escaped by elevation. Noah's son chose elevation:

> *"And it sailed with them amid waves like mountains, and Noah called unto his son, who was apart: O my son, embark with us and be not with the disbelievers. He said: I shall take refuge upon a mountain that will protect me from the water. He said: There is no protector today from the Command of God, save he on whom He has mercy. And the waves came between them, and he was among the drowned."* (Qur'an 11:42–43)

The verse answers the boy's plan with the phrase *lā ʿāṣima al-yawma min amri Allāh* — "there is no protection today from the Command of God." The mountain he named was a *ʿāṣim*, a protector, an object of refuge, and the Qur'an records his confidence in it and then records the waves coming between him and his father. When the sky pours and the earth gushes simultaneously, the two normal escape routes — upward and inward — are both closed. The design of the punishment matches the design of the offense: a people who refused to hear from any direction were given no direction to flee.

**Application: The Simultaneity That Ends Every Plan**

The verse describes a situation every human being recognizes in smaller form: the moment when two separate difficulties arrive together and cancel each remedy. Loss of income and illness in the same month; a professional setback and a family crisis in the same week; the failure of a plan and the failure of the backup plan. Alone, each would be survivable by the strategy suited to it. Together, they remove the strategies.

The Qur'an's treatment of such a moment is not to promise its absence but to relocate the refuge. Noah's son trusted a mountain — a physical object, high, solid, visible. Noah trusted a Command. The verse's phrase *ʿalā amrin qad qudir* says that the mountain was itself inside the measured matter, and that trusting it was trusting one measured thing to override another. The believer's refuge is not in any element of the system but in the One who sets the measure of all elements.

There is a practical corollary, and it is the opposite of passivity. Because everything is measured, the person who understands measurement prepares rather than panics: Noah was told to build the Ark years before the rain, and he built it in a place with no water, enduring mockery for doing so:

> *"And he was making the Ark, and whenever a chieftain of his people passed by him, they mocked him. He said: If you mock us, we shall mock you just as you mock."* (Qur'an 11:38)

The flood was decreed; the Ark was commanded. Verse 12 holds both facts in one sentence — the waters met by a measure already fixed, and a man who had built a boat because he was told to. Belief in *qadar* in the Qur'anic sense has never meant waiting; it means doing exactly what has been instructed and leaving the outcome to the One who measured it.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:13

> **Then We carried him upon a thing of planks and nails.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Ship Described Without Its Name**

*Wa-ḥamalnāhu ʿalā dhāti alwāḥin wa-dusur.* The most famous vessel in sacred history is described here without being named. The Qur'an has a word for a ship — *fulk*, used dozens of times, including in the same Noah narrative: *"And he was making the Ark (al-fulk)"* (11:38). Verse 13 does not use it. It uses the phrase *dhāti alwāḥin wa-dusur* — "the possessor of planks and fastenings," or, in the translation above, "a thing of planks and nails."

*Dhāt* is the feminine of *dhū*, "possessor of," and Arabic uses it to identify something by what it is made of or characterized by. The Qur'an employs the construction for the same rhetorical purpose repeatedly: *dhāt al-ṣadr*, "the possessor of the breast" for the one who knows what is in hearts (100:10); *dhāt rajʿ*, "the sky of returning rain" (86:11); *dhāt al-nūḍ*, "the mountains that shake" — in each case, a thing named by its most characteristic feature rather than by its title.

Here the characteristic feature is the material, and the effect is deliberate diminishment. The salvation of the human race from total extinction is described as boards and nails. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that the phrase points to the lowliness of the means: whatever saves by God's command need not be impressive. Ibn Kathīr emphasizes the same point when he remarks that God, who could have carried Noah upon the water without any vessel at all, chose to have him carried upon a constructed thing, so that the lesson would include the lesson of work.

**Alwāḥ and Dusur: The Two Components of a Boat**

*Alwāḥ* is the plural of *lawḥ*, a plank, a board, a flat piece of wood — the same word used for the Preserved Tablet, *al-lawḥ al-maḥfūẓ* (85:22), and for the tablets of the Torah, *al-alwāḥ* (7:145). A *lawḥ* is anything flat that carries or supports. The Ark's planks are therefore the same kind of object as the tablets that carried the Law: flat surfaces that hold what is placed upon them.

*Dusur* is the plural of *disār* (or *dusār*), and the lexicons give it three related senses. Ibn ʿAbbās, as transmitted in the exegetical collections, explained *dusur* as *masāmīr*, nails. Others understood it as the cords or ropes with which planks are lashed together, from the root *d-s-r*, meaning to thrust, to press, to bind tightly. A third reading takes it as the ribs or the frame of the ship. The three are not competing; they describe one process — binding flat pieces into a single watertight body by force applied at the joints.

The root's basic sense of thrusting and pressing gives the phrase its physical texture. A boat made of *alwāḥ* and *dusur* is a boat held together by pressure: wood pressed against wood, fastening driven into the seam, tension maintaining the shape. The commentators found it significant that the Qur'an names the binding rather than the sails, the rudder, or the cargo. What makes a collection of planks into a vessel is the thing that holds them together, and the verse names that.

There is a social reading of this detail that the tradition did not ignore. A community is also planks and bindings — individuals and the obligations that hold them to one another. The believers aboard the Ark were a handful: Sūrah 11 says *"and none believed with him except a few"* (11:40). A few planks, tightly bound, survived a world's drowning. A great many loosely bound did not. The Qur'an elsewhere makes the same point with the image of a wall:

> *"Truly God loves those who fight in His cause in ranks, as though they were a building firmly bonded (bunyānun marṣūṣ)."* (Qur'an 61:4)

**Ḥamalnāhu: The Verb That Assigns the Carrying**

The verb is the first form of *ḥ-m-l*, to carry, to bear, to transport — the same root as *ḥaml*, a burden, and *ḥāmil*, a pregnant woman, one who carries. The Qur'an uses it for God's act in the flood narrative several times, always with the divine subject:

> *"And a sign for them is that We carried their offspring in the laden Ark, and We created for them the like of it, upon which they ride."* (Qur'an 36:41–42)

> *"And We carried him and those with him in the Ark, and We made that a reminder for you."* (Qur'an 69:11–12)

> *"Indeed, when the water rose beyond measure (ṭaghā al-māʾ), We carried you in the running ship."* (Qur'an 69:11)

The theological claim is precise and worth stating plainly because it is often blurred. Noah built the Ark. The verse says God carried him. Both statements are true in the Qur'an's account, and the second does not cancel the first. The Ark was not the savior; it was the vehicle, and the vehicle was carried. The water that would have drowned the Ark's passengers was itself held in measure by the One who carried them upon it. This is the standard Qur'anic treatment of means (*asbāb*): they are commanded, they are constructed, they are used, and they are not trusted.

The Qur'an makes the same distinction in its description of ships generally:

> *"And His are the ships that sail the seas with their sails raised, like mountains."* (Qur'an 55:24)

> *"And it is He who made the ships subservient to you, that they may run upon the sea by His Command."* (Qur'an 16:14; cf. 45:12)

*Bi-amrihi*, by His Command. The ship is human; the sailing is divine. Ibn ʿAjībah and the Sufi commentators develop the point into a rule for the spiritual life: use the means as though everything depends on them, and rely on the One who made the means as though nothing depends on them. The person who neglects the means is presumptuous, and the person who trusts the means is an idolater of a very subtle kind.

**Built Before Our Eyes: The Ark as an Act of Worship**

The fuller account in Sūrah Hūd supplies the condition under which the construction happened, and it is the detail that makes verse 13 remarkable:

> *"And build the Ark (al-fulk) before Our Eyes (bi-aʿyuninā) and by Our revelation (wa-bi-waḥyinā), and address Me not concerning those who did wrong; truly they shall be drowned."* (Qur'an 11:37)

> *"Make the Ark before Our Eyes and according to Our revelation."* (Qur'an 23:27)

Noah was a prophet, not a shipwright. He was told to build a vessel in a region with no water, on a design given by revelation, while his chieftains walked past and mocked him. The Qur'an records the mockery and his reply:

> *"And he was making the Ark, and whenever a chieftain of his people passed by him, they mocked him. He said: If you mock us, we shall mock you just as you mock. And you shall know upon whom comes a punishment that will disgrace him, and upon whom falls a lasting punishment."* (Qur'an 11:38–39)

The construction therefore had two functions. It saved a family, and it trained a prophet. Working for years at an absurd-seeming task, in public, under ridicule, on instructions that made no sense locally — that is the definition of obedience under conditions where obedience cannot be rewarded by results. Verse 13's summary, *We carried him upon a thing of planks and nails*, is the answer to the mockery: the thing they laughed at became the thing that carried the world's future.

**Application: The Ordinary Materials of Rescue**

The verse has a persistent practical shape. Deliverance in the Qur'an nearly always arrives through unglamorous means: a boat of boards, a staff of wood, a handful of dust thrown into eyes, a small army at Badr, a cave, a spider's web in the traditions of the Hijrah, a few dates, a cup of milk. The person waiting for a rescue proportionate to his crisis will usually wait, because the Qur'anic pattern is a rescue disproportionate in its smallness and total in its effect.

The instruction that follows is twofold. Build the planks — do the ordinary, tedious, verifiable work that the situation requires, even when the water is nowhere in sight and the people walking past are laughing. And do not confuse the planks with the carrying — the boat does not save you, and neither does the job, the savings account, the diagnosis, the plan, or the relationship. They are *dhāt alwāḥ wa-dusur*: a thing made of components, held together by tension, floating on a medium it does not control.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:14

> **Coursing under Our Eyes, as a recompense for one who was rejected.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Tajrī Bi-Aʿyuninā: The Idiom of Supervision**

*Tajrī bi-aʿyuninā* — "it runs before Our eyes," or "coursing under Our Eyes." The verb *tajrī* is from *j-r-y*, to run, to flow, to proceed steadily; the Qur'an uses it for rivers (*tajrī min taḥtihā al-anhār*) and for ships (*al-fulk allatī tajrī fī al-baḥr*). The Ark is described as running, and it is described as running in a location: *bi-aʿyuninā*, within the field of God's sight.

*Aʿyun* is the plural of *ʿayn*, eye — the same word that verse 12 used for the earth's erupting springs. The idiom "before the eyes of" in Arabic means under direct, personal, continuous supervision; it is the language of a matter the responsible party is watching himself rather than delegating. Ibn al-Jawzī explains the phrase as indicating God's protection and care (*ḥifẓ*), and al-Qurṭubī records both that reading and the reading of direct watchfulness.

The Qur'an uses the same expression for Moses at the beginning of his life:

> *"...that thou mightest be brought up under My Eye (ʿalā ʿaynī)."* (Qur'an 20:39)

And for the Prophet ﷺ during the years of patient endurance in Makkah:

> *"And be patient under the judgment of thy Lord, for truly thou art before Our Eyes (bi-aʿyuninā); and glorify the praise of thy Lord when thou risest."* (Qur'an 52:48)

The three uses form a progression worth noticing. Moses was raised under God's Eye as an infant whose life was in immediate danger. Noah sailed under God's Eyes as a family whose life was in immediate danger. Muhammad ﷺ lived under God's Eyes as a man whose mission was in immediate danger. In each case the phrase is addressed to someone with no visible protection, and in each case it is offered not as an explanation of why the danger exists but as an assurance about who is watching it.

**The Attribute and the Principle Behind It**

The phrase raises a question the classical tradition handled with care. Does *bi-aʿyuninā* describe an attribute of God, or does it describe His knowledge and protection in figurative language? Both answers appear in the exegetical record. Al-Qurṭubī cites those who read the phrase as *bi-ḥifẓinā wa-ʿilminā*, "under Our protection and Our knowledge," and those who take the words at face value.

The mainstream position of the tradition, articulated by Ibn Taymiyyah and by the great majority of the early scholars, is stated in the Qur'an itself:

> *"There is nothing like unto Him, and He is the Hearing, the Seeing (al-Samīʿ, al-Baṣīr)."* (Qur'an 42:11)

The verse contains both halves of the orthodox rule in a single sentence: negation of likeness, and affirmation of the attribute. God is Seeing, as He has said; and nothing is like Him, as He has also said. The one who affirms without likening has followed the text; the one who likens has contradicted *"there is nothing like unto Him"*; and the one who denies the attribute altogether has contradicted *"He is the Hearing, the Seeing."* Ibn Taymiyyah summarizes the method as believing in the Names and Attributes as they were revealed, without *taḥrīf* (distortion of meaning), without *taʿṭīl* (denial), without *takyīf* (asking "how"), and without *tamthīl* (comparison to creation).

Whatever position a reader holds on the technical question, the pastoral content of verse 14 is identical: the small boat in an ocean of drowning water was not unobserved. The phrase exists to be believed, not to be solved.

**Jazāʾ: The Word of Settlement**

The verse continues: *jazāʾan li-man kāna kufir* — "as a recompense for one who was disbelieved." *Jazāʾ* is from *j-z-y*, to requite, to repay, to compensate — the same root as *jizyah* and as *al-jazāʾ* in the phrase *yawm al-jazāʾ*, the Day of Recompense. The word carries no inherent direction; it means the settlement of an account, whether in reward or in punishment. The Qur'an uses it for both:

> *"That is the recompense (jazāʾ) of those who do good."* (Qur'an 5:85)

> *"The recompense of an evil deed is an evil the like thereof."* (Qur'an 42:40)

Its appearance here is significant because it attaches the word of settlement to an act of rescue. The Ark's voyage was not an exception to justice; it was justice operating in its second direction. Verse 12 said the waters met *ʿalā amrin qad qudir*, upon a measured matter; verse 14 says the survival was *jazāʾ*, a recompense. Both the drowning and the floating were payments on accounts already opened.

**Li-Man Kāna Kufir: The Passive That Restores Honor**

The phrase *li-man kāna kufir* is grammatically ambiguous in a productive way, and the commentators took both readings seriously. Ibn al-Jawzī records the primary sense: "for one who was disbelieved" — that is, for Noah, the man whose message his people rejected. The passive of *k-f-r* means to be denied, disbelieved, or covered over; Noah is described not as one who failed but as one who was failed by. Al-Ṭabarī records the alternative reading: the recompense is for the fact that [the message] had been disbelieved — that is, the flood itself was the *jazāʾ* of their *kufr*.

Both readings are true in the narrative, and their coexistence is the verse's point. The same event was a recompense for denial and a recompense for the denied. God's justice operated on both parties in one act of water. This is a structural feature of the Qur'an's accounts of destruction, and it distinguishes them from simple stories of catastrophe: nothing is merely a disaster. Every disaster in the Qur'an is simultaneously a verdict on some people and a vindication of others, and the two verdicts are delivered by the same event at the same moment.

The choice of *kufir* — the passive of the root that verse 3 used actively of the Quraysh (*kadhdhabū*) — reconnects Noah's story to the sūrah's opening. The people of Noah denied; the Quraysh deny; the man denied in the first case was carried before God's Eyes; and the man denied in the second case is, at this moment in the revelation, standing under the same description.

**Application: Running While Being Watched**

Verse 14 gives the believer a specific way to understand his own difficult passage. The image is of a small constructed thing moving through an overwhelming medium, unable to control the medium, unable to stop, and visible throughout to the One who placed it there. That is a fairly exact description of a life lived under pressure — illness, bereavement, unemployment, persecution, or simply the long years of a task that has not yet produced results.

The verse's contribution is not a promise of calm water but a statement about visibility. *Bi-aʿyuninā*: your crossing is being watched. The tradition connects this to the very definition of excellence in worship. In the report of Gabriel, recorded by both al-Bukhārī and Muslim, the Prophet ﷺ was asked about *iḥsān* and answered:

> *"That you worship God as though you see Him; and if you do not see Him, He sees you."* (al-Bukhārī 50; Muslim 8, from ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb)

The two directions of the sentence correspond to the two states of the believer. Sometimes a person lives as though he sees God — in prayer, in a moment of clarity, in grief. Most of the time he lives in the second clause: he does not see, and he is seen. Noah's Ark in the flood is the second clause made physical. The rain covered the sky, the water covered the land, and nothing was visible except water; and the Qur'an describes the boat as running *under Our Eyes*.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:15

> **And indeed We left it as a sign; so is there any who remembers?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Taraknāhā: The Verb of Deliberate Preservation**

*Wa-laqad taraknāhā āyatan.* "And We left it as a sign." The verb *taraka* means to leave, to let remain, to abandon — and in divine usage it consistently means to leave something in place on purpose, for a purpose. The feminine pronoun *hā*, "it," has been read by the commentators in three ways, and the three readings build on one another rather than excluding one another.

The first takes the referent as the Ark itself. Ibn Kathīr records the report, transmitted from Qatādah, that the Ark remained upon the mountain where it settled and was visible for generations afterward, so that the earliest Muslims could point to it. The Qur'an names the mountain in Sūrah Hūd:

> *"And it settled upon al-Jūdī."* (Qur'an 11:44)

The second takes the referent as the event or the story — that God preserved the account of the flood within human memory as a warning for later generations. This is the reading al-Ṭabarī prefers, and it is supported by the Qur'an's own statement about the narrative's purpose:

> *"So We saved him and the companions of the Ark, and We made it a sign (āyah) for all beings (li-l-ʿālamīn)."* (Qur'an 29:15)

The third takes the referent as the class of thing — the ship itself, and every ship since. Sūrah al-Ḥāqqah makes this reading explicit:

> *"Indeed, when the water rose beyond measure, We carried you in the running ship, that We might make it a reminder (tadhkirah) for you, and that a remembering ear might remember it (wa-taʿiyahā udhunun wāʿiyah)."* (Qur'an 69:11–12)

Note the address: *We carried you*. Not "your ancestor Noah" but "you." Every human being alive is a descendant of the passengers, and the Qur'an includes all of them in the carrying. The reminder is therefore addressed to people who were, in a genealogical sense, aboard.

**Āyah: The Third Appearance of the Word**

This is the third time *āyah* appears in the sūrah, and the three uses trace an argument. In verse 2 the sign was refused: *"if they see a sign, they turn away."* In verse 4 the reports containing reproof arrived. In verse 15 the sign was left in place by God's deliberate act. The progression moves from sign given, to sign rejected, to sign preserved.

That final stage is what makes the Qur'an's view of history distinctive. A sign that is rejected is not thereby abolished; it is deposited. The Qur'an repeatedly describes the ruins of destroyed peoples as continuing to function:

> *"How many a generation have We destroyed before them! Canst thou see a single one of them, or hear of them the slightest whisper?"* (Qur'an 19:98)

> *"And how many a town have We destroyed whose way of life was insolent! These are their dwellings, uninhabited after them, save a little. And We are the Inheritors."* (Qur'an 28:58)

> *"Indeed in that are signs for those who read the marks (li-l-mutawassimīn). And indeed it is upon a road still standing (bi-imāmin muqīm)."* (Qur'an 15:75–76)

The last of these, about Lot's cities, is remarkable: *bi-imāmin muqīm*, "upon a highway that remains established." The road still runs past the ruins. The sign has not been removed; only the eyes have been closed.

**Fa-Hal Min Mudhakkir: The Question That Is an Invitation**

The verse ends with *fa-hal min mudhakkir* — "so is there any who remembers?" The interrogative particle *hal* combined with *min*, the particle of emphasis used in negative and interrogative contexts, produces a question that expects and desires a positive answer: "is there anyone at all who will take heed?" The form is grammatically interrogative and functionally an invitation. The commentators — al-Zamakhsharī and al-Rāzī among them — classify such questions as *istifhām bi-maʿnā al-ḥath*, a question in the sense of urging.

*Mudhakkir* is the fifth form active participle of *dh-k-r*, to remember, to mention, to recall. The fifth form, *tadhakkara*, is not the bare act of remembering but of taking a reminder to oneself, of recalling with attention and drawing the consequence. It is the difference between the fact passing through the mind and the fact being retained and acted upon. Al-Ṭabarī and others note the reading may be rendered "who takes heed" or "who will be admonished."

The question is posed six times in this sūrah — after Noah (15, 17), after ʿĀd (22), after Thamūd (32), after Lot (40), and at the sūrah's conclusion (51). Its repetition is not filler; it is the refrain of a preacher who, after each catastrophe he recounts, turns around and looks at the congregation. The structure is exactly that of a courtroom in which the judge reads five precedents aloud and after each one asks the defendant whether he has anything to say.

Ibn ʿAjībah adds a further dimension, connecting the question to the primordial covenant:

> *"And when thy Lord brought forth from the children of Adam, from their loins, their progeny, and made them bear witness against themselves: Am I not your Lord? They said: Yea, we bear witness."* (Qur'an 7:172)

On this reading, "is there any who remembers?" means: is there anyone who remembers the pact made before time? The reminder is not new information being introduced to an ignorant mind; it is old knowledge being recovered by a forgetful one. *Dhikr* in this sense is not learning but remembering, and the human problem is not ignorance but amnesia.

**Why the Sign Is Left Rather Than Repeated**

There is a theological reason for the shift from miracle to memorial. The splitting of the moon was a sign given once, to one crowd, at one moment; and it was refused. If God responded to refusal by repeating the miracle, refusal would become a strategy: demand a sign, refuse it, demand another. The Qur'an closes that avenue explicitly:

> *"And nothing prevents Us from sending the signs except that the former peoples denied them. And We gave Thamūd the she-camel as a visible sign, but they wronged her."* (Qur'an 17:59)

The verse states a rule: signs were given, signs were denied, and after denial the practice changed. What God leaves instead is the *āyah* that persists — the story, the ruin, the surviving institution of the ship, the recited Book. These do not require a particular night on the plain of Minā. They are available every day, to everyone, and their refusal has no excuse of absence.

**Application: The Memorial That Does Not Work**

Human beings build signs. They erect monuments at disaster sites, they institute anniversaries, they write "never again" on the walls of museums, they name buildings after the dead. The instinct matches the verse — *taraknāhā āyah*, leave it as a sign — and the instinct is sound.

The verse also explains why the instinct so often fails. A sign left behind is inert until someone performs the second half of the sentence: *fa-hal min mudhakkir*. A memorial without a rememberer is stone. The fifth form of the verb is decisive here — the reminder must be taken in, worked on, retained. Visiting a site, reading a plaque, and photographing a ruin are not *tadhakkur*; they are proximity. *Tadhakkur* is what happens when the visitor returns home and changes something.

This is the difference between the two crowds in the sūrah's first seventeen verses. Both saw the moon split. One crowd had the event and no memory of it, because they immediately filed it under "sorcery" and stopped thinking. The other had the event, and the story, and the question — and the question is still being asked, fourteen centuries later, of anyone reading this verse. *Fa-hal min mudhakkir.* Is there anyone who remembers?

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:16

> **How, then, were My Punishment and My warnings?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The First Instance of the Sūrah's Severe Refrain**

*Fa-kayfa kāna ʿadhābī wa-nudhur.* Four words in Arabic, and they occur four times in this sūrah — after Noah (16), after ʿĀd (21), after Thamūd (30), and, in a variant position, in the address to the Quraysh. It is one of the most recognizable refrains in the Qur'an, and the commentators treat its repetition as structural rather than merely rhetorical: it is the verdict pronounced at the close of each case.

The question form is *istifhām*, and its function here is not inquiry. God is not asking for information. Al-Bayḍāwī and Ibn al-Jawzī identify the device as a question of *taqrīr* and *tahwīl* — confirmation and intimidation. The listener is made to supply the answer himself, and the answer he supplies is more forceful than any answer he could be given. Ibn al-Jawzī glosses the sense as "How severe were My Punishment and My warnings?" — the severity being the obvious content of the reply.

**Kayfa: A Question About Manner**

The particle *kayfa* asks "how," not "whether." It presupposes the event and inquires into its quality. That presupposition is itself a statement: the punishment happened, and the only open question is what it was like. The Qur'an uses the same formula at the end of its accounts elsewhere, and each use carries the same implication:

Al-Qamar is, in fact, the only sūrah in the Qur'an to use this exact refrain. The great Makkan narrative sūrah that runs the same five case studies — Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ — closes each of its accounts with a different pair of verses, and the contrast is instructive:

> *"Truly in that is a sign, but most of them were not believers. And truly thy Lord — He is the Mighty, the Merciful (al-ʿAzīzu al-Raḥīm)."* (Qur'an 26:8–9; repeated at 26:67–68, 103–104, 121–122, 140, 158–159, 174–175, 190–191)

Al-Shuʿarāʾ's refrain names God's might and His mercy after each story; al-Qamar's asks a question about the punishment after each story. The two sūrahs cover almost the same material with opposite cadences, and the difference is one of audience: al-Shuʿarāʾ closes each account with the Names, al-Qamar with an interrogation.

> *"How many a town have We destroyed in its wrongdoing, so it lies collapsed upon its roofs, and a well deserted, and a palace raised! Have they not traveled in the land, that they might have hearts wherewith to understand, or ears wherewith to hear? Truly it is not the eyes that are blind, but the hearts within the breasts."* (Qur'an 22:45–46)

The last sentence of that passage explains why the question is posed rather than answered. The information is available to the eyes and ears of anyone who travels; what is missing is the organ of understanding. So God asks the question, and the asking obliges the hearer to use that organ.

**ʿAdhāb: The Word Whose Root Means Sweetness**

*ʿAdhāb* comes from *ʿ-dh-b*, a root whose primary sense is sweetness and freshness. Water that is *ʿadhb* is sweet, potable, good to drink — the Qur'an uses it in exactly that sense:

> *"And it is He who let loose the two seas, one sweet and palatable (ʿadhbun furāt) and the other salt and bitter."* (Qur'an 25:53; cf. 35:12)

Al-Rāghib al-Iṣfahānī explains that punishment is called *ʿadhāb* because it removes the *ʿudhūbah* — the sweetness — from a life; it makes existence unpalatable. Others connect it to the sense of prevention and hindrance, since punishment prevents the punished from what he desired. Either derivation produces the same insight: *ʿadhāb* is defined by contrast with what was sweet. Punishment is not merely pain; it is the loss of the good that was there, and the awareness of the loss.

The word therefore describes Noah's contemporaries precisely. They had a world of sweet water, and the water became their punishment. The same element in the same quantity, arriving without measure, is *ʿadhāb*. Verse 16's question — "how was My punishment?" — is answered by the fact that the punishment was made of their own supply.

**Nudhur: The Warnings Placed After the Punishment**

*Nudhur* is the plural of *nadhīr*, warning or warner, from *n-dh-r*, to warn of something seen approaching. The word appeared in verse 5 — *fa-mā tughnī al-nudhur*, "the warnings availed not" — and appears here in the same form. The repetition binds the two verses across the entire Noah narrative: the warnings did not avail (5), and how then were My punishment and My warnings (16)?

The order of the two terms is worth attention. The verse names the punishment first and the warnings second: *ʿadhābī wa-nudhur*. A reader might expect the reverse — first the warning, then the punishment. Al-Shawkānī and others explain the order in two ways. Grammatically, the second term answers the first: the punishment was *as* the warnings had described. The warning was the promise and the punishment was its fulfillment, so the two are named together as a single thing in two stages. Theologically, the order establishes that the punishment is the primary subject of the question — the warnings are mentioned so that the reader will understand the punishment was not an ambush.

The Qur'an states the principle governing both explicitly:

> *"And thy Lord never destroys towns until He sends unto their mother city a messenger to recite unto them Our signs. And We never destroy towns, save when their people are wrongdoers."* (Qur'an 28:59)

> *"That thy Lord is not the destroyer of towns unjustly while their people are unaware."* (Qur'an 6:131)

This is why the refrain pairs the two nouns. God's own honor is attached to the sequence: He does not punish before warning, and the warning is included in the question so that the severity of the punishment will be understood as proportionate. Ibn al-Jawzī's second gloss of the refrain is exactly this — that the punishment they received was *justified*, because God had already sent them a warning.

**The Possessive: My Punishment, My Warnings**

Both nouns carry the divine first-person possessive suffix: *ʿadhābī*, *nudhur* (the final *yāʾ* of *nudhurī* is elided in the recitation for the sake of the verse-end rhyme, but the possessive is understood). The attribution is emphatic and personal. God does not say "the flood" or "the disaster" or "what befell them." He says *My* punishment and *My* warnings, claiming both the mercy and the severity as His own acts.

The claim has a purpose beyond emphasis. It removes the event from the category of accident. A flood can be bad luck; *My punishment* cannot. It also removes the event from the category of nature's indifference; the water was not neutral. And it makes the warnings equally personal: the messenger was not a self-appointed critic but an envoy, and rejecting him was rejecting the Sender. The Qur'an states that equation repeatedly:

> *"Whosoever obeys the Messenger has obeyed God."* (Qur'an 4:80)

**Application: The Question Asked After the Fact**

The refrain's form — a question about how something was, asked after it happened — describes a situation every person meets. After a failure, a diagnosis, a bankruptcy, a divorce, a death, people ask: how bad was it? The question is asked of themselves, of witnesses, of records. And the answer is usually known before it is given.

The Qur'an places the question in God's mouth to make a different point. The severity of the outcome is not the interesting part; the interesting part is that the outcome had been announced. *ʿAdhābī wa-nudhur* — My punishment and My warnings — the two named in one breath because in God's economy they are one transaction. Nobody in the flood was surprised in the sense of being uninformed. They were surprised in the sense of being unwilling, which is a different thing and which verse 3 had already diagnosed.

The verse asks the reader to perform the *tadhakkur* verse 15 invited. Look at the punishments that have already happened — in your own history, in your family's, in your nation's — and then look for the warnings that preceded each one. You will find them. They will be in writing, or in the mouth of someone you did not like, or in a symptom you managed for years. That discovery is the entire content of the refrain, and it is available before your own case closes, which is the only time it is useful.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:17

> **And indeed We have made the Qur'an easy to remember; so is there any who remembers?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Refrain, and Why It Follows the First**

*Wa-laqad yassarnā al-Qurʾāna li-l-dhikr, fa-hal min mudhakkir.* This verse appears four times in the sūrah — at 17, 22, 32, and 40 — always immediately after an account of destruction, always after the severe refrain has fallen. The juxtaposition is the single most important structural fact about al-Qamar. God describes a drowning, and then He says: We have made the Qur'an easy. He describes a wind that tore people from the ground, and then: We have made the Qur'an easy. He describes a cry that turned a city into dry sticks, and then again.

Ibn ʿAjībah states the logic of the counterpoint: the first refrain emphasizes the wrath that comes upon those who reject God and His signs, while the second emphasizes the mercy by which God makes access to revelation, and hence to truth, easy. Al-Qushayrī-style readings of the pattern add that the destruction is announced and then the door is reopened in the same breath, so that no reader can leave either refrain believing himself excluded from the offer.

The sequence also mirrors the order of the Divine Names the tradition holds to be foundational: mercy precedes wrath. God says of Himself:

> *"My Mercy encompasses all things.*" (Qur'an 7:156)

And in the report recorded by both al-Bukhārī and Muslim from Abū Hurayrah:

> *"When God finished the creation, He wrote in His Book, which is with Him upon the Throne: My Mercy prevails over My Wrath."* (al-Bukhārī 7404; Muslim 2751)

Verse 17 is that principle built into the architecture of a sūrah about punishment. After every act of severity, the sūrah reopens with facility.

**Yassarnā: The Verb of Facilitation**

*Yassarnā* is the second form of *y-s-r*, to be easy, and the second form makes it causative: "We made easy, We facilitated." The same root gives *yusr*, ease; *yasīr*, easy or slight; *taysīr*, facilitation; and *muyassir*, one who makes things easy. The Qur'an uses the verb of this very Book several times, and each use specifies a dimension of the ease:

> *"And We have only made this easy upon thy tongue (yassarnāhu bi-lisānika), that thou mayest give glad tidings unto the reverent thereby, and that thereby thou mayest warn a contentious people."* (Qur'an 19:97)

> *"Truly We have made it easy upon thy tongue (yassarnāhu bi-lisānika), in their own language, that haply they may take heed."* (Qur'an 44:58)

> *"Then exalted be God, the King, the Truth! And hasten not with the Qur'an before its revelation is completed unto thee, and say: My Lord, increase me in knowledge."* (Qur'an 20:114)

The facilitation described in 19:97 and 44:58 is linguistic — the Book was made easy *on the tongue*, in the language of its hearers. Verse 17's *li-l-dhikr* adds the dimension of retention. Ibn ʿAjībah combines them: the Qur'an is made easy in that its recitation is easy for the tongue, and its memorization and understanding are easy for the mind and heart.

**Li-l-Dhikr: Three Meanings Held Together**

*Dhikr* is from *dh-k-r*, to remember, to mention, to recall. In Qur'anic usage the noun carries at least three senses, and verse 17 works on all of them at once.

First, *dhikr* is remembrance — the act of remembering God, which the Qur'an commands constantly: *"O you who believe, remember God with much remembrance"* (33:41). Second, *dhikr* is a name for revelation itself; the Qur'an calls itself *al-dhikr* and *dhikr ḥakīm*: *"Indeed We have sent down the Reminder (al-dhikr), and indeed We are its Guardian"* (15:9). Third, *dhikr* is admonition and taking heed — the sense the commentators record here, so that *mudhakkir* may be rendered "who will be admonished."

The three senses converge in a single claim: this Book was made easy *in order to be held in the mind*, and holding it in the mind produces admonition, and admonition produces remembrance of God. The facilitation is instrumental. God did not make the Qur'an easy as a courtesy; He made it easy so that no person could plead difficulty as a reason for not having it.

**The Claim Verified by History**

The assertion is falsifiable, and it has not been falsified. The Qur'an is the most widely memorized text in human history, by an order of magnitude that has no parallel. Millions of Muslims carry it complete in memory, and the memorizers include children who do not speak Arabic as a first language, in countries from Senegal to Indonesia, from Bosnia to Xinjiang. Institutions exist whose sole function is producing memorizers, and the practice is unbroken from the first generation: the Prophet ﷺ himself was unlettered (*ummi*), receiving and retaining the revelation orally, and he transmitted it the same way.

> *"Move not thy tongue therewith to hasten it. Truly upon Us is its collection and its recitation."* (Qur'an 75:16–17)

The tradition records the Prophet ﷺ's own framing of the practice:

> *"The best among you are those who learn the Qur'an and teach it to others."* (al-Bukhārī 5027, from ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān)

> *"It will be said to the companion of the Qur'an: Read and ascend, and recite as you used to recite in the world; for your station is at the last verse you recite."* (Abū Dāwūd 1464; al-Tirmidhī 2914, from ʿAbdullāh ibn ʿAmr; graded ḥasan)

> *"Whoever recites a letter of the Book of God has one good deed, and a good deed is tenfold. I do not say 'Alif-Lām-Mīm' is one letter, but Alif is a letter, Lām is a letter, and Mīm is a letter."* (al-Tirmidhī 2910, from Ibn Masʿūd; graded ṣaḥīḥ)

The report quoted in the source commentary — *"If the Qur'an is enclosed by skin [that is, memorized by a human being], God will never burn it in the Fire"* — is transmitted by al-Bayhaqī in *Shuʿab al-Īmān* and other collections; the hadith critics grade its chain as weak, while noting that its meaning is supported by the mass of evidence on the merit of carrying the Book.

**Why It Is Easy: The Design of the Text**

The claim deserves examination rather than acceptance, and the features that make the Qur'an memorizable are describable. The text is composed in rhymed prose (*sajʿ*), with verse-endings that recur and shift in patterns the ear learns quickly. Its units are short — the average verse of al-Qamar is eight words. Its narratives recur, so that a memorizer who knows the story of Moses in one sūrah has already acquired a large part of the vocabulary needed in six others. Its refrains repeat verbatim, as in this very sūrah. Its sound is designed for recitation aloud, and recitation aloud is the most efficient memorization technique known: the ear reinforces the tongue and the tongue reinforces the mind.

The tradition recognized this. The Prophet ﷺ's Companions learned the Qur'an ten verses at a time, and the report from Abū ʿAbd al-Raḥmān al-Sulamī preserves their method: they did not move on from ten verses until they had learned what was in them of knowledge and action. The ease is therefore not merely acoustic; it is pedagogical. The text is built in units small enough to hold, each containing something to understand and something to do.

**Fa-Hal Min Mudhakkir: The Question Returned**

The verse ends with the same question verse 15 ended with, and the pairing is intentional. Verse 15 asked it about a sign left in the world — the Ark, the story, the mountain. Verse 17 asks it about a sign left in the Book. The external memorial and the internal text are two forms of the same provision, and both are followed by the same open question.

Ibn ʿAjībah adds the covenantal reading noted earlier: the question may mean "is there anyone who remembers the pact made with God before time?" (cf. 7:172). On that reading, *yassarnā* and *mudhakkir* belong together as diagnosis and treatment. The human being is a forgetter — Adam's own lapse is described with the same root: *"And We found in him no constancy, and he forgot (fa-nasiya), and We found no resolve in him"* (20:115). The remedy for a forgetful creature is a Book made easy to remember, and the ease is calibrated to the weakness.

**Application: The Excuse Removed**

Verse 17 removes an entire category of religious excuse. The commonest objections to engaging with scripture are objections of capacity: it is too long, too difficult, too foreign, too archaic, requiring years of study and languages I do not speak. The verse answers not by disputing the difficulty of understanding everything in the Book but by asserting the ease of holding it. The door is at the level of the tongue and the memory, and it is open.

The practical consequences are concrete. Memorization is available to people who cannot read — which is how it has historically spread, and how it continues to spread in places without schools. It is available to children, whose retention is naturally better than their reasoning, which is why the tradition has always begun there. It is available in fragments: nobody is required to memorize the whole to benefit from a portion, and the Qur'an's own structure means that a single sūrah carries a complete teaching. Al-Qamar itself is two pages, contains five complete histories, two refrains, and a full statement of the doctrine of decree — and a person who memorizes it has, in a real sense, memorized a summary of the Qur'an's message.

The question at the end is not rhetorical decoration. It is an offer with a deadline that is not specified, which is precisely what makes it urgent. *Fa-hal min mudhakkir* — is there anyone who remembers? The verse was revealed to a crowd in Makkah who had just watched the moon split, and it has been recited ever since by people who have not. The question is asked again every time it is recited, and it is answered every time someone begins.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:18

> **ʿĀd denied. How, then, were My Punishment and My warnings?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Words of Crime, One Question of Verdict**

*Kadhdhabat ʿĀd. Fa-kayfa kāna ʿadhābī wa-nudhur.* The denial occupies two words. The verdict occupies the rest of the verse. Nothing intervenes: no dialogue, no argument, no description of what ʿĀd said, no account of their prophet's preaching, no catalogue of their sins. The sūrah that gave Noah six verses before its refrain gives ʿĀd a single clause, and then asks the question.

The compression is a rhetorical choice with theological content. The Qur'an elsewhere devotes twenty verses to Hūd and his people:

> *"And unto ʿĀd [We sent] their brother Hūd. He said: O my people, worship God; you have no god other than Him. You are but inventors [of lies]... They said: O Hūd, thou hast brought us no clear sign, and we will not abandon our gods upon thy word, and we will not believe thee."* (Qur'an 11:50–53)

Al-Qamar assumes its audience knows that material and reduces it to its outcome.
