Ibn Kathīr explains the method: the sūrah is not telling the story but pronouncing the judgment, and a judgment requires no recapitulation of the trial. The reader is placed in the position of someone who arrives after the case is closed and finds the record marked with a single word — *denied* — and a question about how the sentence was carried out.

**Who ʿĀd Were**

ʿĀd were an Arabian tribe, and their being Arabian matters. Noah's people were distant in time and unnamed in place; ʿĀd were a people the Quraysh knew by geography. Their territory lay in al-Aḥqāf — the sand dunes of southern Arabia between Oman and Hadramawt — and the Qur'an names it:

> *"And mention the brother of ʿĀd, when he warned his people at al-Aḥqāf; and warners have passed before and after him: Worship none but God. Truly I fear for you the punishment of a tremendous day."* (Qur'an 46:21)

Their civilization is described in terms that made them proverbial for the Arabs:

> *"Hast thou not considered how thy Lord dealt with ʿĀd, Iram of the pillars, the like of which was never created in the lands?"* (Qur'an 89:6–8)

*Dhāt al-ʿimād*, "of the pillars" — whether the pillars of their tents, the columns of their buildings, or the men of stature among them, the commentators explain; and *allatī lam yukhlaq mithluhā fī al-bilād*, "the like of which was never created in the lands," is the Qur'an's own superlative. Whatever ʿĀd built, nothing comparable existed. Their physical form matched their architecture:

> *"And as for ʿĀd, they were destroyed by a howling, raging wind, which He imposed upon them seven nights and eight days in succession, so that thou mightest have seen the people laid low therein, as though they were hollow palm trunks."* (Qur'an 69:6–7)

Ibn al-Jawzī notes that they were likened to palm trees because of their great stature, and the Qur'an records Hūd's own people's boast:

> *"As for ʿĀd, they waxed arrogant upon the earth without right, and said: Who is greater than us in strength (man ashaddu minnā quwwah)? Have they not considered that God, Who created them, is greater than them in strength? Yet they rejected Our signs."* (Qur'an 41:15)

The question *man ashaddu minnā quwwah* is the entire case in five words. A civilization asked "who is stronger than us?" and the answer given to it was a wind.

**The Arrogance of Strength, Answered by the Invisible**

There is a fitness in the punishment that the commentators consistently point out. ʿĀd trusted in what could be seen and measured: pillars, buildings, bodies, numbers. They were destroyed by air — the least substantial thing in creation, invisible, ungraspable, incapable of being fought, walled out, or bought off. The instrument was chosen to be the exact opposite of what they valued.

Hūd's preaching had already identified the error:

> *"They said: We believe not in that wherewith thou hast been sent, and we will not abandon our gods upon thy word... Do you dispute with me concerning names you have named, you and your fathers, for which God has sent down no authority? Then await — I am with you among those who await."* (Qur'an 11:53–57 — cf. 7:71)

> *"And they said: O Hūd, thou hast brought us no clear sign."* (Qur'an 11:53)

They demanded a sign from the man who was warning them of a sign. The Qur'an's response to the demand is not to supply one but to record what came instead: *"So We saved him and those with him by mercy from Us, and We cut off the root of those who denied Our signs, and they were not believers"* (7:72). The verse uses the language of horticulture — *qataʿnā dābira*, "We cut off their root" — for a people who built with pillars.

**The Refrain's Function at This Position**

The refrain appears here for the second time, and its second appearance changes its force. After Noah it functioned as a verdict on an ancient and half-legendary people; after ʿĀd it functions as a verdict on neighbors. The Quraysh walked past ʿĀd's territory on the southern caravan route and past Thamūd's at al-Ḥijr on the northern one. The Qur'an says so directly:

> *"And indeed they pass by them in the morning and by night. Will you not then reflect?"* (Qur'an 37:137–138)

The question *fa-kayfa kāna ʿadhābī wa-nudhur* is therefore not addressed to a reader of distant history but to a merchant who has personally seen the site. The refrain's severity lies in its proximity: this happened here, on the road you take twice a year, and the buildings are still standing.

That is why the sūrah places the verdict in the same verse as the crime. ʿĀd had no excuse of ignorance, and the audience of the sūrah has none either. The refrain is asked about them, and it is asked of them.

**Application: The Question Every Confident Institution Should Hear**

Verse 18 is addressed, in practical terms, to anyone in a position of strength. The strength need not be military; it can be technical, financial, institutional, intellectual, or physical. ʿĀd's was architectural and bodily, and the Qur'an grants both without qualification: *"the like of which was never created in the lands."* God does not dispute the claim. He confirms it and then destroys them.

That confirmation is the severe part. A civilization destroyed for lying about its own power would be a simple story. A civilization destroyed while its power was real is a warning, because it says the possession of strength is not a defense and its absence is not the reason for the fall. What destroyed ʿĀd was the question they asked — *who is greater than us in strength?* — which is not a factual error but a category error. They compared themselves to other created things and concluded they were at the top of that ranking, and in doing so they forgot that the ranking had a maker.

The modern form of the question is identical and is asked constantly, in the language of capability rather than of muscle: who can outproduce us, out-innovate us, out-surveil us, out-earn us, outlast us? The Qur'an's answer to every version of it is the same sentence: *"Have they not considered that God, Who created them, is greater than them in strength?"* (41:15). The strength in question is not comparative but derivative, and a derivative strength that forgets its source behaves, in the Qur'an's accounts, exactly as ʿĀd behaved — right up to the day the wind arrives.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:19

> **Truly We sent upon them a howling wind on a day of incessant misfortune.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Ṣarṣar: The Sound and the Cold in One Word**

*Innā arsalnā ʿalayhim rīḥan ṣarṣaran fī yawmi naḥsin mustamirr.* The wind is *ṣarṣar*, a doubled form from the root *ṣ-r-r*, and the doubling in Arabic signals intensity and repetition. The lexicons give the word two concurrent senses: a wind that makes a piercing, whistling sound, and a wind that is bitterly cold. The doubled form captures a noise that does not stop — the continuous shriek of air forced through a gap — and the cold that comes with it.

The Qur'an uses the same adjective in two other descriptions of the same event, each adding a quality:

> *"And as for ʿĀd, they were destroyed by a wind ṣarṣarin ʿātiyah — howling, raging."* (Qur'an 69:6)

> *"But if they turn away, say: I have warned you of a thunderbolt like the thunderbolt of ʿĀd and Thamūd... And as for ʿĀd, We sent upon them a wind ṣarṣaran (barren, screaming)."* (Qur'an 41:13, 16)

*ʿĀtiyah*, in 69:6, means exceeding its limit, out of control, rebellious against any restraint — a word elsewhere used for one who refuses to be governed. Al-Zamakhsharī explains that the wind was *ʿātiyah* in the sense that it had broken past every bound God normally sets on the winds. In 41:16 the same wind is *ʿaqīm*, barren — the word used for a wind that carries no rain and no seed and produces nothing. A sterile wind that screams and does not stop: the exact negation of the winds the Arabs depended on, which brought the rain that fed their pastures.

The Prophet ﷺ named the two winds and assigned them to their events:

> *"I was aided by the east wind (al-ṣabā), and ʿĀd were destroyed by the west wind (al-dabūr)."* (al-Bukhārī 1035; Muslim 900, from Ibn ʿAbbās)

The same elemental force that carried the Muslims' arrows at Badr carried ʿĀd's bodies into the ground. The ḥadīth is a statement about the neutrality of creation: no wind is good or evil in itself, and the difference between them lies entirely in the command behind them.

**Yawm Naḥs: The Day of Ill Fortune, and What the Qur'an Means By It**

*Yawmi naḥsin* — "a day of misfortune," literally a day of ill omen. The Arabic vocabulary of *naḥs* and *saʿd* belongs to the pre-Islamic calendar tradition, in which certain days were considered unlucky and others auspicious, and no significant action was begun on a *naḥs* day. The Qur'an uses the vocabulary — here and in *"days of continuing ill fortune (ayyāmi naḥsin mustamirrāt)"* (41:16) — and it is important to be precise about what it is doing when it does.

The Prophet ﷺ abolished the belief the vocabulary expressed:

> *"There is no contagion by itself (ʿadwā), no omen (ṭiyarah), no hāmah, and no ṣafar."* (al-Bukhārī 5757; Muslim 2220, from Abū Hurayrah)

> *"Ṭiyarah (seeking omens) is shirk; ṭiyarah is shirk; ṭiyarah is shirk."* (Abū Dāwūd 3910; al-Tirmidhī 1614, from Ibn Masʿūd; graded ṣaḥīḥ)

The apparent tension dissolves when the verses are read carefully. The day of ʿĀd's destruction was *naḥs* not because the calendar marked it as unlucky but because a specific event happened in it. The ill fortune is in the event, not in the date. Al-Qurṭubī makes exactly this point in his discussion of 41:16: the days were described as days of misfortune because the misfortune occurred in them, and any day in which God's punishment falls upon a people is a day of *naḥs* for them. The Qur'an thus uses the Arabs' own word and quietly transfers its meaning from astrology to consequence.

The same distinction governs the Prophet's ﷺ instruction about the wind itself. He did not treat it as an omen to be read but as a creature to be addressed:

> *"When the wind blows, do not curse it, but ask God for its good and the good of what it was sent with, and seek refuge in Him from its evil and the evil of what it was sent with."* (al-Tirmidhī 3449; Abū Dāwūd 5097)

ʿĀʾishah reported that when the sky clouded over, the Prophet ﷺ would become visibly anxious, going in and out, until the rain fell — and he explained: *"How can I be certain that it is not a punishment? A people were punished with a wind, and a people have seen the punishment and thought it was a cloud bringing rain"* (Muslim 899), alluding to 46:24. The wind is not a sign to be decoded; it is a means that may carry mercy or punishment, and the believer's response to it is supplication rather than interpretation.

**Mustamirr: The Same Word the Mockers Used**

The most striking feature of the verse is its last word. *Mustamirr* is the identical adjective the Quraysh applied to the miracle in verse 2: *"siḥrun mustamirr"* — "incessant sorcery," or, on the second reading, "powerful sorcery." Verse 19 uses it of their predecessors' punishment: *yawmi naḥsin mustamirr* — "a day of incessant misfortune," or "of severe misfortune."

The echo is not accidental, and the commentators noted it. Al-Ṭabarī records that *mustamirr* here, as in verse 2, can mean continuous or powerful. The word therefore travels through the sūrah from the mouth of the denier to the description of the destroyed. The thing they called persistent turned out to be the quality of what would come for them: persistent, unbroken, powerful, and continuing without relief. Sūrah al-Ḥāqqah supplies the duration:

> *"Which He imposed upon them seven nights and eight days, ḥusūman — in unbroken succession."* (Qur'an 69:7)

*Ḥusūman* is from *ḥ-s-m*, to cut off completely, to cauterize — the word for a wound seared so that no bleeding remains. Applied to days, it means consecutive without interruption, one following the other with no gap. Seven nights and eight days: the count begins and ends with a night, so the eighth day's end is included in the seventh night's beginning, and there was no interval of relief anywhere in it.

The verbal irony teaches something about language. The Quraysh reached for the strongest word they had to dismiss the sign, and the Qur'an took that word and applied it to the fate of the people who had done the same thing before them. Words used to reject a warning become, in the Qur'an's handling, the vocabulary of the warning's fulfillment.

**Sent, Not Occurred**

The verse's verb is *arsalnā* — "We sent." The wind did not arise. It was dispatched. The same verb governs God's sending of messengers (*arsalnā Nūḥan*, *arsalnā Mūsā*), and its use here places the destructive wind in the same category as the prophet: an envoy. Al-Zamakhsharī notes the deliberate parallel — ʿĀd received a messenger they rejected and then received a messenger they could not reject.

Sūrah al-Aḥqāf describes the arrival in terms of misrecognition:

> *"Then when they saw it as a cloud approaching their valleys, they said: This is a cloud bringing us rain. Nay! It is what you sought to hasten — a wind wherein is a painful punishment, destroying everything by the Command of its Lord. And by the morning nothing could be seen save their dwellings. Thus do We requite the guilty people."* (Qur'an 46:24–25)

They announced their own relief in the moment of their destruction, and the Qur'an preserves their sentence — *"this is a cloud bringing us rain"* — as one of the most pitiful lines in the Book. The same people who had said of the sign "this is sorcery" now said of the punishment "this is rain." Both statements were interpretations imposed on evidence to avoid its meaning, and both were wrong in the same direction.

**Application: The Day That Turns**

Verse 19 describes a single day in which a prosperous civilization's fortune reversed completely and did not reverse back. The structure is recognizable in human affairs at every scale, and it is the reason the Qur'an's vocabulary of *naḥs* and *saʿd* is worth keeping while its astrology is discarded.

There are days in which everything that had been working stops working at once, and the person experiencing such a day discovers that his arrangements were never the cause of their own success. A market, a diagnosis, a phone call, a season. The lesson the verse intends is not fatalism but accurate attribution: the good days were sent too. The same hand that sent the rain sent the wind, and the person who thanks God in the first case and curses the calendar in the second has misunderstood both.

The practical instruction the tradition drew from this is the one the Prophet ﷺ practiced: address God when the wind rises. Not decode the weather, not calculate the omen, not curse the day — but ask for its good and take refuge from its evil. That is a complete psychology of uncertainty in one sentence. It refuses both superstition and complacency, and it replaces them with a relationship: the thing you cannot control is in the hands of Someone you can speak to.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:20

> **Tearing out people as if they were uprooted palm trunks.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Tanziʿu: The Wind That Extracts**

*Tanziʿu al-nāsa ka-annahum aʿjāzu nakhlin munqaʿir.* The participle is from *n-z-ʿ*, to pull out, to wrench, to extract by force — the verb used for drawing water from a deep well, for pulling an arrow from a wound, and, in the Qur'an, for the angels who take souls:

> *"By those who tear out violently (al-nāziʿāti gharqā)."* (Qur'an 79:1)

The same word opens a sūrah about the Resurrection and is used here for a wind lifting human beings out of the ground. Ibn al-Jawzī explains the action concretely: the people of ʿĀd dug holes and hid in them from the wind, and the wind wrenched them out and flung them headfirst, so that their necks were crushed and their heads severed from their bodies. Al-Zamakhsharī and al-Bayḍāwī record the same. The image is of shelter becoming useless — the earth itself, the last refuge, refusing to hold them.

Sūrah al-Aḥqāf confirms the totality: *"destroying everything by the Command of its Lord, and by the morning nothing could be seen save their dwellings"* (46:25). The houses remained. The people did not. Whatever ʿĀd had built to outlast them did outlast them, and the survival of the buildings is part of the punishment, because a ruin is a structure whose inhabitants are gone, and it declares that fact permanently to everyone who passes.

**Aʿjāz: The Trunk, Not the Tree**

The simile specifies a part of the palm: *aʿjāz*, plural of *ʿajuz*, the lower portion, the base, the trunk — the part left standing when the crown is gone. The Qur'an does not say they were like palm trees; it says they were like the trunks of palm trees, and then adds *munqaʿir*, the seventh form passive of *q-ʿ-r*, meaning uprooted, torn from its deep base. *Qaʿr* is the bottom, the depth, the far interior; *inqaʿara al-nakhl* means the palm was felled from its roots.

Sūrah al-Ḥāqqah uses a different adjective for the same trunk and thereby completes the picture:

> *"So that thou mightest have seen the people laid low therein, as though they were hollow palm trunks (aʿjāzu nakhlin khāwiyah)."* (Qur'an 69:7)

*Khāwiyah* means hollow, empty within, collapsed inward. Al-Qurṭubī and Ibn Kathīr note the combination across the two sūrahs: trunks that are hollow and uprooted — bodies emptied of life and torn from the ground they had dug into. The two adjectives describe the same scene from two angles, and no reader of both sūrahs can miss the picture: great stature, empty interior, no root.

**Why a Palm Tree**

The choice of the palm is precise on three levels, and each level carries the sūrah's argument.

The first is physical. ʿĀd were famed for great stature; Ibn al-Jawzī notes that they are compared to palms because of their size, and the Qur'an's own description of their buildings — *Iram dhāt al-ʿimād* — reinforces the vertical. A palm is the tallest thing in an Arabian landscape, straight, thick, and imposing. The simile flatters before it devastates: they were tall as palms, and they fell as palms fall — the whole length of the trunk hitting the ground at once, without bending, without resistance, because a palm has no flexibility in it. A tree that cannot bend cannot survive a wind that will not stop.

The second is agricultural. The date palm was the staple of Arabian life — food, shade, timber, fiber, and the measure of wealth. The Qur'an mentions it constantly and always as blessing:

> *"He causes gardens to grow, of vines and palms, and olives and pomegranates."* (Qur'an 6:99)

> *"And the earth — We spread it out and set therein firm mountains, and cause to grow therein every noble pair."* (Qur'an 50:7)

> *"And palms with clustered spathes."* (Qur'an 26:148)

> *"And date palms, their shoots coming forth from within, layered."* (Qur'an 55:11)

And in the parable of the good word:

> *"Hast thou not considered how God strikes a similitude — a good word is like a goodly tree, its root firm and its branches in the sky, yielding its fruit at every season by the leave of its Lord?"* (Qur'an 14:24–25)

The contrast between 14:24 and 54:20 is the whole moral argument of the sūrah in two images. The good word is a tree whose *root is firm* (*aṣluhā thābit*); ʿĀd were trunks *torn from their root* (*munqaʿir*). The difference is not height — both are tall — but where the height is anchored. And the Qur'an names the opposite of the good tree immediately after:

> *"And the similitude of an evil word is like an evil tree, uprooted (mujtaththah) from the earth, having no stability."* (Qur'an 14:26)

*Mujtaththah*, uprooted, having no stability — the same condition as *munqaʿir*. ʿĀd had built the tallest structures in the world upon an evil word: *man ashaddu minnā quwwah*. The verse describes the structures' inhabitants as the Qur'an describes the word they were built on.

The third level is the inversion of blessing. The palm is the Arabs' own symbol of sustenance, and it becomes the image of their corpses. The Qur'an repeatedly performs this operation: the medium of blessing becomes the medium of punishment. Water drowns Noah's people; wind destroys ʿĀd; the she-camel, a gift from God, becomes the occasion of Thamūd's destruction; the sea delivers Moses and swallows Pharaoh. Creation is not sorted into good things and bad things. Every creature is a means whose moral quality is set by the command governing it, and the people of ʿĀd met their end in the image of the plant that had fed them.

**Munqaʿir: The Word That Also Describes a Fall From Power**

The seventh form of *q-ʿ-r* has a further usage worth noting. *Inqaʿara* is used in Arabic for something felled from its foundation — a tree, a building, a dynasty. The word therefore carries the sense of institutional collapse as well as physical uprooting, and the Qur'an's use of it for human bodies produces a deliberate ambiguity: these were not only people, they were a civilization, and the civilization is described in the same verb as the corpses. Sūrah al-Ḥāqqah makes the collapse explicit in its following verse:

> *"So is there any who remembers?"* — and before it: *"And they became as though nothing had been (fa-hal tarā lahum min bāqiyah)."* (Qur'an 69:8)

*Min bāqiyah* — any remainder. The question expects the answer "no." The tallest builders in the world left no remnant of themselves, only a remnant of their walls.

**Application: What Survives and What Does Not**

Verse 20 asks the reader to consider what remained of ʿĀd: dwellings, without inhabitants. The buildings were designed for permanence and achieved it; the people were designed for permanence and did not. The disproportion is the lesson, and it is available in miniature to anyone who has walked through a house where a family once lived and is now elsewhere, or read a company's name on a facade whose business closed decades ago.

The practical question the verse poses is not "should I build?" — the Qur'an permits and even praises building, and Solomon's constructions are described with approval. It is "what am I anchoring the building to?" A palm that is tall and hollow and rootless is a structure whose failure is already determined by its own success; the taller it grows, the harder it falls, and the fall is total because there is nothing at the base to hold it. The verse describes people who invested everything in the visible part of themselves — stature, architecture, strength — and nothing in the part that goes into the ground.

The Qur'an's counter-image is not a shorter tree. It is the same tree with a root: *"a good word is like a goodly tree, its root firm and its branches in the sky."* The branches are in the sky; the ambition is not condemned. The root is firm; the anchorage is the point. ʿĀd had the sky part. Verse 20 reports what happens to that configuration in a wind.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:21

> **How, then, were My Punishment and My warnings?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refrain's Second Appearance, and What Repetition Proves**

The identical question, word for word, in the same position in the narrative structure, a second time. The refrain is not varied — no synonym substituted, no intensifier added, no new noun introduced. *Fa-kayfa kāna ʿadhābī wa-nudhur* appears at verse 16 after Noah and here at verse 21 after ʿĀd, and it will appear again at verse 30 after Thamūd.

The unvaried repetition is itself the content of the verse. A phrase that changes each time it appears expresses a mood; a phrase that does not change expresses a law. The Qur'an is asserting, by the sheer fact of identical repetition, that what happened to Noah's people and what happened to ʿĀd were not two events but one event happening twice — the same cause, the same process, the same result, the same sentence of judgment pronounced in the same words.

This is the Qur'anic doctrine of *sunnat Allāh*, God's way or established practice, and the Qur'an states its constancy in terms that admit no exception:

> *"That is the way of God (sunnat Allāh) with those who passed away before, and thou wilt not find in the way of God any change (tabdīl)."* (Qur'an 33:62)

> *"A sunnah of those We sent before thee of Our messengers, and thou wilt not find in Our sunnah any alteration."* (Qur'an 17:77)

> *"And they will not await anything but the way of the former peoples. And thou wilt not find in the way of God any alteration, nor wilt thou find in the way of God any deviation."* (Qur'an 35:43)

Three verses, three formulations: no change (*tabdīl*), no alteration (*taḥwīl*), no deviation. Al-Qurṭubī and Ibn Kathīr both cite these passages when explaining the refrains of al-Qamar, and the connection is exact: the refrain's verbal identity is the literary enactment of the sunnah's causal identity.

**What the Law States**

The law can be written as a conditional, and the Qur'an writes it as such elsewhere:

> *"That is because God would never change a blessing He has bestowed upon a people until they change what is in themselves."* (Qur'an 8:53; cf. 13:11)

> *"And when We will to destroy a town, We command its affluent ones, and they commit transgression therein; so the word falls due upon them, and We destroy it utterly."* (Qur'an 17:16)

The sequence in verse 21's case is fully documented: ʿĀd were given stature, buildings, and land (the blessing); they said *who is greater than us in strength* (the change in themselves); Hūd warned them (the warning); they denied (the crime); the wind came (the punishment); and God asks how it was (the verdict). Every step is present, and every step had also been present for Noah's people. The two accounts in this sūrah are therefore not a list but a demonstration — two data points establishing that the relationship is not coincidence.

The refrain asks the question in the past tense — *kayfa kāna*, "how was" — which places the verdict beyond argument. The punishment is not threatened here; it is referred to as a completed fact, and the only thing open to inquiry is its quality. This is the same rhetorical posture the Qur'an takes toward the Hour: *"The Command of God has come"* (16:1). Once an event is filed as accomplished, no amount of wishing revises it.

**ʿAdhābī wa-Nudhur in the Case of ʿĀd**

The refrain pairs punishment and warnings, and in the case of ʿĀd the pairing is unusually explicit in the record. Hūd's warnings are preserved in four separate sūrahs — 7:65–72, 11:50–60, 26:123–140, and 46:21–26 — and their content is remarkably consistent. He warns of God's lordship, of their ingratitude for what they were given, of the punishment of a tremendous day, and of the futility of their idols:

> *"O my people, I do not ask of you any reward for it. My reward is only upon Him who created me. Will you not then understand?"* (Qur'an 11:51)

> *"And fear Him who has aided you with what you know — aided you with cattle and sons, and gardens and springs. Truly I fear for you the punishment of a tremendous day."* (Qur'an 26:132–135)

The warnings name the very blessings the people were proud of — cattle, sons, gardens, springs — and turn them into evidence against them. The Qur'an's method of warning is to take what a people considers its achievement and read it as a debt. Then the refrain asks: *wa-nudhur* — and My warnings? Not "and their response to My warnings," but the warnings themselves, standing beside the punishment as the two halves of one divine act.

Ibn al-Jawzī's gloss of the refrain at this position is that the punishment they received was justified *because* the warnings had come. The justification is internal to the refrain's grammar: the two nouns are conjoined as a single possession — *My* punishment and *My* warnings — because in God's ordering of affairs they cannot be separated. A punishment without a warning would be oppression, and the Qur'an denies oppression of God absolutely:

> *"And thy Lord is not unjust to the servants."* (Qur'an 41:46)

> *"Truly God does not wrong men in anything, but men wrong themselves."* (Qur'an 10:44)

**The Question Addressed to the Traveler**

The second appearance of the refrain changes its addressee. After Noah it was addressed to a reader of remote history. After ʿĀd it is addressed to people who traveled through the region. The southern caravan route to Yemen passed within reach of al-Aḥqāf, and the Qur'an insists on the physical availability of the evidence:

> *"And indeed they pass by them in the morning and by night. Will you not then reflect?"* (Qur'an 37:137–138)

> *"Say: Travel in the land and see how was the end of the guilty."* (Qur'an 27:69)

> *"Have they not traveled in the land, that they might have hearts wherewith to understand, or ears wherewith to hear?"* (Qur'an 22:46)

The command to travel — *sīrū fī al-arḍ* — appears more than a dozen times in the Qur'an and is, in effect, an instruction to conduct fieldwork. The refrain at verse 21 is the report of that fieldwork, delivered by God and asking the traveler whether his own observations confirm it. The hollow trunks are gone; the dwellings remain, and the wind still blows through al-Aḥqāf.

**Application: Recognizing a Pattern Rather Than an Event**

The refrain's repetition teaches a specific intellectual habit: identifying a pattern requires at least two instances, and most people will only accept a pattern after the third. The Qur'an supplies five in fifty-five verses, and after each of the first three it stops to ask the same question in the same words, forcing the reader to notice that the words are the same.

The habit is useful well beyond theology. A business that fails once may have had bad luck; a business that fails twice in the same way has a system; a person whose relationships end in the same manner three times has a pattern that will not be visible to him as long as he treats each ending as its own story. The refrain's refusal to vary is a refusal to let the reader file each case separately. *Fa-kayfa kāna ʿadhābī wa-nudhur* — and the answer is: exactly as before.

There is a personal application that is harder to accept. If the pattern is constant on God's side, then the variable in each instance is on the human side, and the variable is always the same one: the warning was received and not acted upon. Verse 21 therefore functions as a mirror. The reader who asks "how was their punishment?" is being led to ask the adjacent question — how is my response to the warnings I have already been given? The refrain does not need to be answered about ʿĀd. It needs to be answered about the person reciting it.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:22

> **And indeed We have made the Qur'an easy to remember; so is there any who remembers?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refrain Returns, and This Time It Answers the Previous Verse**

Placed immediately after the question about ʿĀd's punishment, verse 22 is the sūrah's answer to its own severity. Verse 21 asks how the punishment was; verse 22 declares that the Qur'an has been made easy. The juxtaposition states a proportion that governs the whole chapter: the punishment is described in the past tense and cannot be altered, and the reminder is described in the perfect tense and is available now. One door has closed; the other is standing open, and the sūrah mentions the open one every time it mentions the closed one.

Al-Qushayrī and the commentators in his tradition read the pairing as deliberate mercy. The destruction is announced so that the offer that follows it will be understood in its proper weight. A person who has not been frightened does not value an open door; a person who has been frightened and is then told the way out is easy will use it.

**The Ease Is a Property of the Text's Design**

Verse 17 established the claim; verse 22 permits an examination of the mechanism, and the mechanism is visible in this sūrah more clearly than in almost any other. Al-Qamar is a masterpiece of oral design, and every feature of its design serves retention.

The verse-endings rhyme. The sūrah runs on a small set of terminal sounds — *-īr* (*mustamirr*, *munqaʿir*, *mudhakkir*, *baṣīr*), *-ūr* (*nudhur*, *nukur*, *ʿasur*, *suʿur*), *-ar* (*qamar*, *ghadar*), *-āʾ* (*samāʾ*, *māʾ*), *-ūn*/*-īn* — and the rhymes are not decorative. They function as acoustic punctuation, telling the reciter where a unit ends and telling the listener when the next unit's ending will arrive. A person who has recited al-Qamar twice can predict the sound of a verse-ending he has not yet reached, and prediction is the mechanism of memory.

The verses are short. The average verse in al-Qamar is under ten words, and the shortest — *kadhdhabat ʿĀd* — is two. Human short-term memory holds a small number of units, and the Qur'an's verse structure is calibrated to it. Long periods are broken by *wa* and *fa* into breath-length clauses, each with its own terminal rhyme.

The refrains repeat verbatim. Two of them, four times each, at predictable positions — after each destroyed nation. A memorizer learns eight verses of the sūrah in two recitations, and those eight verses then serve as anchors for everything between them.

The narratives are already known. ʿĀd, Thamūd, Lot, Pharaoh, and Noah were familiar figures to a Makkan audience, and the sūrah alludes rather than narrates. Memorization is far easier when new material attaches to existing structure, and al-Qamar's historical sections are almost entirely attachment: the audience supplies the story and the text supplies the verdict.

**Bi-Lisānika: The Ease of the Tongue**

The Qur'an names the organ of its own facilitation:

> *"And We have only made this easy upon thy tongue (yassarnāhu bi-lisānika), that thou mayest give glad tidings unto the reverent thereby, and that thereby thou mayest warn a contentious people."* (Qur'an 19:97)

> *"Truly We have made it easy upon thy tongue, in their own language, that haply they may take heed."* (Qur'an 44:58)

The phrase *ʿalā lisānika* specifies that the ease is in pronunciation and flow — the text is constructed to be said aloud, and saying it aloud is the primary mode of its transmission. This is why the Qur'an is not properly a book that is read but a recitation that is heard; the word *Qurʾān* itself is a verbal noun from *q-r-ʾ*, "to recite, to read aloud." Its name is an instruction about its medium.

The consequence is that the ease extends beyond Arabic speakers. The recitation is learned by sound before it is learned by meaning, and millions of Muslims in every continent recite verses they cannot translate. A child in Java, in Kano, in Sarajevo, or in Dearborn memorizes al-Qamar in Arabic without knowing Arabic, because the text is designed for the tongue rather than for the lexicon. The claim of verse 17 and verse 22 is therefore verified in a population the Qur'an's first audience could not have imagined, and it is verified by the mechanism the verses name: ease upon the tongue.

**Al-Qurʾān: The Word That Means "The Reciting"**

It is worth pausing on the noun the verse makes easy. *Al-Qurʾān* is a definite verbal noun — "the Recitation," or "the Reading" — and the Qur'an uses it of itself constantly, sometimes in the same breath as the ease:

> *"Indeed upon Us is its collection and its recitation (qurʾānahu). So when We recite it, follow its recitation. Then upon Us is its explanation."* (Qur'an 75:17–19)

> *"And recite the Qur'an with measured recitation (rattili al-Qurʾāna tartīlā)."* (Qur'an 73:4)

> *"This is a Book whose verses are made firm (uḥkimat āyātuhu) and then explained in detail, from One Wise, All-Aware."* (Qur'an 11:1)

The last of these is significant. *Uḥkimat* — made firm, made precise, put in order — is from the same root as *ḥikmah*, which verse 5 called *bālighah*, conclusive. The Book is described as firmly constructed and then explained in detail, and the construction is what makes the explanation possible. Verse 22's ease is not the ease of vagueness, which would allow anyone to read anything into it; it is the ease of a structure so well built that its contents can be carried.

**Levels of Ease**

The commentators distinguish levels at which the facilitation operates, and the distinction is useful because it prevents a false comfort. Ibn ʿAjībah names three: ease of recitation for the tongue, ease of memorization for the mind, and ease of understanding for the heart. Al-Ṭabrisī adds that the ease extends to the acquisition of its meanings for those who seek them.

These are not identical achievements, and conflating them produces a common modern error — the assumption that because the text is easy to memorize it is easy to live by. The Qur'an is easy to carry; obedience is not easy. Verse 22 says *yassarnāhu li-l-dhikr*, "We made it easy for remembrance," and remembrance in the Qur'an's own usage is not a mental state but an act with consequences:

> *"So remind — truly the Reminder benefits the believers."* (Qur'an 51:55)

> *"So remind — thou art but a reminder. Thou art not a taskmaster over them."* (Qur'an 88:21–22)

The ease is in the delivery and the retention. What the retained thing demands is not easy at all, which is why the sūrah's second refrain is followed, each time, by another nation's collapse.

**Fa-Hal Min Mudhakkir: The Question After ʿĀd**

The question at the end of verse 22 lands differently here than it did at verse 17. After Noah's flood, the reader had witnessed a total destruction and a miraculous survival; the question was almost hopeful — surely someone will take heed. After ʿĀd, the reader has just been told that the tallest, strongest, most accomplished civilization in the world was lifted out of the ground and dropped, and that nothing remained but its walls. The question is now asked of a person who has seen the outcome of strength.

The structure of the sūrah is therefore cumulative in its pressure. Each refrain arrives after a heavier case, and each time the same easy offer is repeated in the same words. By the fourth occurrence at verse 40 the reader understands what the repetition means: God is not getting tired of asking. The question is asked after every catastrophe in the chapter because the offer is genuinely renewed after every catastrophe, and because the reader's own catastrophe has not yet come.

**Application: Using the Ease**

Verse 22 makes a promise and, in making it, creates an obligation. If the Qur'an is easy to remember, then not remembering it is not an accident of capacity but a matter of allocation. The person who says he cannot memorize a two-page sūrah is not describing an inability; he is describing a set of priorities, and the sūrah he is reading at that moment is the Qur'an's own refutation of his sentence.

The tradition's method is practical and undemanding. Learn a small number of verses at a time. Recite them aloud, since the ease is in the tongue. Repeat them in prayer, since the Qur'an's own instruction is to recite what is easy of it: *"So recite what is easy of the Qur'an"* (73:20). Do not advance to a new portion until the previous one is retained together with what it means and what it asks of you — the method of the Companions as transmitted by Abū ʿAbd al-Raḥmān al-Sulamī, ten verses at a time, not moving on until knowledge and action had been learned from them.

Al-Qamar is an ideal beginning for anyone who has never memorized a sūrah: fifty-five short verses, two repeated refrains that supply sixteen verses of the total almost for free, five stories the reader already knows, and a rhyme scheme that predicts its own endings. The sūrah that says four times "We have made it easy" is itself one of the easiest things in the Book. That is not a coincidence; it is the claim demonstrating itself in the text that makes it.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:23

> **Thamūd denied the warnings.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Denial Directed at the Warning Itself**

*Kadhdhabat Thamūdu bi-l-nudhur.* The verse is three words long, and its middle word — the preposition *bi* — carries a distinction worth drawing. The verb *kadhdhaba* can take a direct object ("they denied so-and-so") or the preposition *bi* ("they denied by means of / they declared false"), and the second construction is the Qur'an's standard formula for rejecting a revelation: *kadhdhabū bi-āyātinā*, "they denied Our signs"; *kadhdhabū bi-l-nudhur*, "they denied the warnings."

The difference is not merely grammatical. To deny a person is to reject a claimant; to deny *bi* something is to declare the thing itself false. Thamūd did not merely refuse to follow Ṣāliḥ; they declared the warnings he brought to be untrue. Their denial was directed at the content of the message, and verse 23 records the object of the denial as *nudhur* — plural, warnings, all of them.

The plural matters. Ṣāliḥ's warnings were multiple and are preserved across four sūrahs: the warning against idolatry (7:73), the warning against their extravagance and insecurity (26:146–152), the warning about the she-camel (11:64), the warning of a coming punishment (26:156), and the warning of the three-day respite (11:65). The verse says they denied *the warnings*, in the plural, comprehensively. Not one claim among many was rejected; the entire category was.

**Who Thamūd Were, and Why the Qur'an Expected the Audience to Know**

Thamūd were an Arabian tribe established in al-Ḥijr — the region northwest of the Arabian peninsula on the caravan road between Makkah and Syria, known today as Madāʾin Ṣāliḥ, the cities of Ṣāliḥ. Their name appears in pre-Islamic Arabic poetry and in Assyrian records from the eighth century BCE, and their rock-cut architecture survives in quantity: tombs and dwellings carved into sandstone cliffs, with facades of columns and pediments, standing in a desert valley that any Qurayshi caravan passed within sight of.

The Qur'an describes both their prosperity and their technique:

> *"And you carve houses in the mountains with skill (fārihīn)."* (Qur'an 26:149; cf. 7:74)

> *"And remember when He made you successors after ʿĀd and established you in the land, taking palaces in its plains and carving the mountains into houses. So remember the blessings of God and do not spread corruption in the earth as ones who work mischief."* (Qur'an 7:74)

*Fārihīn* is from *f-r-ḥ*, delight — carving with pleasure, with mastery, with the enjoyment of a craft performed well. The verse is not hostile to their achievement; it names it as skill and then asks what they did with it. Sūrah al-Ḥijr, named for their valley, records their security:

> *"And the companions of al-Ḥijr denied the messengers. And We gave them Our signs, but they turned away from them. And they used to carve houses from the mountains, secure (āminīn)."* (Qur'an 15:80–82)

*Āminīn*, secure, feeling safe. That is the specific sin al-Qamar is prosecuting: not the carving but the sense of permanence attached to it. Sūrah al-Shams, revealed in the same period, states the diagnosis in a single word:

> *"Thamūd denied in its transgression (bi-ṭaghwāhā)."* (Qur'an 91:11)

*Ṭughyān* is exceeding the limit — the same root as *ṭāghiyah*, which Sūrah al-Ḥāqqah uses for the thing that befell them: *"And as for Thamūd, We guided them, but they preferred blindness to guidance, so the thunderbolt of an abasing punishment seized them for what they were earning"* (41:17), and *"And Thamūd was destroyed by the overwhelming (bi-l-ṭāghiyah)"* (69:5).

**A Monument That Can Be Visited**

Thamūd is the only destroyed nation in the Qur'an whose principal ruins were a functioning part of the first Muslim community's travel experience, and the Prophet ﷺ gave explicit instructions for passing through them. Ibn ʿUmar narrated that when the army halted at al-Ḥijr on the expedition to Tabūk, people drew water from its wells and made dough with it, and the Prophet ﷺ commanded them to pour out the water and feed the dough to the camels, then said:

> *"Do not enter upon these people who were punished unless you are weeping. And if you do not weep, then do not enter upon them, lest what befell them befall you."* (al-Bukhārī 3379, 4332; Muslim 2980)

The report is one of the most instructive in the whole corpus on how a believer should stand before a ruin. The instruction has three parts. Do not enter casually — the site is not scenery. If you enter, weep — the appropriate response to a destroyed civilization is grief, and grief is treated as a protection rather than as a mood. If you cannot weep, do not enter — because the person who can look at the outcome of arrogance without feeling anything has already moved toward it.

Al-Qurṭubī draws the legal and spiritual conclusions from this report at length, and Ibn Kathīr cites it when commenting on the Thamūd passages precisely to establish that these were real places with real remains, not parables. The verse in al-Qamar, *"Thamūd denied the warnings,"* was therefore spoken to people who could ride to the site.

**Denial After Skill: The Pattern of the Third Case**

The three cases so far in the sūrah form a progression, and the progression is not chronological but categorical. Noah's people were numerous and long-endured; their crime was persistent refusal over centuries. ʿĀd were strong and their crime was confidence in strength. Thamūd were skilled and secure, and their crime was the sense of permanence that comes from having built something durable.

Each people is destroyed by the negation of what it trusted. Noah's people drowned in the water that sustained them. ʿĀd were uprooted by wind, having trusted in stature. Thamūd, who carved their houses into mountains, were destroyed by a sound — the least substantial thing imaginable, requiring no material whatsoever, passing through stone as though it were not there. The mountain they hollowed out could not stop a cry.

The Qur'an makes the point with an almost sarcastic precision in Sūrah al-Shams, where the destruction follows immediately upon the naming of their crime and the description of the most wretched man among them rising to kill the camel:

> *"And the Messenger of God said to them: The she-camel of God, and her drink. But they called him a liar and hamstrung her, so their Lord crushed them for their sin and leveled them (fa-damdamah ʿalayhim bi-dhambihim fa-sawwāhā)."* (Qur'an 91:13–14)

*Fa-sawwāhā* — "and He leveled it," or "made them equal." Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr explain the leveling as the destruction falling equally on all of them, the great and the small, without distinction. The people who had carved hierarchy into stone were made flat by a sound.

**Application: The Security of What You Have Built**

Verse 23 names the third form of the same disease. Noah's people mocked; ʿĀd boasted; Thamūd settled in. The third is the most common and the least noticed, because it does not announce itself in words. It is the feeling produced by durable arrangements — a solid house, a stable income, an established reputation, an institution that has survived decades — the feeling that because the arrangement has held, it will hold.

The Qur'an's treatment of this feeling is unsentimental and constant:

> *"And how many a generation have We destroyed before them, who were better in possessions and outward show?"* (Qur'an 19:74)

> *"Has it not guided them how many a generation We destroyed before them, in whose dwellings they walk? Truly in that are signs for those of understanding."* (Qur'an 20:128; cf. 32:26)

*Yamshūna fī masākinihim* — walking in their dwellings. The verse describes people living inside the houses of the destroyed, which is exactly what a traveler at al-Ḥijr does, and exactly what every person does who inherits an institution, a city, a language, or a tradition built by the dead.

The instruction is not to abandon building. Ṣāliḥ's people are criticized for the *amān*, the false security, not for the *naḥt*, the carving. The Qur'an's own vocabulary of building is positive when the building is accompanied by gratitude: *"So remember the blessings of God and do not spread corruption in the earth"* (7:74). The verse's practical question is whether your durable arrangement has produced in you the two things it should produce — remembrance of the Giver and awareness of the fragility of the given — or the one thing it should not, which is the conviction that you are safe.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:24

> **They said: "Shall we follow a single human being from among us? Then we would surely be astray and mad!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Objections Inside One Phrase**

*A-nattabiʿu basharan wāḥidan minnā.* The interrogative *a-* at the head of the sentence marks it as incredulous: "What — are we to follow...?" The object of the following is described with three qualifiers, and each one is a separate objection.

*Basharan* — a human being. Not an angel, not a celestial being, not something visibly connected to heaven. *Wāḥidan* — a single one. Not a council, not a lineage of sages, not a group whose numbers would lend weight. *Minnā* — from among us. Not a stranger of unknown origin whose mystery could command respect, but a neighbor whose family, trade, income, and history they could recite.

The verse is the Qur'an's most compact statement of the sociological obstacle to prophecy, and the Qur'an treats that obstacle as a permanent feature of human life rather than as Thamūd's particular failing:

> *"And nothing prevented men from believing when guidance came unto them, save that they said: Has God sent a human being as a messenger?"* (Qur'an 17:94)

> *"What ails this Messenger, that he eats food and walks in the markets? Why is not an angel sent down unto him, to be a warner with him? Or a treasure be cast down unto him, or a garden whereby he might eat?"* (Qur'an 25:7–8)

> *"Shall a human being guide us?"* (Qur'an 64:6)

> *"They said: You are but human beings like us, and the Compassionate has sent down nothing. You are only lying."* (Qur'an 36:15)

See also 6:6–9, 50, 91; 11:12, 27; 14:10–11; 21:3; 23:24, 33, 47; 26:154, 186; 41:14; 50:2; 74:25. The objection recurs in essentially identical language across every prophetic narrative in the Book, and the Qur'an's own conclusion is that this is how human beings respond to messengers as such.

**Why Familiarity Is the Hardest Obstacle**

The qualifier *minnā*, "from among us," is the one that does the most damage, and it is the one the Qur'an most often quotes. The people of the town knew the messenger's ordinary life: what he ate, where he walked, whom he married, how much he earned, what he was like as a boy. Knowledge of that kind produces a specific cognitive effect — the inability to see the person as a category apart.

The Qur'an reports the same objection in the case of the Prophet ﷺ and answers it with a question of its own:

> *"And they said: Why was this Qur'an not sent down upon a great man from one of the two towns?"* (Qur'an 43:31)

The two towns were Makkah and Ṭāʾif, and the "great men" in the minds of the speakers were al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīrah in Makkah and ʿUrwah ibn Masʿūd in Ṭāʾif. God's answer is a single verse of extraordinary directness:

> *"Is it they who apportion the mercy of thy Lord? We apportion among them their livelihood in the life of this world, and We have raised some of them above others in degrees, that some may take others in service; and the mercy of thy Lord is better than what they amass."* (Qur'an 43:32)

The argument is structural. The objectors are applying the criteria of economic distribution — which God Himself says He has apportioned unequally for functional reasons, so that people serve one another — to the distribution of prophethood, which operates on entirely different criteria. *"God knows best where to place His message"* (6:124). The man who objects that the messenger is not rich enough has revealed that he believes rank in God's economy is purchased with the same currency as rank in his own.

**The Answer: Humanity as Qualification**

The Qur'an does not answer the objection by denying the messenger's humanity. It answers by affirming it and explaining why it is necessary:

> *"Say: I am only a human being like you, unto whom it has been revealed that your God is one God. So whoever hopes for the meeting of his Lord, let him do righteous work and associate none in the worship of his Lord."* (Qur'an 18:110)

> *"And We sent not before thee any but men unto whom We revealed, from among the people of the towns. Have they not traveled in the land and seen how was the end of those before them?"* (Qur'an 12:109)

> *"Say: If there were upon the earth angels walking at ease, We would surely have sent down unto them from the heaven an angel as a messenger."* (Qur'an 17:95)

The last of these is the decisive argument. A messenger exists to be followed, and a being can only be followed by creatures capable of doing what it does. If an angel came to human beings and said "worship God and be patient," the reply would be immediate: that is easy for you, you have no appetite, no body, no fear of death, no desire for wealth. The humanity of the messenger is what makes his example usable. He ate and was hungry; he fasted. He married and had desires; he was chaste. He was persecuted and injured; he was patient. He was victorious; he was not arrogant. Every virtue he demonstrated was demonstrated under human conditions, which is the only way a demonstration can obligate a human being.

The Prophet ﷺ's own life supplies the case study the objection ignores. He was orphaned, poor, a shepherd, a trader, a husband, a father who buried children, a general, a judge, a statesman — and the Qur'an presents him as *uswatun ḥasanah*, "an excellent example" (33:21), precisely because he occupied the full range of human situations. A single human being from among them was exactly the specification required.

**Innā Idhan La-Fī Ḍalālin Wa-Suʿur: The Sentence That Returns**

The people of Thamūd complete their objection with a prediction about themselves: *innā idhan la-fī ḍalālin wa-suʿur* — "then we would surely be in error and madness." The emphatic *inna* plus the lām of confirmation plus *idhan*, "in that case," produces a sentence of unusual force. They are not merely refusing; they are declaring that following Ṣāliḥ would constitute insanity.

*Suʿur* is from *s-ʿ-r*, a root meaning blaze, fire, and the agitated state produced by heat — hence madness, frenzy, and burning. The same root gives *saʿīr*, the Blazing Fire, one of the Qur'an's names for Hell, used throughout: *"and We have prepared for them the punishment of the Blaze"* (67:5); *"Truly the guilty are in error and burning"* — and there the words are these words.

Verse 47 of this same sūrah says: *inna al-mujrimīna fī ḍalālin wa-suʿur* — "Truly the guilty are in error and burning." The phrase is identical. The two words Thamūd chose to describe the condition they would enter by following a prophet are the two words God chooses, twenty-three verses later, to describe the condition they actually entered by refusing him.

Ibn Kathīr and al-Bayḍāwī both mark the correspondence. It is one of the Qur'an's most economical forms of verdict: no refutation, no argument, no rebuttal — simply the reuse of the accusers' own vocabulary, twenty-three verses later, as a description of their fate. The sentence they pronounced on the hypothetical alternative became the sentence pronounced on them.

**Application: The Ordinary Person You Will Not Listen To**

Verse 24 describes a mechanism that operates constantly and rarely with prophetic stakes. People discount truth in proportion to their familiarity with its source. A medical warning from a sibling is received differently from the same warning from a physician; financial advice from a parent is weighed differently from identical advice from an advisor; moral counsel from a colleague is dismissed in a way that counsel from a stranger is not. The discount is applied because the hearer possesses extraneous information — the speaker's flaws, his ordinary habits, his past mistakes — that has nothing to do with the truth of what he is saying.

The logical name for this is the genetic fallacy, and its social name is contempt. The Qur'an's treatment of it is to expose the criteria being used and to ask whether they are the relevant ones. *Man ashaddu minnā quwwah* was ʿĀd's criterion; *a-ulqiya al-dhikru ʿalayhi min bayninā*, verse 25, is Thamūd's, and it is a criterion of status. Neither criterion has any bearing on whether a statement is true, and the verse's structure — the objection, then the verdict returned in the objectors' own words — is the Qur'an's demonstration of what happens to people who substitute one for the other.

There is a second application, and it is harder. Verse 24's speakers were not stupid. They were prosperous, skilled, secure, and articulate, and their objection had a rational form. What it lacked was any engagement with the content of Ṣāliḥ's claim. Not one word of their sentence addresses what he said; every word of it addresses who he was and how many of him there were. The practical test the verse offers is therefore a diagnostic one: when you reject a claim, check whether your reasons are about the claim. If your sentence could be uttered identically about any messenger from any direction, it is not an argument. It is the sentence Thamūd used, and verse 47 is waiting for it.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:25

> **Has the reminder been cast upon him from among us? Nay — he is an insolent liar.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Ulqiya: The Verb of Things Thrown Down From Above**

*A-ulqiya al-dhikru ʿalayhi min bayninā.* The verb is the fourth form passive of *l-q-y*, to throw, to cast, to cause to fall upon. It is not the ordinary verb for revelation — the Qur'an's standard terms are *awḥā* (to inspire), *anzala* (to send down), *nazzala* (to send down in stages), *atlā* (to recite). *Ulqiya* is more physical, and it carries a note of the objectors' incredulity: was something *dropped* on this man?

The Qur'an uses the same verb elsewhere in contexts of descent from a higher realm:

> *"And thus We have revealed unto thee a Spirit from Our Command (rūḥan min amrinā). Thou knewest not what the Book was, nor faith; but We have made it a Light whereby We guide whomsoever We will of Our servants."* (Qur'an 42:52)

> *"Blessed be He who sent down the Criterion (al-Furqān) upon His servant, that he might be a warner unto all beings."* (Qur'an 25:1)

The objectors' use of *ulqiya* is thus not entirely wrong — it describes what the Prophet claimed was happening — but it is spoken as a question of amazement, and its amazement is directed at the recipient rather than at the act. That is the whole content of their objection: not "revelation does not exist" but "revelation would not come to *him*."

**Min Bayninā: The Comparative Objection**

*Min bayninā*, "from among us," means "rather than from among us" — that is, in preference to the rest of us. The objection is explicitly comparative, and it names a hierarchy the objectors believe God ought to have respected. The same objection is recorded against the Prophet ﷺ in the verse quoted above (43:31), and the Qur'an's answer there is a question about who is doing the apportioning:

> *"Is it they who apportion the mercy of thy Lord?"* (Qur'an 43:32)

The comparative objection always assumes that the objector's ranking is the correct ranking and that God's choice must be measured against it. God's response is that the ranking being applied belongs to a different domain entirely. The criteria of *min bayninā* — seniority, wealth, tribal standing, eloquence, age — are the criteria by which human societies distribute honors, and prophethood is not an honor distributed by a society.

Al-Rāzī develops the point with a further observation: the objectors' criterion, if accepted, would produce a worse outcome by their own lights. A messenger chosen from the elite would be suspected of seeking power, since he already had it; a messenger chosen from the ordinary would be suspect of seeking status, since he lacked it. No choice is safe from the suspicion of a person determined to suspect. The objection therefore cannot be satisfied and is not, in fact, an objection but a disposition.

**Bal: The Particle That Ends the Question**

*Bal huwa kadhdhābun ashir.* The particle *bal* is one of the most important small words in Arabic. It means "nay," "rather," "on the contrary," and it performs two operations at once: it cancels what preceded and it substitutes something else. Its appearance here means the question *a-ulqiya al-dhikru* was not actually a question. It was a pause. The speakers asked it, did not wait for an answer, and answered it themselves with a slur.

Al-Zamakhsharī notes the rhetorical function of *bal* throughout the Qur'an: it marks the point at which a speaker abandons inquiry and declares a position. Its presence is a diagnostic of closed minds, and it appears in the Qur'an dozens of times in the mouths of disbelievers and dozens of times in God's own speech as a correction of human assumptions. When God uses *bal*, He is correcting a false premise. When they use it, they are avoiding a true one.

The structure of the verse is therefore worth stating precisely: a question posed, immediately cancelled by the questioner, and replaced by a character judgment. That sequence — question, *bal*, insult — is the anatomy of a refusal to think, and the Qur'an has preserved it in the record so that later readers can recognize it in themselves.

**Kadhdhāb: The Intensive Form of the Same Verb**

*Kadhdhāb* is the intensive agentive noun from *k-dh-b* — one who lies habitually, repeatedly, as a settled trait. The Qur'an uses the intensive form of this root at verse 3 (*kadhdhabū*, they denied repeatedly) and at verse 9 (*fa-kadhdhabū*, they denied Our servant), and it uses the intensive noun here in the mouths of the objectors.

The echo is intentional and devastating. The word Thamūd used to describe Ṣāliḥ is the word God uses to describe what Thamūd did. Verse 23 has already said *kadhdhabat Thamūd*, "Thamūd denied" — using the same intensive root. The accusers' vocabulary has been applied to them before they finished speaking.

There is a general principle in this. The intensive forms in Arabic denote habit and character rather than single acts, and the Qur'an's use of them for denial (*kadhdhabū*) and for lying (*kadhdhāb*) describes settled dispositions rather than moments. The people who called Ṣāliḥ a habitual liar were themselves habitual deniers, and verse 23 had said so in the verse before. The reader of the sūrah is thus given the two descriptions side by side and can compare them — which is exactly what verse 26 will announce is about to happen.

**Ashir: Insolence Produced by Blessing**

*Ashir* is from *ʾ-sh-r*, a root denoting insolence, arrogance, and ungratefulness — specifically the haughtiness that comes from being spoiled by blessings. The Arabic lexicons distinguish *ashar* from *kibr*: *kibr* is pride as a self-assessment, whereas *ashar* is the insolent behavior of one whose appetites have been indulged and who has never been refused. The camel that has been well fed and behaves with skittish aggression is described with the same root.

The word choice is remarkable in context. Thamūd's own condition — prosperous, secure, skilled, established in carved houses — is precisely the condition that produces *ashar*. They accused the messenger of the exact quality their civilization displayed, and the Qur'an records the accusation with the word that describes them. Al-Qurṭubī notes that *ashir* is applied to one who is ungrateful for blessing and behaves insolently toward those below him, which is the definition of the people who demanded that revelation go to someone of higher rank than a man they considered their inferior.

The same accusation pattern appears in the description of the Qur'anic opponent par excellence:

> *"Nor obey thou every sweaer, contemptible, backbiter, going about with malicious gossip, preventer of good, transgressor, sinful, coarse and crude (ʿutull), moreover ignoble (zanīm)."* (Qur'an 68:10–13)

The list is a catalogue of the qualities the Quraysh attributed to the Prophet ﷺ, reversed and returned to its origin. Al-Ṭabarī records that the passage concerns al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīrah or another of the Makkan chiefs — the man who had just called the Qur'an "sorcery handed down" (74:24). The technique is the same as in verse 25: the accusation is quoted and reassigned.

**Application: The Question That Was Never Asked**

Verse 25 has a diagnostic use that is almost clinical. Watch any conversation in which a serious claim is met with a statement about the claimant's character, preceded by a rhetorical question that the speaker answers himself. *Was this really discovered by him? Of course not — he's a fraud with an axe to grind.* The structure is identical: question, *bal*, slur.

The Qur'an's exposure of the structure does two things. First, it identifies the point at which the conversation stopped being an inquiry. The *bal* is the marker, and once you can hear it, you can hear it everywhere — in political discourse, in academic disputes, in family arguments, in the internal monologue of a person avoiding a decision. Second, it reveals that the accusation is almost always a projection. The people who called Ṣāliḥ insolent were the insolent party, and the verse before had already said so.

There is a private application as well. Every person carries accusations against the truth he has been shown — that it is impractical, that it comes from someone unqualified, that it is exaggerated, that it is a lie told by people with an interest. Verse 25 asks the reader to test the accusations in the same way the Qur'an tests Thamūd's: does the accusation engage the claim, or only the claimant? And whose character does it actually describe?

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:26

> **On the morrow they will know who is the insolent liar.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verdict Delivered as a Question**

*Sa-yaʿlamūna ghadan mani al-kadhdhābu al-ashir.* God does not answer the insult. He schedules it. The verse takes the two words of verse 25 — *kadhdhābun ashir* — and returns them, in definite form, as the object of a future knowledge: *al-kadhdhābu al-ashir*, "the insolent liar." The definite article is the entire content of the reply. They said "he is an insolent liar"; the verse says "tomorrow you will know *the* insolent liar" — the one, the specific, the actual.

The technique is *tawriyah*, the deliberate double reference, and it is used here with maximum economy. The verse does not say "you are the insolent liar," which would be a counter-accusation and would place God in the argument. It leaves the reference open and announces that the identity will be disclosed. Al-Bayḍāwī and al-Zamakhsharī both note that the openness is more severe than a direct statement would have been, because it forces the hearer to consider both possibilities — and the possibility that it is he is the one that produces fear.

**Sa-Yaʿlamūna: The Particle of Imminent Future**

The *sa* prefix marks the near future in Arabic, closer than *sawfa*. *Sa-yaʿlamūna* is "they will know soon," not "they will eventually know." The Qur'an uses the same construction as a standing answer to denial, and its occurrences form a pattern:

> *"And you shall surely know its news after a while (la-taʿlamunna nabaʾahu baʿda ḥīn)."* (Qur'an 38:88)

> *"Nay, but you will come to know. Then nay, but you will come to know."* (Qur'an 102:3–4)

> *"And those who disbelieve will come to know to whom belongs the outcome of the abode."* (Qur'an 28:37 — cf. 13:42; 6:135)

> *"Say: Await — truly I am with you among those who await."* (Qur'an 11:122)

The last of these is Ṣāliḥ's own instruction to his people in the same narrative, and it is echoed by God's command to the Prophet ﷺ at the end of the Qur'an: *"And be patient until the judgment of thy Lord"* and *"And say: The truth has come and falsehood has vanished"* (17:81). The posture the Qur'an cultivates in its messengers is not rebuttal but expectation. They are told, repeatedly, to wait — and the waiting is presented as an active, dignified stance rather than as passivity.

**Ghadan: Tomorrow, and What Tomorrow Means**

*Ghadan*, "tomorrow," is doing double work. Literally, it refers to the imminent future, and in the narrative it is remarkably accurate: Ṣāliḥ tells his people, after they hamstring the camel, that they have three days:

> *"He said: Enjoy yourselves in your homes for three days. That is a promise not to be denied."* (Qur'an 11:65)

Three days. The "tomorrow" of verse 26 arrived within the week. Ibn Kathīr records the traditional account: the camel was killed, the three days passed, and on the morning of the fourth the punishment came.

Eschatologically, *ghadan* in the Qur'an means the Day of Resurrection, and the tradition uses the word in that sense frequently:

> *"And every soul shall consider what it has forwarded for tomorrow (li-ghad)."* (Qur'an 59:18)

The verse in al-Ḥashr is the pivot of the whole Qur'anic ethic of time: the believer is instructed to inventory his deeds in relation to tomorrow, and tomorrow is defined as the Day. Verse 26 uses the same word for the same inventory, conducted by people who will not be able to alter what they find.

Both meanings are present here, and both are true of Thamūd: they knew within three days, and they will know again, permanently, on the Day.

**The Epistemology of the Verse**

There is a serious claim about knowledge in this verse, and it deserves stating. Verse 25 records an assertion made without evidence: he is a liar. Verse 26 announces that the truth of the matter will be *known* — *yaʿlamūna*, from *ʿ-l-m*, the root of knowledge — at a specified future time. The contrast sets up the Qur'an's account of the difference between opinion and knowledge.

An accusation made in the present tense is a claim about a state of affairs that the accuser has not investigated. A knowledge promised for the future is a claim about a state of affairs that will be disclosed. The verse places the two side by side and identifies which one is provisional. The people of Thamūd believed they knew something in verse 25; the verse tells them they will know something in verse 26, and the two knowledges are incompatible.

This is why the Qur'an so often appeals to future disclosure rather than present argument. It is not declining to argue — the Qur'an argues constantly, and with real rigor. It is adding that the argument will be settled by an event, and that the event is nearer than the objectors think. The sūrah's own verse 3 has already established the principle: *wa-kullu amrin mustaqirr* — every matter reaches its settled state. Verse 26 applies it to a specific dispute: this matter will settle, and you will know where it settled.

**A Reading Variant That Changes the Speaker**

The commentators note that the unvocalized Arabic allows *sa-taʿlamūna*, "you will know," which is the reading preferred by some, in which case the verse is Ṣāliḥ's own address to his people rather than God's statement about them. Al-Bayḍāwī records this reading. The difference is instructive in either direction: if God is speaking, the verdict comes from the Sender of the messenger; if Ṣāliḥ is speaking, it comes from the man being insulted, and he says it without anger, without curse, and without prediction of specifics — simply that they will know.

Both readings share the verse's most striking feature, which is what it does not contain. There is no threat of violence, no invocation of revenge, no description of the coming punishment. The man being called an insolent liar responds by saying that the matter will be resolved and the truth disclosed. The Prophet ﷺ is described in the same terms:

> *"So remind — thou art only a reminder. Thou art not a dictator over them."* (Qur'an 88:21–22)

**Application: Not Needing to Win Today**

Verse 26 is a text about the discipline of unresolved disputes. Most human conflicts contain a moment at which one party knows the truth cannot be established then and there, and the temptation at that moment is to escalate — to raise the voice, to bring in the past, to wound, to force an acknowledgment that will not be genuine. The verse's counsel is that the acknowledgment is coming anyway, and that forcing it produces nothing except damage.

The instruction has three practical components. Do not answer an insult with an insult; answer it with a schedule. Do not require the other party to concede now; the concession is not yours to extract. And do not confuse your present inability to prove something with the thing's falsity — the matter has a *mustaqirr*, a settling point, and it will settle.

The tradition gave this posture a name and a practice. The Prophet ﷺ was insulted for thirteen years in Makkah and was commanded repeatedly to be patient and to wait:

> *"And be patient under the judgment of thy Lord, for truly thou art before Our Eyes."* (Qur'an 52:48)

> *"And We know that thy breast is straitened by what they say. So glorify the praise of thy Lord and be among those who prostrate, and worship thy Lord until certainty comes unto thee."* (Qur'an 15:97–99)

The last phrase — *ḥattā yaʾtiyaka al-yaqīn*, "until certainty comes to you" — was understood by the earliest commentators as a reference to death, and by others as a reference to the certainty of the promised disclosure. Either way the instruction is the same: worship, and let the knowledge arrive on its own schedule. Thamūd's arrived in three days. Verse 26 says so, and verse 31 will show how.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:27

> **Behold! We are sending the she-camel as a trial for them; so watch them and be patient.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Innā Mursilū: The Decision Already Made**

*Innā mursilū al-nāqati fitnatan lahum.* The construction — *inna* plus the active participle — denotes an action resolved and imminent: "We are senders of the she-camel," that is, We are about to send her. Arabic uses this form for a matter decided and in motion, and the Qur'an uses it elsewhere for the same purpose:

> *"And [the disbelievers] said: Why has no sign been sent down unto him from his Lord? Say: The Unseen belongs to God; so await — I am with you among those who await."* (Qur'an 10:20)

The naming of the animal is significant. *Al-nāqah*, "the she-camel," appears with the definite article and no further description, as though both parties already knew which camel was meant — and they did. The tradition records that Thamūd demanded a sign, specifying a pregnant she-camel to emerge from a particular rock, and that God produced exactly that. Ibn al-Jawzī and al-Qurṭubī both relate the account. The sign was therefore given on their own specification, which removes any possibility of complaint about its nature.

The Qur'an names this camel in several places, and in two of them with the divine possessive:

> *"This she-camel of God (nāqatu Allāhi) is a sign unto you. Leave her to graze freely upon God's earth, and cause her no harm, lest a painful punishment seize you."* (Qur'an 7:73; cf. 11:64)

> *"And the Messenger of God said unto them: The she-camel of God (nāqata Allāh), and her drink."* (Qur'an 91:13)

She is the only individual animal in the Qur'an to be given God's name as a possessive. The tradition notes the honor and draws the consequence: an animal designated as God's is not property, and harming her is not a crime against an owner but against the One who sent her.

**Mubṣirah: The Sign That Could Be Seen**

Sūrah al-Isrāʾ adds a qualification that clarifies the function of the whole episode:

> *"And We gave Thamūd the she-camel as a visible sign (mubṣirah), but they wronged her (fa-ẓalamū bihā). And We send not the signs except as a warning."* (Qur'an 17:59)

*Mubṣirah* — from *b-ṣ-r*, sight — means manifest, visible, clear to the eye. The camel was not a subtle argument requiring interpretation; she was a large animal that had come out of a rock. And the verse's second clause states the Qur'an's general theology of signs: *wa-mā nursilu bi-l-āyāti illā takhwīfā* — "We send not the signs except as a warning." Signs are not given to satisfy curiosity or to win debates. They are given to frighten people into repentance.

That theology explains a great deal about verse 1 of this sūrah. The moon was split as a sign; the sign's function was warning; and the recipients responded by calling it sorcery. The Qur'an's answer, in 17:59, is that signs are no longer given to demanding crowds, precisely because the former peoples denied them and were destroyed. The moon was one of the last such signs, and the sūrah that records it also records the destructions that followed comparable denials in the past.

**Fitnah: The Test That Reveals Composition**

The camel is described as *fitnatan lahum* — "a trial for them." *Fitnah* is from *f-t-n*, a root whose primary meaning is to smelt or assay metal — to put gold into fire in order to discover whether it is genuine. The derived sense is a test that reveals what a thing is actually made of, and the Qur'an uses it for the trials that separate true belief from claimed belief:

> *"Do men reckon that they will be left to say: We believe, and that they will not be tested? Indeed We tested those before them; and God surely knows those who are true, and surely knows the liars."* (Qur'an 29:2–3)

The trial of Thamūd was specific and instructive. It was not a demand for belief in an abstraction, nor for a ritual, nor for a sacrifice of wealth. It was a demand about water.

**The Water Allocation: A Trial in Resource Sharing**

Verse 28 will describe the arrangement in detail, but its significance belongs here. Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ records Ṣāliḥ's statement of it:

> *"This is a she-camel; she shall have a drink, and you shall have a drink, on an appointed day."* (Qur'an 26:155)

The people were to have the water one day and the camel the next. In an arid valley, that is not a small request; it is a claim on the single scarce resource upon which the entire settlement depends, allocated to a single animal, at God's command, with no productive return.

The trial therefore tested exactly what a prosperous, secure, engineering-minded civilization is worst at: accepting a limit on its consumption imposed by an authority it cannot see, for the benefit of a creature it does not value. Ibn Kathīr notes that they observed the arrangement at first and then grew impatient — the sequence being crucial, since the sin was not immediate rebellion but eroded compliance. They accepted the allocation, lived with it, resented it, and then broke it.

This is the most modern element in the sūrah. The trial of Thamūd was a trial in shared-resource justice: a common pool, a rule of division, a party with no political power, and a population whose short-term interest was served by breaking the rule. The camel could not defend herself, could not be reasoned with by the powerful, and had no constituency. Her protection rested entirely on the people's willingness to obey a command whose wisdom they could not verify. That is the definition of *fitnah* — the assay that shows what the metal is.

**Fa-Rtaqibhum: The Command to Observe**
