*Fa-rtaqibhum wa-ṣṭabir.* The eighth form imperative of *r-q-b*, to watch, to observe, to keep vigil over. *Irtaqib* is not casual looking; it is the sustained attention of someone responsible for monitoring a situation. The Qur'an uses the same verb for God's own watching:

> *"And be patient under the judgment of thy Lord, for truly thou art before Our Eyes."* (Qur'an 52:48)

> *"And whatever state thou art in, and whatever of the Qur'an thou recitest, and whatever work you do — We are witnesses over you while you are engaged therein."* (Qur'an 10:61)

The instruction to Ṣāliḥ places him in a specific role: the observer of a case that will resolve itself. He is not to intervene further, not to argue more, not to organize resistance, not to withdraw from the town. He is to watch. The same command is given to Noah before the flood — *"And build the Ark before Our Eyes"* (11:37) — and to Moses:

> *"That thou mightest be brought up under My Eye."* (Qur'an 20:39)

There is a discipline in being told to watch rather than to act, and it is one of the hardest assignments a person can receive. The prophet sees the trajectory clearly, knows the outcome, and is instructed not to prevent it. He is not permitted to save the people from themselves, because the trial requires their freedom to fail it.

**Wa-Ṣṭabir: The Augmented Form of Patience**

The second command is *iṣṭabir*, the eighth form of *ṣ-b-r*. The base form *ṣabara* means to be patient; the eighth form intensifies it to mean to brace oneself, to endure with sustained effort, to hold on when holding on is difficult. The Qur'an uses the augmented form in places of exceptional demand:

> *"And command thy household with prayer, and be steadfast therein (wa-ṣṭabir ʿalayhā)."* (Qur'an 20:132)

> *"Lord of the heavens and the earth and whatever is between them — so worship Him and be steadfast in His worship (wa-ṣṭabir li-ʿibādatih). Knowest thou any namesake for Him?"* (Qur'an 19:65)

> *"And be patient, for truly God is with the patient."* (Qur'an 8:46 — cf. 2:153)

The pairing of watching with steadfastness defines the posture of a prophet among a people being tried. He is neither indifferent nor interfering. He observes with full attention and endures with full effort, and he leaves the outcome to the One who set the trial.

**Application: Living Inside a Trial You Did Not Design**

Verse 27 describes a situation many people occupy without recognizing it. A test has been set — by circumstances, by a relationship, by an illness, by an institution, by the arrival of something that requires a limit on your consumption or your freedom — and the test is not announced as a test. It arrives as a camel: an inconvenient presence with a claim on your resources and an authority you cannot verify.

The Qur'an's instruction to the person being tested is not to identify the test, since he usually cannot. It is to watch and to be patient — to attend carefully to what is happening and to endure the not-knowing. The instruction to the person administering a test (a parent, a teacher, an employer, a physician) is equally implicit in the verse: state the rule clearly, attach it to an authority the tested party can recognize, and then do not intervene. A test that is explained in advance, or supervised too closely, does not reveal what the metal is.

And the verse's central theological claim is the one that makes both instructions bearable. The camel was *sent*. The trial was not an accident of history, nor a random hardship, nor the product of anyone's malice. It came from the One who sends messengers and signs, and its purpose was *takhwīf* — warning, the summoning of attention. A person inside a trial he did not choose is therefore inside something addressed to him, and the appropriate response is the one verse 27 gives to the prophet: watch it, and be steadfast, and let it disclose what you are made of.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:28

> **And inform them that the water is to be divided among them — each share of drink equitably proportioned.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Wa-Nabbiʾhum: The Command to Announce the Rule**

*Wa-nabbiʾhum anna al-māʾa qismatun baynahum.* The verse is God's instruction to Ṣāliḥ, and its first word returns to the root of verse 4. *Nabbiʾhum* is the second form imperative of *n-b-ʾ*, to bring weighty news — the root of *nabaʾ* and of *nabī*. Verse 4 said that *anbāʾ* had come to the Quraysh, reports of consequence; verse 28 has God commanding a prophet to deliver a *nabaʾ* to his own people. The report in this case is administrative: here is how the water will be divided.

The choice of this verb for this content is deliberate. The allocation of a scarce resource is presented as news of consequence — a *nabaʾ*, not a regulation. In the Qur'an's view, the way a community divides what it depends upon is a matter of revelation, not of custom or force, and it is announced by a prophet in the same register as a prophecy.

**Qismah: The Noun of Apportionment**

*Qismatun*, from *q-s-m*, to divide, to apportion, to distribute. The noun denotes a division already made and the share that results from it. The Qur'an uses the same root for God's own distribution of livelihood, and the passage is one of its clearest statements on economic inequality:

> *"Is it they who apportion the mercy of thy Lord? We apportion among them their livelihood in the life of this world (naḥnu qasamnā baynahum maʿīshatahum fī al-ḥayāti al-dunyā), and We have raised some of them above others in degrees, that some may take others in service. And the mercy of thy Lord is better than what they amass."* (Qur'an 43:32)

The verse establishes that unequal distribution of worldly means is itself part of the divine apportionment and serves a social function — mutual dependence through service. It does not establish that any particular division is just, and it explicitly contrasts the apportionment of livelihood with the apportionment of mercy. What it does establish is that *qismah* is God's business, and that a community which receives a *qismah* from God has received something it did not negotiate.

The same theology of measure governs provision in the Qur'an's most general statement:

> *"And there is not a thing but that its stores are with Us, and We send it not down save in a known measure (bi-qadarin maʿlūm)."* (Qur'an 15:21)

Water in particular is described as sent down by measure:

> *"And We send down water from the sky in a known measure, and We settle it in the earth; and truly We are able to take it away."* (Qur'an 23:18)

The Thamūd allocation therefore rests on a general principle: the water was never theirs in the first place. It was sent down by measure and settled in the earth, and its division among the users was an extension of the same measurement. The camel's share was not a concession extracted from the people's property; it was part of the original apportionment, disclosed.

**Kullu Shirbin Muḥtaḍar: The Turn That Each Attends**

*Kullu shirbin muḥtaḍar* — "every share of drink is attended." *Shirb* is a specific term: a turn of drinking, the right of access to water at an appointed time. It is a technical word in Arabic water law, distinct from the water itself — the *shirb* is the turn, not the quantity. The Qur'an's use of it here is precise legal language inside a narrative.

*Muḥtaḍar* is the eighth form passive participle of *ḥ-ḍ-r*, to be present, to attend, to come to. The commentators explain the phrase in two complementary ways: each party attends its own turn and no other — the people come to the water on their day and the camel on hers, and neither is present at the other's; or, each turn is attended to, observed, and respected, with nobody absent from the arrangement.

Either reading produces the same rule: strict alternation with no encroachment. Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ states it in Ṣāliḥ's own words:

> *"This is a she-camel; she shall have a drink (lahā shirb), and you shall have a drink (wa-lakum shirb), on an appointed day (yawmin maʿlūm)."* (Qur'an 26:155)

*Yawmin maʿlūm* — a known, appointed day. The schedule is fixed and published. There is no discretion in it, no negotiation, no case-by-case adjustment for need, no override for the powerful.

**The Rule's Design, and Why It Was Fair**

The allocation has features worth naming, because they constitute a model rather than an arbitrary command.

It is proportional to need rather than to status. The camel is one animal; the people are a town. Yet the division is by time, not by quantity per capita — each side has the water for a full day. The Qur'an does not say the camel drank less; the tradition records that she drank the valley's water on her day, and that the people benefited from her milk in compensation. Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī both relate that on her day the people milked her and drank from her milk, so the arrangement was not a loss to them but an exchange.

It is reciprocal. The verse says *baynahum* — between them — using a plural pronoun that includes the camel among the parties. She is not an exception to the water regime; she is a participant in it. The Qur'an's phrase *lahā shirb wa-lakum shirb* puts the two shares in the same grammatical structure, hers first.

It is published in advance and fixed. Nothing about the arrangement requires ongoing judgment, which means nothing about it can be captured by the powerful. A rule that must be applied case by case is a rule that will be applied in favor of whoever applies it. A rule of alternation cannot be bent.

It is enforceable only by consent. This is the crucial feature and the reason it was a *fitnah*. Nobody was stationed to guard the well. The camel could be driven off, or killed, at any moment, by any of them, with no immediate physical consequence. The rule's only enforcement was the command of God and the people's willingness to keep it. The trial was therefore a trial in voluntary compliance with a just allocation of a scarce resource — the hardest thing a community is ever asked to do.

**The Prophetic Precedent in Islamic Law**

The arrangement in verse 28 is not an isolated story; it is the root from which Islamic water law grew. The Prophet ﷺ adjudicated actual disputes over *shirb*, and the reports establish the principles.

The most famous case is that of al-Zubayr ibn al-ʿAwwām and an Ansārī neighbor who disputed the water of a stream at al-Ḥarrah, each wanting to irrigate his palms first. The Prophet ﷺ said to al-Zubayr: *"Irrigate, O Zubayr, then let the water flow to your neighbor."* The Ansārī objected — "Is it because he is your cousin?" — and the Prophet's face changed, after which he gave the ruling that recognized the upstream owner's right to hold water to the ankles: *"Irrigate, O Zubayr, then hold the water until it returns to the wall."* Al-Zubayr said afterward that this verse was revealed in connection with it:

> *"But no, by thy Lord, they will not believe until they make thee judge in what disputes between them, and find within themselves no resistance to what thou hast judged, and submit in full submission."* (Qur'an 4:65)

(al-Bukhārī 2359, 2360, 4585; Muslim 2357)

The general rule the Prophet ﷺ then laid down for common resources:

> *"Muslims are partners in three things: water, pasture, and fire."* (Abū Dāwūd 3477; Ibn Mājah 2472; graded ṣaḥīḥ by al-Albānī)

> *"Do not withhold the excess of water, so as to prevent the growth of the herbage."* (al-Bukhārī 2353; Muslim 1566)

And in the most general formulation of resource ethics:

> *"Do not withhold the excess of water, so as to prevent the growth of the herbage."* (al-Bukhārī 2353; Muslim 1566, from Abū Hurayrah)

> *"The merciful are shown mercy by the Merciful. Be merciful to those on the earth, and the One above the heaven will be merciful to you."* (Abū Dāwūd 4941; al-Tirmidhī 1924, from ʿAbdullāh ibn ʿAmr; graded ṣaḥīḥ)

The jurists built an entire chapter on *shirb* from these materials, treating water as a resource that cannot be owned absolutely and whose distribution follows fixed priorities: drinking for humans, then animals, then irrigation, then other uses. Verse 28 is the archetype of the whole field.

**Application: The Test of the Common Pool**

The trial of Thamūd is the trial of every community that shares a finite resource: a river, a fishery, an aquifer, a budget, a hospital's capacity, a household's income, a family's attention. The structure of the problem is identical each time. The resource is limited; the parties have unequal power; the arrangement that is fair is not the arrangement that the powerful prefer; and compliance can only be voluntary, because enforcement is impossible or too expensive.

Verse 28 supplies the Qur'an's model for such an arrangement, and its elements are worth listing as practical criteria. The allocation is stated in advance. It is proportional rather than status-based. It includes the weakest party as a participant with a share rather than as an object of charity. It is fixed so that it cannot be bent by influence. It is presented as a *nabaʾ* — news of consequence — rather than as a technical adjustment, so that violating it is understood as a moral act and not merely a practical one. And it is accompanied by compensation where compensation is possible: the people received the camel's milk.

The failure of Thamūd was not that the arrangement was unjust. Verse 28 makes clear that it was precisely just — *kullu shirbin muḥtaḍar*, each share attended in its turn. Their failure was that they could not accept a limit whose authority they could not see and whose benefit they could not measure. That is the same failure that defeats most modern resource agreements, and the sūrah records its outcome in the next verse in three verbs.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:29

> **Then they called their companion, and he took in hand and hamstrung [her].**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Fa-Nādaw Ṣāḥabahum: The Crime Delegated**

*Fa-nādaw ṣāḥabahum fa-taʿāṭā fa-ʿaqar.* Three verbs, one after another with the particle *fa*, and the sequence is a complete account of how a community commits a crime: they called, he took hold, he struck.

The first verb is plural and the second and third are singular. *Nādaw* — "they called out." The people of Thamūd did not kill the camel collectively; they summoned one man to do it. The commentators name him: Qudār ibn Sālaf, recorded by al-Bayḍāwī and Ibn al-Jawzī, and the tradition describes him as a man of strength and standing among them. The word *ṣāḥib*, companion, is significant — he was one of their own, chosen from among them, not a mercenary or an outsider. The delegation was to a peer.

This detail is the theological heart of the verse, because the Qur'an elsewhere assigns the act to all of them:

> *"And the Messenger of God said unto them: The she-camel of God, and her drink. But they called him a liar and hamstrung her (fa-ʿaqarūhā), so their Lord crushed them for their sin and leveled them."* (Qur'an 91:13–14)

*Fa-ʿaqarūhā* — "they hamstrung her," plural. One man struck the blow; the whole people are recorded as having struck it. And the punishment that follows is described as *bi-dhambihim* — "for their sin," in the singular: one sin, committed by many hands through one hand.

The principle is stated explicitly elsewhere in the Qur'an:

> *"That they may bear their burdens complete on the Day of Resurrection, and some of the burdens of those whom they lead astray without knowledge. Is it not that they bear an evil burden?"* (Qur'an 16:25)

And in the Prophet's ﷺ formulation, recorded by Muslim:

> *"Whoever introduces a good practice in Islam will have its reward and the reward of all who act upon it after him, without that diminishing their rewards in anything. And whoever introduces an evil practice in Islam will bear its burden and the burden of all who act upon it after him, without that diminishing their burdens in anything."* (Muslim 1017, from Jarīr ibn ʿAbdullāh)

The verse therefore teaches something uncomfortable about collective action. A crowd that calls for a crime commits it. The man with the sword is the instrument; the voices that summoned him are the agents. Neither escapes, and neither bears the whole.

**Fa-Taʿāṭā: Reaching for What Is Beyond One's Reach**

The second verb, *fa-taʿāṭā*, is the sixth form of *ʿ-ṭ-w*, and the lexicons give it a specific flavor: to reach out and take hold of something, especially something beyond one's proper reach, and to undertake a matter boldly. Al-Zamakhsharī explains it as reaching for the deed with audacity. The commentators record three concrete readings, all preserved in the tafsīr tradition: he took the sword in hand; he undertook the task; he took hold of the camel.

The three are not alternatives in substance. A man who undertakes a monstrous task reaches for the instrument, and the instrument is in his hand before the decision is complete. The sixth form's connotation of presumptuous reaching captures the psychology: the deed was not within his right, his competence, or his mandate, and he stretched for it anyway.

The Qur'an has a settled vocabulary for exactly this posture, and it names the disappointment built into it:

> *"And every insolent oppressor was disappointed (wa-khāba kullu jabbārin ʿanīd). Behind him is Hell, and he shall be given to drink of purulent water."* (Qur'an 14:15–16)

> *"Throw into Hell every obstinate disbeliever (kulla kaffārin ʿanīd)."* (Qur'an 50:24)

Pharaoh — the paradigm of the reach — is shown going ahead of the people he led:

> *"He goes before his people on the Day of Resurrection, and brings them down to the Fire. And evil is the watering-place to which they are brought down."* (Qur'an 11:98)

The tradition's description of Qudār matches all of it: a man of strength and arrogance, called upon by his people, who reached for a camel designated as God's.

**Fa-ʿAqar: The Specific Act**

*ʿAqara* means to hamstring — to cut the tendons at the back of a camel's leg so that it collapses and cannot rise. It is not a general word for killing. The Arabs hamstring a camel to bring it down before slaughter, since a standing camel cannot be dispatched safely. The word choice is therefore exact and clinical: the deed was performed as a butchery, on a large animal, in a deliberate sequence, by a man who knew what he was doing.

Sūrah al-Shams supplies the description of the doer:

> *"When the most wretched of them rose up (idh inbaʿatha ashqāhā), and the Messenger of God said unto them: The she-camel of God, and her drink. But they called him a liar and hamstrung her, so their Lord crushed them for their sin and leveled them. And He does not fear the consequence (wa-lā yakhāfu ʿuqbāhā)."* (Qur'an 91:12–15)

*Ashqāhā* — the most wretched of them, the superlative of *shaqī*, one given to misery. The man was not merely a criminal but the most wretched member of a wretched people, and his wretchedness consisted in what the last verse names: he did not fear the consequence. *ʿUqbā* is the outcome, the eventual result — the same idea as verse 3's *mustaqirr*, the settling. He acted without reference to where the act would settle, and verse 30 announces that it settled.

The Prophet ﷺ described the man when preaching to his Companions, connecting the Qur'anic phrase to a living example:

> *"When the most wretched of them rose up — a man of might, powerful, wicked, and influential among his people rose up to her..."* (al-Bukhārī 4942; Muslim 2855, from ʿAbdullāh ibn Zamʿah)

The report continues with the Prophet ﷺ comparing him to a known man of strength among the Banū Zamʿah, and its purpose is to establish that the deed required the kind of person a crowd produces when it wants violence done: strong, respected, unafraid, and willing.

**The Crowd's Role, and the Prophet's Warning About Crowds**

The verse's first verb — they called — has an application that the tradition took seriously. Islam's ethical vocabulary contains a specific condemnation of the person who enables a crime without committing it, and a specific warning about the crowd's power to make evil feel authorized.

The Prophet ﷺ said, in a report about the end of time:

> *"There will come upon the people years of treachery, in which the liar is believed and the truthful is disbelieved, and the traitor is trusted and the trustworthy is suspected, and the Ruwaybiḍah speaks."* It was asked: What is the Ruwaybiḍah? He said: *"The worthless man speaking on the affairs of the public."* (Ibn Mājah 4036; Aḥmad; graded ḥasan by al-Albānī)

And on the danger of collective approval:

> *"Whoever among you sees an evil, let him change it with his hand; if he cannot, then with his tongue; if he cannot, then with his heart — and that is the weakest of faith."* (Muslim 49, from Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī)

The hadith establishes a personal obligation to oppose evil at the level one is capable of, precisely because the alternative is the situation in verse 29: a crowd that calls, and individuals who do not. The "weakest of faith" — the heart's refusal — is still refusal, and it is what saves a person from being counted among those who called.

The Qur'an's own account of how the crowd operated is preserved in a detail of the story transmitted by the exegetes: the people were troubled by the camel's share of the water, they complained to one another, and the complaint accumulated until a delegation summoned Qudār. Nobody decided; the decision emerged from the accumulation. That is how collective crimes are structured — no meeting, no vote, no single moment of choice, only a rising volume of grievance and then a man with a sword who is called.

**Application: The Voice in the Crowd**

Verse 29 assigns responsibility with an accuracy that should unsettle any reader who has ever participated in a collective action. The verse does not excuse the man who struck; it says he was called. And it does not excuse the callers; it says they called. Both parties are named, and both are punished in verse 31, in a destruction described in Sūrah al-Shams as falling on all of them equally.

The practical instruction is therefore about the voice. Most people will never hamstring a camel. Most people will, at some point, be in a group that is building toward something wrong — a workplace turning against a colleague, a community turning against a family, a nation turning against a category of people, an online crowd turning against an individual — and the mechanism is always the one in this verse: grievance shared, grievance amplified, a name called, an act performed by someone the group produces for the purpose.

The Qur'an's countermeasure is the ḥadīth of the three levels: change it with your hand, or your tongue, or your heart. The third is described as the weakest of faith, and it is available to everyone in every circumstance. Refusing to add your voice, refusing to laugh, refusing to forward, refusing to attend — these are not nothing in the divine accounting, because verse 29 shows what the alternative produces. A single act of violence required a crowd's call to authorize it. Remove the call and the hand has nothing to reach for.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:30

> **How, then, were My Punishment and My warnings?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refrain Placed Before the Punishment**

The third instance of the severe refrain occupies a position unlike the first two, and the position is the verse's entire significance. After Noah, the sequence was: narrative, punishment, refrain (vv. 9–15, then 16). After ʿĀd, the sequence was doubled: denial and refrain (18), punishment (19–20), refrain again (21). After Thamūd, the sequence is inverted. The narrative runs from verse 23 to verse 29 and ends with a dead camel. Then the refrain asks — *how, then, were My Punishment and My warnings?* — and only then, in verse 31, does the sūrah describe what the punishment was.

The question is therefore a setup. God asks how it was before saying what it was, and the reader is made to hold the question for the length of a verse. Al-Bayḍāwī notes that the interrogative form here functions to summon attention: the listener is asked to prepare an answer, discovers he has none, and receives the answer in the next verse as a thing he was waiting for.

The technique produces a specific effect in recitation. Anyone who has recited al-Qamar aloud knows that verse 30 ends on an open question and verse 31 arrives with the force of an answer nobody expected — *a single cry*. The pause between them is where the fear is generated, and the Qur'an controls the pause.

**The Question of Proportion**

There is an objection that arises naturally at this position, and the refrain's placement invites it. The crime was the killing of one animal. The punishment was the extinction of a civilization. The reader who stops at verse 29 and asks "how was My punishment?" is asking, in effect, whether it was proportionate.

The Qur'an's answer is distributed across several verses, and it does not consist of minimizing the crime.

First, the crime was committed against a sign given on demand. Sūrah al-Isrāʾ states the rule: *"And We gave Thamūd the she-camel as a visible sign, but they wronged her. And We send not the signs except as a warning"* (17:59). A sign requested, delivered exactly as specified, and then violated is not an ordinary offense. It is a declaration about the authority behind the sign, and the Qur'an treats it as such. Ibn Kathīr's comment on this passage is that the destruction of a people who have been given a manifest sign and denied it is God's established practice, which is why the later communities were no longer given signs of that kind.

Second, the crime summarized the entire posture of the people. They had denied the warnings (23), rejected the messenger's humanity (24), called him an insolent liar (25), and then destroyed the single physical object that connected them to God's command. Hamstringing the camel was not an isolated act of cruelty to an animal; it was the practical conclusion of everything they had already said. The verse in al-Shams makes this explicit — *"they called him a liar and hamstrung her"* (91:14) — linking the denial of the messenger and the killing of the camel as one continuous act.

Third, the punishment was preceded by a respite and a warning of its date:

> *"He said: Enjoy yourselves in your homes for three days. That is a promise not to be denied."* (Qur'an 11:65)

Three days, announced, with the nature of the coming event disclosed. Al-Ṭabarī records that in those three days their faces changed color — the tradition describes them turning white, then red, then black — and that repentance was offered and refused. The punishment was therefore neither sudden nor unannounced, and the refrain's second term, *wa-nudhur*, covers precisely this: My warnings.

Fourth, the scale of a punishment in the Qur'an is not measured against the size of the act but against the position of the actor. A person who has been shown a sign, warned by a prophet, given three days, and told the date of his own destruction is in a different moral category from one who has never been addressed. The Qur'an states the general principle:

> *"And thy Lord never destroys towns until He sends unto their mother city a messenger to recite unto them Our signs."* (Qur'an 28:59)

> *"And if We destroyed them with a punishment before this, they would surely say: Our Lord, why didst Thou not send unto us a messenger, that we might follow Thy signs before we were humbled and disgraced?"* (Qur'an 20:134)

The argument in 20:134 is God's own justification for sending messengers before punishing, and verse 30's refrain asks the reader to observe that the condition has been fulfilled. The warning came. The sign came. The respite came. *Fa-kayfa kāna ʿadhābī wa-nudhur.*

**ʿAdhābī wa-Nudhur: The Two Nouns in the Case of Thamūd**

In the first two instances of the refrain, the warnings referred to messengers who had been rejected across centuries. In this instance the pairing is unusually tight, because Thamūd received both a messenger *and* a physical sign — a camel that emerged from a rock at their own specification. They had, in the vocabulary of Islamic theology, both *naqlī* evidence (the report of the prophet) and *ḥissī* evidence (the object before their eyes).

Ibn Taymiyyah's discussion of the kinds of evidence God provides makes the same distinction: the Qur'anic signs are of two types, *āyāt samʿiyyah* (heard, revealed) and *āyāt baṣariyyah* (seen, empirical), and God supplies both so that no capacity is left unaddressed. Thamūd had exhausted the supply. Their response to the heard sign was "he is an insolent liar"; their response to the seen sign was a sword.

That is why the refrain at this position is the most severe of the four. It is asked about a people who had no remaining category of evidence to receive.

**Application: The Question Asked at the Point of No Return**

Verse 30's placement — after the crime, before the consequence — describes a position most people occupy at some point. The act is done. The outcome has not arrived. There is a gap, and the gap feels like safety.

The Qur'an fills the gap with a question. Not with a threat, not with a description, but with *kayfa* — "how was it?" The past tense is the point. The question is asked about an event that has not yet happened in the narrative as though it had already occurred, because in the divine register it has. Verse 3 said so: every matter reaches its settled state, and the settling is not in doubt merely because it has not arrived.

The three days of Thamūd are the sūrah's most useful image for the human situation. A period of apparent normality — "enjoy yourselves in your homes" — during which nothing seems to be happening, and during which the only visible change is internal: the color of the face, the quality of sleep, the inability to enjoy the homes one has been told to enjoy. The tradition reports that they could not. Al-Ṭabarī preserves the account that they knew, in those three days, that they were dying, and that the knowledge was itself part of the punishment.

The practical counsel is to identify the gaps in one's own life and refuse to treat them as verdicts. A person who has done something wrong and has not yet felt the consequence is inside a three-day period, and the sūrah's question is being asked over him. The answer is still, at that point, in his control — because repentance within the gap is repentance, and repentance after the cry is only recognition.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:31

> **Truly We sent upon them a single cry, and they were like a fence-maker's dry sticks.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Ṣayḥah Wāḥidah: One Cry**

*Innā arsalnā ʿalayhim ṣayḥatan wāḥidatan.* The punishment of the civilization that carved houses into mountains was a sound. Not an earthquake, though Sūrah al-Aʿrāf calls it *al-rajfah* (7:78); not a lightning blast, though Sūrah Fuṣṣilat and al-Dhāriyāt call it *al-ṣāʿiqah* (41:13; 51:44); not a flood or a wind. In al-Qamar it is a cry — *ṣayḥah*, from *ṣ-y-ḥ*, the shout of a human or animal voice, a loud single utterance. And it is *wāḥidah*, one.

The Qur'an gives the same description of the Last Day:

> *"And when the trumpet is sounded, that will be a single shout (zajratun wāḥidah)."* (Qur'an 79:13)

> *"But it will be only a single shout, and behold — they will be upon the face of the earth."* (Qur'an 79:13–14)

The parallel is exact and it is not incidental. The destruction of Thamūd is a rehearsal of the Resurrection in miniature: one cry kills a people, one cry raises all people. Sūrah al-Qamar has already prepared the connection, since verse 6 describes the caller calling to a terrible thing and verses 7–8 describe the emergence from the graves. The reader who reaches verse 31 has already seen the trumpet's work once, at the scale of a town.

Al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr, and al-Qurṭubī all address the apparent variety in the descriptions of this punishment — cry, thunderbolt, earthquake, the overwhelming (*al-ṭāghiyah* of 69:5) — and they agree that these are not contradictory reports but aspects of a single event. Al-Qurṭubī's explanation is that the cry came from the sky, the thunderbolt accompanied it, the earthquake shook the ground beneath them, and all three describe one overwhelming destruction viewed from different angles. Ibn Kathīr adds that the punishment of a people can be described by its dominant feature and by its accompaniments without inconsistency, and cites the practice of the Qur'an elsewhere.

The dominant feature here is immediacy. *Wāḥidah* — one. There was no sequence, no escalation, no period of dying. Sūrah al-Shams describes the divine response with a doubled verb of crushing:

> *"So their Lord crushed them for their sin (fa-damdamah ʿalayhim bi-dhambihim) and leveled them (fa-sawwāhā)."* (Qur'an 91:14)

*Damdama* is the intensive of *damama*, to crush, to flatten, to bring down completely — the reduplicated form indicating totality. And *fa-sawwāhā*, "He leveled them," means that the destruction fell equally on all of them, high and low, caller and called, without distinction.

**Ka-Hashīmi al-Muḥtaẓir: The Image of Dead Twigs**

*Fa-kānū ka-hashīmi al-muḥtaẓir.* The simile is the most desolate in the sūrah. *Hashīm* is from *h-sh-m*, to break, to crumble — it denotes dry vegetation that has lost all moisture and cohesion, that crumbles when handled, and that the Arabs used as fodder or as kindling. The Qur'an uses the word for the total end of living growth:

> *"And strike for them the similitude of the life of this world: it is like water We send down from the sky, and the plants of the earth mingle with it, then become dry chaff (hashīman) that the winds scatter."* (Qur'an 18:45)

> *"Know that the life of this world is only play and diversion... like rain whose vegetation pleases the disbelievers; then it withers, and thou seest it yellowing, then it becomes chaff (hashīman)."* (Qur'an 57:20)

*Al-muḥtaẓir* is the eighth form of *ḥ-ẓ-r*, and it denotes the one who makes a *ḥaẓīrah* — an enclosure built of dry twigs and branches for livestock or for shelter. The commentators give several readings: the dry twigs that the fence-maker collects for his enclosure; the twigs that form the enclosure itself, broken and trampled; and, with a variant vocalization *al-muḥtaẓar*, the enclosed thing. Al-Zamakhsharī prefers the sense of twigs gathered and used for a pen, dry and worthless.

The image has three layers of meaning, and each contradicts something Thamūd believed about itself.

They were builders of stone. The Qur'an says they carved houses in the mountains with skill and lived in them secure (15:82; 7:74). The simile reduces them to the least substantial building material in the desert — the twig fence that a herder throws up in an afternoon and that lasts a season. The permanence they engineered is answered with the impermanence they despised.

They were strong. Their stature, their masonry, their security. *Hashīm* is what has no strength left at all; it cannot even hold its own shape.

They were a people, a tribe, a society with names and ranks. Dry twigs in an enclosure are indistinguishable from one another and are gathered as one mass, which is what *fa-sawwāhā*, "He leveled them," describes.

**The Contrast With the Palm Trunks**

The sūrah has now given two destruction similes, and their relationship is instructive. ʿĀd were like uprooted palm trunks — *aʿjāzu nakhlin munqaʿir* (54:20). Thamūd were like a fence-maker's dry twigs. Both are vegetal; both describe the human body as dead plant matter; and the scale descends from the tallest tree in the Arabian landscape to the smallest broken branch.

The descent is not arbitrary. ʿĀd's sin was boastful strength, and their image retains a certain terrible grandeur — full trunks, fallen whole. Thamūd's sin was security and settled permanence, and their image retains nothing: not the tree, not the trunk, only the crumbled cuttings that a fence is made from. The punishment in each case is the negation of the specific pride, and the similes are the record of the negation.

Al-Qurṭubī notes that the Qur'an's use of plant imagery for the dead is consistent: human beings are described as grown from the earth (71:17, *anbatanā mina al-arḍi nabātā*), sustained by it, and returned to it as its own material. The similes of destruction are therefore not degrading inventions but accurate descriptions of what a human body becomes.

**Nothing to Defend Against**

There is a military observation in the verse that the commentators drew out. Every previous punishment in the sūrah involved a material element: water, wind. Both can be anticipated, fled, sheltered against — Noah built a boat; ʿĀd dug holes. A cry cannot. It has no direction, no mass, no duration, and no surface to resist. Their carved houses, the most advanced defensive architecture of their age, were not merely useless against it; they were irrelevant to it, since a sound passes through stone as through air.

The Qur'an makes the same point about the Resurrection's trumpet: there is no shelter from it, and the only question is which of the two blasts a person hears and what he does between them. Verse 31 is a demonstration in advance: the most durable construction human beings can make offers no protection against the least substantial thing God can send.

Sūrah al-Naml adds a detail that makes the irony complete:

> *"And consider how was the end of their planning — that We destroyed them and their people, all of them. So these are their houses, empty (khāwiyah) for what they wronged. Truly in that is a sign for a people who know."* (Qur'an 27:50–52)

Their houses are *khāwiyah* — the same adjective Sūrah al-Ḥāqqah used for the hollow palm trunks. The emptiness that was in the bodies is in the buildings, and the buildings remain to be visited.

**Application: The Sound That Ends the Argument**

Verse 31 has a use beyond its historical content. It describes the moment when a dispute that had been conducted in words is ended by something that is not a word.

Every prolonged conflict contains such a moment. A marriage argument ends when a diagnosis arrives. A business rivalry ends when the market changes. A political struggle ends when the regime falls. A personal denial ends when the consequence appears at the door. In each case the thing that resolves the matter is not an argument, is not persuadable, has no position to negotiate with, and arrives in a single instant.

The Qur'an's counsel in the face of that structure is to settle disputes with God before the cry. The three-day respite given to Thamūd was the mercy inside the judgment: time in which the argument could still be reversed by repentance. Sūrah al-Shams ends its account with the sentence that should be read last and remembered first:

> *"And He does not fear the consequence (wa-lā yakhāfu ʿuqbāhā)."* (Qur'an 91:15)

The commentators differ on whether "He" refers to God or to the man who hamstrung the camel. Both readings are sound, and both are severe: the man did not fear the consequence of his act, and God did not fear the consequence of His. The difference between the two non-fears is the entire moral structure of the universe. One is ignorance and the other is sovereignty, and the person who imitates the first while facing the second is the person verse 31 describes as dry twigs in a fence.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:32

> **And indeed We have made the Qur'an easy to remember; so is there any who remembers?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Third Occurrence, and the Question of Repetition**

The refrain returns for the third time, word for word, after a destruction that took three verses to describe and one cry to execute. Its repetition raises a question the Qur'an itself raises and answers: what is the value of saying the same thing four times in fifty-five verses?

The objection was made to the Prophet ﷺ in his lifetime and is recorded:

> *"And those who disbelieve say: Why was the Qur'an not sent down unto him all at once? Thus — that We may strengthen thy heart thereby (li-nuthabbita bihi fuʾādaka) — and We have recited it in measured recitation (wa-rattalnāhu tartīlā)."* (Qur'an 25:32)

God's answer to the complaint about the manner of revelation is that the manner serves the heart. *Tartīl* — measured, arranged, paced recitation — is presented not as a stylistic choice but as a technology of strengthening. And the same answer covers the refrain's repetition: the sūrah is paced. Destruction, then ease. Question of severity, then offer of mercy. Four times, at intervals short enough that neither can be forgotten before the other arrives.

**Mathānī: The Book That Doubles Back**

The Qur'an describes itself with a technical term that names exactly this feature:

> *"God has sent down the best of discourse, a Book consistent and repeating (mutashābihan mathāniya), whereat shiver the skins of those who fear their Lord; then their skins and their hearts soften unto the remembrance of God."* (Qur'an 39:23)

*Mathānī* is the plural of *mathnā*, from *th-n-y*, to double, to fold, to repeat. The term has several recorded senses — the doubled statements of promise and threat, the repeated narratives, the sūrahs of the seven *mathānī* (interpreted as al-Fātiḥah or the seven long sūrahs) — and all of them describe a text that returns to the same ground. *Mutashābih* alongside it means internally consistent, its parts resembling one another in quality and confirming one another.

The two adjectives together describe a structure whose power depends on recurrence: consistent parts, folded over one another, producing a texture rather than a line. Al-Zamakhsharī explains *mathāniya* as the Qur'an's repetition of its themes — command and prohibition, promise and threat, story and ruling — so that no theme is delivered once and left.

Verse 39:23 also supplies the physiology of the refrain's effect. The skins shiver at the severe material, then soften at the reminder of God. That is the exact sequence of al-Qamar's two refrains: *"How, then, were My Punishment and My warnings?"* produces the shiver; *"We have made the Qur'an easy to remember"* produces the softening. The sūrah alternates them four times, and the human body's response is described in advance by the verse that names the technique.

**Why Repetition Works on Memory and on Character**

The claim of verses 17, 22, 32, and 40 is that the Qur'an is easy for *dhikr* — for retention and remembrance. Repetition is the primary mechanism of both, and the Qur'an's use of it is systematic rather than incidental.

For memory, the mechanism is well known and ancient: material encountered at intervals is retained; material encountered once is lost. The Companions' method of ten verses at a time, not advancing until the portion was learned with its knowledge and its action, is built on the same principle, and the sūrah's refrains give the memorizer four fixed points around which the rest of the text is organized.

For character, the mechanism is different and more important. A statement heard once is information; a statement heard at intervals becomes a disposition. The Qur'an's purpose is not that its reader know the propositions but that he become the kind of person for whom the propositions are obvious. That requires the same content arriving repeatedly, in different emotional contexts, until it ceases to be a claim being evaluated and becomes a lens through which claims are evaluated.

Al-Qamar performs this operation in real time. The reader meets "We have made the Qur'an easy to remember" first after a flood (17), then after a wind that tore people out of the ground (22), then after a cry that reduced a stone-building civilization to twigs (32), and then after a rain of stones on overturned cities (40). By the fourth occurrence the sentence is no longer being read; it is being recognized. The ease it describes has been demonstrated on the reader himself.

**Repetition as Mercy, Not as Redundancy**

There is a further point the tradition draws, and it concerns the psychology of warning. A single warning can be dismissed as an exaggeration. Two can be dismissed as a pattern of alarmism. Four identical warnings, placed after four separate catastrophes, cannot be dismissed at all — not because the argument has been strengthened but because the argument has been shown to be a description of how things happen.

Ibn Kathīr's comment on the refrains of al-Qamar is that God repeats them so that no one can claim the warning was given only to someone else. Each repetition re-addresses the offer to the present reader after each new demonstration. The mercy in the repetition is that it refuses to let the reader settle into the belief that the previous catastrophe was the last one.

Al-Qushayrī and Ibn ʿAjībah draw the pastoral conclusion: the repetition of the offer is a sign of the Offerer's patience. A person who asks once and stops has accepted the refusal. A person who asks four times in the same words, after each catastrophe, has not. The question *fa-hal min mudhakkir* is asked in verse 17, 22, 32, and 40, and its being asked a fourth time is the strongest evidence in the sūrah that the answer is still wanted.

**The Liturgical Repetition That Extends the Refrain**

The refrain's function continues outside the text, in the daily practice of Muslim worship. The same short sūrahs are recited in every prayer, five times a day, year after year — al-Fātiḥah seventeen times daily at minimum, and the short Makkan sūrahs of the final juzʾ constantly. A Muslim who prays the obligatory prayers and recites al-Qamar occasionally will hear "We have made the Qur'an easy to remember" hundreds of times across a lifetime.

The Prophet ﷺ's own practice with this sūrah was public and periodic: Qāf and al-Qamar at the two ʿĪds (Muslim 891), before the largest gatherings of the year. The choice places the refrain at the moments of greatest communal joy, which is the same juxtaposition the sūrah makes internally — ease after destruction, and destruction after ease.

There is also the private practice the tradition recommends for exactly this purpose. The Qur'an is to be recited with *tartīl*, in measured pace, and the recommendation for the one who wishes to retain a portion is to repeat it in prayer:

> *"So recite what is easy of the Qur'an (fa-qraʾū mā tayassara mina al-Qurʾān)."* (Qur'an 73:20)

The verse's own vocabulary — *mā tayassara*, what has been made easy — is the vocabulary of the refrain. God instructs the believer to recite what is easy, and declares that He has made it easy. The two statements close a circuit: the facilitation is divine, the recitation is human, and the point where they meet is memory.

**Application: What You Have Been Told Four Times**

Verse 32 invites a personal inventory. Every reader has warnings that have arrived repeatedly — from a physician, a parent, a spouse, a conscience, a book, a recurring failure with the same shape. The repetition is usually experienced as nagging, and the response is usually to treat the fourth instance as evidence that the speaker is obsessive rather than that the matter is serious.

The sūrah reverses the inference. It repeats its own warnings four times and asks the reader to notice that the repetition is the argument. Something that happens once may be chance; something that happens four times, in the same form, with the same outcome, is a law. The refrain's fourth occurrence at verse 40 will make this explicit by arriving after the fifth destroyed nation, at which point the reader has been shown enough instances that the pattern cannot be denied.

The same logic applies to the mercy refrain. A reader who has been told once that the Qur'an is easy may doubt it. A reader who has been told four times, each time after a catastrophe, and who has found the sūrah itself easy to memorize, has been given a demonstration rather than a claim. The question at the end is therefore not "do you believe this sentence?" but "have you used it?" *Fa-hal min mudhakkir* — is there anyone who remembers? The verb is in the fifth form, which means taking the reminder in and holding it. By the third occurrence, the reader has no excuse of novelty. By the fourth, he will have no excuse at all.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:33

> **The people of Lot denied the warnings.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Fourth Case, and the One Without an Empire**

*Kadhdhabat qawmu Lūṭin bi-l-nudhur.* The construction repeats verse 23 exactly, with the name changed. The sūrah's four historical denials are now recorded in a consistent formula, and the consistency is itself a claim: whatever else differs between these peoples — their era, their technology, their strength, their geography — the denial is the same act, and the Qur'an uses the same verb in the same intensive form each time.

Lot's people differ from the previous three in one respect that the verse does not mention and that the narrative makes clear. Noah's people were numerous. ʿĀd were strong. Thamūd were skilled and secure. Lot's people were none of these in any notable degree; they were towns on a trade route, prosperous enough, and their distinction in the Qur'an is entirely moral. The sūrah's fourth case is not the collapse of a civilization's power but of its standards.

**Lot in the Qur'an: A Prophet of Greater Standing Than the Narratives Suggest**

The source commentary notes that Lot holds greater status in the Qur'an than in the Biblical account, and the difference is worth stating. In the Qur'an Lot is a prophet given judgment and knowledge:

> *"And Lot — We gave him judgment (ḥukm) and knowledge (ʿilm), and We delivered him from the town that committed the abominations. Truly they were an evil people, transgressors."* (Qur'an 21:74)

He is counted among the righteous and the guided:

> *"And We gave him Isaac and Jacob, and guided them all; and Noah We guided aforetime, and of his progeny David and Solomon and Job and Joseph and Moses and Aaron — thus do We requite the virtuous — and Zakariyyā and John and Jesus and Elijah; all were of the righteous. And Ishmael and Elisha and Jonah and Lot; and each one We preferred above all beings."* (Qur'an 6:84–86)

And he is described as one who believed Abraham and migrated with him:

> *"And Lot believed him (fa-āmana lahu Lūṭ), and he said: I am an emigrant unto my Lord."* (Qur'an 29:26)

> *"And We delivered him and Lot unto the land We blessed for all beings."* (Qur'an 21:71)

Sūrah al-Ṣāffāt gives him a full prophetic commission and the standard vindication:

> *"And truly Lot was among the messengers. When We delivered him and his household, all of them, save an old woman among those who remained. Then We destroyed the others. And truly you pass by them in the morning and by night. Will you not then reflect?"* (Qur'an 37:133–138)

The Qur'an's Lot is therefore not a compromised figure but a prophet with judgment, knowledge, migration, belief, and deliverance — and the sūrah's description of him as a warning-bringer (*nudhur*, plural, in verse 33) refers to warnings he delivered in the Qur'an's own recorded words, which are extensive.

**The Three Charges in Lot's Own Indictment**

The Qur'an does not leave the crime vague, and it is significant that Lot's people are accused of three things rather than one:

> *"And Lot, when he said unto his people: Do you commit the abomination (al-fāḥishah) while you see? Do you approach men with desire instead of women? Nay, but you are a people who know not."* (Qur'an 27:54–55)

> *"And Lot, when he said unto his people: Truly you commit the abomination that no one among all beings has preceded you in. Do you approach men, and cut off the road (wa-taqṭaʿūna al-sabīl), and commit evil in your gatherings (wa-taʾtūna fī nādikumu al-munkar)?"* (Qur'an 29:28–29)

> *"Truly you approach men with desire instead of women. Nay, but you are an excessive people."* (Qur'an 7:81)

The three charges are: the sexual transgression itself (*al-fāḥishah*), the cutting off of the road, and the committing of *munkar* in their assemblies. The second has been explained by the commentators as highway robbery, and also as the practice of assaulting travelers — al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī record both. The third is the most important for understanding the case, and the commentators are unanimous that *nādī* means their public gathering place, their council, where men assembled socially.

The charge is therefore that they had made the wrong public. It was not a private failing concealed from the community; it was performed, discussed, and normalized in the place where the community conducted its life. Lot's question — *a-taʾtūna al-munkar fī nādikum* — is a question about culture, not about individuals. And the Qur'an's summary judgment on the towns is that they were *al-muʾtafikāt*, the overturned:

> *"And the overturned (al-muʾtafikah) He brought down, and there covered them what covered them."* (Qur'an 53:53–54)

**The Loneliness of the Warner**

Verse 33's phrase *qawmu Lūṭ*, "the people of Lot," names a relationship that had become an accusation. They were his people — his tribe by marriage and residence, his neighbors, his community — and the Qur'an records that not one of them believed except his household:

> *"So We brought out whoever was among it of the believers, and We found therein not other than a single house of Muslims (baytan mina al-muslimīn)."* (Qur'an 51:36–37)

*A single house.* The towns contained several settlements — the tradition names five — and the total number of believers was one family. Lot himself says of his position:

> *"He said: Would that I had strength against you, or could take refuge in a firm support (ruknin shadīd)!"* (Qur'an 11:80)

The Prophet ﷺ commented on this sentence with a statement that reframes it:

> *"May God have mercy on Lot. He was taking refuge in a firm support"* — meaning God Himself. (al-Bukhārī 3372, 3375; Muslim 151, from Abū Hurayrah)

The mercy invoked is for a prophet's moment of expressed loneliness, and the correction is gentle: the support he wished for was already in place. The report is one of the Qur'an's clearest statements about the condition of a truthful person in a corrupt society — he is alone, he feels it, he says so, and he is not actually unsupported.

**The Answer to Verse 24's Objection**

There is a structural connection between this case and the third one that the sūrah intends. Thamūd objected in verse 24: *"Shall we follow a single human being from among us?"* — the objection to a lone ordinary man. Lot was exactly that. He had no tribe of his own in the town, no wealth, no armed following, and his converts numbered one household. By every criterion Thamūd named, he was disqualified.

The sūrah's answer is given by placing Lot's story immediately after Thamūd's and by recording the outcome: the single human being was saved with his family, and the civilization that objected to being led by one man was leveled. The Qur'an makes the general point elsewhere:

> *"And how many a prophet fought, and with him many devoted men; they did not lose heart for what befell them in the way of God, nor weaken nor submit."* (Qur'an 3:146)

> *"And We made them leaders guiding by Our Command when they were patient, and they were certain of Our signs."* (Qur'an 32:24)

Numbers are not the measure. The Qur'an says so directly:

> *"And if thou obeyest most of those upon the earth, they will lead thee astray from the way of God."* (Qur'an 6:116)

> *"How many a small company has overcome a large company by the leave of God."* (Qur'an 2:249)

**Application: When the Standard Is Publicly Abandoned**

Verse 33 describes a condition that recurs in the life of every society, and its recurrence is why the Qur'an gives Lot's story eight separate treatments — in 7, 11, 15, 21, 26, 27, 29, and 37 — more than any other prophetic narrative except Moses's. The condition is not individual transgression. Every society contains individual transgression, and the Qur'an's treatment of individual sin is dominated by the vocabulary of forgiveness: *tawbah*, *istighfār*, *ʿafw*, *maghfirah*.

The condition is the migration of wrong from private act to public norm — from the individual to the *nādī*, the assembly, the place where the community defines what it approves. That migration is what Lot's people completed, and it is what the Qur'an identifies as the point beyond which a society becomes *muʾtafikah*, overturned.

The mechanism is visible in ordinary life and does not require the specific content of Lot's case. A practice begins as transgression, is then tolerated, is then discussed without disapproval, is then defended, is then celebrated, and finally the person who objects is the one who must explain himself. Each step is small; the sequence is not. The Qur'an's word for the end of the sequence is *munkar fī nādīkum* — evil in your assemblies — and its diagnosis is that the assembly's approval, not the act, is what destroys.

The believer's position inside such a society is described in verse 33 and demonstrated in verses 34–35: he warns, he is disputed with, he is alone, and he is delivered. The Qur'an does not promise that he will change the society. Lot did not change his. It promises that he will be distinguished from it, that the distinction will be recorded, and that the deliverance of one house from several towns is, in God's accounting, a greater event than the destruction of the towns.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:34

> **Truly We sent upon them a torrent of stones, save the family of Lot, whom We delivered at dawn.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Ḥāṣib: The Rain That Is Not Rain**

*Innā arsalnā ʿalayhim ḥāṣiban.* The verb is the same one used for the wind of ʿĀd in verse 19 — *arsalnā*, We sent — and the same verb used for the she-camel in verse 27, *innā mursilū al-nāqah*. God's destructive acts and His provisions are both described as sendings, and the sūrah keeps the vocabulary identical so that the reader cannot file them in different categories.

*Ḥāṣib* is from *ḥ-ṣ-b*, small stones, pebbles, gravel — the same root as *ḥaṣbāʾ*, stony ground, and *ḥaṣā*, pebbles. As an active participle it denotes something that carries or casts small stones: a stoning wind, a shower of gravel. The Qur'an uses the same word as a general warning to all hearers, not only to Lot's towns:

> *"Do you feel secure that He Who is in the heaven will not cause the earth to swallow you, and then it trembles? Or do you feel secure that He Who is in the heaven will not send upon you a storm of stones (ḥāṣibā)? Then you shall come to know how My warning is."* (Qur'an 67:16–17)

The last clause of 67:17 is nearly the refrain of al-Qamar — *fa-sa-taʿlamūna kayfa nadhīr*, "then you will know how My warning is" — and it confirms that the two sūrahs are making the same argument. The *ḥāṣib* is not a historical curiosity but a standing possibility.

The parallel accounts describe the same event with different vocabulary, each adding a detail:

> *"And We made its upper part its lower part, and We rained upon them stones of baked clay (ḥijāratan min sijjīl)."* (Qur'an 15:74)

> *"And when Our Command came, We made its upper part its lower part and rained upon them stones of baked clay, layered (manḍūd), marked (musawwamah) with thy Lord. And they are not far from the wrongdoers."* (Qur'an 11:82–83)

*Sijjīl* — baked clay, or, as some commentators read it, a Persian-derived term for a register, connecting the stones to a written record. *Manḍūd* — stacked, layered, coming in continuous succession. *Musawwamah* — marked, designated, distinguishable. The last adjective is the most striking: the stones were marked *with thy Lord*, which the exegetes explain as marked for their targets. Ibn Kathīr records that each stone carried the name of the one it was to strike. Al-Qurṭubī discusses the report and its meaning: a punishment in which no stone falls randomly and no person is struck by mistake.

And the verse in 11:83 ends with the statement that gives the passage its continuing force: *wa-mā hiya mina al-ẓālimīna bi-baʿīd* — "and they are not far from the wrongdoers." The stones are not archived. Al-Bayḍāwī explains the phrase as a warning to the Quraysh, and Ibn Kathīr reads it as general: the punishment is not distant from anyone who does what they did.

**Wa-Jaʿalnā ʿĀliyahā Sāfilahā: The Overturning**

The phrase in 15:74 and 11:82 describes the towns' inversion — their upper part made their lower part. Sūrah al-Najm names the act and the towns together:

> *"And the overturned (al-muʾtafikah) He brought down (ahwā), and there covered them what covered them."* (Qur'an 53:53–54)

*Ahwā* — He caused to fall, He plunged them. The root *h-w-y*, discussed earlier in connection with *hawā* in verse 3, means to fall and to be empty. The towns that had made caprice their god were plunged by the same verb. Al-Rāzī notes the correspondence: the people followed *hawā*, and their cities were *ahwā* — the appetite became the fall.

The inversion is described in a phrase repeated twice in two sūrahs, and its repetition signals that it is the essential feature of the punishment. The towns were not merely destroyed; they were reversed. The social reversal of verse 29 — a people who made the *munkar* the norm of their assembly — is answered by a physical reversal: what was on top went to the bottom.

**Illā Āla Lūṭ: The Exception**

*Illā āla Lūṭin najjaynāhum bi-saḥar* — "save the family of Lot, whom We delivered at dawn." The exception is the verse's center of gravity, and the Qur'an gives it a theological explanation elsewhere:

> *"And thy Lord would not destroy the towns unjustly while their people were reformers (muṣliḥūn)."* (Qur'an 11:117)

The presence of reformers in a town suspends its destruction. Lot was a reformer, and his household was the fruit of his reform — one house of Muslims in several towns (51:36–37). The exception is therefore not an arbitrary rescue but the operation of a stated principle: God does not destroy a community while it contains people actively working for its good.

The same principle is stated to the Quraysh concerning themselves:

> *"But God would not punish them while thou art among them, nor would God punish them while they seek forgiveness."* (Qur'an 8:33)

Two protections named: the presence of the Prophet ﷺ, and the practice of *istighfār*. Both operate on the same logic as Lot's household — the presence of the righteous delays the punishment of the many, and the practice of seeking forgiveness continues that presence in the community after the prophet is gone. This is why the moon was split and Makkah was not destroyed: the Prophet ﷺ was in it.

**The Wife: Kinship That Does Not Save**

The exception has an exclusion inside it, and the Qur'an states it in five places:

> *"Save his wife; We decreed that she would be among those who remained behind (mina al-ghābirīn)."* (Qur'an 7:83; cf. 11:81; 15:60; 26:171; 27:57; 29:33)

*Mina al-ghābirīn* — among those who remained, or who passed away. She was family, she was in the house, she was commanded to leave with them, and she did not survive. The commentators explain her exclusion by her sympathy with the townspeople and her betrayal of Lot's guests — al-Ṭabarī records that she informed the town of the arrival of the young men.

The Qur'an then uses Lot's wife and Noah's wife as a paired example in a sūrah devoted to such pairings:

> *"God strikes a similitude for those who disbelieve: the wife of Noah and the wife of Lot. They were under two of Our righteous servants, and they betrayed them; so they availed them nothing against God, and it was said: Enter the Fire with those who enter."* (Qur'an 66:10)

And immediately the reverse case:

> *"And God strikes a similitude for those who believe: the wife of Pharaoh, when she said: My Lord, build for me a house with Thee in the Garden, and deliver me from Pharaoh and his work, and deliver me from the wrongdoing people. And Mary daughter of ʿImrān, who guarded her chastity..."* (Qur'an 66:11–12)

The four examples establish the rule with total clarity. Marriage to a prophet does not save; marriage to a tyrant does not condemn. The variable is the individual's own faith and conduct, and the Qur'an's insistence on this point across Sūrah 66 removes every possibility of reading verse 34's exception as tribal or familial. The family of Lot was saved because they believed, not because they were related — and one of them was related and destroyed.

**Bi-Saḥar: The Hour Before Dawn**

The deliverance came *bi-saḥar*, in the *saḥar* — the last portion of the night, the time before dawn begins to break. The word choice has three layers.

The root *s-ḥ-r* is shared with *siḥr*, sorcery, and the two are related in Arabic precisely because both belong to the faint, hidden hour and to things that are subtle and not fully seen. The sūrah opened with the accusation *siḥrun mustamirr* (54:2) and closes its fourth narrative with a deliverance *bi-saḥar*. The hour the accusers associated with deception is the hour God chose for rescue.

Second, *saḥar* is the hour of *istighfār* in the Qur'an's description of the reverent:

> *"And in the hours before dawn (bi-l-ashār) they sought forgiveness (yastaghfirūn)."* (Qur'an 51:18)

> *"The patient, the truthful, the obedient, those who spend, and those who seek forgiveness before dawn (al-mustaghfirīn bi-l-ashār)."* (Qur'an 3:17)

The deliverance arrives at the hour the pious spend asking for forgiveness, which is not a coincidence of timing but a statement of relationship: the household that leaves the town at *saḥar* is a household that had been awake in it.

Third, the timing is a rebuke to the towns. They were asleep. The command to Lot was explicit:

> *"So travel with thy household in a part of the night (bi-qiṭʿin mina al-layl), and let none of you turn back, save thy wife."* (Qur'an 11:81)

The believers walked out while the town slept, and the town woke into the morning described in verse 38. The same hours were, for one group, a journey to safety, and for the other, the last sleep they would have.

**Application: The Exception and the Exit**

Verse 34 has two practical teachings, and they are in tension in the way that true things often are.

The first is that the presence of righteous people protects a community. This is not a mystical claim but a social one with a theological guarantee. A town containing reformers is not destroyed, and the Qur'an says so twice — for Lot's town and for Makkah. The practical consequence is that the believer's decision to remain in a difficult society, working for its good, has effects beyond his own salvation and beyond the reach of his own assessment. Lot converted one household in a lifetime and delayed the punishment of several towns.

The second is that there comes a point at which the righteous are commanded to leave. The instruction to Lot was not to reform but to travel by night, and the instruction included a prohibition on looking back. Not every situation is a mission field, and the Qur'an distinguishes clearly between the prophet who stays and warns and the believer who is told to withdraw. The marker of the difference is the command itself: Lot stayed because he was sent; he left because he was told.

The third teaching is the one the exception's exclusion supplies. Nobody is saved by his company. The wife of a prophet perished; the wife of a tyrant was promised a house in the Garden. Family, marriage, nationality, congregation, and tradition are all real and all valuable, and none of them is transferable at the moment verse 34 describes. The believer who takes comfort in belonging to a righteous group is taking comfort in something that will not carry him, and the believer who fears that he belongs to a corrupt group is fearing something that will not condemn him. The Qur'an is explicit: *"And no bearer of burdens shall bear the burden of another"* (35:18). What carries a person out at *saḥar* is what he carried himself.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:35

> **As a blessing from Us. Thus do We requite whosoever gives thanks.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Niʿmatan Min ʿIndinā: The Source Named Twice**

*Niʿmatan min ʿindinā.* The rescue is described as a *niʿmah* — a blessing, a favor, a conferred good. The root *n-ʿ-m* denotes softness, ease, comfort, and the pleasant condition of a life without hardship; from it comes *naʿīm*, the bliss of the Garden, and *niʿmah*, the specific gift that produces it. The Qur'an's vocabulary of blessing is not abstract. A *niʿmah* is something experienced as ease after difficulty, as softness after roughness, as comfort after exposure.

The phrase *min ʿindinā*, "from Our presence," adds a second layer. *ʿInd* denotes proximity and presence — what is at hand with someone. God's use of *min ʿindinā* elsewhere marks gifts that come directly from Him without intermediary:

> *"Then they found a servant from among Our servants unto whom We had given mercy from Our presence (raḥmatan min ʿindinā) and taught him knowledge from Our presence (ʿilman min ladunnā)."* (Qur'an 18:65)

> *"Say: All is from God (kullun min ʿindi Allāh)."* (Qur'an 4:78)

The deliverance of Lot's household is therefore described twice in terms of source: a blessing, and from Our presence. The doubling excludes every other explanation. It was not their planning, not the darkness, not the speed of their walking, not the town's inattention. It was a gift with a named Giver.

This is the Qur'an's consistent treatment of rescue. Noah is carried — *wa-ḥamalnāhu* (54:13). Lot's family is delivered — *najjaynāhum* (54:34). The verb *najjā*, to deliver, is used throughout with God as subject, and the Qur'an insists that the deliverance is His even when a means is employed:

> *"And We delivered him and those with him by mercy from Us (bi-raḥmatin minnā)."* (Qur'an 7:72 — of Noah)

> *"Then We delivered him and those with him in the laden Ark."* (Qur'an 10:22)

The means is real and the deliverance is God's. Verse 34 supplied the means — departure at dawn; verse 35 supplies the meaning — a blessing from Our presence.

**Kadhālika Najzī Man Shakar: The Same Word as Verse 14**

*Kadhālika najzī man shakar* — "Thus do We requite whosoever gives thanks." The verb *najzī* is from *j-z-y*, the root the sūrah used twenty-one verses earlier in describing Noah's rescue:

> *"Coursing under Our Eyes, as a recompense (jazāʾan) for one who was rejected."* (Qur'an 54:14)

The pairing is exact and deliberate. Verse 14: *jazāʾan li-man kāna kufir* — a recompense for one who was disbelieved. Verse 35: *kadhalika najzī man shakar* — thus We recompense whoever is grateful. The same noun and verb from the same root, attached in one case to the denied prophet and in the other to the grateful household.

The two verses thereby form a small theorem across the sūrah's narrative. The rejected are requited. The grateful are requited. *Jazāʾ* operates in both directions and on both parties, and it is the same operation. The word carries no moral direction in itself; it means the settlement of an account, and verse 3's law — *wa-kullu amrin mustaqirr* — guarantees that every account settles.

The phrase *kadhālika*, "thus," is also significant. It means: in this manner, in the same way. God is not describing a single historical rescue but announcing a pattern with a stated input and a stated output. Whoever is grateful is requited *thus* — with deliverance from the destruction of his society, with a blessing from God's presence, with the preservation of his household. The pattern is available. Ibn Kathīr notes the generality of the phrase and its function as a promise to the Prophet ﷺ's Companions, who were a small believing household inside a town scheduled for judgment.

**Shukr and Kufr: The Two Verbs That Mean Uncovering and Covering**

The verse's key term is *shakar*, to give thanks, from the root *sh-k-r*. Arabic lexicography connects *shukr* to the idea of a thing appearing and increasing: the animal that is *shakūr* is one that shows the effect of good feeding in its body; gratitude is called *shukr* because it makes the blessing visible by acknowledging it. Al-Rāghib al-Iṣfahānī defines *shukr* as the imagination of the blessing and its manifestation — the recognition of the favor in the heart, its mention on the tongue, and its use in obedience.

Its opposite in Qur'anic usage is *kufr*, and the opposition is lexical as well as moral. *K-f-r* means to cover — the farmer covering seed with earth, the night covering the day, the ingrate covering the Giver. The Qur'an uses *kufr* in exactly this sense of ingratitude:

> *"So remember Me; I will remember you. And be grateful unto Me (wa-shkurū lī), and be not ungrateful unto Me (wa-lā takfurūn)."* (Qur'an 2:152)

And in the clearest statement of the pair:

> *"And God strikes a similitude: a town secure and at ease, its provision coming to it in abundance from every side, and it was ungrateful for the blessings of God (fa-kafarat bi-anʿumi Allāh), so God made it taste the garment of hunger and fear for what they used to do."* (Qur'an 16:112)

The town in 16:112 is described in terms that fit Lot's towns exactly — secure, at ease, provision from every side — and its sin is named with the covering verb. Verse 35's *man shakar* is the alternative to that town's *kafarat*. The whole of the sūrah's moral universe can be drawn with these two words: the grateful uncover the blessing and name its Giver; the ingrates cover it and consume it.

The Qur'an states the consequence of gratitude in a promise that is conditional in form and unconditional in assurance:

> *"And when thy Lord proclaimed: If you are grateful, I will surely increase you (la-azīdannakum); and if you are ungrateful, truly My Punishment is severe."* (Qur'an 14:7)

*Lā azīdannakum* — the doubled emphatic form: I will most certainly increase you. Gratitude produces increase, and the increase is not specified, which the commentators take to mean it is comprehensive: increase in the blessing itself, in its duration, in the capacity to enjoy it, and in what follows it.

**Lot as the Model of the Grateful Servant**

The Qur'an describes Lot elsewhere in terms that make verse 35's *man shakar* a description of him specifically:

> *"The people of Lot denied the warnings. Truly We sent upon them a storm of stones, save the family of Lot — We delivered them at dawn as a blessing from Us. Thus do We requite whosoever gives thanks."* (Qur'an 54:33–35)

And in Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ:

> *"And Lot — We gave him judgment and knowledge, and We delivered him from the town that committed the abominations. Truly they were an evil people, transgressors. And We brought him into Our mercy; truly he was of the righteous (mina al-ṣāliḥīn)."* (Qur'an 21:74–75)

Sūrah al-Ṣāffāt records the same deliverance:

> *"And truly Lot was among the messengers, when We delivered him and his household, all of them, save an old woman among those who remained. Then We destroyed the others."* (Qur'an 37:133–136)

The Qur'an reserves the adjective *shakūr* for the rare few — Noah is *ʿabdan shakūrā*, "a grateful servant" (17:3) — and it does not attach the label to Lot by name. It does something more direct: it states the rule and places Lot's household inside it. *Kadhālika najzī man shakar* — thus do We requite whoever gives thanks (54:35) — is spoken immediately after their deliverance, which makes the deliverance itself the evidence of their gratitude.

That placement is the same one the Qur'an gives to the other prophets it calls grateful. Of Abraham it says:

> *"Indeed Abraham was a leader (ummah), devout toward God, upright, and he was not of those who associate. Grateful for His blessings (shākiran li-anʿumihi); He chose him and guided him to a straight path."* (Qur'an 16:120–121)

And of Noah:

> *"Truly he was a grateful servant (ʿabdan shakūrā)."* (Qur'an 17:3)

Three prophets — Noah, Abraham, Lot — described with the same adjective *shakūr*/*shākir*, and each of the three was delivered from a destruction. The Qur'an thereby establishes gratitude not as a mood but as a category of survival. It is the disposition that accompanies deliverance in every one of its narratives.

**Application: Gratitude as the Organ of Preservation**

Verse 35 makes a claim that is easy to reduce to sentimentality and hard to reduce to anything else: the deliverance was a blessing, and blessings are given to the grateful. The claim is not that gratitude attracts material reward — the Qur'an's grateful people are as likely to be poor as prosperous — but that gratitude is the capacity by which a blessing is actually received.

The mechanism is describable. A blessing that is not acknowledged is not registered; a blessing that is not registered cannot be enjoyed, protected, or used correctly; and a person who does not register his blessings will not notice when they begin to change. Ingratitude is therefore not merely rude toward God; it is blindness toward one's own life. The town in 16:112 was secure and well-provisioned, and its covering of the blessing was followed by its inability to see the danger arriving — exactly the condition of Lot's towns, who slept while the believers walked out.

The tradition built a practice around this. *Al-ḥamdu lillāh* is the Muslim's constant acknowledgment, and the Prophet ﷺ instructed that every significant event begin and end with it. The daily prayers contain al-Fātiḥah, whose second verse is *al-ḥamdu lillāhi rabbi al-ʿālamīn*, seventeen times a day at minimum. The practice is not decorative; it is the training of the organ verse 35 identifies as the condition of requital.

And the practical test the verse proposes is uncomfortable. Look at your own security, your own ease, your own provision coming from every side, and ask whether you have been uncovering it or covering it. The town in Sūrah al-Naḥl was described as *āminan muṭmaʾinnā* — secure and at ease — and it was destroyed. Lot's towns were prosperous and were overturned. The difference between them and the household that walked out at dawn was not wealth, location, or intelligence. It was *shukr*. The verse states the rule in the present tense for anyone who will apply it: thus do We requite whoever gives thanks.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:36

> **And indeed he had warned them of Our assault, but they disputed the warnings.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Andharahum: The Warning Delivered in Advance**

*Wa-laqad andharahum baṭshatanā.* The doubled emphasis of *wa-laqad* returns for the third time in this narrative (33, 35, 36), and each time it establishes a fact that removes an excuse. Here the fact is that the warning preceded the assault. *Andhara* is the fourth form of *n-dh-r*, the root of *nadhīr* and of the refrain's *nudhur* — to warn of something seen approaching, to give notice in time for the notice to be useful.

*Baṭshah* is from *b-ṭ-sh*, to seize with violence, to grasp and overpower. It is not a general word for punishment; it denotes a sudden, forceful taking hold. The Qur'an uses the same root for God's seizure of the disobedient:

> *"Truly the assault of thy Lord is severe (inna baṭsha rabbika la-shadīd)."* (Qur'an 85:12)

> *"And thus is the assault of thy Lord when He assaults the towns while they are wrongdoing. Truly His assault is painful, severe."* (Qur'an 11:102)
