The verse in Hūd is the general law of which verse 36 is a case, and it adds a detail that matches this narrative exactly: *wa-hiya ẓālimah*, "while they are wrongdoing." God's assault falls on towns in a state of active injustice, and it is preceded by warning.

The addition of the possessive — *baṭshatanā*, Our assault — attaches the coming violence directly to God. It was not a natural disaster, not a geological event, not the consequence of the towns' own contradictions working themselves out. It was His seizure, announced in advance by His prophet, arriving at a stated time.

**Fa-Tamāraw: The Response of Disputation**

*Fa-tamāraw bi-l-nudhur* — "but they disputed the warnings." The verb is the sixth form of *m-r-y*, and the sixth form denotes reciprocity: *tamārā* means to dispute with one another, to argue back and forth. The response to a warning was not silence, not refusal, not flight — it was debate.

The same root appears in the Qur'an in two other significant places. In Sūrah al-Najm, immediately before the passage this sūrah continues:

> *"And he certainly saw him a second time... Will you then dispute with him (a-fa-tumārūnahu) concerning what he sees?"* (Qur'an 53:13, 12)

And in Sūrah al-Kahf, concerning the sleepers of the cave:

> *"They will say: Three, their dog the fourth... Say: My Lord knows best their number... So dispute not concerning them except with an outward dispute (fa-lā tumāri fīhim illā mirāʾan ẓāhirā), and ask not any of them concerning them."* (Qur'an 18:22)

The second of these defines the prohibited form of argument: *mirāʾ*, disputation conducted as an activity rather than as a search. The Qur'an permits an "outward dispute" — a statement of one's position — and forbids the extended contest whose purpose is winning.

The classical tradition gives *mirāʾ* a precise definition. Al-Rāghib distinguishes *jidāl* (dialectic, which may be legitimate), *munāẓarah* (mutual examination, which may be legitimate), and *mirāʾ* (disputation whose object is to defeat the other rather than to reach the truth). The Qur'an's condemnation of *mirāʾ* is consistent:

> *"Those who dispute concerning the signs of God without any authority having come unto them — there is naught in their breasts but a pride they shall never attain."* (Qur'an 40:56; cf. 40:4, 35)

> *"And of mankind is he who disputes concerning God without knowledge, without guidance, and without an illuminating Book."* (Qur'an 22:3, 8)

*Bi-ghayri ʿilm* — without knowledge. The dispute is characterized by the absence of information on the disputant's side. He is not arguing from evidence; he is arguing instead of examining it.

**What Disputation Accomplishes for the Disputant**

The psychological function of *tamārā* is worth stating plainly, because it is the mechanism by which most warnings are neutralized in ordinary life.

A warning addresses the will. It says: something is coming, and you must change. The immediate response of a person who does not wish to change is not to deny the warning — denial is exhausting and unstable, since the evidence keeps returning — but to convert it into a topic. Once the warning is a topic, it can be questioned, qualified, contextualized, historicized, compared to other warnings that proved false, and discussed at length. The discussion produces the experience of engagement without the consequence of action. The will is never reached, because the matter has been moved to the intellect, where the disputant is more comfortable and more in control.

Lot's people's own recorded response demonstrates the mechanism at its final stage:

> *"And Lot, when he said unto his people: Truly you commit the abomination... And the answer of his people was only that they said: Bring us God's Punishment if thou art among the truthful (fa-tinā bi-ʿadhābi Allāhi in kunta mina al-ṣādiqīn)."* (Qur'an 29:28–29)

> *"They said: O Lot, if thou ceasest not, thou shalt surely be among the expelled. He said: I am, toward your deed, among those who detest it."* (Qur'an 26:167–168)

The progression is complete: warning, dispute, challenge to produce the punishment, and finally expulsion of the warner. They asked him to bring the assault. Verse 36 says he had warned them of it. Verse 38 says it greeted them in the morning. The challenge was answered on the schedule they demanded, and the answer is described in the sūrah's own refrain as a matter to be tasted.

**The Prophetic Prohibition Against This Form of Argument**

The Prophet ﷺ treated *mirāʾ* as a moral danger rather than an intellectual style, and his warnings about it are unusually strong:

> *"The most hateful of men to God is the most contentious opponent (al-ald al-khaṣim)."* (al-Bukhārī 2457, 4523, 7188; Muslim 2668, from ʿĀʾishah)

The word *ald* denotes one given to twisted, oblique argument, and *khaṣim* one who pursues the dispute. The report places this character at the extreme of divine displeasure — a striking ranking, since the Qur'an's list of the worst human types usually begins with the arrogant tyrant.

> *"I am a guarantor of a house in the outskirts of Paradise for one who abandons arguing even when he is right, a house in the middle of Paradise for one who abandons lying even when joking, and a house in the highest of Paradise for one whose character is good."* (Abū Dāwūd 4800, from Abū Umāmah; graded ḥasan by al-Albānī)

The first of the three — abandoning argument while being right — is the exact opposite of *tamārā*. The reward is attached not to winning but to stopping, and the condition specified is the hardest one: being correct and declining to establish it.

> *"No people goes astray after having been guided except that they are given disputation (illā ūtū al-jadal)."* — and he then recited: *"They struck it forth for thee only as a dispute (jadalan); nay, they are a contentious people."* (al-Tirmidhī 3257, from Abū Umāmah; graded ḥasan ṣaḥīḥ — the verse recited is Qur'an 43:58)

The tradition's diagnosis is that communities lose the truth not by failing to receive it but by receiving it and then arguing about it. The verse in al-Qamar is the same diagnosis in narrative form.

**Application: The Warning You Are Currently Debating**

Verse 36 is a mirror. Most readers are not in Lot's town; most readers are in possession of a warning they have been disputing for some time. It may be medical, financial, marital, professional, or spiritual. Its content is known, its source is credible, and its arrival was in time for it to be useful. The response has been to examine it — to seek second opinions, to research contrary evidence, to note the exceptions, to observe that the warner has his own interests, to compare with cases where the warning did not materialize.

Some of that examination is legitimate. The Qur'an commands verification: *"O you who believe, if a wicked person comes to you with news, verify it"* (49:6), and it praises those who listen and follow the best of what they hear (39:18). The distinction is not between arguing and not arguing but between argument whose end is a decision and argument whose end is more argument. The marker of the second is that the person cannot say what evidence would settle the matter. If no possible evidence would end the discussion, the discussion is not an inquiry. It is *tamārā*, and verse 36 records where it ended for its practitioners.

The practical discipline the verse implies is a deadline. Lot's people had no deadline; they disputed until the morning of verse 38 supplied one. The believer sets his own: this matter will be decided by this date, on this evidence, and after that it will be acted upon rather than discussed. The tradition's phrase for the discipline is *tark al-mirāʾ* — abandoning disputation — and its reward, in the report of Abū Dāwūd, is a house in the outskirts of Paradise for a person who was right and stopped anyway.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:37

> **They had sought to lure him from his guests; so We blotted out their eyes. So taste My Punishment and My warnings!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Rāwadūhu: The Gradual Press**

*Wa-laqad rāwadūhu ʿan ḍayfihi.* The verb is the third form of *r-w-d*, and the third form denotes an action directed at another with persistence — to seek to obtain something from someone by gradual, repeated pressing. *Rāwada* is not to demand; it is to work on a person. The Qur'an uses the same verb in the story of Joseph, where it describes the sustained campaign of persuasion directed at him:

> *"And she in whose house he was sought to persuade him from himself (turāwidu fatāhā ʿan nafsihi). She closed the doors and said: Come. He said: God forbid!"* (Qur'an 12:23)

> *"The women in the city said: The wife of the notable is seeking to persuade her young man from himself (turāwidu fatāhā ʿan nafsihi); truly she has gone astray in obvious error."* (Qur'an 12:30)

The verb's connotation of patient, escalating pressure is exactly what the scene in Lot's house required. A crowd at the door does not make one request and leave. It presses, negotiates, threatens, and returns — and the Qur'an's single verb compresses the whole evening into one word.

*ʿAn ḍayfihi*, "from his guest(s)," means they sought to separate him from those he was protecting. *Ḍayf* in Arabic is both singular and plural in form, and the commentators understand it here as the angels who had come in human shape. Sūrah Hūd describes their arrival and the crowd's response:

> *"And when Our messengers came unto Lot, he was distressed for them and felt powerless to protect them, and said: This is a severe day (hādhā yawmun ʿasīr). And his people came rushing unto him (yuhraʿūna ilayh), and before that they had been doing the evil deeds."* (Qur'an 11:77–78)

*Yawmun ʿasīr* — "a severe day." The phrase is the same one the disbelievers will utter in verse 8 of this sūrah about the Day of Judgment: *hādhā yawmun ʿasir*. The Qur'an places the words in Lot's mouth at the door of his house, and then places them in the mouths of all disbelievers on the Day, and the reader who has read both recognizes that Lot's evening was a small version of the Resurrection: the guests arrived, the crowd came running, the warning was disputed, and the outcome was fixed.

**The Hospitality That Was Under Attack**

The gravity of the scene depends on the law the crowd was violating, and the Qur'an states that law with unusual warmth. Hospitality is one of the most sacred obligations in the Qur'anic ethic and in Arabian custom alike, and the arrival of guests is described in terms of honor:

> *"Has the report of Abraham's honored guests (ḍayfi Ibrāhīma al-mukramīn) reached thee?"* (Qur'an 51:24)

The same angels came first to Abraham, who honored them with a roasted calf (51:26–27), and then to Lot, who received them into a town that intended to assault them. The crime in verse 37 is therefore double: the violation of the sexual boundary that Lot had warned them of, and the violation of the guest-right that their own custom held inviolable. A people who will take a stranger from his host's protection have no remaining limit.

Lot's own response is recorded, and it shows the extremity of the situation:

> *"He said: O my people, these are my daughters; they are purer for you. So fear God and do not disgrace me concerning my guests. Is there not among you a single man of right judgment (rajulun rashīd)?"* (Qur'an 11:78)

The commentators explain the offer of his daughters in two ways — his actual daughters in marriage, or the women of the community, whom a prophet is father to, as in the phrase *"the Prophet is nearer to the believers than their own selves, and his wives are their mothers"* (33:6). Either reading makes the same point: Lot offered the only lawful alternative available in order to protect an obligation he could not surrender. And his question — *is there not among you one man of right judgment?* — received no answer, because the crowd was not composed of individuals making judgments. It was a crowd.

**Fa-Ṭamasnā Aʿyunahum: The Effacement**

*Fa-ṭamasnā aʿyunahum.* The verb is from *ṭ-m-s*, to efface, to obliterate a mark, to wipe out a trace so that no sign of it remains. It is used in Arabic for a road whose landmarks have been erased, for writing worn away, for a face whose features have been smoothed. Applied to eyes, *ṭams* means the removal of the organ's function while its form may remain — the eye made smooth, unseeing, its distinguishing feature erased.

The commentators record two levels of reading. The first is literal and physical: the angels struck them with blindness. Ibn Kathīr relates that Gabriel struck their faces with his wing, and that they could not see the door they had been standing at. Al-Ṭabarī records the same, along with the detail that they left groping and returned the next morning. The Biblical account preserves a parallel: "And they struck with blindness the men who were at the door of the house, both small and great, so that they were unable to find the door" (Genesis 19:11).

The second reading is interior. Al-Qurṭubī and others note that *ṭams* can describe the removal of insight rather than of sight, and they cite the Qur'an's own description of the disbelievers' condition:

> *"And upon their eyes is a covering (ghishāwah), and theirs is a great punishment."* (Qur'an 2:7)

> *"And We have set upon their hearts coverings, lest they understand it, and in their ears a heaviness."* (Qur'an 6:25)

> *"And We have made a barrier before them and a barrier behind them, and We have covered them, so they do not see."* (Qur'an 36:9)

The two readings are not in competition. The crowd at Lot's door was already blind in the second sense — they had heard the warnings, disputed them, and looked at what they should not have looked at for years — and the physical effacement was the visible enactment of a condition that had long been invisible. This is a persistent structure in the Qur'an: the punishment in the world makes manifest the state of the soul. The internal covering becomes an external one.

**The Correspondence Between the Organ and the Act**

The tradition consistently notes the fitness of this punishment. The eye is the organ by which the transgression began, and the Qur'an legislates against that beginning with unusual directness:

> *"Say unto the believing men that they lower their gaze (yaghuddū min abṣārihim) and guard their private parts. That is purer for them. Truly God is Aware of what they do. And say unto the believing women that they lower their gaze and guard their private parts."* (Qur'an 24:30–31)

The sequence in the legislation is the sequence in the sin: the gaze first, then the act. Lot's people had reversed the legislation for years, and the effacement was applied to the first term. Al-Qurṭubī comments that the punishment falling on the eyes specifically is a demonstration that the organs used in disobedience are the organs most liable in the punishment.

The Qur'an states the general principle for the Hereafter:

> *"And whosoever turns away from My Remembrance — truly his shall be a straitened life, and We shall gather him on the Day of Resurrection blind. He will say: My Lord, why hast Thou gathered me blind, when I was seeing? He will say: Thus — Our signs came unto thee and thou didst forget them; and so today thou art forgotten."* (Qur'an 20:124–126)

The exchange is exact: *limā ḥashartanī aʿmā wa-qad kuntu baṣīrā* — "why have You gathered me blind when I was seeing?" And the answer identifies the cause as forgetting the signs. The blindness of the Resurrection is the permanence of the blindness at Lot's door. Verse 37 shows the temporary form; 20:124 shows the final one.

**Fa-Dhūqū: The Imperative of Humiliation**

*Fa-dhūqū ʿadhābī wa-nudhur.* "So taste My Punishment and My warnings." The imperative here is not a command in the ordinary sense. Arabic rhetoric recognizes a category the grammarians call *amr li-l-ihānah* — the imperative of humiliation — in which the speaker commands what the addressee has no power to refuse, and the commanding is itself the degradation. Al-Zamakhsharī explains the usage in these verses precisely in those terms: they are told to taste because tasting is unavoidable, and the command announces that their choice in the matter has ended.

The verb *dhāqa*, to taste, is chosen over verbs of experiencing, receiving, or undergoing because it denotes the most direct form of knowledge available to a body. To taste is to take something inside and know it by contact. The Qur'an uses it for punishment more than a dozen times — 3:106, 181; 4:56; 6:30; 7:39; 8:35, 50; 10:52; 22:22; 32:14, 20; 34:42; 46:34 — and always at the transition from being told about a thing to being in it.

The pairing of the two objects — *ʿadhābī wa-nudhur*, My punishment and My warnings — is the refrain's pairing, and its function here is to establish identity. What they taste is not something new. It is the same content they had been warned about, arriving in a different mode. The warning was the punishment described; the punishment is the warning enacted. Nothing has been added.

**Application: The Door and the Crowd**

Verse 37 describes a scene with a structure that recurs: a person protecting something, a crowd pressing for it, and no individual in the crowd willing to be identified as the one asking. Lot's question — *is there not among you one man of right judgment?* — went unanswered not because the answer was no but because in a crowd nobody answers as an individual.

The verse's judgment, however, is on the crowd as a whole and on each member. The effacement fell on "their eyes," plural, including those who had only come along, only shouted, only watched. The Qur'an's principle on collective action is consistent: participation in a mob is participation in its act, and presence with approval is approval. The believer's instruction in such situations is not neutrality but departure, and where departure is impossible, refusal — the heart's refusal that the ḥadīth calls the weakest of faith (Muslim 49).

There is also a lesson in the protection. Lot could not overpower the crowd; he had said so — *"would that I had strength against you, or could take refuge in a firm support"* (11:80) — and the Prophet ﷺ corrected him: the firm support was already there. The effacement of the crowd's eyes was that support arriving. The person defending something good against pressure he cannot match is not therefore undefended, and the verse records what happened to the pressure. It was not argued with. It was switched off.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:38

> **And indeed an enduring punishment greeted them early in the morning.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Ṣabbaḥahum: The Verb of the Dawn Attack**

*Wa-laqad ṣabbaḥahum ʿadhābun mustaqirr.* The verb is the second form of *ṣ-b-ḥ*, morning. In ordinary Arabic *ṣabbaḥahu* means to come upon someone in the morning, and the Arabs used it specifically for a raid conducted at dawn — the hour when a settlement is asleep, unguarded, and least able to respond. The Qur'an uses the same verb in that sense elsewhere:

> *"And truly you pass by them in the morning, and at night. Will you not then reflect?"* (Qur'an 37:137–138)

> *"So the cry seized them at sunrise (fa-akhadhat-humu al-ṣayḥatu mushriqīn)."* (Qur'an 15:73 — of Thamūd)

> *"And truly you pass by them in the morning (muṣbiḥīn), and at night. Will you not then reflect?"* (Qur'an 37:137–138 — of Lot's towns)

> *"And We pursued them at sunrise (fa-tabiʿūhum mushriqīn)."* (Qur'an 26:60 — of Pharaoh's host)

The choice of morning is precise for three reasons, and each carries meaning.

First, it is the hour of maximum vulnerability. The towns had spent the night in their ordinary condition — the blinded crowd had gone home, the houses were shut, the people were asleep. Nothing in the morning suggested that anything had changed. The Qur'an describes a similar scene for the people of the garden in Sūrah al-Qalam:

> *"Truly We tested them as We tested the owners of the garden, when they swore that they would harvest it in the morning, and they made no exception. Then a visitation from thy Lord encompassed it while they were sleeping, and in the morning it was as though plucked (fa-aṣbaḥat ka-l-ṣarīm)."* (Qur'an 68:17–20)

*Baʾt* — a visitation — came while they slept, and the morning revealed the result. The structure is identical to verse 38: night, sleep, and a morning in which the world has been settled differently.

Second, morning in the Qur'an is normally the hour of mercy. Dawn is the time of *istighfār* (51:18; 3:17); the Fajr prayer is the day's first act of worship; the *ṣubḥ* is described as breathing:

> *"By the dawn when it breathes (wa-l-ṣubḥi idhā tanaffas)."* (Qur'an 81:18)

Lot's household had been delivered *bi-saḥar*, in the hour before dawn (54:34). The same hour that was rescue for one group was destruction for the other. The Qur'an uses this simultaneity repeatedly — the night journey of the believers and the morning of the drowned, the sea parted for one people and closed on another — to establish that time is not morally neutral and that the same hour does different things to different people.

Third, the morning is the hour of disclosure. What was hidden in the night is visible at sunrise. Sūrah al-Ḥijr states the timing:

> *"So the cry seized them at sunrise (fa-akhadhat-humu al-ṣayḥatu mushriqīn), and We made its upper part its lower part, and We rained upon them stones of baked clay."* (Qur'an 15:73–74)

*Mushriqīn* — as the sun rose. The destruction is timed to the moment of maximum visibility, so that nothing about it is concealed and so that the survivors — travelers, passersby, later generations — can see the result in daylight.

**ʿAdhābun Mustaqirr: The Word From Verse 3**

The adjective *mustaqirr* is the tenth form of *q-r-r*, to settle, to be fixed, to come to rest. The sūrah used the same word in its third verse, as a universal law:

> *"They denied and followed their caprices — and every matter shall be made to endure (wa-kullu amrin mustaqirr)."* (Qur'an 54:3)

Verse 38 is the fulfillment of verse 3 in a specific case. The law was announced thirty-five verses earlier; the case arrives with the law's own word attached to the punishment. *ʿAdhābun mustaqirr* — a punishment that has settled, that is fixed, that will not be lifted.

Al-Qurṭubī explains *mustaqirr* here as a punishment that does not cease and is not reprieved, and Ibn Kathīr adds that it may refer to the destruction in this world and to what follows it in the next — both of which are settled. Al-Bayḍāwī reads it as an affliction fixed upon them by decree, incapable of being turned away.

The word therefore does double duty. It describes the completed state of the worldly destruction — the towns are finished, permanently, their condition not transitional — and it describes the quality of the punishment that continues. The Qur'an uses the same root for the two states:

> *"Their dwelling is the Fire; every time they wish to come forth from it, they are returned unto it, and it is said unto them: Taste the punishment of the Fire which you used to deny."* (Qur'an 32:20)

> *"And a barrier shall be set between them and that which they desire, as was done with their likes before. Truly they were in disquieting doubt."* (Qur'an 34:54)

The barrier of 34:54 is the settled state — the point at which the matter can no longer be reopened. Verse 38 says the towns reached it in a morning.

**The Settling That Nobody Noticed Happening**

There is a detail in the narrative that gives the verse its particular severity. The people of Lot's towns were not destroyed in the middle of a crime. The crime had been attempted the previous evening and had been prevented — their eyes were effaced and they went home. The destruction came later, in the morning, to a town that had, by every outward appearance, survived its crisis.

This is the pattern the Qur'an calls *istidrāj* — being allowed to continue in a state that feels like safety:

> *"And let not those who disbelieve reckon that Our giving them respite is better for their souls. We give them respite only that they may increase in sin, and theirs is a humiliating punishment."* (Qur'an 3:178)

> *"So when they forgot that wherewith they were reminded, We opened unto them the gates of every thing, until when they rejoiced in what they were given, We seized them suddenly (baghtatan), and behold — they were in despair."* (Qur'an 6:44)

*Baghtatan* — suddenly, without warning at the moment of maximum comfort. The verse in al-Anʿām describes the exact sequence: warning, forgetting, opening of provision, rejoicing, seizure. Lot's towns completed the sequence between the evening of verse 37 and the morning of verse 38. They had been warned, they had disputed, they had been stopped, and they went to sleep — and the sleep was the last item on the list.

**The Passing Road**

The Qur'an insists that the result remained visible, and it addresses the audience as travelers:

> *"And truly you pass by them in the morning and at night. Will you not then reflect?"* (Qur'an 37:137–138)

> *"And indeed they are upon a highway still established (bi-imāmin muqīm). Truly in that is a sign for the believers."* (Qur'an 15:76–77)

> *"And We left therein a sign for those who fear the painful punishment."* (Qur'an 51:37)

> *"And We made it a sign for all beings."* (Qur'an 29:15 — of Noah's Ark)

*Bi-imāmin muqīm* — the road is still there, established, in use. The towns are on a route that continues to be traveled. The sign has not been removed by time; it has been made permanent by it. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the mention of morning and evening in 37:138 corresponds to the twice-yearly caravan journeys — the Quraysh passed the site of Lot's towns on their southern route and al-Ḥijr on their northern one, and the Qur'an is describing their own itinerary.

The verse therefore closes the fourth case with the evidence in place and accessible. The punishment has settled; the remains are visible; the road runs past them. The only variable remaining is whether anyone reflects — which is the question the next verse and the next refrain will put.

**Application: The Night Before the Morning**

Verse 38 describes a population that went to sleep inside a decision it had already made and woke inside the consequence. The night between the two contained no new information, no new argument, and no new opportunity — only sleep. The matter had settled before they lay down; they simply had not observed the settling.

The practical question the verse raises is about one's own nights. Every person is inside decisions that have consequences scheduled for a morning he has not yet reached: a health trajectory, a debt, a relationship being eroded, a habit compounding, a risk accumulating. The Qur'an's description of such a state is not that the person is being punished but that the matter is *mustaqirr* — settling, fixed, becoming what it will be, whether or not the person is attending.

The counsel is therefore not to fear the morning but to use the night. The night before a settled matter is the last interval in which the matter can be unsettled, and the Qur'an gives the interval a name and a practice: *bi-l-ashār yastaghfirūn* — in the hours before dawn they sought forgiveness (51:18). The believers who walked out of Lot's town at *saḥar* used the hour for departure; the reverent described in al-Dhāriyāt use it for repentance. Both uses are the same in substance: doing something about a settled matter while it can still be moved.

The towns did neither. They slept, and the morning greeted them, and the greeting was *ʿadhābun mustaqirr*.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:39

> **So taste My Punishment and My warnings!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Occurrence, and Why the Verse Repeats**

*Fa-dhūqū ʿadhābī wa-nudhur.* The identical command that closed verse 37 returns two verses later. This is the only place in the sūrah where a phrase is repeated inside a single narrative, and the repetition requires an explanation. The commentators supply two, and both are recorded by Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī.

The first reading distinguishes the referents. Verse 37's command was addressed at the worldly punishment — the effacement of their eyes and the destruction that followed. Verse 39's command is addressed at the punishment of the Hereafter, which continues after the towns have settled into ruin. On this reading the two verses describe the same people tasting in two realms, and the repetition marks the transition: they tasted in the world, and they will taste in the next.

The second reading distinguishes the addressees. Verse 37 was God's address to Lot's people at the moment of their destruction. Verse 39 is God's address through them — to the Quraysh, and by extension to every disbeliever who hears the account. On this reading the plural imperative turns outward at the end of the narrative and speaks across four thousand years to the audience in Makkah and to the reader now.

Both readings are sound, and their combination is the more forceful: the punishment is doubled in duration and the address is doubled in reach. The Qur'an frequently performs this operation, closing a historical account with a sentence that is grammatically about the past and functionally about the present:

> *"Thus do We requite the guilty (kadhālika najzī al-mujrimīn)."* (Qur'an 7:40 — and the same phrase closes the accounts at 10:13, 12:75, and 77:18)

**Dhawq: The Verb of Direct Knowledge**

The command rests on a verb worth examining, because the Qur'an uses it as a technical term for a kind of knowing. *Dhāqa*, to taste, denotes knowledge acquired by contact rather than by report. The Arabs distinguished between *ʿilm* — knowledge held about a thing — and *dhawq* — knowledge held in the thing. One may know honey's properties exhaustively and not have tasted it; and one who has tasted it knows something no description conveyed.

The Qur'an uses this verb for the punishments of the Hereafter, and the Prophet ﷺ used the same verb for the highest experience of faith:

> *"Three qualities — whoever has them will taste the sweetness of faith (wajada ḥalāwat al-īmān): that God and His Messenger be more beloved to him than anything else; that he love a person only for God's sake; and that he hate returning to disbelief after God has rescued him from it, as he would hate to be thrown into the Fire."* (al-Bukhārī 16, 21, 6941; Muslim 43, from Anas ibn Mālik)

> *"He has tasted the sweetness of faith who is content with God as Lord, with Islam as religion, and with Muhammad as Messenger."* (Muslim 34, from al-ʿAbbās ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib)

The parallel is exact and it is the verse's central teaching. There are two tastings available to a human being: the sweetness of faith and the punishment of denial. Both are *dhawq* — direct, unargueable, bodily knowledge. Both are certain to be experienced by everyone. The only variable is which one.

The tradition of spiritual theology developed this vocabulary further, using *dhawq* for the stage of knowledge that exceeds inference, and the Qur'an itself supplies the framework:

> *"Nay! If you knew with the knowledge of certainty (ʿilm al-yaqīn), you would surely see the Blaze. Then you shall surely see it with the eye of certainty (ʿayn al-yaqīn). Then you shall surely be questioned that Day concerning bliss."* (Qur'an 102:5–8)

Three stages: knowing about a thing (*ʿilm al-yaqīn*), seeing it (*ʿayn al-yaqīn*), and being in it. *Dhawq* belongs to the third. Verse 39 announces that the disbelievers have moved from the first stage — where they had the warnings, the reports, the signs — to the third.

**ʿAdhābī wa-Nudhur: The Warnings Become the Punishment**

The two objects of the verb are the same two nouns the sūrah's refrain pairs. The pairing here produces an identity that should not be missed: what they are told to taste is *My punishment and My warnings*. The warnings are included among the things tasted.

Al-Bayḍāwī explains the construction as meaning "taste the punishment that My warnings promised" — the warnings are tasted in the sense that their content is now experienced. Ibn Kathīr reads the same. The theological point is that the punishment contains nothing that was not in the warning. Every element of what they are tasting was described to them in advance by a prophet they called an insolent liar. The tasting is not the arrival of new information; it is the arrival of old information in a new medium.

This is why the Qur'an's phrase for the disbelievers' cry in the Fire is always about recognition rather than surprise:

> *"And they will say: Had we but listened or reasoned, we would not be among the companions of the Blaze."* (Qur'an 67:10)

> *"This is what the Compassionate promised, and the messengers spoke truly."* (Qur'an 36:52)

*Wa-ṣadaqa al-mursalūn* — the messengers spoke truly. The confession is of accuracy, not of novelty. They are not learning something; they are confirming something, from inside it.

**The Imperative Applied to the Quraysh**

If verse 39's address extends to the Quraysh, as the second reading holds, then it had a specific historical fulfillment within the lifetime of its first hearers. The Qur'an used the same phrase about them at Badr:

> *"And their prayer at the House was naught but whistling and clapping of hands. So taste the punishment (fa-dhūqū al-ʿadhāb) for what you used to disbelieve."* (Qur'an 8:35)

And the tradition connects the verse to the battle, at which seventy of the Makkan chiefs were killed and seventy taken captive. The sūrah's own verses 45–46 will describe that defeat:

> *"The whole shall be routed and they will turn [their] backs. Nay! The Hour is their tryst, and the Hour is more calamitous and more bitter."* (Qur'an 54:45–46)

Ibn Kathīr records that the Prophet ﷺ recited verse 45 as he came out of his tent at Badr, and that ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb said he did not understand what *al-jamʿ*, "the whole," meant until he saw it that day. The tasting announced in verse 39 to Lot's people was therefore tasted, in its first installment, by the audience to whom the sūrah was revealed — within a few years, at a place they knew, in a battle they had provoked.

**The Sarcastic Imperative Elsewhere**

The Qur'an uses the verb *dhāqa* in one other mode that clarifies its force here — the mode of contemptuous address:

> *"Taste! Truly thou art the mighty, the noble (dhūq innaka anta al-ʿazīz al-karīm)!"* (Qur'an 44:49)

The verse is addressed to the sinner in the Fire, and its content is a quotation of what he said of himself in the world. The Quraysh's chiefs were called *al-ʿazīz al-karīm* — the mighty, the honorable — in their own society's vocabulary, and the verse returns their titles to them as a command to taste. Al-Zamakhsharī explains the construction as *amr li-l-ihānah*, the imperative of humiliation: the command is not an invitation but the announcement that resistance is over.

Verse 39 operates the same way. The people being addressed have no capacity to comply or refuse; the imperative describes a state, and its force lies in the fact that it is addressed as a command to beings who once had commands given to them and disputed them. They disputed the warnings (54:36). They are now told to taste them, and the telling is not disputable.

**Application: Which of the Two Tastings**

Verse 39 leaves the reader with a choice stated in the Qur'an's most concrete vocabulary. Both options are *dhawq*. One is the sweetness of faith, described in the ḥadīth as the experience of a person whose loves and hatreds have been reordered around God. The other is the punishment and the warnings, described here. Neither can be avoided by not choosing, because the tasting is not optional; only its object is.

The practical difference between them lies in timing. The sweetness of faith is tasted in the world, in ordinary days, in the prayer performed without distraction, the fast broken, the wrong forgone, the truth told at cost. The punishment is tasted later, and its defining feature is that it arrives after the opportunity to taste the other has closed. Verse 39's imperative is spoken to people for whom nothing further can be done — which is why the sūrah immediately follows it with the refrain that says the Qur'an has been made easy and asks whether anyone remembers.

That sequence is the verse's real message. The tasting is coming for everyone. The question is whether it will be the tasting described in the ḥadīth of Anas — a person content with God as Lord, Islam as religion, and Muhammad ﷺ as Messenger — or the tasting described here. The first is available now, in this life, at no cost beyond the reordering of loves. The second is available only then, and only to those who declined the first.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:40

> **And indeed We have made the Qur'an easy to remember; so is there any who remembers?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Fourth and Final Occurrence**

The refrain appears for the last time, and its finality gives it a different weight. Verse 17 asked the question after one catastrophe; verse 22 after two; verse 32 after three; verse 40 after four — Noah's flood, ʿĀd's wind, Thamūd's cry, and Lot's stones. The offer has now been issued once for each destroyed nation, in identical words, at identical intervals.

The structure has a rhetorical consequence the reader arrives at only on the fourth occurrence. By this point the refrain is expected. The reciter knows it is coming; the listener recognizes its sound before its words; and the recognition is itself an instance of what the refrain claims. The Qur'an has made itself easy to remember, and it has just demonstrated the fact on the person reciting it.

Al-Zamakhsharī and al-Rāzī both note that the refrains of al-Qamar function as the sūrah's structural pillars, dividing the five narratives into equal movements and giving the chapter the form of a discourse with a recurring chorus. Ibn Kathīr's comment on the repetition is simpler and stronger: God repeats the offer because He has not withdrawn it.

**Why There Is No Fifth Refrain**

The sūrah's next case — Pharaoh, in verses 41–42 — receives no refrain of either kind. The absence is significant, and the commentators explain it structurally. After four nations described at a distance, the fifth is described in two verses and the sūrah turns immediately, in verse 43, to direct address: *"Are your disbelievers better than those?"*

At that point a refrain would be redundant. The refrain exists to bridge the distance between a historical catastrophe and a present audience, and once the audience is being spoken to in the second person, the bridge is unnecessary. The sūrah stops generalizing and starts accusing — or, more precisely, starts asking questions that the audience must answer about itself.

This means verse 40 is the last moment in al-Qamar at which the reader is addressed impersonally. After it, there is no more "is there anyone who remembers?" There is only "are your disbelievers better than those?" The transition from the third person to the second is the sūrah's closing movement, and verse 40 is its hinge.

**The Ease of the Book as the Standard for the Lone Believer**

The fourth occurrence follows the Lot narrative, and the pairing produces a meaning the earlier occurrences did not carry. Lot's case was the case of a single household of believers inside a society that had made wrong into its public norm. The believer in that position has no institutional support, no majority opinion to appeal to, no cultural consensus to rely on. He has, in the words of 51:36–37, one house.

For such a person, the ease of the Qur'an is not a convenience but a necessity. A standard that exists only in the surrounding culture is useless when the surrounding culture has inverted, and a standard that exists only in scholarly books is useless when the books are unavailable. A standard that has been made easy to carry in the memory travels with the person, functions without infrastructure, and cannot be revised by the assembly.

This is why the Qur'an's facility for memorization has had the historical consequence it has had. In every period of disruption — the Mongol destruction of Baghdad, the expulsions from Spain, the colonial dissolution of the madrasah systems, the suppression of religious education in the twentieth century — the text survived in chests rather than in libraries. The sūrah's claim was verified precisely in the conditions the sūrah describes: a town overturned, a household walking out at dawn with nothing, and the reminder carried in the memory of the household's members.

Lot himself is described as a prophet given *ḥukm* and *ʿilm* (21:74), and his household's deliverance was a deliverance of people who carried the standard with them. The refrain at verse 40 asks whether anyone remembers — *mudhakkir* — and in the context of this narrative the question has a specific edge: the remembering that saved Lot's household was the kind that could be taken out of a town at night.

**Dhikr as the Recovery of What the Soul Already Knows**

Ibn ʿAjībah's reading of the refrain, noted at verse 17, gains force at its fourth occurrence. He connects *dhikr* to the primordial covenant:

> *"And when thy Lord brought forth from the children of Adam, from their loins, their progeny, and made them bear witness against themselves: Am I not your Lord? They said: Yea, we bear witness — lest you should say on the Day of Resurrection: Truly we were heedless of this."* (Qur'an 7:172)

On this reading, "is there any who remembers?" means: is there anyone who recovers the acknowledgment his soul already made? The verse's purpose clause in 7:172 — *an taqūlū yawma al-qiyāmati innā kunnā ʿan hādhā ghāfilīn*, "lest you say on the Day of Resurrection: we were heedless of this" — states that the covenant was established precisely to remove the excuse of ignorance. Revelation is then *dhikr* because it reminds the soul of what it once affirmed.

This reading explains why the Qur'an describes its own function so consistently as reminder rather than as information:

> *"So remind — thou art only a reminder."* (Qur'an 88:21)

> *"And remind, for truly the Reminder benefits the believers."* (Qur'an 51:55)

> *"This is no less than a Reminder (dhikr) unto all beings."* (Qur'an 81:27; cf. 12:104, 38:87)

The four refrains of al-Qamar are therefore four invitations to recover something, not to acquire something. And the invitation's ease — *yassarnā* — is calibrated to that: recovering a memory is easier than learning a system, and the Book has been made as easy as the task it performs.

**The Question Asked of a Reader Who Has Now Seen Four Cases**

By verse 40 the reader has witnessed a total destruction by water, by wind, by sound, and by stone. He has seen four separate civilizations, in four separate eras, with four separate technologies and four separate self-images, and he has heard four identical questions about how the punishment was. He has also heard, four times, that the reminder has been made easy.

The question *fa-hal min mudhakkir* at this fourth position is therefore not hopeful but demanding. It is the question of a speaker who has finished his evidence. There is nothing further to show. Four cases have been presented, the refrains have established the pattern, and the sūrah is about to stop narrating and start addressing. The reader who has not been moved by four demonstrations of the same law will not be moved by a fifth, and the sūrah knows it — which is why the fifth case gets two verses and the address that follows gets four.

**Application: Carrying the Standard**

Verse 40's practical instruction is to acquire something portable. Every person's convictions rest on supports, and the supports are usually external: a community, an institution, a family tradition, a culture's consensus, a set of books, a class of people whose opinion he trusts. These supports are real and legitimate — the Qur'an commands consultation, honors scholars, and builds a community. But verse 40 is placed after the account of a society whose supports had inverted, and after the account of a household that left with nothing.

The refrain's answer is that the primary support is designed to be carried. The Qur'an is easy to remember so that the remembering can survive the loss of everything else. A person who has ten verses in his memory has ten verses in prison, in exile, in illness, in a town that has decided otherwise, and on the morning when the stones arrive.

The practical form of the instruction is small and available: retain something. Not the whole Book — the refrain says *what is easy*, and 73:20 commands reciting what is easy of it. Begin with a sūrah short enough to hold. Al-Qamar itself, at fifty-five short verses with two refrains that supply sixteen of them, is a reasonable ambition for a year. A juzʾ of the short Makkan sūrahs is the traditional beginning. The point is not the quantity but the fact of interior possession — of having something that cannot be taken out of the town with you, because it is already in you.

The question at the end has been asked four times now, and it will be asked twice more in the sūrah's remaining verses, in a different form. *Fa-hal min mudhakkir* — is there anyone who remembers? The verse does not wait for an answer. It has already told the reader that the remembering was made easy, which means the answer is in his hands and always was.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:41

> **And indeed the warnings came unto the House of Pharaoh.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Largest Story in the Qur'an, Given One Verse**

*Wa-laqad jāʾa Āla Firʿawna al-nudhur.* The story of Moses and Pharaoh is the most extensive narrative in the entire Qur'an, treated in full in Sūrahs 7, 10, 20, 26, and 28, and referenced in more than twenty others. Pharaoh is the most frequently named individual in the Book — his title appears seventy-four times. And al-Qamar gives him a single verse of introduction before the verdict in the next.

The compression is a technique, not an omission. Ibn Kathīr explains the method of the sūrah's historical section as allusive rather than narrative: the audience already possessed the accounts, and the sūrah's business is judgment, not storytelling. The Quraysh knew the story of Moses — they had heard it from the Prophet ﷺ repeatedly, and they had Jewish and Christian contacts in Yathrib and in the trading towns. Verse 41 could therefore open the case with one clause and assume the file.

There is a further effect. The four preceding cases occupied thirty-two verses. Pharaoh's occupies two. The disproportion is itself a statement about what God considers worth recounting: not the grandeur of the destroyed party but the specificity of the lesson. Noah's case required the details of the Ark and the water because the lesson was about the means of salvation. ʿĀd's required the wind and the palm trunks because the lesson was about strength. Thamūd's required the camel and the water allocation because the lesson was about a rule. Lot's required the guests and the eyes because the lesson was about a norm. Pharaoh's case had already been told in full, many times, in the same revelation — so the lesson here is stated and the file is closed.

**Āla Firʿawna: The House, Not the Man**

The warnings came to *Āla Firʿawna* — the house, family, household, and following of Pharaoh. *Āl* in Arabic denotes those attached to a person by kinship, allegiance, or affiliation; the Qur'an uses it for Abraham's household, for Muhammad's household, for Pharaoh's regime, and for the Pharaoh himself in some constructions. The verse's choice is deliberate: the warning was addressed to a system, not to an individual.

This matters because Pharaoh's tyranny in the Qur'an is never presented as a solitary phenomenon. He has ministers — Hāmān; financiers — Qārūn; magicians; soldiers; a nobility; a population that obeys. The Qur'an names the structure:

> *"And We sent Moses with Our signs and a clear authority, unto Pharaoh and Hāmān and Qārūn; and they said: A sorcerer, a liar (sāḥirun kadhdhāb)."* (Qur'an 40:23–24)

Note the two accusations in that verse: *sāḥir*, sorcerer, and *kadhdhāb*, liar. They are precisely the two accusations this sūrah has recorded — *siḥrun mustamirr* at verse 2 and *kadhdhābun ashir* at verse 25 — combined into a single phrase by Pharaoh's court. The Qur'an is showing that the vocabulary of rejection is not invented afresh by each generation; it is inherited, and the same two words have been used against every messenger.

Sūrah al-Dhāriyāt describes Pharaoh's response with a verb this sūrah has already used:

> *"And in Moses [is a sign], when We sent him unto Pharaoh with a clear authority. But he turned away with his supporters (fa-tawallā bi-ruknihī) and said: A sorcerer or a madman."* (Qur'an 51:38–39)

*Fa-tawallā bi-ruknihī* — he turned away with his strong support, his pillar, meaning his entourage and his power. The verb *tawallā* is the same verb God used in verse 6 to command the Prophet ﷺ to turn away from the mockers. Pharaoh turned away with his regime behind him; the Prophet ﷺ was told to turn away alone toward the Day. The sūrah has now shown both turnings.

**Jāʾa: The Verb of Arrival, Repeated From Verse 4**

The verse's verb is *jāʾa*, "came," and it echoes the sūrah's fourth verse exactly:

> *"Indeed reports have come to them (wa-laqad jāʾahum) wherein is a reproof."* (Qur'an 54:4)

Both verses use the doubled emphasis *wa-laqad* with *jāʾa*. In verse 4 the reports came to the Quraysh; in verse 41 the warnings came to Pharaoh's house. The verbal parallel places the two audiences on the same footing: both have had something arrive, and the arriving is the ground of the case against them.

This is the Qur'an's theology of revelation in a single verb. Truth is not discovered by the seeker; it comes to the sought. It arrives from outside, unbidden, and it establishes responsibility in the one who receives it. Pharaoh's house did not go looking for Moses. Moses came. The Quraysh did not commission the Qur'an. The Qur'an came. In both cases the arrival preceded and produced the obligation, and the Qur'an's judgment on both turns entirely on what was done with what arrived.

**Al-Nudhur: The Warnings Delivered to a Regime**

The subject of the sentence is *al-nudhur*, the plural of *nadhīr* — warnings or warners. The plural is comprehensive, and in Pharaoh's case the comprehensiveness is documented. The Qur'an records the delivery of nine distinct signs:

> *"And We gave Moses nine clear signs (tisʿa āyātin bayyināt)."* (Qur'an 17:101)

> *"So We sent upon them the flood, the locusts, the lice, the frogs, and the blood — signs, distinct (āyātin mufaṣṣalāt)."* (Qur'an 7:133)

And the two great ones before them: the staff and the hand.

> *"Go thou and thy brother with My signs, and be not remiss in My remembrance. Go, both of you, unto Pharaoh — truly he has transgressed. And speak unto him a gentle word (qawlan layyinan); haply he may take heed or fear."* (Qur'an 20:42–44)

The instruction to speak gently to the worst tyrant in the Qur'an is a detail of considerable weight, and it establishes that the warnings were delivered with maximum courtesy and maximum clarity. Nothing was withheld, nothing was ambiguous, and nothing was delivered in a manner calculated to provoke. The verse's *jāʾa al-nudhur* therefore means: they were given everything.

**Application: The Regime That Received the Warning**

Verse 41's use of *Āla Firʿawna* rather than *Firʿawn* has a contemporary application that is difficult to avoid. The warning is addressed to a house — to the apparatus around the tyrant, the ministers, the financiers, the propagandists, the technicians, the people who make the system function. The Qur'an does not treat Pharaoh's regime as Pharaoh plus innocent bystanders. It treats it as a single addressee.

Hāmān is named with him. Qārūn is named with him. The magicians are addressed with him, and notably they are the only members of the regime who change sides — a detail that shows the Qur'an does not consider affiliation irreversible. The soldiers are described as *junūd*, hosts, and they are destroyed with him:

> *"And We seized him and his hosts and cast them into the sea, while he was blameworthy."* (Qur'an 51:40)

The principle for any person inside any system is therefore two-sided. The system receives the warning collectively, which means every member has been warned whether or not he personally attended to it. And the members are not thereby trapped: the magicians heard the same message as Pharaoh, in the same room, at the same moment, and they responded differently. The Qur'an records their response in words that stand against the entire structure they served:

> *"They said: We shall never prefer thee over the clear signs that have come unto us, and over Him who created us. So decree what thou wilt decree; thou canst only decree concerning the life of this world."* (Qur'an 20:72)

That sentence — *you can only decree about this life* — is the whole of the Qur'ananic assessment of political power, and it was spoken by men who had been threatening to be crucified for saying it. Verse 41 says the warnings came to the house. Verse 42 will say what the house did with them, and the answer, uniquely among the sūrah's five cases, is that they denied *all* of them.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:42

> **They denied Our signs — all of them; so We seized them with the seizing of One Mighty, Omnipotent.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Kullihā: The Totality That Makes This Case Different**

*Kadhdhabū bi-āyātinā kullihā.* The four previous denials in the sūrah were stated without qualification. Pharaoh's house is described as denying *all* the signs, and the emphasis is carried by *kullihā*, "all of them," in the accusative of totality attached to the pronoun.

The word is necessary because Pharaoh's case is the only one in the Qur'an in which denial persisted through escalating demonstration. The signs came in sequence, each more intrusive than the last, and each was denied. Sūrah al-Aʿrāf records the cycle with painful precision:

> *"So We sent upon them the flood, the locusts, the lice, the frogs, and the blood — signs, distinct. But they waxed arrogant and were a guilty people. And when the punishment fell upon them, they said: O Moses, call upon thy Lord for us, by the covenant He has made with thee. If thou removest the punishment from us, we shall surely believe thee and send the Children of Israel away with thee. And when We removed the punishment from them unto a term they were to reach, behold — they broke their promise."* (Qur'an 7:133–135)

The sequence repeats five times. Plague arrives, promise is made, plague is lifted, promise is broken. The Qur'an is documenting something rare: a party that has been given repeated empirical demonstration and has repeatedly revised its interpretation of the demonstration after the fact.

This is why the totality matters. A single denial could be explained by insufficient evidence. Two could be explained by bad timing. Nine, each accompanied by a promise and followed by a breach, establishes the thing the sūrah has been arguing since verse 3: the problem was never evidential. It was the *hawā*.

**Fa-Akhadhnāhum: The Verb of Seizure**

*Fa-akhadhnāhum akhdha ʿazīzin muqtadir.* The verb *akhadha*, to take hold of, to seize, to arrest, is the Qur'an's standard term for divine punishment, and its force lies in its physicality. God does not "destroy" here; He takes hold. The Qur'an uses the noun form to describe His action as a class:

> *"Thus is the assault of thy Lord when He assaults the towns while they are wrongdoing. Truly His assault is painful, severe (inna akhdhahu alīmun shadīd)."* (Qur'an 11:102)

> *"Truly the assault of thy Lord is severe (inna baṭsha rabbika la-shadīd)."* (Qur'an 85:12)

> *"And We seized them with the seizure of the Mighty, the Powerful (bi-akhdhi ʿazīzin muqtadir)."* (Qur'an 54:42)

The construction of verse 42 is grammatically notable: the verbal noun *akhdh* followed by the two adjectives in the genitive, *ʿazīzin muqtadir*. Arabic uses this construction to make the quality of the act derive from the quality of the actor — "a seizing of the kind performed by a Mighty, Omnipotent One." The adjectives do not merely describe; they define the nature of the seizure. Al-Zamakhsharī explains that the phrase means a seizure from which there is no escape, no resistance, and no appeal, because the seizer is characterized by two qualities that remove both possibilities.

**Al-ʿAzīz: The One Who Cannot Be Overcome**

*ʿAzīz* is from *ʿ-z-z*, a root denoting strength, rarity, and preciousness. Something *ʿazīz* is strong, and also scarce, and therefore valuable — the same word means "dear" and "mighty." As a Divine Name, *al-ʿAzīz* is the One who cannot be overcome or diminished, who has no equal, and who is beyond any competitor. The Qur'an pairs it with dozens of other Names, and the pairing indicates what the might is used for: *al-ʿAzīz al-Raḥīm*, *al-ʿAzīz al-Ḥakīm*, *al-ʿAzīz al-Ghafūr*.

In verse 42 it is paired with *muqtadir*, and the pairing is about capacity rather than mercy. Al-ʿAzīz denotes the unassailability of the seizer; *muqtadir* denotes the completeness of His power to effect what He wills.

Pharaoh's own claim was to the same attribute. The Qur'an records his boast:

> *"And Pharaoh proclaimed among his people: O my people, is not the kingdom of Egypt Mine, and these rivers flowing beneath me? Do you not then see? Am I not better than this one who is contemptible and can barely express himself?"* (Qur'an 43:51–52)

*Alaysa lī mulku miṣr* — is not the kingdom of Egypt mine? The verse in al-Qamar answers with the two Names: the kingdom belonged to *al-ʿAzīz*, and the seizure came from *muqtadir*. The comparison Pharaoh invited — *am anā khayrun*, "am I not better?" — is the same comparison verse 43 will put to the Quraysh: *a-kuffārukum khayrun min ulāʾikum*, "are your disbelievers better than those?"

**Muqtadir: The Name That Returns at the End of the Sūrah**

*Muqtadir* is the eighth form active participle of *q-d-r* — the root of *qadar*, measure and decree, and of *qudrah*, power. The eighth form denotes one who possesses complete capacity to bring about what he intends. As a Divine Name it appears in the Qur'an in three places, two of them in this sūrah:

> *"So We seized them with the seizing of One Mighty, Omnipotent (muqtadir)."* (Qur'an 54:42)

> *"Upon a seat of truth, before an Omnipotent King (malikin muqtadir)."* (Qur'an 54:55)

> *"...and God is Omnipotent over all things (wa kāna Allāhu ʿalā kulli shayʾin muqtadirā)."* (Qur'an 18:45)

The inclusio is exact and it is the sūrah's theological spine. The Name *muqtadir* appears at the punishment of Pharaoh's house in verse 42 and at the seat of the reverent in verse 55. The same omnipotence drowns a tyrant and seats a believer before a King. Nothing intervenes between the two except the choice each party made about the signs that came to them, and the sūrah's structure says that both outcomes are produced by one identical attribute of God.

This is also the word that links verse 42 to verse 49, the sūrah's doctrinal centre: *inna kulla shayʾin khalaqnāhu bi-qadar* — "Truly We have created everything according to a measure." *Muqtadir* and *qadar* are the same root. The One who seized Pharaoh with the seizing of a *muqtadir* is the One who created everything by *qadar* — and the seizure was itself measured, as verse 12 had already said of the flood: *ʿalā amrin qad qudir*, "upon a matter already measured."

**The Drowning, and the Refusal of the Last-Minute Belief**

The sūrah does not describe the manner of Pharaoh's destruction, but the Qur'an describes it elsewhere with the witnesses named:

> *"And when We divided the sea for you and delivered you, and drowned the people of Pharaoh while you were looking on (wa-antum tanẓurūn)."* (Qur'an 2:50)

> *"So We inspired Moses: Strike the sea with thy staff. And it parted, and each part was like a mighty mountain. And We brought the others near. And We delivered Moses and those with him, all of them. Then We drowned the others."* (Qur'an 26:63–66)

*Wa-antum tanẓurūn* — while you looked on. The deliverance was witnessed by the delivered, and the drowning was witnessed too. There was no ambiguity about what had happened to whom.

The Qur'an then records Pharaoh's final statement and its rejection, in a passage that clarifies what verse 42's totality means:

> *"And We brought the Children of Israel across the sea, and Pharaoh and his hosts pursued them in insolence and enmity, until, when drowning overtook him, he said: I believe that there is no god save Him in whom the Children of Israel believe, and I am among those who surrender. Now? And thou hadst disobeyed before, and wast of the corrupters? So this day We shall deliver thee in thy body, that thou mayest be a sign unto those after thee. And truly many among mankind are heedless of Our signs."* (Qur'an 10:90–92)

*Ālʾāna* — "Now?" The single word is the entire doctrine of the verse. Belief offered at the point where the alternative has become unavoidable is not belief; it is recognition, and recognition after the closing of the door is not accepted. The Qur'an states the general rule:

> *"But their belief could not avail them when they saw Our punishment — the way of God that has passed among His servants."* (Qur'an 40:85)

And God preserved Pharaoh's body as a continuing sign — *li-takūna liman khalfaka āyah* — which the commentators have understood as the physical preservation of his remains.

**Application: The Party That Denied Everything**

Verse 42 describes the terminal case in the sūrah's escalating catalogue. Noah's people denied a messenger for centuries. ʿĀd denied on the strength of their power. Thamūd denied on the strength of their security. Lot's people denied and disputed. Pharaoh's house denied nine signs, each of which they had asked to have removed, and each of which they had promised to believe.

The verse's application is to the person or institution that has been shown enough. There is a point in every dispute at which additional evidence ceases to function, and the sūrah identifies the point: it is reached when the receiver begins producing explanations after the fact rather than before. Pharaoh's court did not predict the plagues, did not explain them, and did not revise its position when they lifted — it made promises contingent on relief and broke them on relief. That pattern is the diagnostic of a closed will.

The two Names at the end of the verse are then the answer to the feeling such a situation produces in the one who has been warning. The warner of a Pharaoh eventually concludes that he has no leverage, and the conclusion is factually correct. The verse's response is not to supply leverage but to name the seizer: *ʿazīz*, unassailable; *muqtadir*, wholly able. Moses's staff divided a sea. The one who divides seas does not require the assistance of the man holding the staff, and He will not be deflected by the denial of the man watching it happen.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:43

> **Are your disbelievers better than those? Or have you some exemption in the scriptures?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah Turns to Face Its Audience**

*A-kuffārukum khayrun min ulāʾikum, am lakum barāʾatun fī al-zubur.* Forty-two verses of history end, and the sūrah addresses the room. The pronoun is second person — *kuffārukum*, your disbelievers — and it is addressed either to the Prophet ﷺ concerning his people or, through him, to the Quraysh themselves. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī record both readings; Ibn Kathīr prefers the direct address to the Quraysh.

The shift in grammatical person is the sūrah's most powerful single move. Everything before it was in the third person: they denied, they said, We sent, We seized. Now the reader is inside the sentence. The five files have been read aloud, and the clerk turns to the defendant.

The two questions are alternatives joined by *am*, "or," and each attacks a different possible defense. The first asks about relative merit: are you better than them? The second asks about documentary immunity: do you have an exemption in writing? Together they close both exits — the claim of superiority and the claim of special dispensation.

**A-Kuffārukum Khayrun Min Ulāʾikum: The Comparison Refused**

Al-Zamakhsharī notes that *khayr* here can mean better generally or better in strength, and the second reading connects the verse directly to ʿĀd's boast at 41:15 — *"who is greater than us in strength?"* The question puts the Quraysh in the same category and asks whether their answer would be different.

The comparison is unfavorable on every measurable axis, and the Qur'an makes the axes explicit elsewhere:

> *"Have they not traveled in the land and seen how was the end of those before them? They were more than them in strength, and they tilled the earth and built it up more than they have built it up, and their messengers came unto them with clear signs. And God would not wrong them, but they wronged themselves."* (Qur'an 30:9)

> *"And how many a generation have We destroyed before them, better in furnishings and outward show (aḥsanu athāthan wa-riʾyā)?"* (Qur'an 19:74)

> *"And they said: We have more wealth and children, and we will not be punished."* (Qur'an 34:35)

The last of these identifies the reasoning the question is attacking: material advantage taken as evidence of divine favor. The Qur'an's answer is uniform — the previous peoples had more, and it did not help them. *Ulāʾikum*, "those," refers to the five just named: Noah's people, ʿĀd, Thamūd, Lot's people, Pharaoh's house. Each was stronger, richer, or more established than the Quraysh, and the Quraysh are being asked to explain what makes their case different.

**Am Lakum Barāʾatun Fī Al-Zubur: The Demand for the Document**

*Barāʾah* is from *b-r-ʾ*, to be free of, to be quit of, to be acquitted. A *barāʾah* is a writ of discharge, a certificate of immunity, a document releasing someone from an obligation or a liability. Al-Shawkānī and al-Ṭabarī explain the verse as asking whether they possess something in the previous scriptures that would excuse their rejection of Muhammad ﷺ.

*Al-zubur* is the plural of *zabūr*, a scripture or written book — the same word used for the Psalms given to David: *"And We gave David a Zabūr"* (4:163; 17:55). The definite plural here means the revealed books generally: the Torah, the Psalms, the Gospel.

The question is therefore a challenge to produce a document. It is one of several such challenges in the Qur'an, and they form a consistent pattern:

> *"Or do you have a book wherein you learn that you shall have therein whatsoever you choose? Or do you have oaths binding upon Us until the Day of Resurrection, that you shall have whatever you ordain? Ask them which of them is a guarantor of that."* (Qur'an 68:37–39 — cf. 68:40)

> *"Or have they a ladder whereon they listen? Then let their listener bring a clear authority."* (Qur'an 52:38)

> *"Or have We sent down unto them an authority, so it speaks of what they associate?"* (Qur'an 30:35)

> *"Say: Bring your book, if you are truthful (fa-tū bi-kitābikum in kuntum ṣādiqīn)."* (Qur'an 37:157)

> *"Or have they taken gods besides Him? Say: Bring your proof (fa-tū bi-burhānikum). This is the Reminder of whosoever is with me and the Reminder of whosoever was before me."* (Qur'an 21:24)

The demand is always for evidence in the possession of the claimant, and it is never met. Al-Rāzī notes the epistemological point: the Qur'an repeatedly shifts the burden of proof onto those asserting an exemption, and the assertion of exemption is the one claim they cannot support, because an exemption from divine law would have to be granted by divine law.

**The Prior Claim of Special Relationship**

There is a specific historical target for the second question, and the Qur'an names it. Both the Quraysh and the People of the Book claimed a privileged relationship with God that they believed placed them outside the ordinary rules:

> *"And the Jews and the Christians say: We are the sons of God and His beloved. Say: Why then does He punish you for your sins? Nay, but you are human beings among those He has created. He forgives whomsoever He will and punishes whomsoever He will."* (Qur'an 5:18)

The answer to the claim of belovedness is immediate and empirical: if you were exempt, you would not be punished. The fact of punishment is the refutation of the claim of exemption. Verse 43 uses the same logic in a different form: if the Quraysh had an exemption in the scriptures, the five destroyed nations would have had one too, since the scriptures do not grant exemptions to rejecters of messengers.

The Quraysh's own version of the claim rested on their custodianship of the Kaʿbah:

> *"And they say: If we follow the guidance with thee, we shall be snatched from our land. Have We not established for them a secure sanctuary, unto which is brought the fruits of all things, as a provision from Our presence?"* (Qur'an 28:57)

They believed Makkah's status as the sacred city guaranteed its inhabitants. The Qur'an answers by pointing out that the provision comes from God and can be withdrawn, and by the sequence of verse 45 — the host shall be routed.

**The Two Questions as a Single Argument**

The two halves of the verse are related as a syllogism, and its form is worth stating.

Premise one, established by verses 9–42: five nations were warned, denied, and were destroyed. The rule is universal and God's way does not change (17:77; 33:62; 35:43).

Question one: are you better than them? If yes, the rule may not apply. If no, it applies.

Question two: do you have a written exemption? If yes, produce it. If no, the rule applies.

Neither question can be answered affirmatively, and the argument closes. Al-Bayḍāwī summarizes the verse as an argument from the equality of cause: the cause of destruction was denial of the signs after warning, and the cause is present in the audience, so the effect follows unless a differentiating factor is produced. The two questions are the demand for a differentiating factor.

The argument's structure is not specifically religious, which is why it remains forceful. Every institution that fails does so while believing itself exempt, and the belief always takes one of two forms: we are better than those who failed, or we have an arrangement that protects us. Verse 43 asks both questions in one verse and leaves them unanswered, which is the Qur'an's way of saying that no answer exists.

**Application: The Two Defenses Everyone Uses**

The verse is a diagnostic instrument. Whenever a person or an organization faces evidence that a course of action leads to destruction, the defenses available are exhaustively two.

The first is comparative superiority: we are smarter, better resourced, more disciplined, more advanced, differently situated, in a different era, with different tools. This is *a-kuffārukum khayrun min ulāʾikum*. The Qur'an's response is to point at the five cases and note that each of them said something equivalent, and that ʿĀd said it most confidently: *man ashaddu minnā quwwah*.

The second is documentary immunity: we have a contract, a charter, a covenant, a promise, a status, a right, a membership, a lineage, a profession of belief, a set of papers. This is *am lakum barāʾatun fī al-zubur*. The Qur'an's response is to demand the document and to note that the only exemption the scriptures contain is for those who believe and do righteous works — an exemption that is earned continuously rather than held permanently.

The verse's power lies in asking both questions together. Most people can answer one of them in their own minds and thereby dismiss the evidence: they feel not especially superior but believe they hold an immunity (a faith, a family, a nationality); or they claim no immunity but believe themselves unusually capable. Asked jointly, the two questions produce the correct result — silence.

That silence is the verse's goal. It is not an argument to be won but a position to be recognized. The Qur'an does not supply the audience's answer because the audience has none, and the reader who has followed forty-two verses of case law arrives at verse 43 already knowing what the answer is. The five destroyed nations had no superiority and no exemption. The question is whether the sixth does.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:44

> **Or do they say: We are a host victorious!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Jamiʿun Muntaṣir: The Slogan of the Confident Group**

*Am hum jamiʿun muntaṣir.* The verse quotes the Quraysh's own confidence and puts it in the third person, as a thing overheard: "or are they a gathered host that will be victorious?" The two words carry the whole claim.

*Jamiʿ* is from *j-m-ʿ*, to gather, to collect, to assemble. It denotes a group that has been brought together — a host, an assembly, a coalition. The word implies both numbers and cohesion: not merely many people, but many people acting as one. *Muntaṣir* is the eighth form active participle of *n-ṣ-r*, the root of help and victory; the eighth form denotes one who seeks or obtains victory for himself, or who avenges himself. *Jamiʿun muntaṣir* is therefore "an assembly that gets itself the win" — a group that needs no outside help and expects no outside interference.

The phrase is a slogan, and the Qur'an quotes it as one. Its function in the Makkan dispute is precisely what verse 2's *siḥrun mustamirr* functioned as: a short, repeatable statement that resolves the question without examining it. The Quraysh did not need to refute the Prophet's ﷺ arguments if their own assembly was sufficient. Numbers settle matters, and they had the numbers.

**The Boast in the Qur'an's Catalogue of Boasts**

The Qur'an collects this kind of statement across history and displays them together, which is its way of showing that they are one statement made by many mouths:

> *"As for ʿĀd, they waxed arrogant upon the earth without right, and said: Who is greater than us in strength?"* (Qur'an 41:15)

> *"And the disbelievers said to those who believe: Which of the two parties is better in position and fairer in assembly (aḥsanu nadiyyā)?"* (Qur'an 19:73)

The question is the Quraysh's own, quoted by the Qur'an: which party has the better seats and the better gathering-place? God's answer comes in the very next verse — how many a generation better in goods and outward show than these We destroyed (19:74).

> *"He said: I have been given it only by knowledge I have. Did he not know that God had destroyed before him generations stronger than he in might and greater in accumulation?"* (Qur'an 28:78)

> *"Did they not travel in the land and see how was the end of those before them? They were more numerous than them, and stronger in might, and their traces in the land. But what they used to earn availed them not."* (Qur'an 40:82)

> *"Is it they who apportion the mercy of thy Lord?"* (Qur'an 43:32)

The last of these belongs to the same argument as verse 43, and 40:82 supplies the refutation in a single clause: *fa-mā aghnā ʿanhum mā kānū yaksibūn* — "what they used to earn availed them not." The same verb the sūrah used at verse 5 — *tughnī*, to avail, to suffice — returns here in the past tense as a report: it did not avail. The accumulation, the might, the numbers, and the traces in the land did not function as protection, because protection was never what they were.

**The Fallacy of the Host**

The logical error in *jamiʿun muntaṣir* is worth naming, because it is the most common error in collective reasoning. A group's confidence is generated by aggregation: each member observes the others' confidence and takes it as evidence, and the total confidence rises without any new information entering the system. Ten people who each believe a thing because the other nine believe it have produced ten times the apparent support for a proposition that has none.

The Qur'an identifies this mechanism directly and forbids the inference:

> *"And if thou obeyest most of those upon the earth, they will lead thee astray from the way of God. They follow naught but conjecture, and they do nothing but lie."* (Qur'an 6:116)

> *"And most men will not believe, however much thou desirest it."* (Qur'an 12:103)

> *"And thou wilt not find most of them grateful."* (Qur'an 7:17)

Majorities are not evidence in the Qur'an's epistemology, and the verse in al-Anʿām states the reason: the majority follows *ẓann*, conjecture. A conjecture held by many remains a conjecture, and its multiplication adds confidence without adding warrant.

The counter-principle is stated twice in two short verses, and the two verses are the Qur'an's answer to every slogan of the confident host:

> *"If God helps you, none can overcome you. And if He forsakes you, who is there that can help you after Him? And upon God let the believers rely."* (Qur'an 3:160)

> *"How many a small company has overcome a large company by the leave of God. And God is with the patient."* (Qur'an 2:249)

*In yanṣurkumu Allāh fa-lā ghāliba lakum* — if God helps you, there is no one who can overcome you. The verse removes the possibility of a *jamiʿun muntaṣir* in the sense the Quraysh intended: no assembly is self-victorious, because victory is not a property of assemblies. It is a property of the help that arrives.

**The Two Hosts That Met**

The Qur'an describes the actual encounter between the confident host and the small one, and the description is the fulfillment of verse 44:

> *"There was a sign for you in two hosts that met — one host fighting in the way of God, and the other disbelieving, seeing them as twice their number with their own eyes. And God supports with His help whomsoever He will. Truly in that is a lesson for those of vision."* (Qur'an 3:13)

> *"And when God showed them unto thee in thy dream as few; and had He shown them as many, you would have lost heart and disputed over the matter. But God delivered you. Truly He is Knower of what is in the breasts. And when He showed them unto you, when you met, as few in your eyes, and He made you appear few in their eyes — that God might accomplish a matter already decreed. And unto God are all matters returned."* (Qur'an 8:43–44)

The second passage is a study in the psychology of verse 44 from both sides. The Muslims saw the enemy as few, which prevented panic; the enemy saw the Muslims as few, which produced exactly the confidence the verse quotes. *Jamiʿun muntaṣir* is what the Quraysh concluded from looking at the numbers, and the Qur'an reports that God arranged the perception so that the conclusion would be reached — *li-yaqḍiya Allāhu amran kāna mafʿūlā*, "that God might accomplish a matter already decreed." The boast was part of the decree; the defeat was its answer.

**The Defeated Army in the Same Vocabulary**

Sūrah Ṣād uses almost the same construction to describe the boast and its end in a single sentence:

> *"They are but a host, there, routed (jundun mā hunālika mahzūm). Before them the people of Noah denied, and ʿĀd, and Pharaoh of the stakes, and Thamūd, and the people of Lot, and the companions of the thicket. Those are the parties (ulāʾika al-aḥzāb)."* (Qur'an 38:11–13)
