*Jundun mā hunālika mahzūm* — a mere army, there, defeated. The structure is identical to verse 44's *jamiʿun muntaṣir*, and the adjective is reversed: confident and victorious in their own estimation, routed in fact. The Qur'an's method is to take the boast's grammar and substitute its outcome.

Verse 45 does the same operation with the very next word.

**Application: The Confidence of the Many**

Verse 44 is a question about every confident collective. The collective may be a nation ("who can compete with our economy"), a political party ("we have the numbers"), a corporation ("we have the market"), a denomination ("we have the tradition"), or a family ("we have always been this way"). In each case the confidence rests on aggregation, and the aggregation is taken as immunity.

The Qur'an's treatment of this confidence is consistent and unsentimental. It does not deny the numbers. ʿĀd were stronger; the generations before were more numerous; the previous peoples had greater traces in the land. The Qur'an grants all of it and then asks the question of verse 43: what did it avail? The answer, in 40:82, is that it did not avail at all.

The practical instruction is to separate two things that aggregation fuses together: the capacity of a group and its legitimacy. A large group has more capacity — that is simply true, and the Qur'an acknowledges it repeatedly. Capacity is not a warrant, and a group that treats its size as evidence of its correctness has made the exact error of verse 44. The error is visible from outside in every case, and invisible from inside in every case, which is why the sūrah asks the question in the third person, as something overheard: *am hum jamiʿun muntaṣir* — "or are they saying: we are a host victorious?" The question is about them, and it is asked within hearing.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:45

> **The host shall be routed, and they will turn their backs.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Boast's Own Word, Returned With Its Opposite**

*Sa-yuhzamu al-jamʿu wa-yuwallūna al-dubur.* The verse takes the noun from verse 44 — *jamʿ*, host, assembly — and makes it the subject of a passive verb of defeat. They said *we are a jamʿ victorious*; the answer is *the jamʿ shall be routed*. The Qur'an does not argue with the boast; it accepts its own noun and reverses its predicate.

*Sa-yuhzamu* — the *sa* of the near future plus the passive of *h-z-m*, to defeat, to rout, to break an army. The passive is significant: the host will be routed by an agent not named. The Qur'an does not say by whom, because the answer is given in the theology rather than in the sentence: *"And you threw not when you threw, but God threw"* (8:17); *"If God helps you, none can overcome you"* (3:160). The routing happens to them; the doing belongs elsewhere.

*Wa-yuwallūna al-dubur* — "and they will turn the back." The idiom uses the singular *dubur* with a plural verb, which Arabic allows for collective nouns and which the commentators note as intensifying the image: not each soldier turning his own back in an orderly withdrawal, but the whole body turning as one mass in the single direction of flight. *Tawliyat al-dubur* is the standard Arabic expression for rout, and it is the exact negation of *muntṣir*, victorious.

**The Announcement Made When It Could Not Be Believed**

The verse was revealed in Makkah, at a moment when its content was, by every available measure, absurd. The Muslims were a small band of refugees who had just been expelled from their own city. They had no army, no territory, no cavalry, no supply lines, and no state. The Quraysh had expelled them, confiscated their property, and were preparing to finish them. The two sides had not yet met in battle, and in every previous encounter the Quraysh had been the stronger party.

Against that background the Qur'an announces, in the near-future tense, that the host will be routed and will turn its back. This is not a general statement about the eventual triumph of truth — though the commentators also read it that way, citing:

> *"Say: Truth has come, and falsehood has vanished. Truly falsehood is ever vanishing."* (Qur'an 17:81)

> *"And We hurl the truth upon falsehood, and it destroys its brain, and behold — it vanishes."* (Qur'an 21:18)

It is a specific prediction about a specific assembly, made in advance, by a party with no capacity to produce it. Al-Bayḍāwī and al-Shawkānī both note that the verse refers in its immediate sense to the disbelievers of Quraysh, and both record the fulfillment.

**Badr: The Verse Recited in the Tent**

The tradition records the fulfillment with unusual precision, and the report is in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī:

> *Ibn ʿAbbās reported: The Prophet ﷺ said while he was in his tent on the day of Badr: "O God, I ask You for Your pledge and Your promise. O God, if You will, You will not be worshipped upon the earth after today." Abū Bakr took him by the hand and said: "O Messenger of God, you have pressed your Lord enough." Then he came out, saying: "The host shall be routed, and they will turn their backs. Nay! The Hour is their tryst, and the Hour is more calamitous and more bitter."* (al-Bukhārī 4877)

The details of the report are worth holding together. The Prophet ﷺ was in his tent, in supplication, with his cloak falling from his shoulders, and Abū Bakr — the closest of the Companions — intervened to tell him he had asked enough. Then he came out reciting verse 45. The supplication and the recitation are the two halves of the same event: the request made privately, and the announcement made publicly.

The tradition also preserves ʿUmar's testimony about the verse:

> *"I did not know what 'the host' meant until the day of Badr."* (reported in the exegetical collections of al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Abī Ḥātim, from ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb)

ʿUmar heard the verse in Makkah and did not know which host it referred to. The verse was not given an interpretation at the time of its revelation; it was given an event, and the event arrived at Badr.

**The Numbers at Badr, and Why They Matter**

The Muslim force at Badr numbered approximately three hundred and thirteen, with two horses and seventy camels shared among them. The Quraysh force numbered close to a thousand, with cavalry, armor, and a full supply train. The Muslims had marched out expecting to intercept a caravan, not to fight an army, and many of them — particularly the Ansār, who had pledged defense within Madinah only — had not consented to a pitched battle.

The Qur'an describes the encounter from both perspectives, and the description explains verse 44's boast:

> *"And when God showed them unto you, when you met, as few in your eyes, and He made you appear few in their eyes."* (Qur'an 8:44)

The Quraysh saw a small, poorly equipped band and concluded, correctly on the evidence, that they were a *jamiʿun muntaṣir*. The Muslims saw a large army and were kept from panic by seeing it as few. Both perceptions were arranged, the verse says, so that a matter already decreed would be accomplished.

The outcome: seventy of the Quraysh killed, including most of their chiefs — Abū Jahl, Umayyah ibn Khalaf, ʿUtbah ibn Rabīʿah, Shaybah ibn Rabīʿah, and al-Walīd ibn ʿUtbah — and seventy taken prisoner. Eight Muslims were killed. The verse's *yuwallūna al-dubur* was enacted within hours of its recitation.

**Prophecy as a Category of Qur'anic Evidence**

Verse 45 belongs to a class of Qur'anic statements that can be verified by their original hearers within their own lifetimes, and the class is deliberate:

> *"The Romans have been defeated in the nearest land, and they, after their defeat, will prevail — within three to nine years. God's is the Command before and after; and on that day the believers will rejoice."* (Qur'an 30:2–4)

> *"God has promised those among you who believe and do righteous works that He will surely make them successors in the earth, as He made successors those before them, and will surely establish for them their religion which He has approved for them, and will surely give them in exchange security after their fear."* (Qur'an 24:55)

> *"Truly God has fulfilled His Messenger's vision in truth: you shall surely enter the Sacred Mosque, if God wills, in security, with your heads shaved and your hair shortened, not fearing."* (Qur'an 48:27)

Each of these was fulfilled within the Prophet's ﷺ lifetime, in public, before witnesses who had heard the announcement. Verse 45 is one of them, and it is the earliest. Its function in the argument of the sūrah is to establish that the One who reports the five destroyed nations also reports the future correctly, and that the report of verse 46 — the Hour as their tryst — rests on the same authority that correctly reported Badr.

**Application: The Announcement Made From Weakness**

Verse 45 has a use that is not primarily evidential. It describes a person announcing an outcome he has no capacity to produce, at the moment of his greatest weakness, in the hearing of the party that will be defeated by it.

That is the structure of every act of faith that matters. Faith is not the conviction that things will work out; it is the willingness to state a future that the present contradicts, and to state it before the contradiction is resolved. The Prophet ﷺ did not wait for Badr to recite verse 45; the verse was revealed in Makkah and recited in the tent at Badr before the fighting. The recitation was itself part of the battle.

The practical counsel for a person in a weak position is therefore not to wait for evidence before speaking. There is a discipline in declaring, on the basis of a promise, what one believes will happen — to oneself, to one's household, to one's community — and the discipline produces two effects. It fixes the expectation, so that the person cannot quietly revise it later when the outcome is inconvenient. And it commits the speaker, since a public statement about the future is a thing one must live with whether or not it comes true. The Prophet ﷺ recited verse 45 out loud, in front of an army that had not yet fought, and if it had not come true he would have been remembered as the man who announced a rout while being routed.

He was not, and the sūrah's next verse explains why: the routing at Badr was not the point. The Hour is the tryst, and Badr was the first installment of an appointment that has not yet been kept.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:46

> **Nay! The Hour is their tryst, and the Hour is more calamitous and more bitter.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Bal: The Correction That Relocates the Event**

*Bal al-sāʿatu mawʿiduhum.* The particle *bal* cancels and substitutes. Verse 45 announced a rout in this world; verse 46 says: no — rather, the Hour is their appointment. The correction is not a retraction. The rout happened, at Badr, exactly as announced. What *bal* corrects is the reader's inference that the rout was the settlement.

Al-Bayḍāwī and al-Shawkānī explain the relationship precisely: verse 45 refers to defeat in this world, and verse 46 refers to punishment in the next, which is more calamitous and more bitter than the destruction and humiliation experienced in this life. The two verses are not alternatives but installments, and the second verse exists to prevent the first from being taken as the whole account.

This is a persistent feature of the Qur'an's treatment of worldly punishment. It reports the destructions, and it reports the victories, and then it consistently declines to let either be final:

> *"And let not those who disbelieve reckon that Our giving them respite is better for their souls. We give them respite only that they may increase in sin, and theirs is a humiliating punishment."* (Qur'an 3:178)

> *"Think not those who rejoice in what they have done, and love to be praised for what they have not done — think not that they are in a place of safety from the punishment. And theirs is a painful punishment."* (Qur'an 3:188)

> *"For them is punishment in the life of this world, and the punishment of the Hereafter is harder (ashaqq), and they have no protector from God."* (Qur'an 13:34)

The last of these states the proportion verse 46 asserts: *ashaqq* — harder, more grievous. The worldly installment is real and it is not the larger one.

**Mawʿid: The Appointment Both Parties Keep**

*Mawʿid* is from *w-ʿ-d*, to promise, to appoint a time or place of meeting. A *mawʿid* is a tryst, a rendezvous, an appointment — and the word carries the sense of a mutual commitment that both parties are expected to honor. Its use for the Hour is one of the Qur'an's most economical images: the Hour is not an accident, an interruption, or a catastrophe that befalls people. It is a meeting they have been scheduled for, and they will attend.

The Qur'an uses the same vocabulary repeatedly and insists on its reliability:

> *"And for everyone there are degrees from what they have done, and that He may repay them for their deeds; and they will not be wronged."* — and: *"They have an appointed time (mawʿid) beyond which they will find no refuge (lan yajidū min dūnihā mawʾilā)."* (Qur'an 18:58)

> *"Our Lord! Truly Thou wilt gather mankind unto a Day wherein is no doubt. Truly God does not fail the appointment (inna Allāha lā yukhlifu al-mīʿād)."* (Qur'an 3:9)

> *"So do not think God will fail His promise to His messengers. Truly God is Mighty, the Owner of Retribution."* (Qur'an 14:47)

*Mīʿād* in 3:9 is the same root as *mawʿid*, and the statement *inna Allāha lā yukhlifu al-mīʿād* — God does not fail the appointment — is the verse's guarantee. An appointment can be missed by either party; this one cannot be missed by the One who set it, and the other party has no capacity to decline.

The image of a tryst also carries the Qur'an's insistence that the meeting is personal. It is *mawʿiduhum* — their appointment, in the plural but attached to them specifically. Not a general cosmic event that happens to include them, but a meeting arranged for them. Sūrah al-Ḥijr uses the same construction for the disbelievers' request that the punishment be hastened, and God's answer:

> *"Say: Yours is an appointed Day (lakum mīʿādu yawm) which you cannot delay by an hour, nor advance."* (Qur'an 34:30)

*Lā tastaʾkhirūna sāʿatan wa-lā tastaqdimūn* — you cannot postpone it by an hour nor advance it. The appointment is fixed to the minute, and neither party can adjust it.

**Adhā Wa-Ammar: The Root That Began With Sorcery**

*Wa-l-sāʿatu adhā wa-amarr* — "and the Hour is more calamitous and more bitter." Two comparatives, and the second is the sūrah's most striking verbal return.

*Ammar* is the elative of *murr*, bitter, from the root *m-r-r*. That is the root of *mustamirr*, the word the Quraysh used at verse 2 — *siḥrun mustamirr*, "incessant sorcery," or on the second reading, "powerful sorcery" — and the word God used at verse 19 of the day of ʿĀd's destruction: *yawmi naḥsin mustamirr*, "a day of incessant misfortune."

The root now returns in its comparative form as the final description of the Hour: *amarr*, more bitter. The arc across the sūrah is complete. They called the sign bitter sorcery; the punishment of ʿĀd came on a day of bitter persistence; and the Hour itself is more bitter than either. The word they chose to dismiss a miracle becomes the word that describes their appointment.

*Adhā* is from *ʾ-dh-y*, harm, injury, annoyance — a word the Qur'an uses for both minor and severe affliction, and the comparative *adhā* means more harmful, more grievous. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that the pairing of the two comparatives covers both dimensions of suffering: harm to the person (*adhā*) and bitterness in the experience (*amarr*). A thing can be harmful without being bitter — a painful surgery — and bitter without being greatly harmful — a humiliation. The Hour is described as both at once and as more so than anything preceding it.

**The Tryst That Cannot Be Avoided by Success**

There is a further function in the verse's placement that deserves attention. Verse 45 promised the believers a rout of their enemies, and verse 46 immediately relativizes the rout. The audience of the sūrah included both parties — the Muslims who wanted victory and the Quraysh who feared it — and the verse addresses both.

To the Quraysh it says: your defeat at Badr will not be the worst thing that happens to you. To the Muslims it says: your victory at Badr will not be the best thing that happens to you. The Hour is the tryst for everyone, and its quality — *adhā wa-amarr* — is specified for the disbelievers, while the sūrah's own conclusion specifies the opposite for the reverent: Gardens, a stream, and a seat of truth before an Omnipotent King (54:54–55).

This balance is why the verse comes where it comes. Without it, verse 45 would read as a promise of political triumph, and the sūrah's argument would collapse into the claim that the winning side is right. With it, the argument is that both sides have an appointment, that the worldly installments are partial, and that the settlement is at the Hour. The Qur'an states the same principle to the believers explicitly:

> *"And fight them until there is no trial, and the religion is all God's. Then if they desist, truly God is Seer of what they do."* (Qur'an 8:39)

And it warns the victorious against the inference that victory settles the account:

> *"Say: Await — truly I am with you among those who await."* (Qur'an 11:122)

**Application: The Person Who Survived the Installment**

Verse 46 addresses a condition common to both parties in a conflict. The one who has suffered a worldly defeat and survived it concludes that the matter is closed — he has been through the worst, he is still standing, and the future belongs to him. The one who has inflicted a worldly defeat concludes the same thing from the other side. Both conclusions are refuted by the same verse, and both refutations use the same word: *mawʿid*, appointment.

The verse's practical teaching is about the incompleteness of every worldly outcome. Nothing that happens in this life is a final verdict, in either direction. A person who has been humiliated publicly, lost financially, defeated politically, or injured physically has experienced an installment; a person who has won in any of those domains has also experienced an installment. The Qur'an's insistence on this is not a consolation prize offered to losers — it is applied symmetrically, and it is applied to the Prophet's ﷺ own victories.

The tryst framing also supplies the discipline. An appointment produces preparation, and preparation for an appointment one cannot move requires attending to the terms on which one will arrive. Verse 46 does not describe what happens at the Hour beyond the two comparatives; it establishes that the Hour is where the matter is decided. Everything before it — Badr, the rout, the turned backs, the five destroyed nations, the split moon — is correspondence about the meeting rather than the meeting itself.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:47

> **Truly the guilty are astray and burning.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sentence They Pronounced on Themselves**

*Inna al-mujrimīna fī ḍalālin wa-suʿur.* Twenty-three verses earlier, the people of Thamūd stated their reason for rejecting Ṣāliḥ:

> *"Shall we follow a single human being from among us? Then we would surely be astray and burning (innā idhan la-fī ḍalālin wa-suʿur)!"* (Qur'an 54:24)

The words are identical. *Fī ḍalālin wa-suʿur* appears in verse 24 as their prediction about themselves if they followed the prophet, and in verse 47 as God's statement about them. The Qur'an does not refute the accusation; it accepts its vocabulary, removes the conditional *idhan*, "in that case," and states it as a fact.

Al-Bayḍāwī and Ibn Kathīr both mark the correspondence, and its force is unusual even by this sūrah's standards. The technique used at verse 26 — returning *al-kadhdhābu al-ashir* as an open question — is here completed without the question. The phrase is simply reassigned. What they said would happen if they obeyed is what happened because they did not.

**Mujrim: The Word of Earned Guilt**

*Al-mujrimīn* is the active participle of *ajrama*, the fourth form of *j-r-m*, a root meaning to cut off, to acquire, and specifically to acquire guilt. The fourth form *ajrama* means to commit a crime, and the participle denotes one who has done so as a settled characteristic.

The root's basic sense of acquisition matters. Arabic uses the same root for earning generally — *jarama* and *ijtaraba* both carry the sense of gaining — and the Qur'an's *mujrim* is therefore not a person who happened to be caught in a bad situation but a person who has acquired guilt, who has earned it. The Qur'an states the principle with the same root:

> *"Nay — whosoever earns evil (man kasaba sayyiʾatan) and his sin encompasses him, those are the companions of the Fire, abiding therein."* (Qur'an 2:81)

> *"And whosoever earns a sin earns it only against his own soul. And God is Knowing, Wise."* (Qur'an 4:111)

And it draws the sharpest possible contrast between the *mujrim* and the believer:

> *"Shall We then make the Muslims like the criminals (a-fa-najʿalu al-muslimīna ka-l-mujrimīn)? What ails you — how do you judge?"* (Qur'an 68:35–36)

The question in al-Qalam is rhetorical and its target is the assumption that outcomes are undifferentiated. Verse 47 is the answer's first half: the criminals are in a specific state, and the state is described with two nouns.

**Fī Ḍalālin: The Preposition of Containment**

The phrase uses *fī*, "in" — not *ʿalā*, upon, nor *maʿa*, with, nor the bare accusative. *Fī ḍalālin wa-suʿur* means they are inside error and inside burning, enclosed by both. Arabic's preposition of containment denotes a state one occupies rather than a quality one has.

Al-Zamakhsharī notes the force of the construction: the *mujrim* is not described as someone who errs but as someone located within error, the way a person is located within a building. The image is of a condition with walls. This is the Qur'an's consistent description of persistent rejection — a covering on the heart (2:7), a barrier before and behind (36:9), a seal (45:23) — all spatial metaphors of enclosure.

*Ḍalāl* is from *ḍ-l-l*, to be lost, to stray from the road. The Qur'an distinguishes between *ḍalāl* as error in belief and *ḍalāl* as loss of direction, and the two are related as cause and effect: the person who believes wrongly about the destination walks in the wrong direction. Verse 47's use of *ḍalāl* alongside *suʿur* therefore describes both the cognitive and the experiential state: they are lost, and they are burning.

**Suʿur: The Blaze and the Madness**

*Suʿur* is from *s-ʿ-r*, and the root carries two connected meanings: blazing fire, and the agitated, frenzied state produced by intense heat. From the same root comes *saʿīr*, the Blaze, one of the Qur'an's names for Hell:

> *"And We have prepared for the disbelievers chains, collars, and a Blaze (saʿīrā)."* (Qur'an 76:4)

> *"Truly God has cursed the disbelievers and prepared for them a Blaze (saʿīrā), abiding therein forever."* (Qur'an 33:64–65)

Al-Bayḍāwī and al-Zamakhsharī record the interpretation that verse 47 means the disbelievers are "burning (*yasʿurūn*) in the Fire," reading *suʿur* as a description of their state in the Hereafter. Others read it as madness or frenzy in this world. Al-Zamakhsharī's synthesis is that they are astray in this world and in the blaze in the next — the two nouns covering the two phases.

Both phases are present in the verse's grammar, since the present-tense meaning of the participial construction allows continuity: they are *in* error and burning, now and then. The error is the condition of this life; the burning is its outcome; and the two are named in one breath because they are one thing seen from two points in time.

There is a further resonance the commentators note. *Suʿur* also describes the wild, unrestrained energy of a camel in rut — *jamalun masʿūr* — an animal driven by an appetite it cannot govern. On this reading the word carries the sense of driven, compulsive agitation: the *mujrim* is not merely burning but burning with a restlessness he cannot direct. The same root appears in the Qur'anic description of the disbelievers' inner state:

> *"Nay, they are in doubt, playing."* (Qur'an 44:9)

> *"And when they meet those who believe, they say: We believe; and when they are alone with their devils, they say: Truly we are with you; we were only mocking."* (Qur'an 2:14)

The agitation of verse 47 is the state of a person whose desires have become his direction — which is precisely what verse 3 diagnosed as *ittibaʿ al-hawā*, following caprice.

**The Two Halves of the Sūrah's Moral Anatomy**

Verse 47 and the sūrah's conclusion form a matched pair, and the pairing is structural. The criminals are *fī ḍalālin wa-suʿur* — in error and burning. The reverent are *fī jannātin wa-nahar* — in Gardens and a river (54:54). Both use *fī*, both use two nouns, and the two nouns in each case describe a location and a condition.

The symmetry is exact:

| | Location | Condition |
|---|---|---|
| 54:47 | *ḍalāl* — lost, without direction | *suʿur* — burning, agitated |
| 54:54 | *jannāt* — Gardens, enclosed and shaded | *nahar* — a river, flowing and supplied |

The *mujrim* is lost and hot; the *muttaqī* is enclosed and watered. The contrast between a burning without direction and a garden with a river is the Qur'an's most compact description of the two outcomes, and al-Qamar places them seven verses apart.

**Application: The Words You Use for the Road Not Taken**

Verse 47 preserves a sentence that people say constantly, in one form or another, about every demand the truth makes on them. *If I follow that, I'll be lost. If I give that up, my life will be a misery. If I do what he says, I'll be crazy.* The sentence has a fixed structure: the alternative is described as ruin, and the description is offered as evidence against the alternative.

The Qur'an's handling of it is remarkable. It does not dispute the description. It records it in verse 24, in the speakers' own words, with its emphatic particles intact — *innā idhan la-fī ḍalālin wa-suʿur* — and then, twenty-three verses later, it applies the description to the speakers. The words were accurate; only their referent was wrong.

The mechanism is worth identifying in oneself. When a person describes the cost of obedience in apocalyptic terms, the description is usually an accurate description of disobedience's cost, projected onto the wrong option. Thamūd believed that following Ṣāliḥ would mean error and burning. Following Ṣāliḥ would have meant the water-sharing arrangement of verse 28 and a continuing life. Not following him meant verse 31. The sentence they used to reject the prophet was the sentence that described their fate — and they said it themselves, out loud, in advance.

The practical discipline the verse implies is to examine the descriptions one produces for the roads not taken, and to ask whether they are descriptions of those roads or confessions. Verse 47 suggests that they are often the second.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:48

> **On the Day they are dragged upon their faces into the Fire: "Taste the touch of Saqar."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**ʿAlā Wujūhihim: The Face as the Surface of Contact**

*Yawma yusḥabūna fī al-nāri ʿalā wujūhihim.* The verb *yusḥabūn* is the passive of *s-ḥ-b*, to drag, to pull along a surface — the word used for dragging a body across the ground, for a train pulling its cars, for a garment trailing behind its wearer. The prepositional phrase *ʿalā wujūhihim*, "upon their faces," specifies the surface against which the dragging occurs.

The specification is the punishment. The face is the organ of human dignity — the part by which a person is recognized, the part he presents, the part he protects. To be dragged upon the face is to be moved in the posture of maximum degradation, with the organ of identity in continuous contact with the thing that destroys.

The Qur'an uses this image repeatedly, and the repetition establishes it as a fixed element of its eschatology:

> *"And We shall gather them on the Day of Resurrection upon their faces (ʿalā wujūhihim) — blind, dumb, and deaf. Their refuge is Hell; every time it subsides, We increase for them its blaze."* (Qur'an 17:97)

> *"Those who shall be gathered upon their faces (ʿalā wujūhihim) unto Hell — those are worst in place and most astray in the way."* (Qur'an 25:34)

> *"And whosoever comes with an evil deed — their faces shall be cast down into the Fire (fa-kubbat wujūhuhum fī al-nār): Taste what you used to do!"* (Qur'an 27:90)

> *"The Fire scorches their faces (talfaḥu wujūhahumu al-nār), and they are therein grimacing (kāliḥūn)."* (Qur'an 23:104)

> *"Their garments shall be of pitch, and the Fire shall cover their faces (wa-taghshā wujūhahumu al-nār)."* (Qur'an 14:50)

> *"And when they are turned therein, they will say: O that we were returned, and did not deny the signs of our Lord."* (Qur'an 6:27)

> *"Nay — but what they used to hide has become manifest to them. And were they returned, they would but return to that which they were forbidden. Truly they are liars."* (Qur'an 6:28)

And in the intermediate state, at the moment of death:

> *"And how, when the angels take them, striking their faces and their backs (yaḍribūna wujūhahum wa-adbārahum)!"* (Qur'an 47:27; cf. 8:50)

The concentration on the face across all these verses is not arbitrary, and the tradition explains it.

**The Harvest of the Tongue**

The Prophet ﷺ connected the two organs directly, in a report that al-Qurṭubī and Ibn Kathīr both cite when commenting on this verse:

> *"Are people thrown into the Fire upon their faces — or upon their nostrils — for anything other than the harvest of their tongues (ḥaṣāʾid alsinatihim)?"* (al-Tirmidhī 2616, from Muʿādh ibn Jabal; graded ṣaḥīḥ)

The report is part of a longer counsel in which Muʿādh asked the Prophet ﷺ what would admit him to Paradise and keep him from the Fire, and after listing the pillars and the virtues the Prophet ﷺ said: *"Shall I not inform you of the head of the matter, its pillar, and its peak?"* — and then took hold of his own tongue and said: *"Restrain this."* Muʿādh asked whether they would be held accountable for what they said, and received the answer above.

*Ḥaṣāʾid* is the plural of *ḥaṣīdah*, a harvest — what is reaped. The tongue's speech is a crop, and the crop is gathered. The image implies both a season and an accounting: what is sown in speech is reaped in the face.

The tradition is consistent on this point:

> *"Whoever guarantees for me what is between his jaws and what is between his legs, I guarantee him the Garden."* (al-Bukhārī 6474, from Sahl ibn Saʿd)

> *"A man speaks a word from God's pleasure that he does not consider significant, and God raises him by it degrees. And a man speaks a word from God's displeasure that he does not consider significant, and he is thrown into the Fire for seventy autumns by it."* (al-Bukhārī 6478, from Abū Hurayrah)

The verses of this sūrah supply the case studies. Verse 2's *siḥrun mustamirr* was a spoken word. Verse 24's *innā idhan la-fī ḍalālin wa-suʿur* was a spoken word. Verse 25's *kadhdhābun ashir* was a spoken word. Verse 44's *jamiʿun muntaṣir* was a spoken word. Every speech act in al-Qamar is recorded, quoted verbatim, and answered; and verse 48 describes the destination of the speakers.

**Dhūqū Massa Saqar: The Touch That Is Tasted**

*Dhūqū massa saqar.* The command returns for the third time in the sūrah (37, 39, 48), and here it is given an object more specific than before: not *ʿadhābī wa-nudhur* but *massa saqar*, "the touch of Saqar."

*Mass* is from *m-s-s*, to touch, to make contact — the least mediated form of physical knowledge. The Qur'an uses it for both harm and benefit: *"And We test you with evil and good as a trial"* (21:35); *"affliction touched them"* (2:214). Its use here with *dhūqū* combines two sensory verbs: taste and touch. The person addressed is not being informed; he is being given contact.

*Saqar* is from *s-q-r*, meaning to scorch, and specifically the effect of intense heat on skin — the discoloration, the burning, the alteration of the surface. The Qur'an gives Saqar its own exposition in Sūrah al-Muddaththir:

> *"I shall roast him in Saqar. And what will make thee know what Saqar is? It leaves nothing and spares nothing (lā tubqī wa-lā tadhar), scorcher of the skin (lawwāḥatun li-l-bashar). Over it are nineteen."* (Qur'an 74:26–30)

*Lā tubqī wa-lā tadhar* — it does not leave, it does not spare; the two negatives cover both the preservation of anything intact and the cessation of the process. *Lawwāḥatun li-l-bashar* — it alters and scorches the outer skin, the *bashar*, the visible surface of a human being. The commentators differ on whether *Saqar* is a proper name of Hell or an attribute of it; al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī record both. Either way, the word's etymology ties the punishment to the organ it acts upon: Saqar is that which scorches the surface, and the surface is what verse 48 drags along the ground.

**The Face in Prostration and the Face in the Fire**

There is a contrast the tradition drew between this verse and the Qur'an's description of the believing face, and the contrast is one of the most economical summaries of Islamic practice.

The believer places his face on the ground voluntarily, seven times daily at minimum, in prostration — the posture of maximum physical humility, in which the organ of dignity touches dust by choice. The Qur'an describes the act and its reward:

> *"And the places of prostration belong to God, so invoke not alongside God anyone."* (Qur'an 72:18)

> *"Their mark is upon their faces, from the trace of prostration (sīmāhum fī wujūhihim min athari al-sujūd)."* (Qur'an 48:29)

> *"The closest a servant is to his Lord is when he is in prostration; so make much supplication therein."* (Muslim 482, from Abū Hurayrah)

And on the Day, the faces are described in the same organ and the opposite condition:

> *"Faces that Day shall be radiant, looking upon their Lord."* (Qur'an 75:22–23)

> *"And faces that Day shall be downcast (bāsirah), expecting that a calamity will be done to them (taẓunnu an yufʿala bihā fāqirah)."* (Qur'an 75:24–25)

> *"Faces that Day will be dust-covered (ʿalayhā ghabarah), and darkness will cover them (tarhaquhā qatarah). Those are the disbelievers, the wicked."* (Qur'an 80:40–42)

> *"On the Day when some faces will be whitened and some faces will be blackened..."* (Qur'an 3:106)

The Qur'an's eschatology is written on the face. The face that was voluntarily lowered in prayer is raised and made radiant; the face that was raised in arrogance is lowered and dragged. Verse 48 is the second case, and the practice of *sujūd* is the daily rehearsal of the first.

**Application: The Organ of Presentation**

Verse 48 concentrates the whole of the sūrah's moral argument into one body part. What a person says with his tongue and what he presents with his face are the two things this chapter has tracked: the slogans (*siḥrun mustamirr*, *majnūn*, *kadhdhābun ashir*, *jamiʿun muntaṣir*) and the posture (the humbled eyes of verse 7, the scrambling of verse 8, the dragging of verse 48).

The practical counsel is therefore about both. The tongue's harvest is gathered, which means that speech is not discharge but deposit — every word placed in an account that will be presented. The Prophet ﷺ's instruction on this is short and total: *"Whoever believes in God and the Last Day, let him say good or remain silent"* (al-Bukhārī 6018; Muslim 47). The alternative to good speech in the ḥadīth is not clever speech or effective speech but silence.

The face's posture is a matter of habit rather than of moment. The person who has never lowered his face voluntarily will find it lowered involuntarily, and the lowering will not be worship but humiliation. This is why the tradition treats prostration as the central physical act of Islam — it is the daily, embodied refusal of the condition verse 48 describes, performed by the same organ, in the same direction, for the opposite reason.

And the verse's final imperative is addressed to the reader who is still able to respond to it. *Dhūqū massa Saqar* is spoken by God to people for whom the speaking and the tasting have become the same event. The reader of the verse is in the earlier position: he has been told about the touch, in words, at a distance, with time remaining. Verse 39 established the difference between being told and tasting. Verse 48 describes the tasting. The interval between them is the whole of a life, and it is the only part of the sentence the reader controls.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:49

> **Truly We have created everything according to a measure.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah's Doctrinal Centre**

*Innā kulla shayʾin khalaqnāhu bi-qadar.* Five words in Arabic, and they became one of the most discussed sentences in the history of Islamic theology. The sūrah, which had spent forty-eight verses on signs, histories, and warnings, stops at verse 49 and states a metaphysical principle. The principle is the answer to everything that preceded it: the punishments were measured, the deliverances were measured, the timings were fixed, and the record was written.

The occasion reported for these verses connects them to a live dispute. Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī, and al-Ṭabarī all record that verses 47–49 were revealed in connection with a dispute between the Prophet ﷺ and the idolaters of Quraysh concerning God's *qadar* — His measuring out — referring to the manner in which God gives or withholds provision for all creation. The dispute was therefore not academic. It concerned why some are given and some withheld, why some are destroyed and some delivered, and whether the distribution of outcomes reflects divine justice.

**Qadar: The Root of Measure, Power, and Decree**

*Qadar* is from *q-d-r*, and the root's range is unusually rich. It denotes measuring and apportioning; it denotes power and capacity (*qudrah*, the ability to effect); it denotes determination and decree; and, in an apparently unrelated direction, it denotes a cooking pot (*qidr*), because the pot holds a measured quantity. The same letters therefore produce the ideas of measure, power, and containment — three notions the verse combines.

The Qur'an uses the root constantly and consistently:

> *"And He created everything and measured it with due measure (fa-qaddarahu taqdīrā)."* (Qur'an 25:2)

> *"Who created and proportioned, and who measured and guided (alladhī qaddara fa-hadā)."* (Qur'an 87:2–3)

> *"And there is not a thing but that its stores are with Us, and We send it not down save in a known measure (bi-qadarin maʿlūm)."* (Qur'an 15:21)

> *"God has appointed for everything a measure (qad jaʿala Allāhu li-kulli shayʾin qadrā)."* (Qur'an 65:3)

> *"And everything with Him is by measure (wa-kullu shayʾin ʿindahu bi-miqdār)."* (Qur'an 13:8)

> *"And the heaven — We raised it, and We set the balance (al-mīzān), that you transgress not in the balance."* (Qur'an 55:7–8)

The consistency across these passages is the first thing the verse establishes. Measurement is not an occasional feature of creation but its form. Nothing is unmeasured, and the Qur'an's God is described as measuring before, during, and after creating: *"who measured and guided"* — the measuring precedes the guiding, and the guiding is the measure's purpose.

**The Grammar That Divided the Schools**

The verse's syntax became a theological fault line, and the source commentary records the debate in detail. As translated, *kulla shayʾin* is read in the accusative (*kulla*), making it the object of *khalaqnāhu* fronted for emphasis: "Everything — We created it by measure." This is the reading of the standard recitations in all schools.

The alternative reading places *kull* in the nominative (*kullu shayʾin khalaqnāhu bi-qadar*): "Everything that We created is by measure." The difference is not stylistic. The accusative reading makes the verse a statement that all things are created by God and are measured. The nominative reading makes it a statement that whatever God created is measured — which leaves open the question whether some things are not created by God.

The classical sources report that adherents of both the early Qadariyyah and the Muʿtazilah held fast to the nominative reading, because it permits the position that human acts are not created by God but performed by human beings, who are therefore fully responsible for them. The majority reading — the accusative — forecloses that position at the grammatical level, since *kulla shayʾin*, "everything," includes acts.

The dispute is worth following carefully, because it is the clearest case in the Qur'an of a single verse's vocalization carrying a school of theology.

**Four Positions on the Verse**

The classical debate produced four main positions, each of which the commentators record, and each of which uses verse 49.

**The Jabariyyah (Compulsionists).** The earliest determinists held that all human acts are determined by God, including acts of belief, disbelief, good, and evil. From their perspective verse 49 supports total divine determination, particularly read with:

> *"And you do not will, except that God wills. Truly God is Knowing, Wise."* (Qur'an 76:30; cf. 81:29)

The Ashʿarite school found this position, in the words of the source commentary, monstrously fatalistic — reducing human beings to passive agents with no will of their own, and making the language of command, prohibition, reward, and punishment unintelligible.

**The Qadariyyah.** They held that human beings are free and are the authors of their own acts, and that this does not contravene God's omnipotence. The name is ironic and historically inverted: the *Qadariyyah* were so called because they denied *qadar* in the sense of divine determination of human acts, and the label stuck as a term of reproach. The tradition records the Prophet's ﷺ reported statement about them:

> *"Every community has its Magians, and the Magians of my community are those who say that there is no measuring out (qadar)."* (Abū Dāwūd 4691; al-Tirmidhī 2149; the hadith scholars differ on its chain, with some grading it weak and others accepting its corroborated versions)

**The Muʿtazilah.** They held that human beings are fully responsible for the creation of their own evil deeds and that God punishes them accordingly — a position designed to absolve a just and merciful God from the apparent injustice of creating the acts for which He punishes. Their reading of verse 49, however, does not claim the verse as support. Instead, as the source notes, they focus on the Divine Wisdom behind the precise measure with which the universe is created. Al-Zamakhsharī writes:

> *"We have created everything in a determined measure, ordered according to that which wisdom has decreed, or in a measure written on the Tablet, known [to God] before its existence; We knew its state and its duration."*

The Shiite theologian al-Ṭabrisī emphasizes the same dimension: nothing has been created haphazardly, but only as decreed by Divine Wisdom. Everything is created according to a known measure and for a known purpose —

> *"the tongue for speech, the hand for striking, the eye for seeing, the ear for hearing, the stomach for food; and if anything increased or decreased from what We measured, the objective of creation would not be attained."*

**The Ashʿariyyah.** They rejected both the Jabarite position and the Muʿtazilite one. Their objection to the Muʿtazilah is that it posits a creative agent alongside God, contradicting:

> *"God is the Creator of all things."* (Qur'an 13:16; 39:62; cf. 40:62)

Their objection to the Jabariyyah is that it makes responsibility meaningless. Their solution is the doctrine of *kasb*, acquisition: God creates all acts, including human acts; human beings do not create anything, but they choose among the options God has created and thereby *acquire* the acts, and it is the acquisition that grounds responsibility. Al-Ashʿarī's formulation preserved both terms of the Qur'an's language — God as Creator of everything, and the human as doer of his act.

The Ashʿarites further distinguished two aspects of the decree. *Qaḍāʾ* is the pretemporal decree of God, pertaining to His Essence and His Knowledge — neither created nor finite. *Qadar* is the decree that brings things into existence in the temporal order, rendering to each its measure and limit — an attribute of Divine Action, contingent by virtue of its association with contingencies. Al-Rāzī used the distinction to argue against the philosophers' necessitarianism:

> *"Qaḍāʾ is what pertains to [God's] Knowledge, while qadar is what pertains to [God's] Will. Therefore His saying, 'Truly We have created everything according to a measure,' means by His Measure with His Will — and not, as they claim, that it is necessitated."*

The point is that the measure is willed, not compelled. A God whose act follows necessarily from His nature is not, in al-Rāzī's view, the God of verse 49; the verse says *We have created*, an act attributed to a willing subject.

**Ibn Taymiyyah's Position**

Ibn Taymiyyah addressed the same verse from within the tradition of the Salaf, and his treatment is worth setting out because it avoids both the Ashʿarite terminology and the Muʿtazilite conclusion. His position, stated in *al-ʿAqīdah al-Wāsiṭiyyah* and developed at length in the *Majmūʿ al-Fatāwā*, holds that God is the Creator of everything, including the acts of His servants, and that His servants are nonetheless truly the doers of their acts — both statements true, neither reduced to the other.

He rejects the *kasb* distinction as a verbal solution that does not preserve real agency, and he rejects the Muʿtazilite position as positing a creator besides God. His alternative is to insist that the relationship is not one of competition: God's creating the act and the human's performing it are not two acts of the same kind vying for the same space. The human will is itself created, and its operation is the operation of a created faculty — so God's creation of the act includes the creation of the human's choosing it, without the human's choosing being thereby unreal.

The analogy he uses is that of a person's own faculties. Your hand's movement is yours, and it depends at every instant on a sustaining cause that is not you; the dependence does not transfer the movement to the sustainer in the way a competing claim would. Applied to verse 49: everything is created by measure, including the measure of your willing, and your willing is nonetheless yours.

**The Preserved Tablet and the Writing**

Verse 49's *bi-qadar* connects to the Qur'an's doctrine of a written decree, which verses 52–53 will state explicitly:

> *"Nay, but it is a glorious Qur'an, in a Preserved Tablet (fī lawḥin maḥfūẓ)."* (Qur'an 85:21–22)

> *"No calamity befalls in the earth or in yourselves but that it is in a Book before We bring it into being. Truly that is easy for God."* (Qur'an 57:22)

The tradition supplies the narrative of this writing:

> *"The first thing God created was the Pen, and He said to it: Write. It said: My Lord, what shall I write? He said: Write the measures of everything until the Hour."* (Abū Dāwūd 4700; al-Tirmidhī 2155, from ʿUbādah ibn al-Ṣāmit; graded ṣaḥīḥ by al-Albānī)

> *"God wrote down the measures of the creation fifty thousand years before He created the heavens and the earth, and His Throne was upon the water."* (Muslim 2653, from ʿAbdullāh ibn ʿAmr)

And the article of faith is stated in the ḥadīth of Gabriel, when the Prophet ﷺ was asked about *īmān* and listed its six elements, the last of which is:

> *"...and that you believe in the divine decree (al-qadar), its good and its evil."* (Muslim 8, from ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb)

The phrase "its good and its evil" requires the precision the tradition supplied: the decree is God's act and is therefore wholly good in its originating; what is called "evil" is the created thing decreed, not the decreeing. Ibn Taymiyyah and Ibn al-Qayyim both state the rule: evil is not attributed to God in the sense of being an evil act of His, but is among the things He creates; the Prophet ﷺ's own supplication models the usage — *"evil is not attributed to You"* (Muslim 771, the *istiftāḥ* supplication of ʿAlī).

ʿUbādah ibn al-Ṣāmit reported the Prophet ﷺ saying to his son at the point of death:

> *"No servant of God truly believes until he believes in qadar, its good and its evil, and until he knows that what has befallen him was not going to miss him, and that what missed him was not going to befall him."* (al-Tirmidhī 2144; graded ṣaḥīḥ by al-Albānī)

The second half of the statement is the practical content of the first: belief in *qadar* is defined as the knowledge that outcomes were not adjustable by one's anxiety.

**Ibn ʿAjībah: The Verse as Therapy**

The spiritual commentators found in verse 49 not a metaphysical puzzle but a treatment for sorrow. Ibn ʿAjībah writes:

> *"It washes the heart of sorrows and of all that is other [than God], and relieves the servant from the toil of [trying to] arrange and choose; because when a person of intellect knows with the knowledge of certainty that his affairs, his states, and all that comes upon him have been embraced by the measure — with nothing preceding or coming after its measured moment — he entrusts his affair to God, surrenders to the decrees of his Master, and meets all that comes upon him with contentment (riḍā) and acceptance, be it good or evil."*

The passage names three effects: the removal of sorrow, the removal of the compulsion to arrange one's own outcomes, and the arrival of *riḍā*. The second is the least obvious and the most valuable. Much of human exhaustion comes not from events but from the effort to control events that are not controllable, and Ibn ʿAjībah's point is that the doctrine of measure identifies precisely which category those events belong to.

Al-Maybudī compresses the same teaching:

> *"One who is deprived [of God], though he may have found everything, has found nothing; whereas one who has found Him, though he may have found nothing, has found everything."*

**The Prophet's ﷺ Practical Instruction**

The Prophet ﷺ did not leave the doctrine at the level of belief. He gave it two operational rules, and the two together define the balance Islam requires.

The first is action without fatalism:

> *"Strive for what benefits you, seek help from God, and do not feel helpless. And if something befalls you, do not say: If only I had done such and such. But say: God decreed, and what He willed He did. For 'if' opens the work of Satan."* (Muslim 2664, from Abū Hurayrah)

The instruction is sequenced: strive first, and only after the outcome say *qaddara Allāh*. The fatalist reverses the sequence and says *qaddara Allāh* instead of striving. The same principle is in the famous exchange:

> *"Tie her, and trust (iʿqilhā wa-tawakkal)."* (al-Tirmidhī 2517, from Anas; graded ḥasan)

The second is the prohibition of dispute about the decree. The tradition reports that the Prophet ﷺ came out upon Companions arguing about *qadar* and forbade it, and the reports on the subject — including *"When qadar is mentioned, hold back"* — vary in strength, but the practice of the earliest generation was consistent in avoiding the disputation while affirming the belief. The distinction they preserved is between believing the decree and litigating it.

**The Sufi Reading: Freedom as a State of Being**

For thinkers in the school of Ibn ʿArabī and Mullā Ṣadrā, as the source commentary records, the question is approached from a different direction. Human beings bear within them the potential to know all the Names (2:31), and among the Names are *al-Murīd*, He Who Wills, and *al-Qadīr*, the Powerful. Human freedom is itself a divine creation, and therefore cannot be in ultimate conflict with God's creative act.

Where some schools of *kalām* see two competing wills, these thinkers see the human will as a manifestation of divine Power and Will. Freedom is then not primarily a matter of choosing between options on a given plane but a characteristic of a state of being: human beings realize true freedom only insofar as they rise above egotistical desire and submit their individual will to God. Those who attain that freedom will only what God wills, and thus display something like total determination while being totally free.

Ibn ʿArabī's paradox illustrates the point: the saints most capable of miracles are the least likely to perform them, because they are most content with God's will for the world and for themselves. Capacity and exercise diverge, and the divergence is the measure of freedom. On this reading *qadar* — one's "lot" — is ultimately the manifestation of one's own nature as known to God before creation, and since God transcends the dualities of the world, He transcends the duality of free will and determinism; human beings were already free in God by virtue of the breathing of His Spirit into the first of them (32:9).

**Application: The Two Errors the Verse Prevents**

Verse 49 is a boundary stone, and boundaries are marked to prevent movement in two directions.

The first error is fatalism: the conclusion that because everything is measured, effort is pointless. The Qur'an and the Sunnah refuse this at every turn. Noah was told to build the Ark. The Muslims were told to fight at Badr. The camel was to be tied. The Prophet ﷺ's formulation — *strive for what benefits you* — comes before *say God decreed*. A person using measure as an excuse for inaction has inverted the verse's order.

The second error is self-deification: the conclusion that because one acts, one originates. This is the error of Pharaoh — *"I have been given it only by knowledge I have"* (28:78) — and of the Quraysh's *jamiʿun muntaṣir* (54:44), a host that gets itself its own victory. Verse 49 removes it in three words: *khalaqnāhu bi-qadar*. Whatever you have done was done within a measure you did not set, using faculties you did not make, in a world you did not create.

Between the two errors stands the position the tradition actually holds: act as though the outcome depended on your effort, and receive the outcome as though it never did. Muslim 2664 is the operating manual for that position, and its final clause — *"for 'if' opens the work of Satan"* — identifies the specific mental act to avoid. The regretful *if* is the sentence of a person who believes he could have altered the measure. Verse 49 says he could not, and the freedom in that statement is the freedom Ibn ʿAjībah described: the heart washed of sorrow, the servant relieved of the toil of arranging what was arranged before him.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:50

> **And Our Command is naught but one, like the blinking of an eye.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Amrunā: The Command and the Affair**

*Wa-mā amrunā illā wāḥidatun ka-lamḥin bi-l-baṣar.* *Amr* in Arabic means both a command and an affair, a matter, a state of affairs — and the Qur'an uses it in both senses, sometimes in a single sentence. Verse 12 used it in the second sense: *ʿalā amrin qad qudir*, "upon a matter already measured." Verse 50 uses it in the first: Our Command.

The two senses are connected in the Qur'an's theology because God's command is what brings affairs about. The *amr* as decree and the *amr* as event are the same reality viewed from two sides:

> *"His is the creation and the Command (alā lahu al-khalqu wa-l-amr). Blessed be God, Lord of all beings."* (Qur'an 7:54)

> *"Truly His Command, when He intends a thing, is only that He says unto it: Be! — and it is. So glory be to Him in Whose Hand is the dominion of all things, and unto Whom you will be returned."* (Qur'an 36:82–83)

> *"God is He Who created seven heavens and of the earth the like thereof; the Command descends between them, that you may know that God is over all things Powerful and that God encompasses all things in knowledge."* (Qur'an 65:12)

The last of these places the *amr* in motion — descending between the heavens and the earth — and gives its purpose: that human beings may know God's power and knowledge. Verse 50 is a statement about the mode of that descent.

**Wāḥidah: The Singleness That Removes Sequence**

*Wa-mā amrunā illā wāḥidatun* — "Our Command is nothing but one." The word *wāḥidah*, feminine to agree with the implied *amrah* (a single act of commanding) or with *kalimah* (a word), denotes oneness in the strictest sense: not one among many, but a single unit with no parts and no repetition.

The commentators — Ibn al-Jawzī, al-Bayḍāwī, al-Qurṭubī — explain the phrase as meaning that there is no difference between God's willing a thing and its coming into being, and no time lag between the two. When God decrees a thing, He says to it "Be," and it is:

> *"The Originator of the heavens and the earth. When He decrees a thing, He only says unto it: Be! — and it is (kun fa-yakūn)."* (Qur'an 2:117)

> *"It is not for God to take a son — glory be to Him! When He decrees a matter, He only says unto it: Be! — and it is."* (Qur'an 19:35)

> *"Truly the similitude of Jesus with God is as the similitude of Adam: He created him from dust, then said unto him: Be! — and he was."* (Qur'an 3:59)

> *"And Our Command is but one, as the twinkling of an eye."* — and: *"Our word unto a thing, when We intend it, is only that We say unto it: Be! — and it is."* (Qur'an 16:40)

See also 3:47; 40:68; 36:82. The formula *kun fa-yakūn* appears eight times in the Qur'an, and each occurrence asserts the same absence of interval. Al-Zamakhsharī explains *wāḥidah* as meaning that God does not command twice; a single command suffices, and the second would be redundant because the first cannot fail.

There is a further implication in the singleness. Human commands require repetition because they are addressed to agents who may not hear, may not understand, may not comply, or may need reminding. The need for a second command is a measure of the weakness of the first. God's command is one because it is effective on its first utterance — which is another way of saying that the distinction between commanding and causing, which holds for human beings, does not hold for Him.

**Ka-Lamḥin Bi-l-Baṣar: The Fastest Thing a Human Being Experiences**

*Laḥa* is from *l-m-ḥ*, and it denotes the swift movement of the eye — a glance, a flash, the briefest visual event the body can produce. *Bi-l-baṣar*, "with the sight," specifies the organ. *Ka-lamḥin bi-l-baṣar* is therefore "like a glance of the eye" — and the phrase was proverbial in Arabic for the shortest imaginable interval.

The Qur'an uses the same phrase for the Hour itself, and adds a correction:

> *"And unto God belongs the Unseen of the heavens and the earth, and the Command of the Hour is but as the twinkling of the eye (ka-lamḥi al-baṣar), or nearer (aw huwa aqrab). Truly God is over all things Powerful."* (Qur'an 16:77)

*Aw huwa aqrab* — "or nearer." The comparison is offered and then exceeded: the Hour is like a blink, or closer than that. Al-Rāzī explains that the phrase describes not the speed of the event but the absence of process. A blink has no stages; it is not fast because it covers distance quickly but because it has no distance to cover. God's command is like it in the second sense: there is nothing between the willing and the being.

This is the theological content of the simile, and it distinguishes the Qur'an's account from every account of a fast god. A fast agent traverses the interval quickly. God's act has no interval. The Qur'an states the point in a verse that also explains why the similitude is offered at all:

> *"They ask thee to hasten the punishment; and God will not fail His promise. And truly a day with thy Lord is as a thousand years of what you reckon."* (Qur'an 22:47)

> *"And they ask thee to hasten the punishment. Were it not for a term appointed, the punishment would have come upon them; and it will come upon them suddenly, while they perceive not."* (Qur'an 29:53)

*Baghtatan wa-hum lā yashʿurūn* — suddenly, while they do not perceive. The suddenness is the same one verse 38 described in Lot's towns and verse 6:44 describes as the general method: provision opened, rejoicing achieved, seizure arriving without transition.

**The Sūrah's Theme of Oneness**

Verse 50 completes a pattern the sūrah has been building, and the pattern is numerical. The punishments in al-Qamar are consistently single: one cry (*ṣayḥatan wāḥidah*, verse 31); one command (*amrunā illā wāḥidah*, verse 50). The Resurrection is single: *"it will be only a single shout"* (79:13). The Hour is single. And the God who acts is One — *wāḥid* — which is the Qur'an's central claim.

The correspondence between Thamūd's single cry and God's single command is exact, and it explains why the sūrah places verse 50 where it does. The cry that leveled a civilization in an instant was an instance of the command described in the next verse. The single cry was a single command. The reader who has just been told that everything is created by measure (49) is now told that the measure is applied instantaneously (50) — and the example is already in the sūrah, in verse 31.

**Application: The Interval You Are Living In**

Verse 50 has a practical application that is uncomfortable in the way the sūrah's best verses are. It states that there is no interval between God's decision and its arrival, which means that the interval a person experiences between the decision and its appearance in his life is not a buffer. It is a concealment.

Human beings organize their lives around intervals — the time between a risk and its consequence, between a habit and its result, between a warning and its fulfillment. The intervals feel like safety because they permit adjustment, and they do permit adjustment, which is why the Qur'an commands repentance and effort. But the interval is not a property of the event; it is a property of human perception. Verse 50 locates the event on God's side, where it is already complete, and the interval on the human side, where it is a delay in noticing.

The tradition's phrase for this is *baghtah* — suddenness. The Prophet ﷺ described death as arriving suddenly, and the Companions described the Hour the same way: *"It will come upon them suddenly, while they perceive not"* (29:53). The practical consequence is not panic but attention. A person who believes an event is distant prepares for it in the abstract; a person who believes it is a blink away prepares for it now, in the specific, in the actual decision in front of him.

There is also a consoling direction to the same claim, and the sūrah supplies it. The deliverance of Lot's household, the carrying of Noah, the rescue of the Muslims at Badr — each was equally instantaneous on God's side. The interval in which a person feels abandoned is not a period in which nothing is happening; it is a period in which the command has been issued and the blink has not yet completed. Verse 50 does not tell the reader how long the blink lasts. It tells him what is inside it.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:51

> **We have indeed destroyed the likes of you; so is there any who remembers?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Ashbāʿakum: Not "Those Before You" but "Your Equals"**

*Wa-laqad ahlaknā ashbāʿakum.* The verse could have said "We destroyed the former generations," or "We destroyed those before you." It says instead: *We destroyed your likes* — *ashbāʿakum*, the plural of *shabīʿ*, meaning a peer, an equal, one of the same kind and disposition. The commentators gloss it uniformly as *amthāl*, likes.

The word choice carries the whole argument. A warning drawn from "those before you" can be dismissed as a difference of era, technology, culture, or circumstance. A warning drawn from "your likes" cannot, because likeness is precisely the ground on which an analogy holds. The verse asserts that the resemblance is not general but specific: the destroyed parties were of the same kind as the addressed parties in every respect that matters.

The Qur'an makes the same assertion with a direct comparison to the Prophet's ﷺ own audience:

> *"And how many a town, stronger than thy town which has expelled thee, have We destroyed — and they had no helper."* (Qur'an 47:13)

*Ashaddu quwwatan min qaryatika* — stronger in might than your town. The comparison is not flattering to Makkah. The towns destroyed were stronger, and they had no helper; the town that expelled the Prophet ﷺ is weaker, and it is being told so.

> *"Say: Travel in the land and see how was the end of those before. Most of them were associators."* (Qur'an 30:42)

> *"Is it not guidance for them — how many a generation We destroyed before them, in whose dwellings they walk? Truly in that are signs. Will they not then hear?"* (Qur'an 32:26)

The phrase *fī masākinihim yamshūn*, "in whose dwellings they walk," is the physical basis of the likeness: the audience of these verses walked through the settlements of the destroyed. The resemblance was not argued from texts; it was available on foot.

**The Argument From Likeness, and Its Conditions**

The logic of the verse is analogical, and the Qur'an uses analogical reasoning explicitly and repeatedly. Its general form: A and B share the relevant properties; A produced outcome X; therefore B will produce X unless the relevant properties differ. The verse's task is to establish the first premise — the sharing — and its word for that sharing is *ashbāʿ*.

The Qur'an elsewhere names the principle that makes the analogy valid:

> *"That is the way of God (sunnat Allāh) with those who passed before, and thou wilt not find in the way of God any change."* (Qur'an 33:62)

> *"And thou wilt not find in Our way any alteration."* (Qur'an 17:77)

> *"And they await naught but the way of the former peoples."* (Qur'an 35:43)

*Sunna* means an established practice, a way that does not vary. Once a *sunna* is identified, the inference from case to case is legitimate, and the burden shifts: the party claiming exemption must identify the differentiating property. Verse 43 already asked for it — *are your disbelievers better than those? do you have an exemption in the scriptures?* — and received nothing. Verse 51 states the conclusion: We destroyed your likes.

Al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī both note the verse's addressee. The second-person plural is directed at the Quraysh primarily, and through them at every subsequent audience. Ibn Kathīr reads the phrase as a general warning to disbelievers in every age, and cites the string of parallel passages: 6:6; 7:4; 18:59; 19:74, 98; 20:128; 22:45; 28:58; 32:26; 50:36.

**The Final Occurrence of the Question**

*Fa-hal min mudhakkir* appears for the sixth and last time in the sūrah — after Noah's Ark (15), after the flood refrain (17), after ʿĀd (22), after Thamūd (32), after Lot (40), and here at the close of the historical section. Its final placement is the most direct of all, because the sentence preceding it is in the second person.

The progression of the six occurrences traces the sūrah's narrowing distance. The first asks the question about a distant event. The next three ask it after increasingly familiar nations. The fifth asks it after four cases. The sixth asks it about "your likes" — and at that point the question is no longer about anyone else.

There is a rhetorical effect in the finality. After verse 51 the sūrah asks no more questions about remembering. It moves to the record (52–53) and the outcome (54–55). The reader who has not answered by verse 51 will not be asked again; the remaining verses describe what happens to those who did and those who did not.

**Ahlaknā: The Perfect Tense of Completed Destruction**

The verb is in the perfect tense — *ahalaknā*, "We destroyed" — and the destruction is stated as accomplished. The Qur'an's habit of describing future divine acts in the past tense, noted at verse 1, appears here about the past in its plain sense: these things happened, they are finished, and the parties involved are gone.

The root *h-l-k* denotes destruction with the connotation of perishing utterly — not defeat, not subjugation, not relocation. The Qur'an uses it with quantifiers that emphasize the number of instances:

> *"And how many a generation have We destroyed before them (wa-kam ahlaknā min qablihim min qarn)!"* (Qur'an 19:74; cf. 20:128, 32:26, 50:36)

*Kam* — how many — with the verb of destruction. The Qur'an is not describing an exception but a rate. Al-Qurṭubī notes that *qarn*, generation, is used because the destroyed parties are counted in generations rather than in individuals: whole cohorts, entire civilizational units.

The verse's phrase *wa-laqad ahlaknā ashbāʿakum* therefore combines a repeated act (*qad* with the perfect indicating multiplicity as well as certainty), a completed act, and an act upon peers. The three features together produce the warning's force: this has happened often, it is finished rather than threatened, and it happened to people like you.

**Application: The Sentence Every Declining Institution Says**

Verse 51 exists because of a specific human reflex. When presented with the record of others' failures, the confident party's first move is to dispute the likeness. We are not like them — our technology is different, our institutions are more robust, our information environment is richer, our era has learned the lessons, our situation has unique features.

The reflex is sometimes correct. Not every resemblance is relevant, and a superficial analogy can mislead. The Qur'an's own method respects this: it specifies the relevant property in each case. Noah's people were destroyed for persistent denial over centuries; ʿĀd for strength-based arrogance; Thamūd for security-based permanence; Lot's people for a publicly normalized transgression; Pharaoh's house for the denial of all signs. The likeness asserted in verse 51 is a likeness in those properties, not in architecture or geography.

The reflex is therefore testable, and the test is specific: identify the property the Qur'an names as causal, and ask whether you possess it. Do you deny warnings that have arrived? Do you say "who is greater than us in strength"? Do you feel secure in your construction? Have you made wrong into your assembly's norm? Do you have a document of exemption?

The verse's question — *fa-hal min mudhakkir* — is asked of the person who performs that test. The one who does not perform it has answered the question already, in the manner of Lot's people at verse 36: by disputing the warnings rather than by examining the likeness.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:52

> **Everything that they have done is in the scriptures.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Fī Al-Zubur: The Same Word as Verse 43**

*Wa-kullu shayʾin faʿalūhu fī al-zubur.* The noun *zubur* is the plural of *zabūr*, a written book or scripture — the same word the Qur'an uses for the Psalms given to David (4:163; 17:55), and the same word this sūrah used nine verses earlier:

> *"Or have you some exemption in the scriptures (am lakum barāʾatun fī al-zubur)?"* (Qur'an 54:43)

The repetition is the verse's central device. Verse 43 asked whether the audience possessed an exemption written in the books; verse 52 states what the books actually contain. They contain no exemptions. They contain the record of everything done.

Al-Zamakhsharī and al-Bayḍāwī both note the connection, and it gives the two verses the structure of a question and its answer, separated by the account of the Hour's bitterness and the criminals' burning. The person who asks "do I have a document of immunity?" is answered: "there is a document, and it is about you."

**Two Readings of the Books**

The commentators give two interpretations of *al-zubur*, and the source records both.

The first takes the books as the scrolls in which deeds are recorded — the registers kept by the angels, produced on the Day of Judgment. Most commentators, including Ibn al-Jawzī and al-Ṭabarī, hold this reading, which means the verse asserts that God knows all that people have done and has caused it to be written. The Qur'an describes these records extensively:

> *"And every human being — We have fastened his deed to his neck, and We shall bring forth for him on the Day of Resurrection a Book which he will find spread open: Read thy Book (iqraʾ kitābaka)! Thy own soul suffices as a reckoner against thee this day."* (Qur'an 17:13–14)

> *"And the Book shall be set down, and thou wilt see the guilty fearful of what is in it. And they will say: O woe unto us! What a Book this is! It leaves out nothing, small or great, save that it has taken account thereof. And they find present [therein] whatsoever they did. And thy Lord wrongs no one."* (Qur'an 18:49)

> *"And the earth shall shine with the light of her Lord, and the Book shall be set down, and the prophets and the witnesses shall be brought, and it shall be judged between them in truth, and they shall not be wronged."* (Qur'an 39:69)

> *"This is Our Book, speaking against you in truth (yanṭiqu ʿalaykum bi-l-ḥaqq). Truly We were transcribing (nastansikh) what you used to do."* (Qur'an 45:29)

The verb *nastansikh* in 45:29 — We were making copies, transcribing — indicates that the record is a copy of something that happened, not a composition. The deeds were performed; the books were made from them. This is why the guilty cannot dispute the contents: the Book speaks *bi-l-ḥaqq*, in truth, and the reader of it is the doer of what is in it.

The second reading takes *al-zubur* as the previous revelations, so that the verse refers to God's warnings recorded in all the scriptures — the warnings people failed to heed, and for which they are punished. On this reading the verse continues the argument of verse 43 in a different direction: the books contain the warnings, in every dispensation, and their content has been consistent.

The two readings converge in the Qur'an's doctrine of mercy preceding wrath. Because God's mercy encompasses all things (7:156) and precedes His wrath, every human collectivity is given both guidance and warning before judgment falls:

> *"And thy Lord never destroys towns until He sends unto their mother city a messenger to recite unto them Our signs. And We never destroy towns, save when their people are wrongdoers."* (Qur'an 28:59)

See also 6:131–32; 10:13; 15:4; 26:208–9. The record in the books is therefore a record of a party who had been informed.

**The Two Books and the One Pen**

The tradition distinguishes two registers, and verse 52 can be read as referring to both.

The first is the pre-temporal record — the Preserved Tablet:

> *"Nay, but it is a glorious Qur'an, in a Preserved Tablet (fī lawḥin maḥfūẓ)."* (Qur'an 85:21–22)

> *"Knowest thou not that God knows what is in the heaven and the earth? Truly that is in a Book. Truly that is easy for God."* (Qur'an 22:70)

> *"No calamity befalls in the earth or in yourselves but that it is in a Book before We bring it into being."* (Qur'an 57:22)

The second is the temporal record of individual deeds, kept by appointed scribes:

> *"And truly over you are watchers — noble scribes (kirāman kātibīn) — who know whatever you do."* (Qur'an 82:10–12)

> *"When the two receivers receive, seated on the right and on the left. He utters no word but that there is with him an observer, ready (raqībun ʿatīd)."* (Qur'an 50:17–18)

> *"Truly We give life to the dead, and We record what they sent forward and their traces (wa-āthārahum). And everything We have enumerated in a clear Register (imāmin mubīn)."* (Qur'an 36:12)

The last of these is the most expansive. *Mā qaddamū*, what they sent forward — the deeds performed in life. *Wa-āthārahum*, their traces — the consequences that continue after them, the institutions they built, the practices they introduced, the examples they set. Both are recorded, which means that a person's file does not close at his death.

The tradition connects both registers to a single origin:

> *"The first thing God created was the Pen, and He said to it: Write. It said: My Lord, what shall I write? He said: Write the measures of everything until the Hour."* (Abū Dāwūd 4700; al-Tirmidhī 2155, from ʿUbādah ibn al-Ṣāmit)

> *"God wrote the measures of the creation fifty thousand years before He created the heavens and the earth, and His Throne was upon the water."* (Muslim 2653, from ʿAbdullāh ibn ʿAmr)

The writing preceded the creation, and verse 49's *bi-qadar* is the content of what was written. Verse 52 adds that the deeds performed within that measure are also written — so that the record contains both the plan and its execution.

**Kullu Shayʾin Faʿalūhu: The Completeness of the Record**

The verse's phrase *kullu shayʾin faʿalūhu*, "everything they have done," is unrestricted. The relative clause attaches to *kull*, and there is no qualifier limiting it to major acts, public acts, or acts of a particular kind. Verse 53 will state the extension explicitly — small and great — and the two verses together define the record's scope as total.

The Qur'an makes the same claim with an image of exhaustive coverage:

> *"And with Him are the keys of the Unseen; none knows them but He. And He knows whatever is in the land and the sea. Not a leaf falls but that He knows it, nor a grain in the darknesses of the earth, nor anything moist or dry, but that it is in a clear Book (kitābin mubīn)."* (Qur'an 6:59)

> *"And those who disbelieve say: The Hour will not come upon us. Say: Nay, by my Lord, it will surely come upon you — Knower of the Unseen, from Whom escapes not the weight of an atom in the heavens or in the earth, nor smaller than that, nor greater, but that it is in a clear Book."* (Qur'an 34:3)

*Lā yaghību ʿanhu mithqālu dharrah* — not the weight of an atom escapes Him. The Qur'an's chosen unit of smallness is the *dharrah*, historically understood as an ant or a speck of dust, and functionally the smallest thing the language could name.

**Application: The Record and the Reader**

Verse 52 has two applications, and they run in opposite directions.

The first is a warning. Everything is written, including what was done in private, including what was said once, including what was intended and not performed — the Qur'an records intentions as well as acts (*wa-āthārahum*, their traces, covers the consequences of what was done, and the tradition holds that an intended good is recorded as a good and an intended evil is not recorded until performed). A person who believes that unwitnessed acts are unrecorded has misread the verse, and the verse's own audience — the Quraysh who asked for an exemption in the books — is the case study.

The second is a comfort, and it is often overlooked. If everything is written, then nothing is lost. The small kindness nobody noticed, the patience exercised in private, the wrong forgone when it could have been committed with impunity, the prayer made at three in the morning, the money given without being seen — all of it is in the *zubur*. The Qur'an's insistence on the completeness of the record is as much a guarantee for the unappreciated as it is a threat to the unrepentant.

> *"And whoever does an atom's weight of good shall see it, and whoever does an atom's weight of evil shall see it."* (Qur'an 99:7–8)
