The verb in both clauses is *yarah*, "he shall see it" — the same visual verb verse 52's records imply. Nothing is lost, and nothing is invented. The believer's practical relation to the record is therefore not anxiety but accuracy: do the thing, do not perform the thing, and leave the accounting to the Book.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:53

> **Everything small and great is inscribed.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Muṣṭar: Written in Rows**

*Wa-kullu ṣaghīrin wa-kabīrin mustaṭar.* The final word is the eighth form passive participle of *s-ṭ-r*, a root meaning to write in lines, to arrange in rows. From it comes *saṭr*, a line of writing, and *masṭūr*, something written. The eighth form *istaṭara* intensifies the sense: to be thoroughly written, to be inscribed in an organized, continuous manner. *Muṣṭar* is therefore not merely recorded but set down in rows — the image of a document, laid out, complete, and readable.

The Qur'an uses the same root in its opening oaths:

> *"By the Pen and what they inscribe (wa-mā yasturūn)."* (Qur'an 68:1)

> *"By the Mount, and a Book inscribed (wa-kitābin masṭūr), in an unrolled scroll (fī raqqin manshūr)."* (Qur'an 52:1–3)

The image in 52:2–3 is specific: a book written on parchment, unrolled, open. The scroll is not sealed and the writing is not hidden. Verse 53's *mustaṭar* belongs to the same picture — a document in the condition of being read.

**Small and Great: The Two Ends That Leave Nothing Out**

*Ṣaghīr* and *kabīr* — small and great — form the standard Arabic pair for total coverage. Naming both extremes excludes everything between them, and the Qur'an uses the pair repeatedly for exactly this purpose:

> *"What ails this Book, that it leaves out nothing, small or great (lā yughādiru ṣaghīratan wa-lā kabīratan), save that it has taken account thereof?"* (Qur'an 18:49)

> *"And they will be brought before thy Lord in ranks: Now you have come unto Us as We created you the first time. Nay, but you claimed that We would not appoint for you a tryst."* (Qur'an 18:48)

> *"And the Book shall be set down..."* — and: *"Nor smaller than that nor greater, but it is in a clear Book."* (Qur'an 34:3)

> *"And We shall set up the just balances for the Day of Resurrection, so no soul shall be wronged in anything. And if it be the weight of a grain of mustard seed, We shall bring it. And sufficient are We as reckoners."* (Qur'an 21:47)

The mustard seed in 21:47 is the Qur'an's chosen example of the *ṣaghīr*, and the verse adds a statement about the reckoner: *wa-kafā binā ḥāsibīn*, "sufficient are We as reckoners." The claim is not that the record is approximate but that the accounting is exact and the accountant is adequate.

There is a further precision in the pairing. Human accounting systems invariably have a threshold — a de minimis level below which items are not recorded, because the cost of recording them exceeds their value. Verse 53 states that the divine register has no threshold. This is a claim about the nature of the recorder rather than about the items recorded: only a knower for whom recording is costless can record everything, and the Qur'an says so:

> *"Truly that is easy for God."* (Qur'an 22:70; cf. 57:22)

**The Small Deeds: Two Reports That Define the Category**

The tradition took the verse's *ṣaghīr* seriously and built an ethic around it. The Prophet ﷺ spoke of small acts in both directions, and his statements are among the most quoted in the corpus.

On the small good:

> *"Do not belittle any act of good, even that you meet your brother with a cheerful face."* (Muslim 2626, from Abū Dharr)

> *"Protect yourselves from the Fire, even with half a date; and if you do not find it, then with a good word."* (al-Bukhārī 1417; Muslim 1016, from ʿAdī ibn Ḥātim)

> *"O ʿĀʾishah, beware the small sins (muḥaqqarāt al-dhunūb), for they have a pursuer from God."* (al-Nasāʾī; Ibn Mājah; graded ṣaḥīḥ by al-Albānī)

> *"Beware the small sins. The parable of the small sins is that of a people who halted in a valley, and each of them went and brought a stick, until they gathered enough to bake their bread. Truly the small sins, when they accumulate upon a man, destroy him."* (Aḥmad; al-Ṭabarānī, from Ibn Masʿūd; graded ṣaḥīḥ by al-Albānī)

The parable of the sticks is the mechanism of the *ṣaghīr*: no single item is significant, and the accumulation is decisive. This is the practical content of *mustaṭar* — written in rows, one line after another, until the document is full.

On the small act with a great outcome, the tradition gives two paired reports that no commentator on this verse omits:

> *"A woman was punished because of a cat she confined until it died of hunger, and she entered the Fire because of it."* (al-Bukhārī 3482; Muslim 2242)

> *"While a man was walking along a road, thirst overcame him, and he found a well and went down into it and drank. Then he came out and found a dog panting and licking the dust from thirst. He said: This dog has been afflicted with thirst as I was. So he went down into the well, filled his shoe with water, and held it in his teeth until he climbed up, and gave the dog to drink. And God was grateful to him and forgave him."* It was asked: Messenger of God, is there a reward for us in animals? He said: *"In every living being with a moist liver there is a reward."* (al-Bukhārī 2363, 6009; Muslim 2244)

Two small acts, two eternal outcomes, and neither act was observed by anyone whose opinion mattered. The reports are the commentary on verse 53 in narrative form: the *ṣaghīr* is not small in its consequences, only in its visibility.

**Al-Ṣaghīr and the Sūrah Itself**

There is an application of the verse to the sūrah containing it, and the tradition did not miss it. Al-Qamar is a small sūrah — fifty-five verses, two pages, among the shortest of the Makkan chapters. Its content, on verse 53's own logic, is not thereby small: it contains five complete case studies, the doctrine of divine measure, the description of the Resurrection, the account of the record, and the promise of the Garden.

The same principle governs the Qur'an's own arrangement. The short sūrahs at the end of the muṣḥaf are the most memorized, the most recited, and the most frequently heard; the length of a passage is not a measure of its weight. Verse 53's *ṣaghīr* therefore applies to the reader's own practice as well as to his deeds: the small portion retained, the short chapter recited, the brief supplication made — all of it is *mustaṭar*, inscribed, and none of it is disqualified by its size.

**The Balance and the Book**

Verse 53 belongs to a pair of images the Qur'an uses together for the Day: the Book and the Balance.

> *"And We shall set up the just balances (al-mawāzīna al-qisṭ) for the Day of Resurrection, so no soul shall be wronged in anything."* (Qur'an 21:47)

> *"And as for him whose scales are heavy (fa-man thaqulat mawāzīnuh), he shall be in a pleasant life. And as for him whose scales are light, his refuge shall be the Abyss."* (Qur'an 101:6–9)

> *"And the weighing that Day shall be the truth. So as for those whose scales are heavy — those are the successful. And as for those whose scales are light — those are they who have lost their souls, because they wronged Our signs."* (Qur'an 7:8–9)

The Book records; the Balance weighs. The two instruments answer two different questions — what happened, and what it amounted to — and verse 53 supplies the first. Its insistence that nothing small is omitted is what makes the weighing possible, since a balance that ignored small weights would be unjust in exactly the way the Qur'an denies of God:

> *"And thy Lord wrongs no one."* (Qur'an 18:49)

> *"Truly God does not wrong men in anything, but men wrong themselves."* (Qur'an 10:44)

**Application: The Threshold Problem**

Verse 53 addresses a habit of mind that governs most ethical failure. Human beings operate with thresholds: matters below a certain size are not treated as matters at all. The small unkindness is not counted because it was small. The small dishonesty is not counted because nobody lost anything. The small prayer skipped is not counted because the rest were performed. The small good deed is not counted because it was insignificant.

The verse removes the threshold from one direction and installs it from the other. Nothing is too small to be inscribed — which means the small bad is real. And nothing is too small to be inscribed — which means the small good is real too. The second half is the one the tradition emphasized, because the first half produces scrupulosity if held alone and the second produces diligence.

The practical instruction that follows is a change of attention rather than a change of effort. Most people cannot do great things consistently; almost everyone can do small things constantly. The verse says the small things are inscribed in rows, and the report of the sticks says rows of small things accumulate into a fire large enough to bake bread — in either direction. The believer's arithmetic is therefore not "how large was the act?" but "how many were there, and which way were they going?"

That is the last statement of the sūrah's doctrine of record, and it leads directly to its last two verses: what the inscribed small and great add up to, for the two parties described in verses 47 and 54.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:54

> **Truly the reverent are amidst Gardens and a stream.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Al-Muttaqūn: The Word That Means the Shielded**

*Inna al-muttaqīna fī jannātin wa-nahar.* After the criminals of verse 47 and the record of verses 52–53, the sūrah turns to its last party: *al-muttaqūn*, the reverent, the ones who guard themselves. The word is the active participle of the eighth form of *w-q-y*, a root whose basic sense is to shield, to ward off, to place something between oneself and what would harm. From the same root comes *wiqāyah*, protection, and *wiqāyah* is precisely what *taqwā* is.

The classical definitions all preserve the root's physical sense. Ṭalq ibn Ḥabīb's formulation, transmitted in the earliest generations and quoted by commentators on every verse containing *taqwā*, is the most complete:

> *"Taqwā is that you act in obedience to God upon a light from God, hoping for God's reward; and that you abandon disobedience to God upon a light from God, fearing God's punishment."*

Four elements — the act, the knowledge, the hope, and the fear — and the removal of any of them produces something else. Obedience without knowledge is custom; obedience without hope is servitude to a terror; avoidance without fear is merely prudence.

Ibn Masʿūd's explanation of the Qur'an's strongest formulation of the term supplies the standard:

> *"O you who believe, fear God with the fear He is due (ḥaqqa tuqātihi), and die not except as ones who submit."* (Qur'an 3:102)

He explained *ḥaqqa tuqātihi* as: that He be obeyed and not disobeyed, remembered and not forgotten, thanked and not shown ingratitude. The three pairs cover the whole of religious life — the limbs, the mind, and the heart.

**The Thorny Path: The Analogy the Tradition Itself Gave**

The most widely transmitted explanation of *taqwā* is an exchange between two Companions, recorded by Ibn Abī Ḥātim and cited by Ibn Kathīr in his commentary on the opening of Sūrah al-Baqarah:

> *ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb asked Ubayy ibn Kaʿb about taqwā. Ubayy said: "Have you ever walked a path with thorns in it?" ʿUmar said: "Yes." He said: "What did you do?" ʿUmar said: "I gathered my garment, and I looked at where I was placing my feet, and I stepped carefully." Ubayy said: "That is taqwā."*

The analogy is complete and does not require elaboration, but its components are worth naming because each corresponds to a feature of religious practice. The path is not of the walker's choosing — it is there, with thorns in it. The garment is gathered, meaning the person protects what could be torn and reduces what could be caught. The feet are watched, meaning attention is directed at the immediate next step rather than at the destination. The stepping is careful, meaning the pace is adjusted to the surface.

Nothing in the analogy involves fear of the path itself. The walker is not terrified of the road; he is attentive to it. This is the difference between *taqwā* and anxiety, and it is why the Qur'an pairs the two states of mind so deliberately:

> *"Indeed there is no fear upon them, nor shall they grieve."* — the description of the believers in 2:38, 2:62, 2:112, and elsewhere

> *"And take provision, for truly the best provision is taqwā. And fear Me, O you of understanding."* (Qur'an 2:197)

The modern version of the same image is familiar to anyone who drives: the careful driver is not the frightened one but the one who watches the road, keeps distance, and adjusts speed to conditions. Fright produces either paralysis or recklessness. Attention produces safety. *Taqwā* is attention applied to a road that contains genuine hazards, and the promise attached to it in verse 54 is that the road ends in a garden.

**Fī Jannātin Wa-Nahar: The Mirror of Verse 47**

The verse's structure is the exact counterpart of verse 47, seven verses earlier:

> *"Truly the guilty are astray and burning (inna al-mujrimīna fī ḍalālin wa-suʿur)."* (Qur'an 54:47)

> *"Truly the reverent are amidst Gardens and a stream (inna al-muttaqīna fī jannātin wa-nahar)."* (Qur'an 54:54)

The parallel is total. Both begin with *inna* and a definite plural subject. Both use *fī*, the preposition of containment — the parties are *inside* their conditions, not merely near them. Both supply two nouns joined by *wa*. And the two nouns in each case describe a location and a quality.

The criminals are *fī ḍalāl* — inside error, lost, without direction — *wa-suʿur*, and burning. The reverent are *fī jannāt* — inside Gardens, enclosed, shaded, cultivated — *wa-nahar*, and a river. Lost and burning; enclosed and watered. The two states are negations of one another in every element, and the sūrah places them seven verses apart so that the reader encounters both within a minute of recitation.

Al-Zamakhsharī notes the grammatical point that sharpens the contrast: *jannāt* is plural and *nahar* is singular. The plural indicates multiplicity of dwelling — Gardens, not a Garden — and the singular, on the reading of al-Bayḍāwī, al-Rāzī, and al-Zamakhsharī, indicates the genus: the rivers of the Garden, all of them, named by their kind. Alternatively, the singular denotes one specific river among them — Kawthar:

> *"Truly We have given thee al-Kawthar (abundance). So pray unto thy Lord and sacrifice. Truly thy enemy is the one cut off."* (Qur'an 108:1–3)

or Tasnīm, the spring reserved for the highest rank:

> *"And its mixture is from Tasnīm — a spring whence those brought near drink."* (Qur'an 83:27–28)

On either reading the singular does the same rhetorical work: it names the water as one thing while the dwellings are many, so that every Garden is supplied from the same source.

**Jannah: The Covering That Answers the Accusation**

*Jannah*, garden, is from *j-n-n*, to cover — the garden is so called because its ground is covered by its foliage, and its interior is hidden from the outside. The root is one of the most productive in the Qur'an: *jinn* (concealed beings), *janīn* (the embryo, hidden in the womb), *junnah* (a shield, which covers), *jannah* (a garden, and also the Garden), and *majnūn* — one whose intellect is covered.

The sūrah used that last word in verse 9, as the insult thrown at Noah: *wa-qālū majnūn*, "they said: possessed, covered in his mind." Verse 54 uses the same root as the name of the reward. The covering that was an accusation becomes the covering that is a gift; the word used to disqualify a prophet becomes the word used to describe his destination and that of everyone who followed him.

The Qur'an performs this operation on the sūrah's vocabulary throughout, and the pattern is worth seeing whole. *Suʿur*, burning, in the mouth of Thamūd at verse 24, returns as God's description of them at verse 47. *Mustamirr*, their word for the miracle at verse 2, returns as His word for their day of misfortune at verse 19, and as *amarr*, more bitter, at verse 46. *Jannah*, their word for madness at verse 9, returns as His word for the Garden at verse 54. In every case the vocabulary of the deniers is retained and reassigned — sometimes as verdict, sometimes as reward. The sūrah's language is a courtroom in which the parties' own words are entered into the record.

**The Water Arc of the Sūrah**

Verse 54 completes a pattern that runs through the whole chapter, and the pattern is water.

In verses 11–12, water is destruction: the gates of the sky opened with pouring water, the earth bursting with springs, the two waters meeting upon a measured matter, and every living thing drowned.

In verse 28, water is a test: *"inform them that the water is to be divided among them — each share of drink equitably proportioned."* The scarce resource allocated by divine rule, accepted at first, resented afterward, and the resentment ending in a dead camel and a leveled town.

In verse 54, water is reward: the reverent amidst Gardens and a river.

The three uses are not a decorative motif. They are the Qur'an's demonstration that the element itself is morally neutral and that its character is set entirely by the command governing it and the posture of the one receiving it. The same water drowned a world, tested a civilization, and refreshes the Garden. Sūrah al-Raḥmān, the sūrah immediately following, describes the Garden's water as flowing:

> *"In both of them are two springs flowing (fīhimā ʿaynāni tajriyān)."* (Qur'an 55:50)

> *"Reclining upon couches lined with silk brocade, and the fruits of both Gardens near at hand."* (Qur'an 55:54)

And the Qur'an's fullest description of the Garden's rivers supplies what verse 54's singular compresses:

> *"The similitude of the Garden promised to the reverent: therein are rivers of water unspoiled, and rivers of milk whose taste changes not, and rivers of wine delicious to the drinkers, and rivers of honey purified; and therein is for them every fruit, and forgiveness from their Lord."* (Qur'an 47:15)

Note the last term. Alongside the rivers is *maghfirah* — forgiveness. The Garden's provision and the believer's pardon are named together, which is the sūrah's own balance: the reverent are not described as sinless but as shielded, and the shield is accompanied by forgiveness for what got through.

**The Reversal of the Five Destructions**

Each of the five destroyed nations was undone by an element of its world, and verse 54's Garden is the reversal of all five.

Noah's people drowned; the Garden is watered but not flooded, its rivers flowing beneath rather than over. ʿĀd were torn out by wind and left like hollow trunks; the Garden's inhabitants are firmly seated, and its trees are not hollow but fruiting. Thamūd carved houses in mountains and were leveled by a sound; the Garden's dwellings are given rather than built, and its speech is peace. Lot's people were struck by a torrent of stones in the morning; the Garden's morning is the greeting of its inhabitants. Pharaoh's house was seized and drowned while pursuing; the Garden's inhabitants are received while at rest.

The Qur'an makes the reversal explicit in its own vocabulary, describing the Garden as a place where nothing harmful arrives:

> *"Abiding therein forever, they will desire no change from it (lā yabghūna ʿanhā ḥiwalā)."* (Qur'an 18:108)

> *"They will not hear therein idle talk or incitement to sin — only the saying: Peace, peace."* (Qur'an 56:25–26)

> *"And We shall remove whatever resentment is in their breasts; rivers flow beneath them, and they shall say: Praise be to God who guided us to this, and we would not have been guided had God not guided us."* (Qur'an 7:43)

The last of these contains the Garden's own explanation of itself: the inhabitants attribute their arrival to guidance rather than to their own qualification. The *muttaqī* who enters does not enter because his *taqwā* earned it; he enters because he was guided to it, and his guarding was itself part of the guidance. This is the same balance verse 49 established — everything created by measure — now seen from inside the outcome.

**The Faces That Look**

The Garden's highest description in the Qur'an is not its rivers but the vision:

> *"Faces that Day shall be radiant (nāḍirah), looking upon their Lord (ilā rabbihā nāẓirah)."* (Qur'an 75:22–23)

> *"For those who do good is the best (al-ḥusnā) and more (wa-ziyādah)."* (Qur'an 10:26)

The Prophet ﷺ explained the *ziyādah* in a report Muslim records from Ṣuhayb:

> *"When the people of the Garden enter it, God will say: Do you desire anything more that I may give you? They will say: Have You not whitened our faces? Have You not admitted us to the Garden and delivered us from the Fire? Then the veil will be lifted, and nothing will have been given to them more beloved than looking at their Lord."* (Muslim 181)

And the promise of that sight was given in this life:

> *"You will see your Lord on the Day of Resurrection as you see this moon, and you will not be harmed in seeing Him."* (al-Bukhārī 554; Muslim 633)

Verse 54's Gardens and river are the setting; verse 55 will name the occasion. The sūrah that opened with a moon split before a crowd closes with a river, a seat, and a King — and the tradition's report that the Prophet ﷺ recited this sūrah at the two ʿĪds (Muslim 891) places its closing verses before the largest assembly of the Muslim year, which is where the Qur'an intends them to be heard.

**Application: The Road and the Guarding**

Verse 54 is the sūrah's answer to its own opening, and the answer is available before the Hour arrives. The Hour has drawn near (1); the reverent are in Gardens (54). Between the two lies the whole of a life, and the verse names what occupies it: *taqwā*, the guarding.

The guarding is not a mood but a practice, and the practice is describable in the terms Ubayy ibn Kaʿb gave ʿUmar. Gather the garment — reduce the surface area of your life that can be caught; the Qur'an's legislation on the gaze, the tongue, the ear, income, and company is all garment-gathering. Watch the feet — direct attention at the next step rather than at the general direction, since the general direction is set and the next step is not. Step carefully — adjust the pace to the road rather than insisting that the road accommodate the pace.

The verse's promise is that this is not a life of deprivation. The thorny path is the world, not the guarding, and the destination is a Garden with a river — enclosed, shaded, watered, and seated. The criminals of verse 47 are inside error and burning; the reverent of verse 54 are inside Gardens and a river. Both are inside something, and the choice of which is made in the small steps of an ordinary day, on a road that nobody chose, toward a destination that verse 55 will finally name.

---
## Sūrah al-Qamar 54:55

> **Upon a seat of truth, before an Omnipotent King.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Fī Maqʿadi Ṣidqin: The Seat That Does Not Deceive**

*Fī maqʿadi ṣidqin ʿinda malikin muqtadir.* The sūrah's final verse has no verb. It is a pure locative statement — the reverent are in Gardens and a river, in a seat of truth, in the presence of a King Omnipotent — and the absence of a verb means the absence of an action. Nothing is being done in the final image of al-Qamar. Nobody is striving, fleeing, scrambling, disputing, hammering the camel, carving the mountain, or being dragged on his face. The sūrah that began with verbs of approaching, splitting, turning, opening, gushing, carrying, and running ends with a preposition and a noun.

*Maqʿad* is from *q-ʿ-d*, to sit. It is a noun of place: a sitting-place, a seat, a position occupied. The Qur'an uses the same construction of praise elsewhere, and the set of passages defines what *maqʿad ṣidq* means:

> *"And say: My Lord, cause me to enter a truthful entering (mudkhala ṣidq) and cause me to exit a truthful exiting (mukhraja ṣidq), and grant me from Thy presence an assisting authority."* (Qur'an 17:80)

> *"And give me a tongue of truth among the later generations (lisāna ṣidqin fī al-ākhirīn)."* (Qur'an 26:84)

> *"...and give glad tidings unto those who believe that theirs is a foot of truth (qadama ṣidqin) with their Lord."* (Qur'an 10:2)

The construction — a noun plus *ṣidq* in the genitive — produces the sense of "a true and honored X," an X that is what it claims to be. A *lisān ṣidq* is a reputation that survives because it was earned; a *qadam ṣidq* is a precedence that will not be revoked; a *maqʿad ṣidq* is a seat that will not prove false.

Al-Zamakhsharī explains it as a place of contentment, a position that satisfies. Al-Qurṭubī, al-Shawkānī, and al-Ṭabarī connect it to the Qur'an's description of the Garden's speech:

> *"They shall not hear therein idle talk (laghw) or incitement to sin (taʾthīm)."* (Qur'an 52:23)

> *"They shall not hear therein idle talk or lying (kidhdhābā)."* (Qur'an 78:35)

> *"They shall not hear therein idle talk — only: Peace, peace."* (Qur'an 19:62; cf. 56:25–26)

> *"Therein you shall hear no empty talk (lā tasmaʿu fīhā lāghiyah)."* (Qur'an 88:11)

A seat of truth is therefore a seat in which nothing false is spoken, nothing vain is heard, and nothing said will turn out to have been a lie. Al-Ṭabarī adds that it is the seat of those who gathered for the sake of truth — the company matching the place.

**The Seat Against the Assembly**

The contrast the sūrah has built makes this verse's meaning unmistakable. Lot's people were condemned for the third of their three crimes in these words:

> *"And do you commit evil in your assemblies (wa-taʾtūna fī nādīkumu al-munkar)?"* (Qur'an 29:29)

*Nādī* is the assembly, the gathering place of the town's men — the seat of the society's conversation. It is where approval is given and withheld, where norms are set, where a person's standing is decided. Lot's people had made their *nādī* a place of *munkar*, and the Qur'an condemns not only the act but the venue.

Verse 55 is the opposite venue. The *maqʿad ṣidq* is a seat where nothing false is present, where the company is gathered for truth, and where the standing of the person seated is not at issue, because it has been settled by the One he is seated before.

The Quraysh had their own *nādī*, and the sūrah's original audience knew it precisely. *Dār al-Nadwah*, the House of Council, was the assembly building beside the Kaʿbah where the chiefs of Makkah met, and it was there that the decision to kill the Prophet ﷺ was taken, and there that the slogans of verse 2 and verses 24–25 and verse 44 had their origin. The sūrah's final verse places the reverent in a seat of truth *ʿinda malik* — in the presence of a King — and the implicit comparison is with every assembly room in which men decide matters without Him.

There is also the comparison the sūrah has already drawn with Pharaoh's court, where the magicians said the sentence that defines the difference:

> *"They said: We shall never prefer thee over the clear signs that have come unto us, and over Him who created us. So decree what thou wilt decree; thou canst only decree concerning the life of this world."* (Qur'an 20:72)

A seat before a tyrant is a seat that can be revoked by the tyrant; the magicians knew it and stood anyway. A seat before *malikin muqtadir* cannot be revoked by anyone, because there is no one with the power to revoke it.

**ʿInda Malikin Muqtadir: The Name That Returns From Verse 42**

The sūrah's last word is *muqtadir*, and the word has already appeared — at verse 42, in the description of Pharaoh's destruction:

> *"So We seized them with the seizing of One Mighty, Omnipotent (ʿazīzin muqtadir)."* (Qur'an 54:42)

The inclusio is exact and it is the theological conclusion of the chapter. The same Name describes the seizure of a tyrant's regime and the presence in which the reverent are seated. The omnipotence that drowned Pharaoh's house is the omnipotence that hosts the believers, and the two are not different powers or different dispositions but one attribute operating in the two directions verse 49 announced: everything is created by measure, and the measure includes both outcomes.

*Muqtadir* is the eighth form of *q-d-r* — the root of *qadar*, measure, and of *qudrah*, power. The King before whom the reverent sit is therefore the King who measures, and their seat is itself measured. Verse 49's doctrine and verse 55's promise are the same statement: nothing is outside the measure, including the Garden, including the seat, including the position of the one seated.

*Malik* is one of the Qur'an's most frequent titles for God, and its occurrences define the term:

> *"Master of the Day of Judgment (māliki yawmi al-dīn)."* (Qur'an 1:4)

> *"Exalted be God, the King, the Truth (al-maliku al-ḥaqq)."* (Qur'an 20:114; 23:116)

> *"The King, the Holy (al-maliku al-quddūs)."* (Qur'an 62:1; cf. 59:23)

> *"The King of mankind (maliki al-nās)."* (Qur'an 114:2)

> *"Say: O God, Owner of all dominion (mālika al-mulk), Thou givest dominion unto whomsoever Thou willest and takest dominion from whomsoever Thou willest."* (Qur'an 3:26)

The last of these is the sūrah's answer to Pharaoh and to the Quraysh's *jamiʿun muntaṣir*. Dominion is given and taken by the One who owns it, and verse 55 places the reverent in the presence of its actual Owner rather than in the presence of those temporarily holding it.

**The Posture: Seated, Not Scrambling, Not Dragged**

The sūrah has described three postures of the condemned, and verse 55 answers all three.

Verse 8 described them *muhṭiʿīna ilā al-dāʿ* — scrambling toward the caller, running with heads down, moving without choice toward a voice they cannot refuse. Verse 48 described them *yusḥabūna ʿalā wujūhihim* — dragged upon their faces, the organ of dignity in contact with the fire. Verse 7 described them emerging from the graves like scattered locusts, humbled in their gazes.

Verse 55's posture is *qāʿid* — seated. The seat is the negation of the drag and of the scramble. The one who is seated has arrived, has been received, and is no longer in motion. And the seat is described as a *maqʿad ṣidq*, a true seat, which excludes the possibility of the seated person being mistaken about his position — the possibility that torments the ambitious in this world, where every seat is contested, provisional, and revocable.

The tradition connects the seat to the highest of the Garden's experiences. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Truly you will see your Lord as you see this moon; you will not be crowded in seeing Him."* (al-Bukhārī 554; Muslim 633)

And the report in Muslim from Ṣuhayb describes the scene that verse 55's *ʿinda*, "in the presence of," implies:

> *"Then the veil will be lifted, and nothing will have been given to them more beloved than looking at their Lord."* (Muslim 181)

The Qur'an states it:

> *"Faces that Day shall be radiant, looking upon their Lord."* (Qur'an 75:22–23)

The faces that in verse 7 were humbled and in verse 48 dragged upon the ground are, in this description, radiant and looking. The organ that was lowered involuntarily is raised by the sight it was made for.

**Ṣidq: The Qualification for the Seat**

If the seat is a seat of truth, the qualification for it is truthfulness, and the tradition states the connection in a report both collections record from ʿAbdullāh ibn Masʿūd:

> *"Hold to truthfulness, for truthfulness leads to righteousness (al-ṣidqa fa-inna al-ṣidqa yahdī ilā al-birr), and righteousness leads to the Garden. A man continues to be truthful and to strive for truth until he is written with God as a ṣiddīq (truthful one). And beware of lying, for lying leads to wickedness, and wickedness leads to the Fire. A man continues to lie and to strive for lying until he is written with God as a liar."* (al-Bukhārī 6094; Muslim 2607)

*Yuḥtamu ʿindallāhi ṣiddīqan* — he is written with God as a truthful one. The verb is writing, and the sūrah has just spent two verses on writing: everything they did is in the books (52), everything small and great is inscribed (53). The *maqʿad ṣidq* of verse 55 is the seat of the one whose record says *ṣiddīq*, and the record was written in the small repeated acts of verse 53.

The Prophet ﷺ identified the most difficult and most honored form of that truthfulness:

> *"The best of jihad is a word of truth (kalimat ḥaqq) before a tyrant ruler."* (Abū Dāwūd 4344; al-Tirmidhī 2174; Ibn Mājah 4011; Aḥmad; graded ḥasan ṣaḥīḥ)

The verse in al-Qamar is the reward for exactly that act. The people who spoke truth in assemblies that punished truthfulness — the magicians before Pharaoh, the man who came from the farthest part of the city in Sūrah Yāsīn (36:20), Lot before his *nādī*, Ṣāliḥ before Thamūd, Noah before nine centuries of mockery, Muhammad ﷺ before Dār al-Nadwah — are the people verse 55 seats. Their truthfulness cost them standing in every human assembly they belonged to, and the seat of truth is the standing they were given instead.

**Al-Maybudī on the Occasion of the Seat**

The commentator al-Maybudī, in his Persian exegesis, describes the occasion to which the verse refers:

> *"The people of the Garden will enter in upon the Invincible twice a day and recite the Qur'an for Him. Each person among them will sit in his own sitting place, on a pulpit of pearls, rubies, emeralds, gold, and silver, in accordance with his works. Their eyes will never be so delighted as they are by this. They will never have heard anything more tremendous and more beautiful. Then they will turn back to their lodging places, blissful, their eyes delighted, until the like of it on the morrow."*

The description gathers the sūrah's themes into one scene. The recitation of the Qur'an — the thing made easy to remember in verses 17, 22, 32, and 40 — is performed before its Sender. The sitting place is *in accordance with his works*, which is verse 53's record determining verse 55's position. And the King is *al-Muqtadir*, the same word the sūrah used for Pharaoh's seizing.

The tradition supplies the same picture in a report of the Prophet ﷺ:

> *"Truly the people of the Garden will see the inhabitants of the lofty chambers above them as you see a brilliant star on the horizon, in the east or the west — because of the difference in their degrees."* (al-Bukhārī 3256; Muslim 2831)

The degrees differ and the sight is shared, and the Qur'an's description of the seating's permanence closes the matter:

> *"Abiding therein forever, they will desire no change from it (lā yabghūna ʿanhā ḥiwalā)."* (Qur'an 18:108)

**The Sūrah Complete: From a Split Moon to a Settled Seat**

Verse 55 ends where verse 1 began, and the two ends are held together by the sūrah's own vocabulary. The Hour has drawn near — and the seat is before the King to whom the Hour belongs. The moon was split before a crowd that called it sorcery — and the reverent are seated before a King whose presence they will look upon without being harmed. Every matter reaches its settled state (3) — and the seat is *mustaqirr* in the truest sense, a position that will not be moved. Reports have come containing reproof (4) — and the reports are recited before the One who sent them. Conclusive wisdom did not avail (5) — and the wisdom's recipients are now seated in its presence.

The five nations are gone: drowned, uprooted, leveled, stoned, seized. The Quraysh's host was routed at Badr and the Hour remains their tryst. The guilty are astray and burning, dragged upon their faces, and their record is inscribed down to the atom. The reverent are in Gardens and a river, upon a seat of truth, before an Omnipotent King. Everything was created by measure, and the command is one, like the blinking of an eye.

That is the whole of al-Qamar in fifty-five verses: a miracle in the sky, five graves in the earth, one record, and two destinations. And the question the sūrah asked six times — *fa-hal min mudhakkir*, is there anyone who remembers? — is answered by the existence of the party named in its last two verses. There is. They remembered, and they are seated.

---
**[End of the commentary on Sūrah al-Qamar]**
